The Socket.IO Book

From "what is real-time?" to running Socket.IO at scale: every concept explained simply and then in depth, with production practices, a troubleshooting manual, labs, and an interview question bank for entry-level to senior roles.

  • 24 chapters
  • Socket.IO 4.8.4
  • Engine.IO protocol v4 · Socket.IO protocol v5
  • Examples run, not just written

How this book was verified

  • Every protocol example (handshake packets, wire format) was captured from a real running Socket.IO 4.8.4 server, and every default value was checked against the library's source.
  • Code was run, not just written: integration tests, labs and exercise solutions; servers in Python (python-socketio, Flask-SocketIO), Go, Rust (socketioxide) and Java (netty-socketio) tested against the official JS client; the Java client; WebTransport over HTTP/3; a custom adapter across two servers via Redis; a custom parser; Bun and Deno; the offline outbox and E2EE examples.
  • The nginx and HAProxy configurations were run in front of three nodes with the Redis adapter (Chapter 8, Lab B in Chapter 23).
  • Version history was checked against the published npm packages, and the security table (Chapter 6.10) against the npm/GitHub advisory database.
  • Not executable in the build environment, and checked against source code/docs instead: the Swift and Dart clients, Electron, and the deno.land/x Deno port. These spots are marked in their chapters.

If you remember one paragraph

Socket.IO is a library that gives you a two-way, event-based, real-time channel between a client (usually a browser or mobile app) and a server. Under the hood it uses a lower layer called Engine.IO, which opens a connection using HTTP long-polling or WebSocket (or WebTransport), keeps it alive with heartbeats, and upgrades to the best transport available. On top of that, Socket.IO adds named events, acknowledgements (request/response), automatic reconnection, rooms and namespaces (grouping), broadcasting, middleware, binary support and multi-server scaling via adapters (e.g. Redis). It is not a plain WebSocket library: a plain WebSocket client cannot talk to a Socket.IO server and vice-versa.

Foundations: What "Real-Time" Means and Why Socket.IO Exists

Goal of this chapter: understand the problem Socket.IO solves before touching any code. Most interview failures on Socket.IO come from not understanding this chapter, not from forgetting an API name.

1.1 The "explain like I'm 5" version

Imagine you are waiting for a pizza.

  • Way 1 — Keep asking (Polling): every 10 seconds you phone the pizza shop and ask, "Is my pizza ready?" Most of the time they say "No." You waste a lot of phone calls, and if the pizza becomes ready 1 second after you hung up, you only find out 9 seconds later.
  • Way 2 — Ask and wait on the line (Long-polling): you call and say, "Don't answer me until my pizza is ready." The shop keeps you on hold. As soon as the pizza is ready they tell you, you hang up, and you immediately call again to wait for the next news.
  • Way 3 — An open walkie-talkie (WebSocket): you and the shop each hold a walkie-talkie. Either of you can talk at any time, instantly, without dialling again.

Socket.IO is like a very smart helper who:

  1. Tries to give you the walkie-talkie (WebSocket). If walkie-talkies don't work in your building (old proxy, strict firewall), it falls back to "ask and wait on the line" (long-polling) so you still get your news.
  2. Keeps checking "are you still there?" (heartbeats) so it knows when the line is dead.
  3. If the line breaks, it automatically calls back (reconnection).
  4. Lets you give each message a name ("pizza-ready", "driver-left") — those are events.
  5. Lets you ask a question and get a reply ("Did you get my order?" → "Yes!") — those are acknowledgements.
  6. Lets the shop shout to groups of customers at once ("Everyone on Main Street, your driver is near!") — those are rooms and broadcasts.

Keep this picture in mind. Every chapter just zooms into one of these points.

1.2 How the normal web works: request/response (HTTP)

The web was built on HTTP, a request/response protocol:

Browser  ── "GET /messages" ──▶  Server
Browser  ◀── "here are your messages" ── Server
(connection is idle or closed; the server cannot speak first)

Key property: the client always speaks first. The server can only answer. A server cannot say "Hey, a new message just arrived!" on its own.

This is perfect for loading pages, but terrible for:

  • chat apps (new messages from other people),
  • notifications ("Someone liked your post"),
  • live dashboards (stock prices, sports scores, server metrics),
  • multiplayer games,
  • collaborative editing (Google-Docs-style),
  • delivery / ride tracking (driver's position updates),
  • live auctions / bidding,
  • IoT device telemetry and commands.

All of these need the server to push data to the client the moment something happens. That is what "real-time" means on the web: low-latency, server-initiated (push) communication, usually bi-directional.

1.3 The techniques for real-time on the web (history and comparison)

You must know all of these for interviews. Socket.IO uses two of them internally.

1.3.1 Short polling

The client asks every N seconds with a normal HTTP request.

setInterval(async () => {
  const res = await fetch("/api/messages?since=" + lastId);
  const messages = await res.json();
  render(messages);
}, 5000);
Pros Cons
Dead simple, works everywhere Wasteful: most requests return nothing
Stateless, easy to scale Latency up to N seconds
Every request carries full HTTP headers (cookies, etc.)

1.3.2 Long polling (a.k.a. "Comet")

The client sends a request; the server holds it open until it has data (or a timeout happens). When the response arrives, the client immediately sends a new request.

Client: GET /poll ───────────────(server waits… 12 s…)──▶ response: [msg]
Client: GET /poll (immediately again) ───(waits)───────▶ …
Pros Cons
Near-instant delivery One HTTP request per message batch (header overhead)
Works through almost every proxy/firewall Client→server messages need separate requests
Uses plain HTTP Server must hold many open requests

Socket.IO's HTTP long-polling transport is exactly this. It uses GET requests to receive data (held open by the server) and POST requests to send data.

1.3.3 Server-Sent Events (SSE / EventSource)

A standard browser API where the server keeps an HTTP response open and streams text events down it.

const es = new EventSource("/stream");
es.onmessage = (e) => console.log(e.data);
Pros Cons
Simple, standard, auto-reconnects with Last-Event-ID One-way only (server → client)
Plain HTTP, works with HTTP/2 Text only (no binary)
Under HTTP/1.1, browsers limit ~6 connections per domain

Socket.IO does not use SSE.

1.3.4 WebSocket

A standard protocol (RFC 6455) that starts as an HTTP request with an Upgrade: websocket header, then switches the TCP connection into a persistent, full-duplex (both sides talk at the same time) message channel.

GET /chat HTTP/1.1
Host: example.com
Upgrade: websocket
Connection: Upgrade
Sec-WebSocket-Key: dGhlIHNhbXBsZSBub25jZQ==
Sec-WebSocket-Version: 13

HTTP/1.1 101 Switching Protocols
Upgrade: websocket
Connection: Upgrade
Sec-WebSocket-Accept: s3pPLMBiTxaQ9kYGzzhZRbK+xOo=

After 101 Switching Protocols, both sides exchange small frames (2–14 bytes of overhead instead of hundreds of bytes of HTTP headers).

const ws = new WebSocket("wss://example.com/chat");
ws.onopen = () => ws.send("hello");
ws.onmessage = (e) => console.log(e.data);
Pros Cons
True bi-directional, very low overhead and latency No built-in reconnection
Text and binary No built-in events/rooms/acks — just raw messages
Supported in all modern browsers Some corporate proxies/firewalls/antivirus break it
No built-in heartbeat at the application level (browsers can't send ping frames)
You must build scaling/broadcast across servers yourself

1.3.5 WebTransport

A newer API built on HTTP/3 (QUIC). Supports multiple streams and unreliable datagrams. Socket.IO supports it since v4.7.0 (opt-in; it requires HTTP/3 and TLS and a separate HTTP/3 server such as @fails-components/webtransport). Browser support is still not universal (notably Safari support has lagged), so it is an option, not the default.

1.3.6 Comparison table (memorise this)

Feature Short polling Long polling SSE WebSocket Socket.IO
Direction C→S (pull) S→C push (emulated) S→C only Both Both
Latency High Low Low Lowest Lowest (on WS)
Overhead per message High High Low Very low Low (small protocol header)
Binary Yes (HTTP) Yes No Yes Yes
Auto-reconnect N/A Manual Yes No Yes
Fallback when WS blocked — — — No Yes (polling)
Named events No No Yes (event field) No Yes
Request/response (acks) Yes (HTTP) No No No Yes
Rooms / broadcast No No No No Yes
Multi-server broadcasting — — — Build it yourself Adapters (Redis etc.)

1.4 So what exactly is Socket.IO?

Official definition: Socket.IO is a library that enables low-latency, bidirectional and event-based communication between a client and a server.

It has two parts:

  1. Server library — socket.io (Node.js). There are also community/official implementations in other languages (Python python-socketio, Java netty-socketio, Go, Rust socketioxide, Deno, etc.).
  2. Client library — socket.io-client (browser & Node.js). There are also official clients for Java/Android, Swift/iOS, C++, Dart, Python (python-socketio also includes a client), .NET (community), Rust, etc.

And it is built in two layers:

┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│ Your application code: socket.emit("chat", msg)            │
├────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│ Socket.IO  (events, acks, rooms, namespaces, broadcasting,  │
│            reconnection, middleware, binary, adapters)      │
├────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│ Engine.IO  (the low-level connection: handshake, transports │
│            polling / websocket / webtransport, upgrade,     │
│            heartbeats, packet encoding)                     │
├────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│ HTTP long-polling   |   WebSocket   |   WebTransport        │
├────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│ TCP (or QUIC for WebTransport)                              │
└────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

ELI5: Engine.IO is the phone line (it makes sure there is a working line and checks it's still alive). Socket.IO is the language you speak on the phone (with names for messages, ways to reply, groups of listeners, etc.).

1.4.1 What Socket.IO is NOT (very common interview trap)

  1. It is not a WebSocket implementation. It uses WebSocket as one transport, but it adds its own protocol on top. Every message has extra metadata (packet type, namespace, ack id). So: - A plain new WebSocket("ws://server") client cannot connect to a Socket.IO server (well — it can open the TCP/WebSocket connection to /socket.io/?EIO=4&transport=websocket, but it won't speak the protocol unless you implement it by hand). - A Socket.IO client cannot connect to a plain WebSocket server (like one written with ws).
  2. It is not a message queue / broker (like Kafka or RabbitMQ). By default there is no persistence and at-most-once delivery: if the client is disconnected when you emit, the message is gone (unless you use connection state recovery or build your own storage — see Chapter 5).
  3. It is not meant for server-to-server communication at scale (use gRPC, HTTP, a message broker). It can be used from Node to Node with socket.io-client, but that is not its primary purpose.
  4. It is not HTTP/REST replacement for everything. Loading data, uploads of large files, cacheable resources → use HTTP. Use Socket.IO for push and live interaction.

1.5 Why use Socket.IO instead of raw WebSocket? (the "features" list)

Each of these is explained in depth in later chapters, but you should be able to recite this list in an interview:

  1. HTTP long-polling fallback — if a WebSocket connection cannot be established (proxies, firewalls, antivirus, old networks), the connection still works.
  2. Automatic reconnection — with exponential back-off and jitter.
  3. Heartbeat / disconnection detection — Engine.IO pings regularly so both sides know if the other is gone, even when TCP doesn't notice (e.g. a laptop lid closed).
  4. Packet buffering — events emitted while disconnected are buffered on the client and sent upon reconnection (by default).
  5. Acknowledgements — callback-based or Promise-based request/response over the socket.
  6. Named events — emit("order:created", data) instead of parsing raw strings.
  7. Automatic serialization — send JS objects, arrays, numbers, Buffer/ArrayBuffer/ Blob; no manual JSON.stringify.
  8. Binary support mixed with JSON.
  9. Broadcasting — to all clients, to all except one, to rooms.
  10. Rooms — server-side groups of sockets (e.g. a chat channel, a user's devices).
  11. Namespaces — split one connection into separate logical channels (multiplexing), each with its own handlers and middleware.
  12. Middleware — authentication and validation at connection time and per event.
  13. Horizontal scaling — adapters (Redis, Redis Streams, MongoDB, Postgres, cluster, cloud pub/sub) to broadcast across many server processes.
  14. Connection state recovery (v4.6+) — after a short disconnection, restore the socket's id, rooms, data, and missed packets.
  15. Admin UI, TypeScript types, many client implementations.

When raw WebSocket might be better:

  • You need minimal overhead and control (e.g. high-frequency trading feeds, games with custom binary protocols) and you are happy to implement reconnection/heartbeats.
  • You need to interoperate with non-Socket.IO clients (third-party devices, standard protocols like GraphQL subscriptions over graphql-ws, MQTT over WebSocket).
  • You have extreme connection counts and want to use something like uWebSockets.js directly (although Socket.IO can also run on uWebSockets.js — see Chapter 11).

Alternatives you should be able to name: raw ws library, uWebSockets.js, SockJS, SignalR (.NET), Phoenix Channels (Elixir), Action Cable (Rails), Centrifugo, Pusher/Ably (hosted), Firebase Realtime Database / Firestore listeners, Supabase Realtime, AWS API Gateway WebSocket APIs, AWS AppSync, Azure Web PubSub (which can even host Socket.IO), GraphQL subscriptions, MQTT (IoT), gRPC streaming, SSE.

1.6 Key vocabulary (you will see these words everywhere)

Term ELI5 meaning Technical meaning
Server (io) The pizza shop The Server instance from socket.io, attached to an HTTP server
Client You The socket.io-client running in a browser/app
Socket Your personal phone line with the shop One connection between one client and one namespace. On the server, one Socket object per client per namespace
Event A message with a label A named message: socket.emit("name", ...args)
emit Say something Send an event
on Listen for something Register a handler for an event
Acknowledgement (ack) "Got it!" reply A callback the receiver calls to respond to a specific emit
Broadcast Shout to many Send an event to many sockets at once
Room A group chat A server-side named group of sockets you can broadcast to
Namespace A separate department in the shop A communication channel (/, /admin, /chat) sharing the same underlying connection
Transport The type of phone line polling, websocket, webtransport
Upgrade Switching from hold-music line to walkie-talkie Moving from polling to WebSocket after connecting
Heartbeat (ping/pong) "Still there?" "Yes!" Periodic packets to detect dead connections
Handshake Saying hello and exchanging IDs The first request; server returns session id (sid) and settings
Adapter The shop's internal messenger between branches The component that stores rooms and forwards broadcasts (in-memory, or Redis etc. across servers)
Middleware The bouncer at the door Functions run before a connection is accepted or before an event is handled
Sticky session Always talking to the same shop branch Load-balancer setting that routes all requests of a client to the same server

1.7 The life of a Socket.IO connection in 10 steps (big picture)

You'll see each of these in detail later. Here is the whole story once:

  1. The client calls io("https://example.com").
  2. The client sends an HTTP GET to /socket.io/?EIO=4&transport=polling (the Engine.IO handshake). (If you configured transports: ["websocket"], it opens a WebSocket directly instead.)
  3. The server replies with an Engine.IO OPEN packet containing a session id and timing settings. A real response captured from Socket.IO 4.8.4:
0{"sid":"HSJR3YuCOE9jPqHEAAAA","upgrades":["websocket"],"pingInterval":25000,"pingTimeout":20000,"maxPayload":1000000}
  1. The client sends a Socket.IO CONNECT packet for the namespace (40 for the main namespace /, optionally with an auth payload).
  2. The server runs middleware (e.g. auth). If accepted, it replies 40{"sid":"..."} (a CONNECT with the Socket.IO socket id — which is different from the Engine.IO sid) and fires io.on("connection"). If rejected, it replies with a CONNECT_ERROR (44) and the client receives connect_error.
  3. Meanwhile the client tries to upgrade to WebSocket in the background. If it works, all further traffic moves to the WebSocket; polling is closed.
  4. Both sides exchange events (42["chat","hi"]) and acks (43...).
  5. Every pingInterval (25 s) the server sends a PING (2); the client must reply PONG (3) within pingTimeout (20 s), or the server considers it disconnected. The client also expects pings: if none arrives in pingInterval + pingTimeout, it considers the connection dead.
  6. If the connection dies, the server fires disconnect with a reason, and the client starts reconnecting with exponential back-off.
  7. On reconnection, the client gets a new socket id (unless connection state recovery succeeded), so the server must rejoin rooms etc. (often in the connection handler, e.g. based on the authenticated user id).

1.8 Versions and compatibility (frequently asked)

Socket.IO server Engine.IO server Socket.IO protocol Engine.IO protocol Notes
1.x 1.x 3 3 Ancient
2.x 3.x 4 3 Client sends pings; reconnect events on socket; auto-CORS for all origins
3.x 4.x 5 4 Big breaking release (Nov 2020)
4.x 5.x / 6.x 5 4 Current; v4 is backward-compatible with v3 clients

Important compatibility facts:

  • A v3/v4 client works with a v3/v4 server (same protocols).
  • A v2 client can connect to a v3/v4 server only if the server enables allowEIO3: true.
  • A v3/v4 client cannot connect to a v2 server.
  • Client and server versions are independent packages but the protocol must match.

Big changes in v3 (vs v2) — classic interview question:

  1. CORS is no longer enabled by default. You must configure the cors option.
  2. Heartbeat direction reversed — the server sends PING, the client answers PONG (in v2 the client pinged). This works better with browsers that throttle timers in background tabs.
  3. Reconnection events moved from the Socket to the Manager (socket.io.on("reconnect", ...) instead of socket.on("reconnect", ...)).
  4. socket.rooms is a Set (was an object).
  5. io.set() removed; configuration is via constructor options.
  6. No more implicit connection to the default namespace when connecting to another namespace.
  7. socket.binary() removed, binary detection is automatic.
  8. The auth option: credentials sent in the CONNECT packet, read via socket.handshake.auth.
  9. Payload encoding changed (in Engine.IO v4: record separator \x1e instead of length prefixes, binary as base64 with b prefix in polling).
  10. Default maxHttpBufferSize reduced from 100 MB to 1 MB.
  11. The cookie (io cookie) is disabled by default.
  12. perMessageDeflate disabled by default (WebSocket compression).
  13. Middleware errors: next(err) sends a CONNECT_ERROR; the client receives connect_error with err.message and err.data.
  14. The Socket#use() per-socket middleware signature changed and was later re-added in 3.0.5.
  15. Written in TypeScript.

Additions during v4 (approximate release markers — know the features, versions are a bonus):

Version Notable additions
4.0 (Mar 2021) Typed events (TypeScript generics), io.except(), utility methods io.socketsJoin() / socketsLeave() / disconnectSockets() / fetchSockets()
4.1 io.serverSideEmit() (events between Socket.IO servers in a cluster)
4.4 io.attachApp() — run on uWebSockets.js; socket.timeout(ms) for acks on the client
4.5 Acks with timeout on the server (socket.timeout(ms).emit(...)), broadcast with acks (io.timeout(ms).emit(...)), onAnyOutgoing() / prependAnyOutgoing(), serverSideEmit() with ack
4.6 (Feb 2023) Connection state recovery, emitWithAck() (Promise API), client retries + ackTimeout (at-least-once from client), io.engine.use() for Express middleware, cleanupEmptyChildNamespaces
4.7 (Jun 2023) WebTransport support
4.8 (Sep 2024) Client: fetch-based polling transport (useful in service workers / runtimes without XMLHttpRequest), custom transport implementations, tryAllTransports option

Interview tip: you will not be failed for not knowing the exact minor version. You will be asked "what changed from v2 to v3/v4?" and "what is connection state recovery?".

1.9 When should you (and shouldn't you) choose Socket.IO? — decision guide

Choose Socket.IO when:

  • You build a web/mobile product needing bi-directional real-time features (chat, notifications, presence, live updates, collaboration, light games).
  • Your users may be behind restrictive networks (enterprises, schools, hotels) — fallback matters.
  • You want rooms, broadcasting and multi-server fan-out without building them.
  • Your team uses Node.js (or a language with a mature Socket.IO server).

Consider alternatives when:

  • One-way server→client updates only and HTTP/2 → SSE might be simpler.
  • You must interoperate with standard WebSocket clients → raw WebSocket / ws.
  • You need durable messaging with replay and exactly-once processing → a broker (Kafka, RabbitMQ, NATS JetStream) behind your Socket.IO servers.
  • You don't want to operate infrastructure → hosted services (Ably, Pusher, Azure Web PubSub, AWS AppSync/API Gateway WebSockets, Firebase).
  • Extremely latency-sensitive games → custom UDP / WebRTC data channels / WebTransport datagrams.

1.10 Chapter summary

  • HTTP is request/response: the server can't speak first. Real-time apps need push.
  • Techniques: short polling, long polling, SSE, WebSocket, WebTransport.
  • Socket.IO = Engine.IO (connection, transports, heartbeats, upgrade) + Socket.IO layer (events, acks, rooms, namespaces, broadcast, reconnection, middleware, adapters).
  • Socket.IO is not WebSocket; it's a protocol on top of WebSocket/polling.
  • Default delivery guarantee: at-most-once, ordered.
  • v3 was the big breaking release (CORS, heartbeat reversal, reconnect events on Manager, rooms as Set, auth option, 1 MB buffer default). v4 is compatible with v3 clients.

Next: Chapter 2 — Getting started

Getting Started: Your First Server and Client

Goal: build a working real-time app from scratch, understand every line, and learn the different ways to set up the server (standalone, with http, with Express, with HTTPS, with other frameworks) and the client (CDN, bundler, Node).

2.1 Install

# server
npm install socket.io

# client (when using a bundler such as Vite/Webpack, or in Node.js)
npm install socket.io-client
  • socket.io (server) also serves the client bundle by default at /socket.io/socket.io.js (option serveClient: true). Handy for demos; in real projects you normally install socket.io-client via npm and bundle it.
  • Requirements: a supported Node.js version (Node 10+ is technically supported by socket.io 4; use a current LTS in practice).

Optional performance add-ons for the ws WebSocket engine (native modules):

npm install --save-optional bufferutil utf-8-validate

(See Chapter 11. On recent Node versions ws can use Node's built-in buffer.isUtf8(), so utf-8-validate matters less; bufferutil still speeds up frame masking/unmasking.)

2.2 The smallest possible server

// server.js  (CommonJS)
const { Server } = require("socket.io");

const io = new Server(3000); // creates an HTTP server listening on port 3000

io.on("connection", (socket) => {
  console.log("a client connected:", socket.id);

  socket.emit("welcome", "Hello from the server!"); // send to THIS client

  socket.on("ping-me", (msg) => {                    // listen to THIS client
    console.log("client says:", msg);
  });

  socket.on("disconnect", (reason) => {
    console.log("client left:", socket.id, reason);
  });
});

ESM version:

// server.mjs
import { Server } from "socket.io";
const io = new Server(3000);

Line-by-line (ELI5):

  • new Server(3000) — "Open a pizza shop at door number 3000."
  • io.on("connection", (socket) => {...}) — "Every time a new customer walks in, give me their personal phone line (socket)."
  • socket.id — a random unique id for this connection (e.g. "KykvYCRwmIsIifIyAAAA"). It changes on every reconnection — never use it as a user id.
  • socket.emit("welcome", ...) — "Say 'welcome' to this customer only."
  • socket.on("ping-me", ...) — "When this customer says 'ping-me', do this."
  • socket.on("disconnect", ...) — "When this customer leaves (or the line dies)."

2.3 The smallest possible client (Node.js)

// client.js
const { io } = require("socket.io-client");

const socket = io("http://localhost:3000");

socket.on("connect", () => {
  console.log("connected with id", socket.id);
  socket.emit("ping-me", "hi server!");
});

socket.on("welcome", (msg) => console.log(msg));

socket.on("disconnect", (reason) => console.log("disconnected:", reason));

Run node server.js then node client.js. You'll see both sides talk.

2.4 The browser client — three ways

(a) Served by the server itself

<script src="/socket.io/socket.io.js"></script>
<script>
  const socket = io(); // no URL → connects to the host that served the page
</script>

(b) From a CDN

<script src="https://cdn.socket.io/4.8.1/socket.io.min.js"
        integrity="(use the SRI hash from the official docs)"
        crossorigin="anonymous"></script>
<script>
  const socket = io("https://api.example.com");
</script>

(Also available from jsDelivr/cdnjs/unpkg. Pin the exact version.)

(c) With a bundler (the normal way in React/Vue/Angular apps)

import { io } from "socket.io-client";
const socket = io("https://api.example.com");

Bundle size: the 4.8.4 socket.io.min.js is ~47 KB minified, ~14.8 KB gzipped. There is also a socket.io.msgpack.min.js build using the msgpack parser, and an "es" build for tree-shaking.

URL forms of io()

io();                                 // same origin, main namespace "/"
io("/admin");                         // same origin, namespace "/admin"
io("https://api.example.com");        // other origin, namespace "/"
io("https://api.example.com/admin");  // other origin, namespace "/admin"
io("https://api.example.com", { path: "/my-custom-path/" }); // custom HTTP path

Very common confusion: the part after the host in the URL is the namespace, not the HTTP path! io("https://example.com/chat") connects to namespace /chat using the HTTP path /socket.io/. To change the HTTP path you use the path option (and the same path on the server).

2.5 Attaching to an existing HTTP server (the usual way)

Your app usually already has an HTTP server (for REST endpoints, static files…). Attach Socket.IO to it so both share the same port.

With Node's http

const { createServer } = require("node:http");
const { Server } = require("socket.io");

const httpServer = createServer((req, res) => {
  res.end("regular HTTP response");
});

const io = new Server(httpServer, { /* options */ });

io.on("connection", (socket) => { /* ... */ });

httpServer.listen(3000);

Socket.IO intercepts only requests whose path starts with /socket.io/ (configurable); all other requests go to your handler.

With Express (most common in interviews and in companies)

const express = require("express");
const { createServer } = require("node:http");
const { Server } = require("socket.io");

const app = express();
const httpServer = createServer(app);   // <-- wrap the express app in an http server
const io = new Server(httpServer, {
  cors: { origin: "http://localhost:5173" }, // the frontend dev server origin
});

app.get("/", (req, res) => res.send("API is up"));

io.on("connection", (socket) => {
  console.log("connected", socket.id);
});

httpServer.listen(3000, () => console.log("listening on :3000"));

Bug #1 of all time: calling app.listen(3000) instead of httpServer.listen(3000). app.listen() creates a new, different HTTP server that Socket.IO is not attached to, so the client gets 404 on /socket.io/.... Always listen on the server you passed to new Server().

(Alternatively: const server = app.listen(3000); const io = new Server(server); — this works because app.listen returns the http server it created.)

With HTTPS

const { readFileSync } = require("node:fs");
const { createServer } = require("node:https");
const { Server } = require("socket.io");

const httpsServer = createServer({
  key: readFileSync("/path/to/key.pem"),
  cert: readFileSync("/path/to/cert.pem"),
});
const io = new Server(httpsServer);
httpsServer.listen(443);

In production TLS is usually terminated by a load balancer / reverse proxy (nginx, ALB, Cloudflare), and Node listens on plain HTTP behind it. The client still uses https:///wss://.

With HTTP/2

const { readFileSync } = require("node:fs");
const { createSecureServer } = require("node:http2");
const { Server } = require("socket.io");

const httpServer = createSecureServer({
  allowHTTP1: true, // REQUIRED: WebSocket upgrade still uses HTTP/1.1
  key: readFileSync("key.pem"),
  cert: readFileSync("cert.pem"),
});
const io = new Server(httpServer);
httpServer.listen(3000);

With other frameworks

  • Fastify: fastify-socket.io plugin, or new Server(fastify.server) after fastify.ready().
  • Koa: const server = http.createServer(app.callback()); new Server(server).
  • NestJS: @nestjs/websockets + @nestjs/platform-socket.io, @WebSocketGateway() (Chapter 9).
  • Next.js: a custom server, or a separate Socket.IO service (Chapter 9). Serverless functions (Vercel functions, AWS Lambda) are not suitable for hosting long-lived Socket.IO servers.
  • Deno / Bun: there is an official Deno port of the server; Bun runs the Node package through its Node compatibility layer. Test your exact runtime version before relying on it in production.
  • uWebSockets.js: io.attachApp(uwsApp) (Chapter 11).

Standalone (Socket.IO creates the HTTP server)

const io = new Server(3000, { /* options */ });
// or
const io = new Server({ /* options */ });
io.listen(3000);

2.6 CORS — you will hit this on day one

If the page is served from http://localhost:5173 and the Socket.IO server runs at http://localhost:3000, the browser treats that as cross-origin. Since v3 Socket.IO does not allow cross-origin requests by default. Symptom in the browser console:

Access to XMLHttpRequest at 'http://localhost:3000/socket.io/?EIO=4&transport=polling&t=...'
from origin 'http://localhost:5173' has been blocked by CORS policy

Fix on the server:

const io = new Server(httpServer, {
  cors: {
    origin: ["http://localhost:5173", "https://app.example.com"],
    methods: ["GET", "POST"],      // optional; defaults of the `cors` package are fine
    credentials: true,             // only if you need cookies (see below)
  },
});

The cors option is passed to the popular cors npm package, so it accepts the same options (origin can be a string, array, RegExp, or a function (origin, callback) => callback(null, true|false)).

If you need cookies (e.g. session-cookie authentication), the client must also set:

const socket = io("https://api.example.com", { withCredentials: true });

and the server must set credentials: true and a specific origin (not "*" — browsers reject Access-Control-Allow-Origin: * with credentials).

Important nuance (interview favourite): CORS applies to the HTTP long-polling requests (they are XHR/fetch). The WebSocket handshake is not subject to CORS in browsers — any website can open a WebSocket to your server. So to protect WebSocket connections from malicious sites you must check the Origin header yourself (with allowRequest) and/or use proper authentication (tokens). See Chapter 6 (Cross-Site WebSocket Hijacking).

2.7 A complete first project: a mini chat (with explanations)

Server — index.js

const express = require("express");
const { createServer } = require("node:http");
const { join } = require("node:path");
const { Server } = require("socket.io");

const app = express();
const server = createServer(app);
const io = new Server(server);

app.get("/", (req, res) => {
  res.sendFile(join(__dirname, "index.html"));
});

io.on("connection", (socket) => {
  // 1. Tell everybody else someone joined
  socket.broadcast.emit("system", "A user joined");

  // 2. When this user sends a chat message, send it to EVERYONE (including the sender)
  socket.on("chat message", (msg, ack) => {
    if (typeof msg !== "string" || msg.length === 0 || msg.length > 500) {
      return typeof ack === "function" && ack({ ok: false, error: "invalid message" });
    }
    io.emit("chat message", { text: msg, from: socket.id, at: Date.now() });
    if (typeof ack === "function") ack({ ok: true });
  });

  // 3. Typing indicator — to everyone except the sender, and it's OK to lose it
  socket.on("typing", () => socket.broadcast.volatile.emit("typing", socket.id));

  socket.on("disconnect", () => io.emit("system", "A user left"));
});

server.listen(3000, () => console.log("http://localhost:3000"));

Client — index.html

<!doctype html>
<html>
  <body>
    <ul id="messages"></ul>
    <p id="typing"></p>
    <form id="form"><input id="input" autocomplete="off" /><button>Send</button></form>

    <script src="/socket.io/socket.io.js"></script>
    <script>
      const socket = io();
      const form = document.getElementById("form");
      const input = document.getElementById("input");
      const messages = document.getElementById("messages");

      function addLine(text) {
        const li = document.createElement("li");
        li.textContent = text;           // textContent, NOT innerHTML → prevents XSS
        messages.appendChild(li);
      }

      form.addEventListener("submit", (e) => {
        e.preventDefault();
        if (!input.value) return;
        socket.emit("chat message", input.value, (res) => {
          if (!res.ok) alert(res.error);   // server acknowledged
        });
        input.value = "";
      });

      input.addEventListener("input", () => socket.emit("typing"));

      socket.on("chat message", (m) => addLine(m.from.slice(0, 5) + ": " + m.text));
      socket.on("system", (t) => addLine("[system] " + t));
      socket.on("typing", (id) => {
        document.getElementById("typing").textContent = id.slice(0, 5) + " is typing…";
        clearTimeout(window.__t);
        window.__t = setTimeout(() => (document.getElementById("typing").textContent = ""), 1000);
      });
    </script>
  </body>
</html>

What you just used (each is a later chapter):

Code Meaning
socket.emit send to one client
io.emit send to all connected clients (of the main namespace)
socket.broadcast.emit send to all except this socket
volatile OK to drop if the client isn't ready to receive
(msg, ack) => ack(...) acknowledgement (request/response)
textContent basic XSS prevention for user-generated content

2.8 The "emit cheat-sheet" (memorise — asked in nearly every interview)

io.on("connection", (socket) => {
  // to the sender only
  socket.emit("hello", "you");

  // to all clients in the current namespace, including the sender
  io.emit("hello", "everyone");

  // to all clients except the sender
  socket.broadcast.emit("hello", "everyone but you");

  // to all clients in room "game", except the sender
  socket.to("game").emit("hello", "players but you");

  // to all clients in room "game1" and/or "game2", except the sender
  socket.to("game1").to("game2").emit("hello");

  // to all clients in room "game", including the sender
  io.in("game").emit("hello");          // .in() is an alias of .to()

  // to all clients in namespace "/admin"
  io.of("/admin").emit("hello");

  // to room "notifications" in namespace "/admin"
  io.of("/admin").to("notifications").emit("hello");

  // to an individual socket by id (private message) — each socket auto-joins a room named by its id
  io.to(otherSocketId).emit("hello");

  // to everyone except those in room "banned"
  io.except("banned").emit("hello");

  // with acknowledgement
  socket.emit("question", "do you like pizza?", (answer) => {});

  // with acknowledgement + timeout
  socket.timeout(5000).emit("question", (err, answer) => {});

  // promise style
  const answer = await socket.timeout(5000).emitWithAck("question");

  // without compression
  socket.compress(false).emit("uncompressed", "that's rough");

  // may be dropped if the client is not ready to receive
  socket.volatile.emit("maybe", "do you really need it?");

  // to all clients on THIS node only (when using multiple nodes + adapter)
  io.local.emit("hi", "my lovely babies");

  // to all connected clients
  io.emit("an event sent to all connected clients");
});

Client side:

socket.emit("hello", "server");                       // basic
socket.emit("question", (answer) => {});              // with ack
socket.timeout(5000).emit("question", (err, answer) => {}); // ack + timeout
const answer = await socket.emitWithAck("question");  // promise (no timeout unless ackTimeout/timeout set)
socket.volatile.emit("mouse-move", { x, y });         // droppable
socket.compress(false).emit("x");

A client cannot broadcast. socket.broadcast exists only on the server. If a client wants to send to others, it sends to the server and the server relays.

2.9 Server options at a glance (details in later chapters)

const io = new Server(httpServer, {
  // ---- Socket.IO-level options ----
  path: "/socket.io/",                // HTTP path handled
  serveClient: true,                  // serve /socket.io/socket.io.js
  adapter: undefined,                 // e.g. createAdapter(pub, sub) for Redis
  parser: undefined,                  // custom parser e.g. require("socket.io-msgpack-parser")
  connectTimeout: 45000,              // ms a client has to join a namespace after the Engine.IO handshake
  connectionStateRecovery: undefined, // { maxDisconnectionDuration: 120000, skipMiddlewares: true }
  cleanupEmptyChildNamespaces: false, // for dynamic namespaces

  // ---- Engine.IO-level options ----
  pingInterval: 25000,                // ms between server pings
  pingTimeout: 20000,                 // ms to wait for a pong before closing
  upgradeTimeout: 10000,              // ms to complete a transport upgrade
  maxHttpBufferSize: 1e6,             // max message size in bytes (1 MB) before closing the socket
  allowRequest: undefined,            // (req, callback) => callback(err, allowed)
  transports: ["polling", "websocket"], // (+ "webtransport" if enabled)
  allowUpgrades: true,
  perMessageDeflate: false,           // WebSocket compression (off by default)
  httpCompression: true,              // gzip/deflate for polling (threshold 1024 bytes)
  wsEngine: require("ws").Server,     // WebSocket server implementation
  cors: undefined,                    // options passed to the `cors` package
  cookie: false,                      // the "io" cookie (sticky session helper)
  allowEIO3: false,                   // accept Socket.IO v2 clients
  initialPacket: undefined,
});

All defaults above were verified against socket.io@4.8.4 / engine.io@6.6.11 source.

2.10 Client options at a glance

const socket = io("https://api.example.com", {
  // ---- Manager (connection/reconnection) options ----
  reconnection: true,
  reconnectionAttempts: Infinity,
  reconnectionDelay: 1000,     // initial delay
  reconnectionDelayMax: 5000,  // max delay
  randomizationFactor: 0.5,    // jitter: delay ± 50%
  timeout: 20000,              // connection timeout (before connect_error)
  autoConnect: true,           // connect immediately on io()
  parser: undefined,

  // ---- Socket options ----
  auth: { token: "abc" },      // or (cb) => cb({ token: getToken() })
  ackTimeout: undefined,       // default timeout for acks (used with retries)
  retries: undefined,          // number of retries for emits (at-least-once)

  // ---- Engine.IO (low-level) options ----
  path: "/socket.io/",
  transports: ["polling", "websocket", "webtransport"], // order to try (client default)
  upgrade: true,               // try to upgrade polling -> websocket
  rememberUpgrade: false,      // skip polling next time if WS worked before
  tryAllTransports: false,     // (4.8+) try next transport if the first fails
  query: { roomHint: "x" },    // extra query params on the handshake
  extraHeaders: { "x-foo": "bar" }, // NOT applied to browser WebSocket (browser API can't set headers)
  withCredentials: false,      // send cookies cross-origin (polling)
  forceNew: false,             // create a new Manager (separate connection)
  multiplex: true,             // share connection across namespaces
  closeOnBeforeunload: false,  // close silently on page unload
  timestampRequests: true,     // cache-buster `t=` on polling requests
});

On the client the default transports includes "webtransport", but it is only tried if the server enables it — with the default server setup you get polling → websocket.

2.11 Project structure for anything bigger than a demo

Don't put everything inside io.on("connection"). A maintainable layout:

src/
  server.ts              # creates http server + io, wires everything
  socket/
    index.ts             # createSocketServer(httpServer) → io
    middlewares/
      auth.ts            # io.use(authMiddleware)
      rateLimit.ts
    handlers/
      chat.handler.ts    # registerChatHandlers(io, socket)
      presence.handler.ts
      notification.handler.ts
    schemas/             # zod/joi payload schemas
    events.ts            # TypeScript event maps (shared with client!)
  services/              # business logic (NOT socket-aware)
  repositories/          # DB access

// socket/handlers/chat.handler.js
module.exports = (io, socket) => {
  const sendMessage = async (payload, ack) => { /* validate → service → emit */ };
  const deleteMessage = async (payload, ack) => { /* ... */ };

  socket.on("chat:send", sendMessage);
  socket.on("chat:delete", deleteMessage);
};

// socket/index.js
const registerChatHandlers = require("./handlers/chat.handler");
const registerPresenceHandlers = require("./handlers/presence.handler");

io.on("connection", (socket) => {
  registerChatHandlers(io, socket);
  registerPresenceHandlers(io, socket);
});

This is the structure recommended by the official docs ("application structure") and used by most companies. Chapter 13 expands it into a full organisational standard.

2.12 Chapter summary

  • new Server(httpServer, options) attaches Socket.IO to an existing server; listen on that same server.
  • The client connects with io(url, options); the URL path is the namespace; the HTTP path is the path option.
  • CORS must be configured since v3; WebSocket handshakes are not protected by CORS.
  • Emit cheat sheet: socket.emit, io.emit, socket.broadcast.emit, socket.to(room), io.to(room), io.of(nsp), io.except(), acks, timeout, emitWithAck, volatile, compress, local.
  • Organise handlers by feature, keep business logic out of socket handlers.

Next: Chapter 3 — Events and acknowledgements

Events, Emitting, Listening and Acknowledgements

Goal: master the core of Socket.IO — sending and receiving events, what data you can send, request/response with acknowledgements, timeouts, promises, catch-all listeners, volatile and compressed events, reserved event names, and ordering.

3.1 ELI5: events are labelled envelopes

Every message in Socket.IO is like an envelope with:

  • a label written on the front (the event name, e.g. "chat:message"),
  • things inside (zero or more arguments: strings, numbers, objects, files…),
  • optionally a reply slip ("please sign and send back") — the acknowledgement.

The receiver has a box per label (socket.on("chat:message", handler)); when an envelope with that label arrives, the handler opens it.

3.2 emit and on — the basics

// sender
socket.emit("hello", "world");

// receiver
socket.on("hello", (arg) => {
  console.log(arg); // "world"
});

Works in both directions with the same API:

Direction Sender Receiver
client → server socket.emit() in browser socket.on() inside io.on("connection")
server → client socket.emit() / io.emit() on server socket.on() in browser

Multiple arguments

You can send any number of arguments:

socket.emit("move", 10, 20, { piece: "knight" }, Uint8Array.from([1, 2, 3]));

socket.on("move", (x, y, meta, bytes) => { /* ... */ });

Best practice: prefer one object argument for anything non-trivial (emit("move", { x, y, piece })). It's self-documenting, easier to validate, extend and version, and avoids argument-order bugs.

What data can you send? (serialization rules)

Socket.IO's default parser serializes arguments with JSON.stringify semantics, plus special handling for binary.

Type Supported? What arrives
string, number, boolean, null ✅ same
plain objects, arrays ✅ deep copies
Buffer (Node), ArrayBuffer, typed arrays (Uint8Array…), Blob/File (browser) ✅ binary In Node receiver: Buffer; in browser receiver: ArrayBuffer (default binaryType)
Date ⚠️ becomes an ISO string (toJSON) — convert back yourself
Map, Set ❌ become {} — convert to arrays/objects first ([...map], Object.fromEntries(map))
undefined ⚠️ object properties with undefined are dropped; undefined in arrays becomes null
NaN, Infinity ⚠️ become null
BigInt ❌ JSON.stringify throws TypeError — convert to string
functions ❌ (except as the last argument, which becomes an ack callback) —
class instances ⚠️ only own enumerable properties; prototype/methods lost; toJSON() is honoured
circular references ❌ throws 
// ❌ Common mistake
socket.emit("stats", new Map([["a", 1]])); // receiver gets {}

// ✅ Fix
socket.emit("stats", Object.fromEntries(map));

With the msgpack parser (socket.io-msgpack-parser) the rules differ (e.g. Dates and binary are encoded natively by msgpack). Both sides must use the same parser.

Binary data example

// server: send a file
const fs = require("node:fs");
socket.emit("image", fs.readFileSync("cat.png")); // Buffer

// browser
socket.on("image", (buf) => {             // ArrayBuffer
  const blob = new Blob([buf], { type: "image/png" });
  img.src = URL.createObjectURL(blob);
});

// browser: upload a file selected in <input type="file">
input.addEventListener("change", () => {
  socket.emit("upload", input.files[0], (status) => console.log(status));
});

// server
socket.on("upload", (file, ack) => {        // Buffer
  fs.writeFile("/tmp/upload", file, (err) => ack({ ok: !err }));
});

Binary is sent as separate WebSocket binary frames (a BINARY_EVENT packet with placeholders + attachments — see Chapter 7). Over polling it is base64-encoded.

Limits: a single message larger than maxHttpBufferSize (default 1 MB) causes the server to close the connection (the client sees a disconnect, typically "transport close"/"transport error"). For big files use HTTP uploads (multipart, pre-signed S3 URLs) or chunk the file into pieces with acks for flow control (Chapter 13 shows a chunked upload pattern).

3.3 Listener management

Server-side and client-side Socket listener methods

Both server and client sockets behave like an EventEmitter:

socket.on("event", listener);        // add listener
socket.once("event", listener);      // add one-time listener
socket.off("event", listener);       // remove a specific listener (alias: removeListener)
socket.off("event");                 // remove all listeners for this event (client)
socket.removeAllListeners("event");  // remove all listeners for this event
socket.removeAllListeners();         // remove ALL listeners (careful: also reserved ones like disconnect)
socket.listeners("event");           // array of listeners

On the client, socket.off() with no arguments removes all listeners.

Catch-all listeners (onAny) — great for logging/debugging

// incoming
socket.onAny((eventName, ...args) => {
  console.log("IN ", eventName, args);
});

// outgoing (v4.5+)
socket.onAnyOutgoing((eventName, ...args) => {
  console.log("OUT", eventName, args);
});

socket.prependAny(listener);         // run before other catch-all listeners
socket.offAny(listener);             // remove one
socket.offAny();                     // remove all
socket.listenersAny();               // list
socket.prependAnyOutgoing(listener);
socket.offAnyOutgoing(listener);
socket.listenersAnyOutgoing();

Notes: - onAny does not receive acknowledgement packets, and the ack callback (if any) is the last element of args. - Reserved events (connect, disconnect, …) are not passed to onAny.

Server-side per-event middleware: socket.use()

Runs for every incoming event on that socket before the handler (Chapter 6 covers it fully):

socket.use(([event, ...args], next) => {
  if (event === "admin:action" && !socket.data.user?.isAdmin) {
    return next(new Error("unauthorized"));
  }
  next();
});

// errors from socket.use() are emitted as an "error" event on the server socket
socket.on("error", (err) => {
  if (err && err.message === "unauthorized") socket.emit("app:error", "unauthorized");
});

3.4 Acknowledgements (request/response)

ELI5

Normally, emit is "fire and forget" — like throwing a paper plane over a wall. You don't know if anyone caught it. An acknowledgement is like attaching a self-addressed envelope: the receiver puts their answer inside and sends it back to you specifically.

Callback style (works in both directions)

// client
socket.emit("order:create", { item: "pizza" }, (response) => {
  console.log(response); // { ok: true, orderId: 42 }
});

// server
socket.on("order:create", async (payload, callback) => {
  const order = await orders.create(payload);
  callback({ ok: true, orderId: order.id });
});

Server → client works the same:

// server
socket.emit("are-you-there", (answer) => console.log(answer));

// client
socket.on("are-you-there", (callback) => callback("yes"));

Rules (all asked in interviews):

  1. The ack function must be the last argument of emit.
  2. The receiver gets it as the last argument of the handler.
  3. Call it at most once. Calling twice: the second call is ignored (the ack id is consumed after the first reply).
  4. You can pass multiple arguments to the callback: callback(err, data).
  5. Arguments follow the same serialization rules (JSON + binary).
  6. Not calling the callback means the sender waits forever (memory held for the pending ack) unless a timeout is used. Always reply — even on errors.
  7. The handler cannot know in advance whether the sender passed an ack, so validate: if (typeof callback !== "function") return; (malicious or buggy clients might not pass one, and calling undefined() throws inside your handler).

The error-first convention

Socket.IO itself doesn't impose a format. Pick one convention for the whole organisation. Two popular choices:

// (a) Node-style error-first
callback(null, { orderId: 42 });           // success
callback({ code: "OUT_OF_STOCK" });         // error

// (b) Envelope style (recommended — easy to type & evolve)
callback({ ok: true, data: { orderId: 42 } });
callback({ ok: false, error: { code: "OUT_OF_STOCK", message: "Pizza unavailable" } });

Acks with timeout

What if the other side never answers (network loss, buggy handler)? Use timeout():

// client or server (server-side since v4.5)
socket.timeout(5000).emit("order:create", payload, (err, response) => {
  if (err) {
    // the other side did not acknowledge the event in the given delay
    return showError("Server did not respond");
  }
  console.log(response);
});

When timeout() is used, the callback gets an extra first argument err (an Error with message "operation has timed out") — null on success. This is the #1 confusion with timeouts: with .timeout(), the first callback arg is the error.

Promise style: emitWithAck() (v4.6+)

// client
try {
  const response = await socket.timeout(5000).emitWithAck("order:create", payload);
  console.log(response);
} catch (err) {
  // timed out (only possible when a timeout was set)
}

// server, to one client
const answer = await socket.timeout(3000).emitWithAck("are-you-there");

Without any timeout (.timeout() or the client's ackTimeout option), the promise only settles when the other side answers — it may hang forever.

Broadcast with acknowledgements (server, v4.5+)

io.timeout(5000).emit("health-check", (err, responses) => {
  if (err) {
    // some clients did not answer in time; `responses` has the ones that did
  }
  console.log(responses); // one entry per client that answered
});

// promise version
try {
  const responses = await io.timeout(5000).emitWithAck("health-check");
} catch (e) {
  // some clients did not acknowledge in time
}

// to a room
const answers = await io.to("room-42").timeout(5000).emitWithAck("vote");
  • Promise vs callback when some clients don't answer (verified): emitWithAck rejects on timeout, so you lose the answers that did arrive. The callback form gives you both: io.timeout(1000).emit("poll", q, (err, answers) => …) — err is set, and answers still contains every reply received in time. Use the callback form for polls, health checks and anything where partial results are useful.
  • Always use .timeout() with broadcast acks. (In the 4.8 source, the broadcast timer is setTimeout(..., this.flags.timeout); without a timeout it fires almost immediately with an error.)
  • Works across a cluster with adapters that support it (Redis adapter etc.).

Acks don't work with socket.broadcast the same way?

socket.broadcast.timeout(…).emit(…, cb) and socket.to(room).timeout(…).emit(…, cb) are also BroadcastOperators, so they behave like io.timeout(): one callback with an array of responses.

Client-side automatic retries — at-least-once from client (v4.6+)

const socket = io({
  ackTimeout: 10000, // wait up to 10 s for the server to ack each emit
  retries: 3,        // then retry up to 3 times
});

// this event will be retried until acknowledged (or 3 retries exhausted)
socket.emit("payment:submit", payload, (err, res) => {
  if (err) {/* all retries failed */}
});

How it works:

  • Packets are queued and sent one at a time (in order); the next packet waits for the previous one's ack.
  • If no ack arrives within ackTimeout, the packet is re-sent (up to retries times).
  • The server may receive the same event more than once → your server handler must be idempotent (e.g. dedupe by a client-generated message id). That's the definition of at-least-once.
// client: include a unique id
socket.emit("message:send", { id: crypto.randomUUID(), text });

// server: dedupe
socket.on("message:send", async (msg, ack) => {
  const inserted = await db.messages.insertIfNotExists(msg.id, msg); // unique index on id
  ack({ ok: true, duplicate: !inserted });
});

You can also apply retries to a single emit with a timeout: socket.timeout(5000).emit(...) (no retries) vs. the retries option (global).

Ack patterns in production

  1. "Command" events (create order, send message, join room) → always ack, with a timeout, with an envelope { ok, data | error }.
  2. "Fire-and-forget" signals (typing, cursor move, mouse position) → no ack, often volatile.
  3. Server-originated notifications → generally no ack (client may be offline). If you need confirmation of delivery, store in DB and let the client "mark as read" via an event or HTTP call.

3.5 Volatile events

socket.volatile.emit("cursor", { x, y });
io.volatile.emit("price", tick);

ELI5: "If you can't hear me right now, forget it; I'll say a newer thing soon anyway."

Behaviour:

  • Server side: the packet is discarded if the underlying transport is not currently writable (e.g. a polling request isn't open, or the socket is buffering) — instead of being buffered.
  • Client side: the packet is discarded if the low-level transport is not currently writable — which includes the time when the socket is disconnected — instead of being added to sendBuffer like a normal emit.

Use for high-frequency, superseded data: positions, typing indicators, live prices, game state ticks, sensor readings. Never for things that matter individually (payments, chat messages).

3.6 Compression

socket.compress(false).emit("blob", bigBuffer); // disable compression for this packet
  • Applies to WebSocket per-message deflate (if enabled with perMessageDeflate — off by default on the server since v3) and to HTTP compression of polling responses (httpCompression, on by default, threshold 1 KB).
  • Default compress flag is true (i.e. "compress if the transport supports it and it is enabled").
  • Disable for already-compressed data (images, video, gzip) → saves CPU.

Why is perMessageDeflate off by default? Each WebSocket with compression keeps a zlib context (~hundreds of KB of memory per connection with default window bits), and compression costs CPU — at 10k+ connections it can be a major memory/CPU cost (and historically caused memory fragmentation issues in Node). Enable it only when messages are big and compressible, and tune threshold, zlibDeflateOptions, serverNoContextTakeover.

3.7 Reserved event names (cannot emit these)

Calling socket.emit("connect") throws: "connect" is a reserved event name.

Side Reserved names
Server Socket connect, connect_error, disconnect, disconnecting, newListener, removeListener
Client Socket connect, connect_error, disconnect, disconnecting, newListener, removeListener

Also avoid naming your own events like Manager events (reconnect, reconnect_attempt, error, ping) — they're on a different object (socket.io), so it technically works, but it confuses readers. The built-in "message" event is used by socket.send():

socket.send("hi");        // same as socket.emit("message", "hi")
socket.on("message", ...);

3.8 Event naming conventions (organisational best practice)

Pick a convention and enforce it (lint rule / TypeScript event maps):

  • domain:action — chat:message, chat:typing, order:created, room:join. (Recommended in the official docs' application-structure guide.)
  • Use past tense for facts the server announces (order:created, user:joined) and imperative for commands the client requests (order:create, room:join).
  • Lowercase, no spaces, no dynamic parts in names (put ids in the payload, not in the event name — room:message + { roomId }, not room-123-message). Dynamic event names break typing, documentation and metrics, and can cause listener leaks.
  • Version breaking payload changes: order:created:v2 or a version field.
  • Keep a single shared file describing events (TypeScript interfaces shared by client and server, or an AsyncAPI document).

3.9 Ordering guarantees

  • Socket.IO guarantees message ordering for a given socket (packets are sent and received in emit order), in both directions, regardless of the transport and even during the polling→WebSocket upgrade (Engine.IO pauses polling and flushes before switching).
  • Ordering is not guaranteed across different sockets (e.g. events from user A and user B), and not across server processes in a cluster for broadcasts originating on different nodes.
  • With async handlers, processing may finish out of order even if arrival was in order:
// ⚠️ arrival order: A then B — completion order can be B then A
socket.on("doc:edit", async (op) => {
  await db.apply(op);          // A may take longer than B
});

If processing order matters, serialize per socket (or per document) with a queue:

const queue = [];
let busy = false;
socket.on("doc:edit", (op, ack) => { queue.push([op, ack]); drain(); });

async function drain() {
  if (busy) return;
  busy = true;
  while (queue.length) {
    const [op, ack] = queue.shift();
    try { await db.apply(op); ack?.({ ok: true }); }
    catch (e) { ack?.({ ok: false, error: e.message }); }
  }
  busy = false;
}

(Libraries like p-queue with concurrency: 1 do this neatly. For collaborative editing you'd also use OT/CRDT — Chapter 13.)

3.10 Async handlers and errors — a production must-know

Socket.IO does not catch errors thrown inside your event handlers for you, and it does not catch rejected promises from async handlers:

// ❌ An exception here goes up to the EventEmitter → can crash the process
socket.on("x", (data) => {
  JSON.parse(data); // throws on bad input
});

// ❌ An unhandled rejection: in Node 15+ unhandled rejections crash the process by default
socket.on("y", async () => {
  await somethingThatRejects();
});

Fix: wrap every handler. A small helper:

const safe = (handler) => async (...args) => {
  const ack = typeof args[args.length - 1] === "function" ? args.pop() : null;
  try {
    const result = await handler(...args);
    ack?.({ ok: true, data: result });
  } catch (err) {
    logger.error({ err }, "socket handler failed");
    ack?.({ ok: false, error: { code: err.code ?? "INTERNAL", message: "Something went wrong" } });
  }
};

socket.on("order:create", safe(async (payload) => {
  const valid = OrderSchema.parse(payload);   // zod validation throws on bad input
  return orderService.create(socket.data.user.id, valid);
}));

This gives you: validation, consistent ack envelopes, logging, no crashes. Combine with process.on("unhandledRejection") logging as a last resort (not as error handling).

3.11 send() and message

socket.send("hello");               // emits a "message" event
socket.on("message", (data) => {}); // receives it
io.send("to everyone");

Rarely used in modern code; named events are clearer.

3.12 Emitting from outside a connection handler

You often need to emit from a REST controller, a cron job, or a queue consumer.

// keep a reference to io (module export, DI container, app.set("io", io) in Express…)
app.post("/api/orders", async (req, res) => {
  const order = await createOrder(req.body);
  req.app.get("io").to(`user:${order.userId}`).emit("order:created", order);
  res.status(201).json(order);
});

From a different process (worker, microservice) that is not a Socket.IO server, use an emitter (@socket.io/redis-emitter, or the Postgres/MongoDB emitters) — see Chapter 8.

Emitting to a user rather than a socket: make each socket join a room named after the user id in the connection handler (socket.join("user:" + userId)), then emit to that room. This handles multiple tabs/devices and reconnections (socket ids change; user ids don't). This is the standard pattern.

3.13 Chapter summary

  • emit(name, ...args) / on(name, handler) in both directions; prefer one object arg.
  • Data: JSON-serializable + binary. Dates→strings, Map/Set→{}, BigInt throws.
  • Acks = request/response; last argument; call once; always reply; validate it's a function.
  • timeout(ms) adds an err first argument; emitWithAck returns a Promise.
  • Broadcast acks require .timeout() and give an array of responses.
  • Client retries + ackTimeout = at-least-once from client → make handlers idempotent.
  • volatile = droppable; compress(false) for pre-compressed data.
  • Reserved names: connect, connect_error, disconnect, disconnecting, newListener, removeListener.
  • Order is guaranteed per socket, but async processing can reorder — queue if needed.
  • Wrap handlers to catch errors (sync and async) and always ack.

Next: Chapter 4 — Rooms, namespaces and broadcasting

Rooms, Namespaces and Broadcasting

Goal: understand how to send messages to groups of clients. Rooms and namespaces are the most-asked Socket.IO interview topic after "what is Socket.IO".

4.1 ELI5

Imagine a big school (the Socket.IO server).

  • The school has separate buildings: the main building, the sports building, the teachers' building. Those are namespaces (/, /sports, /admin). You choose which building to enter, and each building can have its own guard (middleware) and rules (handlers). Students choose buildings.
  • Inside each building there are classrooms: "Math 101", "Art club". Those are rooms. The principal (server) puts students into classrooms and takes them out. Students can't walk into a room by themselves — they have to ask the server, which decides.
  • The principal can use the loudspeaker to talk to: everyone in a building (io.of("/sports").emit), everyone in a classroom (io.to("math-101").emit), everyone except one kid (socket.broadcast.emit), and so on. That's broadcasting.
  • Every student also has a private room with their own name on it (the socket's id) — so the principal can whisper to one student by sending to that room.

4.2 Rooms

What a room is

A room is an arbitrary server-side channel name that sockets can join and leave. It is used to broadcast events to a subset of clients.

Key facts:

  1. Rooms are a server-only concept. The client doesn't know which rooms it's in (unless you tell it). There is no socket.join() on the client.
  2. A room is just a string (any string: "room-42", "user:123", "org:9:admins").
  3. Rooms are created automatically when the first socket joins and deleted automatically when the last socket leaves.
  4. Every socket automatically joins a room named after its own id (socket.id). That's how io.to(socketId).emit() works for private messages.
  5. Sockets automatically leave all their rooms on disconnect. You don't need to clean up.
  6. Rooms belong to a namespace: room "chess" in / is unrelated to room "chess" in /games.
  7. A socket can be in any number of rooms.

Join / leave

io.on("connection", (socket) => {
  socket.join("some room");                 // join one room
  socket.join(["room1", "room2"]);          // join several
  socket.leave("some room");                // leave one
  console.log(socket.rooms);                // Set { <socket.id>, "room1", "room2" }
});

socket.join() returns void with the default in-memory adapter, but may return a Promise with some adapters (the type is Promise<void> | void). Writing await socket.join(room) is safe either way and future-proof.

Broadcasting to rooms

io.to("room1").emit("event");                    // everyone in room1 (incl. the sender, if in it)
socket.to("room1").emit("event");                // everyone in room1 EXCEPT the sender
io.to("room1").to("room2").emit("event");        // union: in room1 OR room2 — each socket receives it ONCE
io.to(["room1", "room2"]).emit("event");         // same, array form
io.except("room3").emit("event");                // everyone except sockets in room3
io.to("room1").except("room2").emit("event");    // in room1 but not in room2
io.to(socketId).emit("private", "hi");           // one specific socket
io.in("room1").emit("event");                    // `in` is an alias of `to`

Interview trap: joining to("a").to("b") is a union, not an intersection, and a socket in both rooms receives the event only once (the adapter de-duplicates). There's no built-in intersection — implement it with a dedicated room ("a&b") or fetchSockets() + filtering.

Interview trap 2: socket.to(room).emit() excludes the sender. io.to(room).emit() includes everyone in the room (the sender too, if it's in the room).

Typical room usages (patterns)

Use case Room naming
Chat channel / conversation conversation:<id>
All devices/tabs of a user user:<userId> ← most important pattern
Organisation/tenant broadcast org:<orgId>
Role-based broadcast org:<orgId>:role:admin
A document being edited doc:<docId>
A game match match:<matchId>
Subscribers to a resource (e.g. stock) ticker:AAPL
Geographic/area geo:<geohash>

The per-user room pattern (memorise)

io.use(authMiddleware); // sets socket.data.user

io.on("connection", (socket) => {
  const userId = socket.data.user.id;
  socket.join(`user:${userId}`);   // all tabs/devices of this user share this room
});

// anywhere in the app (REST controller, job, etc.)
io.to(`user:${userId}`).emit("notification", payload);

Why: socket ids change on every reconnection and a user may have several tabs/devices; the user room is stable and covers all of them. Never store "userId → socketId" in a plain variable map — it breaks with multiple tabs, reconnections and multiple servers.

Room lifecycle events (adapter events)

io.of("/").adapter.on("create-room", (room) => console.log(`room ${room} was created`));
io.of("/").adapter.on("delete-room", (room) => console.log(`room ${room} was deleted`));
io.of("/").adapter.on("join-room", (room, id) => console.log(`socket ${id} joined ${room}`));
io.of("/").adapter.on("leave-room", (room, id) => console.log(`socket ${id} left ${room}`));

Note: these fire for the socket's own private room too (room === id). These events are emitted locally on each server (with the Redis adapter each node only sees its own sockets' joins).

Inspecting rooms

io.of("/").adapter.rooms;   // Map<Room, Set<SocketId>>  (LOCAL node only)
io.of("/").adapter.sids;    // Map<SocketId, Set<Room>>  (LOCAL node only)

// cluster-aware (works with adapters):
const sockets = await io.in("room1").fetchSockets();
sockets.length;                                       // number of sockets in room1

const ids = await io.in("room1").allSockets();        // Set<SocketId> (older API)

adapter.rooms contains both real rooms and every socket's private id-room. To list "real" rooms only, filter out keys that are also in adapter.sids: js const { rooms, sids } = io.of("/").adapter; const realRooms = [...rooms.keys()].filter((r) => !sids.has(r));

disconnecting vs disconnect — read rooms before they're gone

socket.on("disconnecting", (reason) => {
  // socket.rooms is still populated here
  for (const room of socket.rooms) {
    if (room !== socket.id) socket.to(room).emit("user:left", socket.id);
  }
});

socket.on("disconnect", (reason) => {
  // socket.rooms is EMPTY here
});

4.3 Server-side utility methods (bulk operations) — v4+

These work on the local server and across a cluster (when the adapter supports it):

// make all sockets join room1
io.socketsJoin("room1");

// make all sockets in room1 also join room2 and room3
io.in("room1").socketsJoin(["room2", "room3"]);

// make the socket with id `theSocketId` join room1 (works even if it's connected on another node!)
io.in(theSocketId).socketsJoin("room1");

// make all sockets leave room1
io.socketsLeave("room1");
io.in("room1").socketsLeave(["room2", "room3"]);

// disconnect all sockets / those in a room
io.disconnectSockets();
io.in("room1").disconnectSockets();
io.in(theSocketId).disconnectSockets(true); // true = close the underlying connection too

// fetch sockets (RemoteSocket objects when on another node)
const sockets = await io.fetchSockets();
const roomSockets = await io.in("room1").fetchSockets();
for (const s of sockets) {
  s.id; s.handshake; s.rooms; s.data;      // readable
  s.emit("hello");                         // writable operations work remotely
  s.join("room2"); s.leave("room2");
  s.disconnect();
}

Practical examples

// a user was removed from an organisation → kick all their sockets out of the org room
io.in(`user:${userId}`).socketsLeave(`org:${orgId}`);

// a user was banned → disconnect every device
io.in(`user:${userId}`).disconnectSockets(true);

// how many people are viewing document 42 right now (cluster-wide)?
const viewers = (await io.in("doc:42").fetchSockets()).map((s) => s.data.user.name);

Performance warning: fetchSockets() across a cluster asks every node and waits for responses (with a timeout, default 5000 ms in the Redis adapter's requestsTimeout). Don't call it on hot paths (every message). For presence counts at scale, maintain counters in Redis instead (Chapter 13).

socket.data is the place to store per-socket info (user id, name, roles). It is serialized and returned by fetchSockets() on other nodes, so keep it small and JSON-serializable.

4.4 Broadcasting — all the flavours

Broadcast = send to many sockets with one call. The server builds/encodes the packet once and writes it to every target socket (efficient).

io.emit("x");                       // all sockets in the main namespace "/"
io.of("/admin").emit("x");          // all sockets in namespace /admin
socket.broadcast.emit("x");         // all sockets in the socket's namespace except the sender
socket.to("r").emit("x");           // room r, except sender
io.to("r").emit("x");               // room r
io.except("r").emit("x");           // all except room r
io.local.emit("x");                 // only sockets connected to THIS server node (cluster)
io.volatile.emit("x");              // droppable
io.compress(false).emit("x");
io.timeout(5000).emit("x", (err, responses) => {}); // with acks

Modifiers chain in any order before emit:

io.of("/game").to("match:7").except("spectators").volatile.compress(false).emit("tick", state);

The "broadcast to everyone except the sender" is a server responsibility

The client can't broadcast. Pattern:

// client
socket.emit("cursor:move", { x, y });

// server: relay to others in the same doc
socket.on("cursor:move", (pos) => {
  socket.to(`doc:${socket.data.docId}`).volatile.emit("cursor:move", { id: socket.id, ...pos });
});

Broadcasting from multiple servers

With several Socket.IO servers (processes/containers), io.emit() on server A only reaches clients connected to server A unless you configure an adapter (Redis etc.) that forwards the broadcast to all other servers. That's Chapter 8. io.local lets you deliberately skip the adapter.

4.5 Namespaces

What a namespace is

A namespace is a communication channel that lets you split the logic of your application over a single shared connection (this sharing is called multiplexing).

Each namespace has its own:

  • event handlers (io.of("/admin").on("connection", ...)),
  • rooms (independent per namespace),
  • middleware (io.of("/admin").use(...)).

The default (main) namespace is "/". io.sockets is an alias for io.of("/"), and io.on("connection"), io.emit(), io.use(), io.to() are shortcuts for the main namespace.

// server
io.of("/").on("connection", (socket) => {});        // same as io.on("connection")
io.of("/orders").on("connection", (socket) => {});
io.of("/users").on("connection", (socket) => {});

// client
const mainSocket = io("https://example.com");          // namespace "/"
const ordersSocket = io("https://example.com/orders"); // namespace "/orders"
const usersSocket = io("https://example.com/users");   // namespace "/users"
// ↑ all three share ONE underlying WebSocket connection (multiplexing)

When to use namespaces vs rooms (classic interview question)

Namespace Room
Who decides membership Client chooses which namespace to connect to Server joins/leaves sockets
Separate handlers/middleware ✅ Yes ❌ No (same handlers)
Scope Separation of concerns/features/permissions Grouping of users for broadcasting
Number Few, mostly static (/, /admin, /chat) — or dynamic for multi-tenancy Many, dynamic (one per conversation/user/doc)
Connection Each namespace = separate Socket object; shared transport Rooms live inside a namespace
Typical use /admin with admin-only auth middleware; separating product areas; multi-tenant /tenant-<id> chat channels, user rooms, game matches

Rule of thumb: use rooms by default; use namespaces when you need different middleware/authorization or a separate set of event handlers.

Namespace middleware (per-namespace auth)

const admin = io.of("/admin");

admin.use(async (socket, next) => {
  const user = await verify(socket.handshake.auth.token);
  if (user?.role !== "admin") return next(new Error("forbidden"));
  socket.data.user = user;
  next();
});

admin.on("connection", (socket) => {
  socket.on("user:ban", banHandler);
});

Clients connecting to /admin without being admins get connect_error; their connection to / is unaffected.

Dynamic namespaces

Create namespaces on the fly (great for multi-tenancy):

// with a RegExp
const workspaces = io.of(/^\/workspace-\w+$/);

workspaces.use(tenantAuth);                   // middleware for every matching child namespace

workspaces.on("connection", (socket) => {
  const workspace = socket.nsp;               // the child Namespace, e.g. "/workspace-acme"
  console.log(workspace.name);
  workspace.emit("hello");                    // broadcast to everyone in THIS child namespace
});

// broadcast to ALL child namespaces matched by the parent
workspaces.emit("maintenance", "in 5 minutes");

// with a function (async check, e.g. tenant exists in DB)
io.of(async (name, auth, next) => {
  const ok = name.startsWith("/tenant-") && (await tenants.exists(name.slice(8)));
  next(null, ok);   // (error, allowed)
});
  • Child namespaces are created on first connection. With cleanupEmptyChildNamespaces: true (v4.6+, server option), a child namespace with no sockets left is removed (prevents memory growth with many tenants).
  • Connecting to a namespace that doesn't exist (and doesn't match any dynamic pattern) gives the client a connect_error with message "Invalid namespace".

Multiplexing in detail (client side)

const a = io("https://example.com");        // Manager #1 created for this URL
const b = io("https://example.com/admin");  // reuses Manager #1 → same connection
const c = io("https://example.com/admin", { forceNew: true }); // new Manager → new connection
const d = io("https://example.com", { multiplex: false });     // new Manager too
  • A client-side Manager owns the Engine.IO connection (transport, reconnection); each Socket belongs to a Manager and represents one namespace.
  • Access the Manager via socket.io (yes, the property is literally named io).
  • Two io() calls for the same URL and the same namespace: the client's lookup function notices the namespace is already used on the cached Manager and creates a new Manager → a second, separate connection. This is a classic source of "duplicate connections" bugs (e.g. calling io() inside a React component that re-renders). Create the socket once and share it (Chapter 9).
  • Two io() calls for the same URL but different namespaces reuse the cached Manager → one shared connection (multiplexing).
  • Query parameters/extraHeaders are per Manager, shared by all namespaces on it; auth is per namespace Socket.

Namespace-level vs main-namespace properties

Property Meaning
socket.nsp the Namespace the socket belongs to
io.of("/x").sockets Map<SocketId, Socket> of local sockets in this namespace
io.of("/x").adapter the adapter instance for this namespace
io._nsps Map<string, Namespace> of all namespaces (private-ish)

4.6 Socket IDs — what they are and aren't

  • socket.id (Socket.IO) is a random 20-character id, generated per namespace connection. It is not the same as the Engine.IO id (socket.conn.id) since v3 — exposing the engine id would allow session hijacking of the underlying connection.
  • On the client, socket.id is undefined until connected and changes after each reconnection (unless connection state recovery restored the session).
  • Since it's random and ephemeral, don't use it as an identity; use your authenticated user id. Don't trust a socket id sent by a client as authorization ("delete messages of socket X") — always use socket.data.user from auth.
  • You can customise the Engine.IO id with io.engine.generateId = (req) => uuid(). (The Socket.IO-level id is generated internally.)

4.7 Worked example: a chat with channels and private messages
