io.use(authMiddleware); // → socket.data.user = { id, name }

io.on("connection", (socket) => {
  const { id: userId, name } = socket.data.user;
  socket.join(`user:${userId}`);

  socket.on("channel:join", async ({ channelId }, ack) => {
    if (!(await acl.canJoin(userId, channelId))) return ack?.({ ok: false, error: "FORBIDDEN" });
    await socket.join(`channel:${channelId}`);
    socket.to(`channel:${channelId}`).emit("channel:user-joined", { channelId, userId, name });
    ack?.({ ok: true, data: { history: await messages.last(channelId, 50) } });
  });

  socket.on("channel:leave", async ({ channelId }, ack) => {
    await socket.leave(`channel:${channelId}`);
    socket.to(`channel:${channelId}`).emit("channel:user-left", { channelId, userId });
    ack?.({ ok: true });
  });

  socket.on("channel:message", async ({ channelId, text, clientMsgId }, ack) => {
    if (!socket.rooms.has(`channel:${channelId}`)) return ack?.({ ok: false, error: "NOT_A_MEMBER" });
    const msg = await messages.save({ channelId, userId, text, clientMsgId }); // persisted first!
    io.to(`channel:${channelId}`).emit("channel:message", msg); // includes sender's other tabs
    ack?.({ ok: true, data: { id: msg.id } });
  });

  socket.on("dm:send", async ({ toUserId, text }, ack) => {
    const msg = await dms.save({ from: userId, to: toUserId, text });
    io.to(`user:${toUserId}`).to(`user:${userId}`).emit("dm:message", msg); // both parties, all devices
    ack?.({ ok: true });
  });
});

Things to notice (interviewers notice them too): - membership is authorized server-side before joining; - messages are persisted before broadcasting (so reconnecting clients can fetch them); - the sender's other tabs get the message (io.to not socket.to); - DMs use user rooms, not socket ids; - every command is acked.

4.8 Chapter summary

  • Rooms: server-side groups; join/leave on server; auto-created/deleted; auto-left on disconnect; every socket is in its own id-room.
  • io.to() includes sender; socket.to()/socket.broadcast excludes sender; multiple to() = union (no duplicates); except() excludes.
  • Utility methods: socketsJoin, socketsLeave, disconnectSockets, fetchSockets — cluster-aware.
  • Use the user:<id> room pattern instead of a userId→socketId map.
  • Namespaces: client-chosen channels with their own handlers, middleware and rooms, multiplexed over one connection; dynamic namespaces for multi-tenancy.
  • Use rooms for grouping, namespaces for separation of concerns/permissions.

Next: Chapter 5 — Connection lifecycle

Connection Lifecycle: Connect, Disconnect, Reconnect, Heartbeats, Recovery and Delivery Guarantees

Goal: know exactly what happens from the first byte to the last, every event fired on both sides, every disconnect reason, how reconnection works, how dead connections are detected, what happens to messages sent while offline, and how to get stronger delivery guarantees. This chapter is where senior interviews are won or lost.

5.1 ELI5

  • Connecting is like dialling a friend: first you say "hello, it's me" (handshake), your friend may check who you are (middleware), then says "come in" (connect).
  • Heartbeat: every 25 seconds your friend asks "still there?" (ping) and you answer "yes!" (pong). If you don't answer in 20 seconds, your friend hangs up.
  • Disconnect: the call ends — because someone hung up on purpose, or the line broke.
  • Reconnect: if the line broke by accident, your phone redials automatically, waiting a bit longer each time (1 s, 2 s, 4 s… up to 5 s, with a bit of randomness so a thousand phones don't all redial at the exact same moment).
  • Buffering: things you said while the line was dead are written on a notepad and said again when the line comes back.
  • Connection state recovery: if you were away only a short time, your friend remembers what group chats you were in and tells you what you missed.

5.2 Server-side lifecycle events

// Engine.IO level (low-level connection, before any namespace)
io.engine.on("initial_headers", (headers, req) => { /* modify handshake response headers */ });
io.engine.on("headers", (headers, req) => { /* modify headers of every HTTP response */ });
io.engine.on("connection", (rawSocket) => { /* Engine.IO socket established */ });
io.engine.on("connection_error", (err) => {
  console.log(err.req);      // the request object
  console.log(err.code);     // the error code, e.g. 1
  console.log(err.message);  // the error message, e.g. "Session ID unknown"
  console.log(err.context);  // extra context
});

// Socket.IO level (per namespace)
io.use((socket, next) => { /* middleware: runs before "connection" */ next(); });

io.on("connection", (socket) => {
  socket.on("disconnecting", (reason, description) => { /* still in rooms */ });
  socket.on("disconnect", (reason, description) => { /* rooms already left */ });
  socket.on("error", (err) => { /* errors from socket.use() middleware */ });
});

io.on("connect") is an alias of io.on("connection").

Engine.IO connection_error codes (know these for debugging)

Code Message Typical cause
0 Transport unknown Client asked for a transport not enabled on the server (e.g. server transports: ["websocket"], client polling)
1 Session ID unknown The sid isn't known by this server → missing sticky sessions in a multi-server setup, or the server restarted
2 Bad handshake method Handshake not done with GET
3 Bad request Malformed request (e.g. invalid Origin header, bad query)
4 Forbidden Rejected by allowRequest
5 Unsupported protocol version v2 client (EIO=3) on a v3/v4 server without allowEIO3: true

Server-side disconnect reasons (memorise)

Reason Meaning Side that initiated
server namespace disconnect You called socket.disconnect() on the server Server
client namespace disconnect The client called socket.disconnect() Client
server shutting down The server is closing (io.close()) Server
ping timeout Client didn't answer a PING within pingTimeout Network / client
transport close The connection was closed (user closed tab, lost network, proxy cut it) Network / client
transport error The connection encountered an error Network
parse error Server received an invalid packet Client bug / attack
forced close The low-level Engine.IO connection was closed by the server (e.g. socket.conn.close()) Server
forced server close The server closed the whole underlying connection — e.g. socket.disconnect(true) on one namespace closes the connection, and the client's sockets in other namespaces get this reason Server

The second argument description (when available) gives low-level details (e.g. the underlying error/close code).

Interview note: socket.disconnect(true) on the server closes the underlying connection (all namespaces of that client); socket.disconnect() (or false) only leaves that namespace.

5.3 Client-side lifecycle events

const socket = io(URL);

// --- Socket events (per namespace) ---
socket.on("connect", () => {
  console.log(socket.id, socket.connected, socket.recovered);
});

socket.on("connect_error", (err) => {
  // fired when: low-level connection can't be established, OR middleware rejected (next(err))
  console.log(err.message);   // e.g. "xhr poll error", "websocket error", "timeout", or your middleware message
  console.log(err.data);      // extra data from server middleware (err.data = {...})
  console.log(socket.active); // true → will retry automatically; false → you must call socket.connect()
});

socket.on("disconnect", (reason, details) => {
  console.log(reason);        // see table below
  console.log(socket.active); // true if automatic reconnection will happen
});

// --- Manager events (the connection; one per multiplexed connection) ---
socket.io.on("open", () => {});                        // Engine.IO connection opened
socket.io.on("error", (err) => {});                    // connection error
socket.io.on("ping", () => {});                        // a PING was received from the server
socket.io.on("packet", (packet) => {});                // any packet
socket.io.on("close", (reason, description) => {});    // underlying connection closed
socket.io.on("reconnect_attempt", (attempt) => {});    // before each attempt
socket.io.on("reconnect_error", (err) => {});          // an attempt failed
socket.io.on("reconnect", (attempt) => {});            // success after `attempt` tries
socket.io.on("reconnect_failed", () => {});            // gave up (reconnectionAttempts reached)

// --- Engine.IO level (transport) ---
socket.io.engine.on("upgrade", (transport) => console.log("upgraded to", transport.name));
socket.io.engine.transport.name; // "polling" | "websocket" | "webtransport"

v2 → v3 trap: in v2 you wrote socket.on("reconnect", …). Since v3 the reconnection events are on the Manager (socket.io.on("reconnect", …)). Writing socket.on("reconnect") in v3/v4 silently does nothing — a very common bug when migrating.

Client-side disconnect reasons

Reason Meaning Auto-reconnect?
io server disconnect Server called socket.disconnect() No — call socket.connect() yourself if appropriate
io client disconnect You called socket.disconnect() on the client No
ping timeout No PING from server within pingInterval + pingTimeout Yes
transport close Connection closed (user lost network, server restarted, proxy timeout) Yes
transport error Connection error (e.g. server killed during a polling cycle) Yes
parse error Client received an invalid packet Yes 
socket.on("disconnect", (reason) => {
  if (reason === "io server disconnect") {
    // the server kicked us (e.g. session expired, banned).
    // Decide: refresh the token and reconnect, or show a "you've been logged out" UI.
  }
  // otherwise the client will try to reconnect automatically
});

Client socket properties

Property Meaning
socket.id Socket id, undefined until connected; new value on each reconnection (unless recovered)
socket.connected / socket.disconnected Current state
socket.active true if the socket is connected or will try to reconnect. false after disconnect() or a server-side disconnect/middleware rejection
socket.recovered true if connection state recovery succeeded on the last connect (v4.6+)
socket.io The Manager
socket.auth The auth payload (can be updated before reconnecting)
socket.sendBuffer Packets emitted while disconnected, waiting to be sent
socket.receiveBuffer Packets received before the connect was processed

Client methods

socket.connect();     // (alias: socket.open()) — connect manually (autoConnect: false, or after a server disconnect)
socket.disconnect();  // (alias: socket.close()) — disconnect; no auto-reconnection
socket.io.reconnection(false); // turn off automatic reconnection on the fly
socket.io.opts.query = { ... }; // change query for the NEXT connection attempt (engine-level)
socket.auth = { token: newToken }; // change auth for the NEXT CONNECT packet

5.4 The complete connection sequence (step by step with real packets)

These packets were captured from a real Socket.IO 4.8.4 server using the polling transport:

1) GET /socket.io/?EIO=4&transport=polling
   ← 0{"sid":"HSJR3YuCOE9jPqHEAAAA","upgrades":["websocket"],"pingInterval":25000,"pingTimeout":20000,"maxPayload":1000000}
      ^ Engine.IO OPEN packet (type 0) with session id, possible upgrades, heartbeat settings, max payload

2) POST /socket.io/?EIO=4&transport=polling&sid=HSJR3YuCOE9jPqHEAAAA
   body: 40
      ^ Engine.IO MESSAGE (4) containing Socket.IO CONNECT (0) to namespace "/"
   ← ok

3) GET /socket.io/?EIO=4&transport=polling&sid=HSJR3YuCOE9jPqHEAAAA
   ← 40{"sid":"KykvYCRwmIsIifIyAAAA"}
      ^ Socket.IO CONNECT reply with the Socket.IO socket id (different from the Engine.IO sid)

4) POST … body: 421["hello","world"]
      ^ MESSAGE(4) + EVENT(2) + ack id 1 + ["event", ...args]

5) GET …
   ← 431[{"ok":true}]
      ^ MESSAGE(4) + ACK(3) + ack id 1 + [response args]

Then, typically within milliseconds, the upgrade to WebSocket:

6) GET /socket.io/?EIO=4&transport=websocket&sid=HSJR3YuCOE9jPqHEAAAA   (Upgrade: websocket)
   → 2probe            (client sends PING with "probe" over the new WebSocket)
   ← 3probe            (server answers PONG "probe")
   (client pauses polling: waits for in-flight polling requests to finish)
   → 5                 (client sends UPGRADE packet over the WebSocket)
   (server closes the polling transport with a NOOP (6) to the pending GET if needed)
   — from now on everything flows over the WebSocket —
   ← 2                 (server PING every pingInterval)
   → 3                 (client PONG)

If the WebSocket fails (blocked by proxy/firewall), the client just stays on polling; the user doesn't notice except for higher latency/overhead.

Why start with polling and then upgrade? (classic question)

  1. Reliability first: polling works nearly everywhere. Some proxies/antivirus accept the WebSocket handshake but then never deliver data — if the client went WebSocket-only, the user would wait for a timeout (could be many seconds) before falling back. Starting with polling gives an immediately working connection.
  2. Then optimise: upgrade to WebSocket in the background for lower latency/overhead.

Trade-offs of the default:

  • More HTTP requests at start; requires sticky sessions when you have multiple servers (the polling requests and the WebSocket upgrade must hit the same server).
  • You can skip polling with transports: ["websocket"] on the client → no sticky sessions needed and faster connection — but no fallback if WebSocket is blocked. Many companies do this for internal/mobile apps where the network is known. A middle ground: transports: ["websocket", "polling"] (try WebSocket first) and on connect_error revert to ["polling", "websocket"]:
const socket = io({ transports: ["websocket", "polling"] });
socket.on("connect_error", () => {
  // revert to classic upgrade
  socket.io.opts.transports = ["polling", "websocket"];
});

(With 4.8+, the client option tryAllTransports: true makes the client try the next transport in the list if the first one fails, instead of giving up.)

5.5 Heartbeat (ping/pong) mechanism in depth

Why we need heartbeats

TCP itself might not notice a dead peer for a very long time: if a phone goes into a tunnel, or a laptop sleeps, or a NAT/proxy silently drops the mapping, no FIN/RST packet is ever sent. The server would keep a "zombie" socket forever (memory leak, wrong presence status: "Alice is online" when she isn't). Proxies and load balancers also cut idle connections (e.g. AWS ALB default idle timeout 60 s, nginx proxy_read_timeout default 60 s) — regular traffic keeps them alive.

How it works in Socket.IO v3/v4 (Engine.IO v4)

server ──PING(2)──▶ client           every pingInterval (default 25 000 ms)
server ◀──PONG(3)── client           client must reply within pingTimeout (default 20 000 ms)
  • Server side: after sending a PING, if no PONG arrives within pingTimeout, the server closes the connection with reason ping timeout.
  • Client side: the client expects a PING at least every pingInterval + pingTimeout (45 s by default). If nothing arrives in that window, it closes with ping timeout and starts reconnecting.
  • Since 4.8 the client also tracks the expiration timestamp of that timer, because timers may be throttled/frozen (laptop suspended, phone locked); when the app wakes up and tries to emit, it detects the expired heartbeat instead of writing into a dead connection (the packet is buffered and sent after reconnection).
  • The values are sent to the client in the handshake (pingInterval, pingTimeout), so you configure them on the server only.

Worst-case detection time of a dead client on the server ≈ pingInterval + pingTimeout = 45 s by default.

Tuning

const io = new Server(httpServer, {
  pingInterval: 10000, // ping every 10 s
  pingTimeout: 5000,   // 5 s to answer
});
  • Lower values → faster dead-connection detection, more traffic (tiny packets though), more false positives on slow mobile networks or with busy event loops.
  • Higher values → fewer packets, slower detection.
  • Keep pingInterval below the idle timeout of every proxy/load balancer in the path (ALB, nginx, Cloudflare (100 s), corporate proxies), otherwise idle connections get cut → transport close loops every N seconds. Classic bug: "clients disconnect exactly every 60 seconds" = proxy idle timeout < heartbeat interval or nginx proxy_read_timeout.
  • Event loop blocking: if your Node.js process blocks the event loop for longer than pingTimeout (heavy sync computation, huge JSON.parse, sync crypto), it can't process PONGs/send PINGs on time → mass ping timeout disconnects. Monitor event loop lag.
  • Browser background tabs: browsers throttle timers in background tabs (≥1 s, or even 1 min for intensive throttling in Chrome after 5 min hidden). Because the server sends pings (v3+), the client only needs to respond, which works fine even when throttled (receiving a message isn't throttled the same way). This was the main reason for reversing the heartbeat direction in v3/Engine.IO v4.

5.6 Automatic reconnection in depth

The algorithm (exponential back-off with jitter)

Client options (with defaults, verified in source):

io(URL, {
  reconnection: true,            // enable auto-reconnection
  reconnectionAttempts: Infinity,// max attempts before "reconnect_failed"
  reconnectionDelay: 1000,       // initial delay (ms)
  reconnectionDelayMax: 5000,    // max delay (ms)
  randomizationFactor: 0.5,      // jitter factor (0 ≤ x ≤ 1)
  timeout: 20000,                // each connection attempt's timeout
});

Delay for attempt n (0-based) is computed roughly as:

base   = reconnectionDelay * 2^n             // 1000, 2000, 4000, 8000, …
jitter = random * randomizationFactor * base // up to ±50%
delay  = base ± jitter, capped at reconnectionDelayMax

So with defaults: ~1 s (0.5–1.5 s), ~2 s (1–3 s), ~4 s (2–5 s), then 5 s max for every next attempt (the cap applies after jitter).

Why jitter? If a server restarts with 50 000 connected clients and they all reconnect after exactly 1 s, 2 s, 4 s… the server gets 50 000 simultaneous handshakes in waves — the thundering herd, which can crash the server again (and again). Randomization spreads reconnections over time. For very large deployments you may increase reconnectionDelayMax (e.g. 10–30 s) and keep the randomization.

What triggers / doesn't trigger reconnection

  • Triggers: ping timeout, transport close, transport error, parse error, and failure of the initial low-level connection (server down → connect_error and retry).
  • Does NOT trigger:
  • client called socket.disconnect() → "io client disconnect";
  • server called socket.disconnect() → "io server disconnect";
  • middleware rejection (next(new Error(...)) in io.use) → connect_error with socket.active === false — the client will not retry automatically, because retrying with the same credentials would fail again. You fix the cause (e.g. refresh token) and call socket.connect().
socket.on("connect_error", async (err) => {
  if (socket.active) {
    // temporary failure, the socket will automatically try to reconnect
  } else {
    // the connection was denied by the server (middleware)
    if (err.message === "TOKEN_EXPIRED") {
      socket.auth.token = await refreshToken();
      socket.connect();
    } else {
      showLoginScreen();
    }
  }
});

State that is lost on reconnection (unless recovered)

On the server, a reconnection creates a brand-new Socket object with a new id:

  • rooms are empty (only its own id-room),
  • socket.data is empty (middleware runs again and must set it),
  • event listeners are registered again in io.on("connection").

Therefore: derive all state from the authenticated user in the connection handler (e.g. re-join user:<id> and the user's conversation rooms from the DB), and/or let the client tell the server what it wants to re-subscribe to on each connect:

// client
socket.on("connect", () => {
  // re-subscribe after every (re)connection
  socket.emit("subscribe", { rooms: [...currentSubscriptions] });
});

On the client, the same Socket object is reused and your listeners stay registered — which leads to the next famous bug.

Famous bug: registering listeners inside connect

// ❌ every reconnection adds ANOTHER "chat" listener → messages appear 2x, 3x, 4x…
socket.on("connect", () => {
  socket.on("chat", renderMessage);
});

// ✅ register once, outside
socket.on("chat", renderMessage);
socket.on("connect", () => { /* only connection-related logic */ });

(Same bug happens in React components that call socket.on() in useEffect without a cleanup socket.off() — Chapter 9.)

5.7 Client buffering (offline emits) and its dangers

By default, when the client is disconnected, socket.emit() doesn't fail — the packet is pushed into socket.sendBuffer, and all buffered packets are sent when the connection is (re)established (in order).

Good: user sends a chat message during a 2-second network blip → delivered after reconnection.

Bad: a 10-minute offline period with a "mouse position" emitted 60 times/second → 36 000 stale packets flood the server on reconnection.

Fixes:

// 1. Use volatile for droppable events (not buffered)
socket.volatile.emit("cursor", pos);

// 2. Check connection state
if (socket.connected) socket.emit("cursor", pos);

// 3. Clear the buffer on reconnect if stale data shouldn't be sent
socket.on("connect", () => { socket.sendBuffer = []; });  // drop what was queued while offline

Server-side there is no per-socket offline buffer for disconnected clients: once a socket is disconnected, the server-side Socket object is gone; emits to it (or to rooms it was in) simply don't reach it. (Exception: connection state recovery, below.)

5.8 Connection State Recovery (v4.6+) — deep dive

The problem

A client loses its connection for 3 seconds (elevator, Wi-Fi handover). Without recovery:

  • it reconnects with a new id, no rooms, no socket.data;
  • every event broadcast during those 3 s is lost for that client.

The feature

const io = new Server(httpServer, {
  connectionStateRecovery: {
    // how long the server keeps the session (and the packets) after a disconnection
    maxDisconnectionDuration: 2 * 60 * 1000, // default: 2 minutes
    // skip the middlewares upon successful recovery
    skipMiddlewares: true,                    // default: true
  },
});

io.on("connection", (socket) => {
  if (socket.recovered) {
    // recovery was successful: socket.id, socket.rooms and socket.data were restored,
    // and missed packets were (or will be) sent to the client
  } else {
    // new or unrecoverable session → do the normal setup (join rooms, etc.)
  }
});

Client:

socket.on("connect", () => {
  if (socket.recovered) {
    // any event missed during the disconnection period has been received
  } else {
    // new or unrecoverable session → re-fetch state via HTTP/acks
  }
});

How it works under the hood (verified in source)

  1. When CSR is enabled, the server sends the client a private session id (pid) in the CONNECT reply (alongside sid).
  2. Every event packet sent through the adapter's broadcast (and, with CSR enabled, even socket.emit() to a single socket is routed through adapter.broadcast to the socket's own room) gets a unique offset appended as an extra last argument. The client strips it and remembers the last offset it processed.
  3. When the socket disconnects for a recoverable reason (transport error, transport close, forced close, ping timeout, server shutting down, forced server close), the adapter persists the session: { sid, pid, rooms, data }. (A deliberate disconnect() by client or server is not recoverable.)
  4. On reconnection, the client sends { pid, offset } in the CONNECT packet's auth payload.
  5. The server looks up the session; if found, not expired, and the offset is known, it restores id, rooms, data, sets socket.recovered = true, and sends every stored packet after that offset that matches the socket's rooms.

Limitations (very important for real systems)

  • Packets with acknowledgements are not stored (a callback can't be serialized), and volatile packets are not stored.
  • The in-memory adapter keeps all broadcast packets in memory for maxDisconnectionDuration → memory cost proportional to broadcast volume. Size it.
  • It's a best-effort optimisation, not a guarantee: it fails if the server restarted (with the in-memory adapter), the duration expired, or the offset is unknown. Your app must still handle socket.recovered === false (re-sync state from the DB/API).
  • Adapter support (from each adapter's official feature table): ✅ default in-memory adapter, ✅ Redis Streams adapter, ✅ MongoDB adapter (since 0.3.0). ❌ classic Redis pub/sub adapter (pub/sub has no storage), ❌ Postgres adapter. Check your adapter's docs before relying on it.
  • With skipMiddlewares: true, middleware doesn't re-run on recovery — fine for auth if tokens can't be revoked within 2 minutes; set it to false if you need re-auth.

5.9 Delivery guarantees (at-most-once vs at-least-once vs exactly-once)

Default guarantees

  • Ordering: guaranteed (per socket connection, both directions).
  • Arrival: at-most-once. A message may be lost if:
  • the connection breaks while the message is in flight,
  • the client is disconnected when the server emits (server→client),
  • the server crashes after receiving but before processing,
  • a volatile packet is dropped.
  • Client→server while disconnected: buffered and sent on reconnection (but lost if the page is closed/reloaded before reconnecting, because the buffer is in memory).

Getting at-least-once: client → server

  1. Use acks: the client knows the server got it.
  2. Retry on timeout (retries + ackTimeout options in v4.6+, or manually).
  3. Make server handlers idempotent (client-generated unique id + unique DB index).
  4. Optionally persist pending messages client-side (IndexedDB/localStorage) to survive reloads ("outbox pattern").

Getting at-least-once: server → client

Option A — connection state recovery (best-effort, short disconnections).

Option B — offset/sequence-based sync (the robust, standard approach):

  1. Persist every message in a database with a monotonically increasing id/timestamp per conversation (or a global sequence).
  2. The client keeps track of the last id it processed (per conversation), stored in memory/localStorage.
  3. On every (re)connection, the client asks "give me everything after id X" (via an ack or HTTP), then continues receiving live events.
  4. De-duplicate on the client by id (an event may arrive both live and via sync).
// server
io.on("connection", async (socket) => {
  socket.on("sync", async ({ conversationId, afterId }, ack) => {
    if (!(await acl.canRead(socket.data.user.id, conversationId))) return ack({ ok: false });
    const missed = await messages.findAfter(conversationId, afterId, { limit: 500 });
    ack({ ok: true, data: missed });
  });
});

// client
let lastId = loadLastId();
socket.on("connect", async () => {
  const res = await socket.timeout(10000).emitWithAck("sync", { conversationId, afterId: lastId });
  res.data.forEach(handleMessage);
});
socket.on("message", handleMessage);

const seen = new Set();
function handleMessage(m) {
  if (seen.has(m.id)) return;     // dedupe
  seen.add(m.id);
  lastId = Math.max(lastId, m.id);
  render(m);
}

Option C — per-message client acks (the server re-sends unacknowledged messages): heavier; mostly used for critical notifications.

Exactly-once?

Over a network, "exactly-once delivery" is impossible in general; what you implement is at-least-once delivery + idempotent processing = effectively-once processing. Say exactly this in interviews.

5.10 Timeouts summary (one table to rule them all)

Setting Side Default What it controls
pingInterval server 25 000 ms Time between PINGs
pingTimeout server 20 000 ms Time to wait for PONG
upgradeTimeout server 10 000 ms Time to complete polling→WebSocket upgrade
connectTimeout server 45 000 ms Time for a client to send the CONNECT for a namespace after the Engine.IO handshake (else the raw connection is closed)
maxDisconnectionDuration server (CSR) 120 000 ms How long a session is kept for recovery
timeout client 20 000 ms Connection attempt timeout → connect_error "timeout"
reconnectionDelay client 1 000 ms Initial reconnection delay
reconnectionDelayMax client 5 000 ms Max reconnection delay
randomizationFactor client 0.5 Jitter
ackTimeout client none Default ack timeout (used with retries)
socket.timeout(ms) both none Per-emit ack timeout
requestsTimeout Redis adapter 5 000 ms Timeout for inter-server requests (fetchSockets, etc.)

5.11 Handling token expiry / re-authentication on long-lived connections

A WebSocket connection can outlive the access token used to authenticate it (tokens expire in 15 min; connections last hours). Options:

  1. Validate only at connection time (most common): when the token expires the connection stays authenticated until it drops. Acceptable for many apps.
  2. Server-side expiry timer: at connection, schedule a disconnect at token expiry:
io.use((socket, next) => { /* verify JWT → socket.data.exp = payload.exp */ next(); });
io.on("connection", (socket) => {
  const ms = socket.data.exp * 1000 - Date.now();
  const t = setTimeout(() => {
    socket.emit("auth:expired");
    socket.disconnect(true);
  }, ms);
  socket.on("disconnect", () => clearTimeout(t));
});

Client: on io server disconnect or auth:expired, refresh the token, set socket.auth, call socket.connect(). 3. In-band refresh: client sends auth:refresh with a new token before expiry; server verifies and updates socket.data.exp (and resets the timer). 4. Revocation (logout, ban, password change): io.in("user:" + id).disconnectSockets(true) — cluster-wide with an adapter. 5. Per-event authorization checks for sensitive operations (check permissions at the time of the action, not only at connect).

Use auth as a function so every reconnection picks up the latest token:

const socket = io(URL, {
  auth: (cb) => cb({ token: localStorage.getItem("accessToken") }),
});

5.12 Page lifecycle (browser specifics)

  • Page reload/close: the browser closes the connection; the server sees transport close (it can't distinguish "closed tab" from "lost network" — use a grace period for presence, Chapter 13).
  • closeOnBeforeunload (client option, default false in current versions; it was true in some earlier 4.x releases): when true, the client closes the connection silently on beforeunload, so every browser behaves the same (Firefox closes the connection on beforeunload, Chrome doesn't) and no disconnect event fires at the Socket.IO level while the page is unloading. Only matters if your client code reacts to disconnect during a reload.
  • Mobile background: iOS/Android suspend apps; the connection dies; reconnect when foregrounded. Use push notifications (APNs/FCM) for delivery while the app is in the background — Socket.IO can't deliver to a suspended app.
  • navigator.onLine / online event: you can call socket.connect() on the browser's online event to reconnect faster than waiting for the back-off.
  • Visibility: pause high-frequency subscriptions when document.hidden.

5.13 Chapter summary

  • Server events: connection, disconnecting, disconnect, error; Engine connection_error with codes 0–5 (code 1 = Session ID unknown = sticky session issue).
  • Client events: connect, connect_error, disconnect; Manager: reconnect*, error, ping, open, close.
  • Disconnect reasons and which ones auto-reconnect (not io server disconnect/ io client disconnect, not middleware rejection).
  • Heartbeat: server PINGs every 25 s, 20 s to PONG; client times out after 45 s of no PING.
  • Reconnection: exponential back-off with jitter (1 s → 5 s max, ±50%).
  • Client buffers emits while disconnected (use volatile or clear sendBuffer).
  • Connection state recovery restores id, rooms, data, missed packets for short disconnections — best effort; not for packets with acks or volatile; adapter dependent.
  • Default delivery: at-most-once, ordered. At-least-once = acks + retries + idempotency (client→server) and persisted messages + offset sync + dedupe (server→client).

Next: Chapter 6 — Middleware, authentication and security

Middleware, Authentication and Security

Goal: protect your real-time API like you protect your REST API — authenticate every connection, authorize every action, validate every payload, limit abuse, and know the attacks specific to WebSockets.

6.1 ELI5

Your server is a party.

  • The bouncer at the door (connection middleware io.use) checks every guest's ticket (token/cookie) before they enter. No ticket → "Sorry, you can't come in" (connect_error).
  • Inside, a staff member near the VIP room (per-event middleware socket.use, or checks inside handlers) checks if you're allowed to do specific things.
  • Guests may try to shout too much (spam) → rate limiting.
  • Guests may bring weird packages (bad payloads) → validation.
  • A sneaky neighbour may try to sneak in using your ticket via a fake website → Cross-Site WebSocket Hijacking protection (origin checks).

6.2 The three middleware levels

Level API Runs Typical use
HTTP / Engine.IO io.engine.use((req, res, next) => …) (v4.6+), or allowRequest option On every HTTP request of the Engine.IO connection (handshake, polling requests, WebSocket upgrade request) Express middleware: express-session, helmet, passport, cookie parsing
Namespace (connection) io.use((socket, next) => …) / io.of("/x").use(…) Once per connection to that namespace, before connection Authentication, loading the user, connection limits
Socket (per event) socket.use(([event, ...args], next) => …) Every incoming event for that socket Authorization per event, rate limiting, validation, logging

6.2.1 Namespace (connection) middleware — io.use

io.use((socket, next) => {
  // runs for every incoming connection on the main namespace
  if (isValid(socket.handshake.auth.token)) {
    next();                                   // accept
  } else {
    const err = new Error("not authorized");  // reject
    err.data = { code: "AUTH_INVALID", hint: "please log in again" }; // extra details for the client
    next(err);
  }
});

Rules and facts:

  1. You must call next() — otherwise the client stays stuck "connecting". If it has not joined any namespace after connectTimeout (default 45 s), the server closes the underlying connection and the client retries — and gets stuck again. Forgetting next() in one code path is a classic bug ("client never connects, no error").
  2. Middlewares run in registration order; next(err) stops the chain.
  3. next(err) → the client receives connect_error with err.message and err.data. The client does not auto-reconnect after a middleware rejection (socket.active === false).
  4. The socket is not yet connected in a middleware: don't emit to it or join rooms there — do that in the connection handler. Also, if the connection is eventually rejected, no disconnect event fires for it, so don't allocate resources in middleware that you only release on disconnect.
  5. Middleware can be async:
io.use(async (socket, next) => {
  try {
    socket.data.user = await auth.verify(socket.handshake.auth.token);
    next();
  } catch (e) {
    next(new Error("unauthorized"));
  }
});
  1. Middlewares are per namespace. io.use = main namespace only. To protect /admin, use io.of("/admin").use(...). (Dynamic namespace parent: io.of(/regex/).use.)
  2. With connection state recovery and skipMiddlewares: true, middleware is skipped for recovered sessions.

6.2.2 Engine-level middleware — io.engine.use (v4.6+)

Runs Express-compatible middleware on the raw HTTP request(s):

const session = require("express-session");

const sessionMiddleware = session({
  secret: process.env.SESSION_SECRET,
  resave: false,
  saveUninitialized: false,
  cookie: { httpOnly: true, secure: true, sameSite: "lax" },
});

app.use(sessionMiddleware);        // for Express routes
io.engine.use(sessionMiddleware);  // for Socket.IO requests

io.on("connection", (socket) => {
  const session = socket.request.session;   // same session as Express
  if (!session.userId) return socket.disconnect(true);
  socket.join(`user:${session.userId}`);
});

With Passport (session-based):

function onlyForHandshake(middleware) {
  return (req, res, next) => {
    const isHandshake = req._query.sid === undefined; // first request has no sid yet
    if (isHandshake) middleware(req, res, next);
    else next();
  };
}

io.engine.use(onlyForHandshake(sessionMiddleware));
io.engine.use(onlyForHandshake(passport.session()));
io.engine.use(onlyForHandshake((req, res, next) => {
  if (req.user) next();
  else { res.writeHead(401); res.end(); }
}));

io.on("connection", (socket) => {
  const user = socket.request.user;
});

helmet can also be applied: io.engine.use(helmet()).

Before 4.6, people used a wrapper: const wrap = (mw) => (socket, next) => mw(socket.request, {}, next); io.use(wrap(sessionMiddleware)); — you'll see it in older code bases.

Note: with the WebSocket transport, there's no res object to write headers to after the upgrade, so cookies set in middleware during a WebSocket-only connection can't be sent back. That's why session cookies should be created by your normal HTTP login route, not by the socket.

6.2.3 allowRequest — the earliest gate

const io = new Server(httpServer, {
  allowRequest: (req, callback) => {
    const origin = req.headers.origin;
    const allowed = ALLOWED_ORIGINS.includes(origin);
    callback(null, allowed);   // (err: string | null | undefined, success: boolean)
  },
});
  • Runs for the handshake / upgrade requests, before any Socket.IO logic.
  • A false result → HTTP 403 → Engine connection_error code 4 Forbidden; client connect_error (with socket.active === true, it will keep retrying — because at this level it looks like a transport failure).
  • Great place for origin checks (against CSWSH), IP allow/deny lists, quick connection rate limiting.

6.2.4 Per-event middleware — socket.use

io.on("connection", (socket) => {
  socket.use(([event, ...args], next) => {
    logger.debug({ event, sid: socket.id }, "incoming event");
    next();
  });

  socket.use(([event], next) => {
    if (event.startsWith("admin:") && !socket.data.user.isAdmin) {
      return next(new Error("forbidden"));
    }
    next();
  });

  socket.on("error", (err) => {
    // errors passed to next(err) in socket.use() land here; they are NOT sent to the client automatically
    socket.emit("app:error", { message: err.message });
  });
});
  • Calling next(err) prevents the event handler from running and emits an error event on the server socket.
  • The ack callback (if any) is the last element of args — you can reply directly:
socket.use(([event, ...args], next) => {
  const ack = typeof args.at(-1) === "function" ? args.at(-1) : null;
  if (rateLimited(socket)) return ack ? ack({ ok: false, error: "RATE_LIMITED" }) : undefined;
  next();
});
  • Not calling next() silently drops the event.

6.3 What's available on socket.handshake

io.use((socket, next) => {
  socket.handshake === {
    headers: {/* the headers of the initial HTTP request */},
    time: "Fri Oct 16 2026 10:00:00 GMT+0000",  // creation date as string
    address: "::ffff:127.0.0.1",                // IP of the client (or of the proxy!)
    xdomain: false,                             // cross-domain?
    secure: false,                              // TLS?
    issued: 1760608800000,                      // creation date (ms timestamp)
    url: "/socket.io/?EIO=4&transport=polling&t=…", // request URL
    query: { EIO: "4", transport: "polling", t: "…" /* + your query params */ },
    auth: { token: "…" /* from the client's auth option */ },
  };
  next();
});

Also: socket.request (the first http.IncomingMessage), socket.conn (Engine.IO socket: socket.conn.transport.name, socket.conn.remoteAddress, socket.conn.protocol).

Getting the real client IP behind a proxy

socket.handshake.address is the IP of the direct peer — your load balancer. Read X-Forwarded-For only if you trust your proxy (otherwise anyone can spoof it):

function clientIp(socket) {
  const xff = socket.handshake.headers["x-forwarded-for"];
  return xff ? xff.split(",")[0].trim() : socket.handshake.address;
}

(With Cloudflare: cf-connecting-ip. With nginx, make sure it sets X-Forwarded-For/ X-Real-IP and that clients cannot reach Node directly.)

6.4 Authentication strategies

6.4.1 Token in the auth payload (recommended for SPAs/mobile)

// client
const socket = io("https://api.example.com", {
  auth: { token: accessToken },
  // or dynamic (re-evaluated on every connection attempt → picks up refreshed tokens):
  // auth: (cb) => cb({ token: localStorage.getItem("accessToken") }),
});

// server
const jwt = require("jsonwebtoken");

io.use((socket, next) => {
  const token = socket.handshake.auth?.token;
  if (!token) return next(new Error("NO_TOKEN"));
  try {
    const payload = jwt.verify(token, process.env.JWT_PUBLIC_KEY, {
      algorithms: ["RS256"],            // ALWAYS pin algorithms
      audience: "realtime-api",
      issuer: "https://auth.example.com",
    });
    socket.data.user = { id: payload.sub, roles: payload.roles, exp: payload.exp };
    next();
  } catch (e) {
    const err = new Error(e.name === "TokenExpiredError" ? "TOKEN_EXPIRED" : "TOKEN_INVALID");
    next(err);
  }
});

Why auth and not the query string?

  • The auth payload travels inside the CONNECT packet (the message body), not the URL.
  • Query strings end up in server/proxy/CDN access logs, browser history and monitoring tools → token leak.
  • auth is per namespace (each namespace can authenticate differently).

Why not headers? Browsers' WebSocket API cannot set custom headers. extraHeaders works for polling in browsers and for everything in Node.js clients, but in the browser the WebSocket handshake won't carry them. So Authorization headers are unreliable for browser clients → use auth (or cookies).

6.4.2 Cookie / session-based

  • Use io.engine.use(sessionMiddleware) (above).
  • Cross-origin: client withCredentials: true; server cors: { origin: <exact>, credentials: true }; cookie SameSite=None; Secure if truly cross-site.
  • Same-origin deployments (frontend and API on the same domain) avoid most of this pain.
  • Cookies are sent automatically by the browser → vulnerable to CSWSH (next section) unless you check Origin.

6.4.3 Short-lived "socket ticket" pattern (high-security)

  1. Client calls POST /api/socket-ticket (authenticated via normal session/JWT).
  2. Server returns a single-use, 30-second random ticket stored in Redis (ticket → userId).
  3. Client connects with auth: { ticket }.
  4. Middleware atomically consumes the ticket (GETDEL) and loads the user.

Benefits: long-lived credentials never travel over the socket; replay window tiny.

6.4.4 Authorization is not authentication

Authenticating the connection is not enough. Every event that touches data must check permissions:

socket.on("conversation:join", async ({ conversationId }, ack) => {
  // ❌ trusting the client: "I'm allowed in conversation 42"
  // ✅ checking server-side:
  const allowed = await acl.isMember(socket.data.user.id, conversationId);
  if (!allowed) return ack?.({ ok: false, error: "FORBIDDEN" });
  await socket.join(`conversation:${conversationId}`);
  ack?.({ ok: true });
});

Never accept identity fields from the payload ({ userId: 5, text }) — always use socket.data.user.id.

6.5 Cross-Site WebSocket Hijacking (CSWSH) — the WebSocket-specific attack

ELI5: a bad website (evil.com) tells your browser "please open a WebSocket to bank.com". The browser automatically attaches your bank.com cookies. If bank.com authenticates sockets only by cookie and doesn't check where the request came from, evil.com now has a live, authenticated socket as you — it can read your data and send commands.

Why it happens: the Same-Origin Policy / CORS does not apply to WebSocket handshakes. Browsers send the Origin header but don't block cross-origin WebSocket connections; the server must decide.

Note: with the default ["polling", "websocket"] transports, the initial polling request is subject to CORS, which helps — but an attacker can force transports: ["websocket"] to skip polling entirely, so you must protect the WebSocket path too.

Proof (tested on socket.io 4.8.4): with cors: { origin: "http://localhost:5173" }, a raw WebSocket handshake to /socket.io/?EIO=4&transport=websocket carrying Origin: https://evil.example was accepted — the server answered with the Engine.IO open packet 0{"sid":…}. The cors option only adds CORS headers to HTTP polling responses; it does not reject WebSocket upgrades. (python-socketio, by contrast, rejected the same request with HTTP 403 — see Chapter 17.)

Defences (use several):

  1. Check Origin on the handshake and upgrade (allowRequest) against an allow-list.
  2. Token-based auth in auth (not automatically sent by the browser) instead of, or in addition to, cookies.
  3. SameSite=Lax/Strict cookies (cross-site WebSocket requests won't carry Strict/ Lax cookies in modern browsers — but don't rely on this alone).
  4. Short-lived tickets.
const ALLOWED = new Set(["https://app.example.com", "https://admin.example.com"]);

const io = new Server(httpServer, {
  cors: { origin: [...ALLOWED], credentials: true },
  allowRequest: (req, cb) => cb(null, ALLOWED.has(req.headers.origin)),
});

Verified result of this exact configuration (4.8.4): allowed origin → accepted; forged Origin: https://evil.example → polling request answered HTTP 403 {"code":4,"message":"Forbidden"}, WebSocket upgrade refused (HTTP 400), and io.engine.on("connection_error") logged code 4 Forbidden.

(Non-browser clients — mobile apps, Node scripts — may not send Origin. Decide explicitly: allow missing origin only if authentication is token-based.)

6.6 Input validation (never trust the client)

Anything the client sends can be malicious: wrong types, huge strings, prototype pollution keys (__proto__), negative numbers, deeply nested objects.

const { z } = require("zod");

const SendMessage = z.object({
  conversationId: z.string().uuid(),
  text: z.string().trim().min(1).max(2000),
  clientMsgId: z.string().uuid(),
});

socket.on("message:send", async (raw, ack) => {
  if (typeof ack !== "function") return;          // require ack for commands
  const parsed = SendMessage.safeParse(raw);
  if (!parsed.success) return ack({ ok: false, error: "VALIDATION", details: parsed.error.flatten() });
  // ... use parsed.data only
});

Checklist:

  • Validate types, lengths, ranges, formats, and number of arguments.
  • Reject unknown keys (.strict()), especially before spreading into DB queries (NoSQL injection: { "$gt": "" }).
  • Sanitize/escape user content at render time on clients (textContent, React's escaping) to prevent XSS — broadcasting HTML received from one user to everyone is a fast way to an XSS worm.
  • Use parameterized queries (SQL injection still exists over sockets!).
  • Limit the size of arrays/nesting to avoid CPU exhaustion.
  • Validate binary payloads (type, size, magic bytes) before storing.

6.7 Rate limiting and abuse prevention

Per-socket event rate limiting

const { RateLimiterMemory } = require("rate-limiter-flexible"); // or RateLimiterRedis for clusters

const limiter = new RateLimiterMemory({ points: 20, duration: 1 }); // 20 events / second

io.on("connection", (socket) => {
  socket.use(async ([event, ...args], next) => {
    try {
      await limiter.consume(socket.data.user?.id ?? socket.id); // key per user (or per IP)
      next();
    } catch {
      const ack = typeof args.at(-1) === "function" ? args.at(-1) : null;
      ack?.({ ok: false, error: "RATE_LIMITED" });
      // optionally disconnect repeat offenders
    }
  });
});

Use per-user keys (users can open many sockets) and Redis-backed limiters in a cluster (RateLimiterRedis) so limits are global, not per process.

Connection limits

// limit concurrent connections per user (register this as the LAST middleware)
io.use(async (socket, next) => {
  const key = `conn:${socket.data.user.id}`;
  const count = await redis.incr(key);
  await redis.expire(key, 3600);
  if (count > 10) { await redis.decr(key); return next(new Error("TOO_MANY_CONNECTIONS")); }
  next();
});

io.on("connection", (socket) => {
  socket.on("disconnect", () => redis.decr(`conn:${socket.data.user.id}`));
});

(Careful: process crashes leave counters inflated → in real systems prefer a Redis sorted set of socketId → lastSeen cleaned by expiry, so stale entries disappear by themselves.)

Connection-rate limits per IP at the edge (nginx limit_conn/limit_req, WAF, Cloudflare) are cheaper than in Node.

Payload size

  • maxHttpBufferSize (default 1 MB) — max size of a single message. Larger → the server closes the connection. Lower it (e.g. 1e5 = 100 KB) if you only send small JSON, to limit memory abuse.
  • On polling, this also limits the request body size.

Other DoS considerations

  • Slow consumers: a client that doesn't read causes server-side write buffers to grow. Monitor socket.conn.transport.writable/buffer sizes; disconnect clients whose buffers exceed limits (e.g. check socket.conn.writeBuffer.length).
  • Too many rooms / namespaces created by client input (dynamic namespace names or room names from user input) → memory growth. Validate names, cap counts, use cleanupEmptyChildNamespaces.
  • Expensive handlers: an event that triggers a heavy DB query can be spammed → rate limit and cache.
  • Pending acks: server-side socket.emit(..., ack) without timeout to a client that never answers keeps callbacks in memory → always use timeout().

6.8 Transport security

  • Always use TLS (https:// → wss://) in production. Without it, tokens and data are readable by anyone on the network, and many proxies mangle unencrypted WebSocket traffic (another reason WebSocket "doesn't work" on some networks — TLS hides it from them).
  • Terminate TLS at the load balancer or in Node (https.createServer).
  • HSTS on your domains.
  • Don't disable certificate validation in Node clients (rejectUnauthorized: false) except in local development.

6.9 Information leakage

  • Don't send internal error messages/stack traces to clients (err.message from DB drivers can leak schema/SQL). Map to error codes.
  • Don't broadcast full user objects (emails, phone numbers) to rooms — send minimal public projections.
  • socket.data is sent between servers with fetchSockets() — fine, but don't put secrets there unnecessarily.
  • Remove serveClient (set false) if you bundle the client yourself — less attack surface, no version disclosure.
  • The Admin UI must be protected with authentication (auth: { type: "basic", … }) and preferably not exposed publicly (Chapter 10).

6.10 Keep dependencies updated — real vulnerabilities have happened

Real advisories have been published for every layer of the stack. This table was pulled from the npm / GitHub advisory database (October 2026); each row links to the advisory by its GHSA id (https://github.com/advisories/<id>):

Package Affected versions Severity Issue GHSA id
socket.io < 2.4.0 moderate CORS misconfiguration (all origins allowed by default) GHSA-fxwf-4rqh-v8g3
socket.io < 2.5.1, and ≥ 3.0.0 < 4.6.2 moderate Unhandled error event (can crash the process) GHSA-25hc-qcg6-38wj
engine.io ≥ 4.0.0 < 6.2.1 moderate Uncaught exception from a crafted request GHSA-r7qp-cfhv-p84w
engine.io ≥ 5.1.0 < 6.4.2 moderate Uncaught exception vulnerability GHSA-q9mw-68c2-j6m5
engine.io ≥ 4.1.0 < 6.6.7 high Polling transport connection exhaustion GHSA-r635-g3xr-vw7x
socket.io-parser ≥ 4.0.0 < 4.0.5 critical Insufficient validation when decoding a packet GHSA-qm95-pgcg-qqfq
socket.io-parser ≥ 4.0.4 < 4.2.3 moderate Insufficient validation when decoding a packet GHSA-cqmj-92xf-r6r9
socket.io-parser ≥ 4.0.0 < 4.2.6 high Unbounded number of binary attachments GHSA-677m-j7p3-52f9
socket.io-parser ≥ 4.0.0 < 4.2.7 high Zero-attachment memory exhaustion GHSA-2m8v-j782-fhvr
ws ≥ 8.0.0 < 8.17.1 high DoS with a request containing many HTTP headers GHSA-3h5v-q93c-6h6q
ws ≥ 8.0.0 < 8.20.1 moderate Uninitialized memory disclosure GHSA-58qx-3vcg-4xpx
ws ≥ 8.0.0 < 8.21.0 high Memory exhaustion DoS from tiny fragments GHSA-96hv-2xvq-fx4p

(Older major lines — e.g. ws 5–7, socket.io-parser 3.x — had their own advisories and backported fixes; check the advisory pages for your exact version.)

A fresh npm install socket.io@4.8.4 in October 2026 resolved to engine.io@6.6.11, socket.io-parser@4.2.7 and ws@8.21.3 — all past the fixes above. But transitive versions are pinned by your lockfile: an old package-lock.json can keep a vulnerable ws or socket.io-parser even after you bump socket.io. Run npm ls ws engine.io socket.io-parser to see what you actually ship.

Notice the pattern: almost every issue is "a malicious client sends a malformed or oversized packet and crashes or exhausts the server". That's why maxHttpBufferSize, rate limits and patching matter even for internal apps.

Lessons: run npm audit / Dependabot / Snyk in CI, keep socket.io, engine.io, socket.io-parser and ws patched, and add a process.on("uncaughtException") logger + process manager restarts as defence in depth.

6.11 Security checklist (copy into your team's PR template)

  • [ ] TLS everywhere (wss://).
  • [ ] cors.origin is an explicit allow-list (never "*" with credentials).
  • [ ] allowRequest checks Origin (CSWSH protection), especially with cookie auth.
  • [ ] Connection authenticated in io.use (token in auth, not query string).
  • [ ] Token expiry handled (disconnect/refresh), revocation via disconnectSockets.
  • [ ] Every event handler authorizes the action and validates input (schema).
  • [ ] No identity taken from payloads; use socket.data.user.
  • [ ] Room joins authorized server-side.
  • [ ] Rate limits per user/IP (Redis-backed in clusters); connection caps.
  • [ ] maxHttpBufferSize sized for your messages.
  • [ ] Errors mapped to codes, no stack traces to clients.
  • [ ] User-generated content escaped on render (XSS).
  • [ ] Admin UI protected or disabled in production.
  • [ ] serveClient: false if not needed.
  • [ ] Dependencies scanned and up to date.
  • [ ] Handlers wrapped (no crash on throw/reject).

6.12 Chapter summary

  • Three middleware levels: io.engine.use (HTTP), io.use (connection), socket.use (per event), plus the allowRequest gate.
  • next(err) in connection middleware → client connect_error with message/data, no auto-reconnect.
  • Authenticate with auth payload (or cookies + origin checks); never trust identity from payloads; authorize every action.
  • CSWSH: WebSocket isn't covered by CORS → check Origin.
  • Validate inputs, rate-limit, limit sizes, escape output, keep deps patched.

Next: Chapter 7 — Under the hood

Under the Hood: Engine.IO, Transports, the Protocol, Parsers and Adapters

Goal: understand exactly what travels on the wire and which internal component does what. This is what lets you debug anything with the browser's Network tab, and what senior interviews probe ("explain the upgrade mechanism", "what's an adapter?", "why sticky sessions?").

All packet captures below come from a real Socket.IO 4.8.4 server.

7.1 ELI5

Sending a Socket.IO message is like sending a parcel:

  1. You write a letter ("chat", "hi") — your event.
  2. The Socket.IO parser puts it in an envelope and writes on it: "this is an EVENT, for department /admin, reply slip #7" → 42/admin,7["chat","hi"].
  3. Engine.IO puts the envelope in a box marked "MESSAGE" → adds a 4 in front.
  4. The transport is the delivery truck: either a long-polling truck (HTTP requests) or a WebSocket truck (an always-open road). If the road is blocked, the old truck still delivers.
  5. On arrival, everything is unpacked in reverse order.

The adapter is the post office sorting room: it knows which people are in which groups (rooms) and, with several post offices (servers), it forwards parcels between them.

7.2 The architecture (server side)

                   ┌──────────────────────────── Server (io) ───────────────────────────┐
HTTP server ──▶    │  Engine.IO Server (io.engine)                                       │
(node:http)        │    └─ Engine.IO Socket (socket.conn) ── Transport (polling|ws|wt)  │
                   │          ▲                                                          │
                   │          │ 1 per client connection                                  │
                   │  Client (internal) ── decoder/encoder (socket.io-parser)            │
                   │     └─ 1 Socket per joined Namespace                                │
                   │  Namespace "/" ─── Adapter (rooms: Map<Room, Set<SocketId>>)       │
                   │  Namespace "/admin" ─── Adapter                                     │
                   └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
Component Package Responsibility
Server socket.io Entry point, options, namespaces, attaches to HTTP server
Namespace socket.io Group of sockets with own handlers/middleware/adapter
Socket socket.io One client in one namespace; emit/on/join/leave
Client socket.io (internal) One per Engine.IO connection; demultiplexes packets to namespace Sockets
Adapter socket.io-adapter (+ Redis etc.) Stores room membership, performs broadcasts, inter-server communication
Parser socket.io-parser Encodes/decodes Socket.IO packets (JSON + binary attachments)
Engine.IO Server / Socket engine.io Handshake, transports, upgrade, heartbeat, low-level packets
Engine.IO parser engine.io-parser Encodes Engine.IO packets and payloads
ws ws Default WebSocket server implementation (wsEngine)

Client side mirrors it: Socket (per namespace) → Manager (connection, reconnection) → engine.io-client Socket → transports.

7.3 Engine.IO protocol (v4)

Engine.IO packet types

Type Name Direction Purpose
0 open S→C Handshake response (sid, upgrades, pingInterval, pingTimeout, maxPayload)
1 close both Request to close the transport
2 ping S→C (also 2probe C→S during upgrade) Heartbeat
3 pong C→S (also 3probe S→C during upgrade) Heartbeat reply
4 message both Carries an application (Socket.IO) packet
5 upgrade C→S Completes the transport upgrade
6 noop S→C Used to close a pending poll during upgrade

The handshake (open packet) — real capture

GET /socket.io/?EIO=4&transport=polling&t=<cache-buster>
← 0{"sid":"HSJR3YuCOE9jPqHEAAAA","upgrades":["websocket"],"pingInterval":25000,"pingTimeout":20000,"maxPayload":1000000}

Query parameters:

Param Meaning
EIO=4 Engine.IO protocol version (v3/v4 clients send 4; v2 clients send 3)
transport polling / websocket / webtransport
sid Session id (on every request after the handshake)
t Cache-busting timestamp (polling)
+ your query option params

Note "upgrades":["websocket"] — what this transport may be upgraded to. When the handshake itself happens over WebSocket the list is empty (nothing to upgrade to):

(WebSocket-only connection) ← 0{"sid":"u1NewvylZUVY8NNBAAAA","upgrades":[],"pingInterval":2000,"pingTimeout":1000,"maxPayload":1000000}

maxPayload tells the client the max bytes per polling payload (= server's maxHttpBufferSize), so the client can split its writes across several requests.

HTTP long-polling transport

  • Receiving (server → client): the client keeps one GET pending. The server answers it when it has packets (or when it sends a ping). Then the client sends a new GET immediately.
  • Sending (client → server): the client sends POST requests with packets in the body. Only one POST at a time; packets emitted meanwhile are batched in the next POST.
  • Payload format (EIO v4): multiple packets in one HTTP body are separated by the record separator character \x1e (ASCII 30).
  • Binary over polling: encoded as base64 with a b prefix.

Real capture: one polling GET returning three packets (CONNECT reply, a binary event header, and its binary attachment), separated by \x1e:

40/admin,{"sid":"l_dmkQgfXzZ8Ld6XAAAB"}␞451-/admin,["bin",{"_placeholder":true,"num":0},"txt"]␞bAQID

bAQID = b + base64(01 02 03) — the bytes [1,2,3].

Why polling needs sticky sessions: the session (sid) lives in the memory of one server. Each GET/POST is a separate HTTP request; if the load balancer sends one to another server, that server answers 400 {"code":1,"message":"Session ID unknown"}.

WebSocket transport

  • One WebSocket per connection; each Engine.IO packet = one WebSocket frame.
  • Text packets are sent as text frames (4… for messages, 2/3 for ping/pong).
  • Binary packets are sent as binary frames with the raw bytes (no prefix, no base64).

Real capture over WebSocket (connecting to namespace /admin, receiving a binary event, sending an event with an ack, heartbeat):

→ 40/admin,
← 0{"sid":"u1NewvylZUVY8NNBAAAA","upgrades":[],"pingInterval":2000,"pingTimeout":1000,"maxPayload":1000000}
← 40/admin,{"sid":"O8qU3PmyyZeZYZZHAAAA"}
← 451-/admin,["bin",{"_placeholder":true,"num":0},"txt"]
← (binary frame) 01 02 03
→ 42/admin,7["q"]
← 43/admin,7["answer"]
← 2
→ 3

The upgrade mechanism (polling → WebSocket)

Client                                         Server
  |--- GET  ?transport=polling  (handshake) ----->|
  |<-- 0{"sid":…,"upgrades":["websocket"]…} ------|
  |--- POST 40 (CONNECT) ------------------------>|
  |<-- 40{"sid":…} (via pending GET) -------------|
  |                                                |
  |=== WS  ?transport=websocket&sid=… (upgrade) ==>|   (101 Switching Protocols)
  |=== 2probe ====================================>|   test the new road
  |<== 3probe =====================================|   it works!
  |  (client pauses polling: waits for the pending  |
  |   GET/POST to complete; server ends the pending |
  |   GET with a 6 "noop")                          |
  |=== 5 (upgrade) ===============================>|   switch!
  |<== buffered packets now flow over WebSocket ===|
  • The upgradeTimeout (default 10 s) limits how long the server waits for the upgrade to complete; on timeout the WebSocket is closed and the client stays on polling.
  • Because polling is paused and flushed before switching, no packet is lost or reordered during the upgrade.
  • rememberUpgrade: true (client) → if a previous connection successfully used WebSocket, the next one starts directly with WebSocket (stored in memory per page — uses a static flag).
  • upgrade: false (client) → stay on the first transport.

WebTransport (v4.7+)

  • HTTP/3 (QUIC) based transport; requires HTTPS and an HTTP/3 server (e.g. @fails-components/webtransport), plus enabling "webtransport" in the server's transports.
  • The client lists "webtransport" in its default transports; it's used if the server advertises it in upgrades.
  • Benefits: no head-of-line blocking across streams, faster connection setup (0/1-RTT). Use for advanced cases; WebSocket is the practical default.

7.4 Socket.IO protocol (v5)

Socket.IO packet types

Type Name Example Meaning
0 CONNECT 0 / 0/admin,{"token":"…"} (C→S) ; 0{"sid":"…"} (S→C) Connect to a namespace (with auth payload); server reply carries the socket id (and pid with CSR)
1 DISCONNECT 1/admin, Leave a namespace
2 EVENT 2["hello","world"] / 2/admin,13["q"] An event, optional ack id
3 ACK 313["answer"] Acknowledgement for ack id 13
4 CONNECT_ERROR 4{"message":"not authorized","data":{…}} Namespace connection refused (middleware error / invalid namespace)
5 BINARY_EVENT 51-["bin",{"_placeholder":true,"num":0}] Event with N binary attachments
6 BINARY_ACK 61-15[{"_placeholder":true,"num":0}] Ack with binary attachments

On the wire each Socket.IO packet travels inside an Engine.IO message (4), which is why you see 40, 42, 43, 44, 45… in the Network tab.

Packet format

<packet type>[<# of binary attachments>-][<namespace>,][<ack id>][JSON-stringified payload]
  • Namespace is omitted when it's the main namespace /.
  • Ack id is present only when the sender expects an acknowledgement.

Decoding real examples:

Raw (as seen in DevTools) Engine.IO Socket.IO Meaning
40 message CONNECT / client asks to join /
40{"sid":"KykvYCRwmIsIifIyAAAA"} message CONNECT reply joined /, socket id
421["hello","world"] message EVENT, ack id 1 emit("hello","world", cb)
431[{"ok":true}] message ACK 1 cb({ok:true})
42/admin,7["q"] message EVENT in /admin, ack 7
451-/admin,["bin",{"_placeholder":true,"num":0},"txt"] message BINARY_EVENT, 1 attachment, /admin followed by 1 binary frame
44{"message":"not authorized"} message CONNECT_ERROR middleware rejected
41 message DISCONNECT /
2 / 3 ping / pong — heartbeat

Binary handling

  1. The parser walks the arguments, finds Buffer/ArrayBuffer/Blob etc.
  2. Replaces each with a placeholder {"_placeholder":true,"num":<index>}.
  3. Sends the JSON header packet (BINARY_EVENT with the count) followed by each binary attachment as a separate Engine.IO message (binary frame / b-base64 in polling).
  4. The receiver buffers the header until all attachments arrive, then reconstructs the arguments.

Implications: - One logical binary event = several frames. - A message with many small buffers is less efficient than one combined buffer. - Security: attackers can send malformed attachment counts — keep socket.io-parser patched (historic CVEs) and keep maxHttpBufferSize sane.

Why can't a plain WebSocket client talk to Socket.IO?

Because it would need to: open the right URL (/socket.io/?EIO=4&transport=websocket), parse the 0{...} open packet, send 40 to join a namespace, answer every 2 with 3 (or be disconnected after pingTimeout), and encode/decode 42[...]/binary attachments. That is the Socket.IO protocol — which is exactly what client libraries implement.

7.5 Parsers

The parser turns packets into wire format and back. Both sides must use the same parser.

Parser Package Notes
Default socket.io-parser JSON + separate binary attachments; human-readable in DevTools
MessagePack socket.io-msgpack-parser Binary encoding of everything; smaller payloads for some data, handles binary inline (no attachments); not human-readable; numbers/strings encoding differs slightly
Custom implement Encoder/Decoder e.g. protobuf-based, compression, encryption 
// server
const customParser = require("socket.io-msgpack-parser");
const io = new Server(httpServer, { parser: customParser });

// client
import customParser from "socket.io-msgpack-parser";
const socket = io(URL, { parser: customParser });

When does msgpack help? Payloads with lots of numbers/binary; when it doesn't: mostly textual payloads (similar size, more CPU in JS). Measure before switching. Also note the Redis adapter has its own parser option for messages between servers (msgpack by default via notepack.io).

7.6 Adapters (internals)

What an adapter does

Every namespace has an adapter instance (io.of("/").adapter). Its job:

  1. Store room membership: rooms: Map<Room, Set<SocketId>> and sids: Map<SocketId, Set<Room>>.
  2. Broadcast: given { rooms, except, flags }, compute the target sockets and write the (once-encoded) packet to each.
  3. In a cluster: forward broadcasts and requests (fetchSockets, socketsJoin, serverSideEmit, broadcast acks…) to other servers and handle theirs.
  4. For connection state recovery: persist sessions and packets (SessionAwareAdapter in the in-memory adapter, or Redis Streams / MongoDB adapters).

The default in-memory adapter does (1) and (2) only for the local process — which is why a multi-server deployment needs another adapter.

Broadcast algorithm (simplified, in-memory adapter)

broadcast(packet, { rooms, except, flags }):
  encoded = encoder.encode(packet)        # encode ONCE
  if rooms is empty → targets = all sockets in namespace
  else targets = union of sockets of each room (each socket once)
  remove sockets in any "except" room
  for each target socket → socket.client.writeToEngine(encoded, flags)

Encoding once and reusing the buffer for every recipient is a key performance property (io.emit to 10 000 sockets doesn't serialize 10 000 times).

Redis adapter internals (pub/sub)

Server A: io.to("room1").emit("x")
   ├─ broadcast locally (A's sockets in room1)
   └─ PUBLISH socket.io#/#room1# <msgpack([serverUid, packet, {rooms, except, flags}])>
      (to a single room → channel "socket.io#/#room1#"; otherwise "socket.io#/#")

Server B and C: PSUBSCRIBE "socket.io#/#*" → receive → ignore if it's their own uid
               → broadcast locally to THEIR sockets in room1
  • The classic adapter uses a pattern subscription (PSUBSCRIBE socket.io#/#*), so every server receives every broadcast, even for rooms where it has no sockets. At very large scale this fan-out becomes the bottleneck.
  • Requests like fetchSockets() / socketsJoin() / serverSideEmit() use dedicated channels: socket.io-request#/# and socket.io-response#/#, with requestsTimeout (default 5 s).
  • The sharded adapter (createShardedAdapter, Redis 7+ sharded pub/sub) has a subscriptionMode option: "static" (fixed channels per namespace), "dynamic" (default — one channel per public room, a server subscribes only to rooms it has sockets in), "dynamic-private" (also creates channels for private socket-id rooms).
  • Redis pub/sub is fire-and-forget: if a server is disconnected from Redis momentarily, it misses broadcasts during that time. The Redis Streams adapter fixes this (it can resume reading the stream from the last offset) and supports connection state recovery.

More in Chapter 8.

Writing a custom adapter (rare, but shows mastery)

Adapters extend Adapter from socket.io-adapter and override methods like broadcast, addAll, del, delAll, fetchSockets, addSockets, delSockets, disconnectSockets, serverSideEmit, broadcastWithAck, persistSession, restoreSession. The ClusterAdapter / ClusterAdapterWithHeartbeat base classes in socket.io-adapter let you implement only the transport (how messages travel between nodes) — that's how the Redis Streams, Postgres, MongoDB, cluster and cloud adapters are built.

7.7 What happens during socket.emit("chat", "hi") (server → client), step by step

  1. Socket#emit checks the name isn't reserved, builds { type: EVENT, data: ["chat","hi"], nsp: "/" }, and registers an ack callback if the last arg is a function (assigns an id).
  2. (With connection state recovery enabled, it goes through adapter.broadcast to the socket's own room so the packet gets an offset and is stored.)
  3. The parser encodes: 2["chat","hi"] (plus attachments if binary).
  4. Client#writeToEngine → if volatile and transport not writable → dropped.
  5. Engine.IO wraps it as a message: 42["chat","hi"], pushes it to the writeBuffer, and flushes when the transport is writable (immediately for WebSocket; on the next pending GET for polling — several packets may be batched with \x1e).
  6. ws writes a WebSocket frame (compressed if perMessageDeflate enabled and above threshold).
  7. Client side: engine.io-client receives the frame, decodes the Engine.IO packet type 4, passes 2["chat","hi"] to the Manager's decoder.
  8. The decoder yields the packet; the Manager routes it to the Socket for namespace /.
  9. If the socket isn't connected yet, the packet is stored in receiveBuffer; otherwise onAny listeners run, then chat listeners with "hi".

7.8 Debugging the wire yourself

  • Browser DevTools → Network → filter "socket.io":
  • polling requests appear as XHR/fetch with the payloads in Response/Payload tabs;
  • the WebSocket appears as ?EIO=4&transport=websocket with a Messages tab showing every frame (2, 3, 42[...]). Use this to verify what's actually sent.
  • Node: DEBUG=socket.io*,engine* (server) or DEBUG=socket.io-client:*,engine.io-client:* (Node client); in the browser localStorage.debug = "socket.io-client:*" then reload. (Chapter 10.)
  • curl handshake test (is the server reachable and speaking Socket.IO?):
curl "https://api.example.com/socket.io/?EIO=4&transport=polling"
# expect: 0{"sid":"…","upgrades":["websocket"],"pingInterval":25000,…}

If you get HTML/404 → wrong path or proxy routing. {"code":0,"message":"Transport unknown"} → transport disabled. {"code":5,…} → protocol version mismatch.

7.9 Chapter summary

  • Two protocols: Engine.IO v4 (open/close/ping/pong/message/upgrade/noop = 0–6) and Socket.IO v5 (CONNECT/DISCONNECT/EVENT/ACK/CONNECT_ERROR/BINARY_EVENT/BINARY_ACK = 0–6).
  • 42["event",…] = Engine.IO message (4) + Socket.IO event (2).
  • Polling: GET to receive (held open), POST to send, \x1e separator, binary as b+base64.
  • WebSocket: one frame per packet, binary frames raw.
  • Upgrade: 2probe/3probe, pause polling, 5, no loss/reordering.
  • Parsers: default JSON+attachments, msgpack optional; both sides must match.
  • Adapters: room storage + broadcast + inter-server communication; in-memory by default.

Next: Chapter 8 — Scaling

Scaling: Multiple Servers, Sticky Sessions, Adapters, Load Balancers and Kubernetes

Goal: run Socket.IO on many processes and machines correctly. The two words you must say in any scaling interview are "sticky sessions" and "adapter" — and you must be able to explain why each is needed.

8.1 ELI5

One pizza shop (server) can only answer so many phones. So you open more branches. Two new problems appear:

  1. "Which branch am I talking to?" If you use the "ask and wait on the line" method (long-polling), every call must go to the same branch, because only that branch remembers your order. The receptionist who routes calls must always send you to the same branch → sticky sessions.
  2. "How do I tell everyone in every branch?" If branch A wants to announce "free pizza for room 'Main Street'", customers of Main Street might be on branch B and C. Branches need an intercom connecting them → the adapter (e.g. Redis pub/sub).

8.2 How far can one server go?

A single Node.js process can typically hold tens of thousands of mostly-idle WebSocket connections (10k–100k+ depending on memory, message rate, payload size, compression, and your handler logic). The bottlenecks are usually:

  • CPU (single-threaded event loop): JSON encoding/decoding, broadcast fan-out, your handler logic, TLS (if terminated in Node).
  • Memory: per-connection buffers (more with perMessageDeflate), socket.data, rooms, pending acks, connection-state-recovery packet storage.
  • File descriptors: each connection = 1 fd → raise ulimit -n.
  • Fan-out: broadcasting 1 message to 10 000 sockets = 10 000 writes. At 100 msg/s → 1 M writes/s. Messages × recipients is the metric that matters.

Load test your real workload (Chapter 10) — don't trust generic numbers.

Then you scale horizontally: several Node processes (on one machine via cluster/PM2) and/or several machines/containers behind a load balancer.

8.3 Problem 1 — Sticky sessions (session affinity)

Why they are required

With the HTTP long-polling transport, one Socket.IO connection = many HTTP requests (handshake GET, data GETs, POSTs, then the WebSocket upgrade request). The Engine.IO session (sid) lives in the memory of the server that did the handshake.

Client ── GET handshake ──▶ LB ──▶ Server A   (creates sid=abc)
Client ── POST sid=abc ───▶ LB ──▶ Server B   ✗ 400 {"code":1,"message":"Session ID unknown"}

So the load balancer must route all requests of a session to the same server.

Even when polling upgrades to WebSocket quickly, the first few requests are polling, and the WebSocket upgrade request itself must reach the server that holds the sid.

When you DON'T need sticky sessions

If clients use WebSocket only (transports: ["websocket"]), one connection = one TCP connection that stays on one server → no sticky sessions needed.

// client
const socket = io(URL, { transports: ["websocket"] });
// (optionally also restrict the server: new Server({ transports: ["websocket"] }))

Trade-off: no polling fallback (users behind WebSocket-hostile proxies fail to connect). Many companies accept this for mobile apps and internal tools.

Ways to get sticky sessions

Method How Notes
IP hash nginx ip_hash / hash $remote_addr consistent; HAProxy balance source Simple; uneven distribution behind NAT (whole office on one server); breaks if a client's IP changes (mobile)
Cookie-based LB sets a cookie identifying the server (AWS ALB stickiness, HAProxy cookie, nginx Plus sticky, Traefik sticky cookies, ingress-nginx affinity) Best distribution. Cross-origin: client needs withCredentials: true, server CORS credentials: true so the browser sends the LB cookie on polling requests
Socket.IO cookie option new Server({ cookie: { name: "io", httpOnly: true, sameSite: "strict", maxAge: 86400 } }) → Engine.IO sets an io=<sid> cookie that the LB can hash on Useful with LBs that can route on an arbitrary cookie value
Node cluster @socket.io/sticky (master routes by sid) + @socket.io/cluster-adapter Single machine, multiple cores
PM2 @socket.io/pm2 (a PM2 fork with sticky sessions) + cluster adapter Single machine
Query/URL-based routing e.g. route by ?shard= or by subdomain Custom architectures

8.4 Problem 2 — Broadcasting across servers: adapters

Why it's required

User A connected to Server 1, in room "game:7"
User B connected to Server 2, in room "game:7"

Server 1: io.to("game:7").emit("move")   → reaches only User A ✗

The default in-memory adapter only knows local sockets. A distributed adapter forwards the broadcast to all other servers, which deliver it to their local sockets.

          ┌──────────── Redis / Postgres / Mongo / Pub-Sub / IPC ────────────┐
          │                                                                   │
   ┌──────┴─────┐               ┌─────────────┐               ┌─────────────┐ │
   │  Server 1  │──── publish ──▶ (broker) ───▶ │  Server 2   │ ─▶ │ Server 3 │─┘
   │ io.to(r)…  │               └─────────────┘               └─────────────┘
   └────────────┘

Official / well-known adapters

Adapter Package Backend Highlights
Redis @socket.io/redis-adapter Redis Pub/Sub Most popular; also a sharded variant for Redis 7 sharded pub/sub; ❌ no connection state recovery
Redis Streams @socket.io/redis-streams-adapter Redis Streams Survives temporary Redis disconnections without losing packets; ✅ connection state recovery
MongoDB @socket.io/mongo-adapter MongoDB change streams (replica set required) ✅ CSR (since 0.3.0)
Postgres @socket.io/postgres-adapter LISTEN/NOTIFY (+ attachment table for payloads > 8 KB) ❌ CSR
Cluster @socket.io/cluster-adapter Node.js cluster IPC Multiple cores on one machine
GCP Pub/Sub @socket.io/gcp-pubsub-adapter Google Cloud Pub/Sub
AWS SQS @socket.io/aws-sqs-adapter AWS SNS + SQS
Azure Service Bus @socket.io/azure-service-bus-adapter Azure Service Bus
Azure Web PubSub @azure/web-pubsub-socket.io Managed service Azure hosts the connections for you
Community e.g. AMQP (RabbitMQ), NATS adapters Check maintenance status

All official ones support "socket management" (fetchSockets, socketsJoin, …), inter-server communication (serverSideEmit) and broadcast with acks.

Redis adapter — setup (redis package)

const { createServer } = require("node:http");
const { Server } = require("socket.io");
const { createClient } = require("redis");
const { createAdapter } = require("@socket.io/redis-adapter");

async function main() {
  const pubClient = createClient({ url: process.env.REDIS_URL });
  const subClient = pubClient.duplicate();   // a subscriber connection can't publish → 2 clients

  pubClient.on("error", (err) => logger.error({ err }, "redis pub error"));
  subClient.on("error", (err) => logger.error({ err }, "redis sub error"));

  await Promise.all([pubClient.connect(), subClient.connect()]);

  const httpServer = createServer();
  const io = new Server(httpServer, {
    adapter: createAdapter(pubClient, subClient, {
