      key: "socket.io",          // channel prefix (namespace your apps if they share Redis!)
      requestsTimeout: 5000,     // for fetchSockets etc.
    }),
  });

  httpServer.listen(3000);
}
main();

With ioredis:

const { Redis } = require("ioredis");
const pubClient = new Redis(process.env.REDIS_URL);
const subClient = pubClient.duplicate();
const io = new Server({ adapter: createAdapter(pubClient, subClient) });

With a Redis Cluster (ioredis):

const { Cluster } = require("ioredis");
const pubClient = new Cluster([{ host: "redis-1", port: 6379 }, { host: "redis-2", port: 6379 }]);
const subClient = pubClient.duplicate();

Sharded pub/sub (Redis ≥ 7, with the redis package):

const { createShardedAdapter } = require("@socket.io/redis-adapter");
const io = new Server({ adapter: createShardedAdapter(pubClient, subClient /*, { subscriptionMode: "dynamic" } */) });

Gotchas: - Always attach error listeners to Redis clients; an unhandled error event crashes Node. - Two different apps sharing one Redis must use different key prefixes, or they'll receive each other's broadcasts. - The subscriber connection can't run normal commands; use the pub client (or another client) for app data. - If Redis goes down: local broadcasts still work; cross-server broadcasts are lost while it's down (pub/sub has no buffering). - With the classic adapter every node receives every broadcast (pattern subscription) — the sharded adapter in dynamic mode reduces this.

Redis Streams adapter

const { createClient } = require("redis");
const { createAdapter } = require("@socket.io/redis-streams-adapter");

const redisClient = createClient({ url: process.env.REDIS_URL });
await redisClient.connect();

const io = new Server({
  adapter: createAdapter(redisClient, {
    streamName: "socket.io",   // default
    maxLen: 10_000,            // approximate stream trimming
    readCount: 100,            // XREAD batch size
  }),
  connectionStateRecovery: { maxDisconnectionDuration: 2 * 60 * 1000 },
});
  • Uses one Redis connection (it reads with XREAD BLOCK).
  • A node that loses Redis briefly resumes from its last stream offset → no lost broadcasts (as long as maxLen wasn't exceeded).
  • Sessions for connection state recovery are stored as Redis keys (sio:session: prefix).

Postgres adapter (if you already run Postgres and don't want Redis)

const { Pool } = require("pg");
const { createAdapter } = require("@socket.io/postgres-adapter");

const pool = new Pool({ connectionString: process.env.DATABASE_URL });
await pool.query(`
  CREATE TABLE IF NOT EXISTS socket_io_attachments (
    id bigserial UNIQUE,
    created_at timestamptz DEFAULT NOW(),
    payload bytea
  );
`);
const io = new Server({ adapter: createAdapter(pool) });

NOTIFY payloads are limited to 8000 bytes; bigger packets go through the attachments table. Fine for moderate traffic; Redis is usually faster for high volume.

Node.js cluster (multi-core on one machine)

const cluster = require("node:cluster");
const http = require("node:http");
const { availableParallelism } = require("node:os");
const { Server } = require("socket.io");
const { setupMaster, setupWorker } = require("@socket.io/sticky");
const { createAdapter, setupPrimary } = require("@socket.io/cluster-adapter");

if (cluster.isPrimary) {
  const httpServer = http.createServer();
  setupMaster(httpServer, { loadBalancingMethod: "least-connection" }); // sticky sessions
  setupPrimary();                                                         // adapter IPC hub
  httpServer.listen(3000);
  for (let i = 0; i < availableParallelism(); i++) cluster.fork();
  cluster.on("exit", (worker) => { console.log(`worker ${worker.process.pid} died`); cluster.fork(); });
} else {
  const httpServer = http.createServer();
  const io = new Server(httpServer);
  io.adapter(createAdapter());
  setupWorker(io);              // receives connections handed over by the primary
  io.on("connection", (socket) => { /* ... */ });
}

Why can't plain cluster work? Node's cluster module distributes TCP connections round-robin; HTTP polling requests of one session use different TCP connections and would land on different workers. @socket.io/sticky makes the primary inspect the sid and route to the right worker.

8.5 Emitting from outside Socket.IO servers: emitters

A background worker (e.g. processing payments) wants to notify users but is not a Socket.IO server (no HTTP server, no client connections). Use an emitter: it publishes into the adapter's channel; Socket.IO servers deliver.

const { Emitter } = require("@socket.io/redis-emitter");
const { createClient } = require("redis");

const redisClient = createClient({ url: process.env.REDIS_URL });
await redisClient.connect();
const emitter = new Emitter(redisClient /*, { key: "socket.io" } must match the adapter */);

emitter.to(`user:${userId}`).emit("payment:succeeded", { orderId });
emitter.of("/admin").emit("stats", stats);
emitter.except("banned").emit("announcement", text);
emitter.in("room1").socketsJoin("room2");
emitter.in(`user:${userId}`).disconnectSockets();
emitter.serverSideEmit("cache:invalidate", key);

Limitations: emitters can't receive acks and can't fetch sockets (no response channel). Equivalent packages exist for Postgres (@socket.io/postgres-emitter) and MongoDB (@socket.io/mongo-emitter). The emitter must match the adapter: the Redis emitter speaks the classic Redis pub/sub adapter's format, so check your adapter's docs for a compatible emitter before mixing them (e.g. with the Redis Streams adapter).

Architecture tip: many companies use a message broker (Kafka/RabbitMQ/SQS) for domain events, and a thin "realtime gateway" service (Socket.IO servers) that consumes those events and emits to rooms. The gateway owns connections; other services never talk to sockets directly.

8.6 Inter-server communication: serverSideEmit

// on any server
io.serverSideEmit("config:reload", { version: 42 });

// every OTHER server receives it (not the sender)
io.on("config:reload", ({ version }) => reloadConfig(version));

// with acknowledgement (v4.5+)
io.timeout(5000).serverSideEmit("ping", (err, responses) => {
  // responses: one per other server
});
const responses = await io.timeout(5000).serverSideEmitWithAck("health");

Use for cluster-wide cache invalidation, config reloads, coordination. Reserved names apply (connection, connect, new_namespace).

8.7 Load balancer and reverse proxy configuration

Lab-verified (October 2026): the nginx and HAProxy configurations in this section were run (nginx 1.24, HAProxy 2.8) in front of three Socket.IO 4.8.4 nodes sharing the Redis adapter. Results:

Setup Result
nginx config below (hash on client IP, Upgrade headers) connected, upgraded to WebSocket
HAProxy round-robin + cookie SERVERID insert, polling-only client with withCredentials: true connected every time, connections spread over nodes 5001/5002/5003
Same HAProxy, client without withCredentials failed every time (xhr poll error / xhr post error); nodes logged engine error code=1 Session ID unknown — the LB cookie wasn't sent back, so requests landed on random nodes
HAProxy round-robin without stickiness, polling-only failed: Session ID unknown (code 1)
HAProxy round-robin without stickiness, WebSocket-only client connected — no stickiness needed
Client on node 5001 broadcasts, client on node 5002 listening received via the Redis adapter

The full lab (configs, node script, test script) is in Chapter 23 so you can reproduce it.

nginx

http {
  map $http_upgrade $connection_upgrade {   # recommended: only set "upgrade" when needed
    default upgrade;
    ''      close;
  }

  upstream socket_nodes {
    # sticky sessions (pick one)
    hash $remote_addr consistent;   # whole client IP
    # ip_hash;                      # first 3 octets of IPv4 (coarser)
    # sticky cookie srv_id expires=1h; # nginx Plus only

    server app01:3000;
    server app02:3000;
    server app03:3000;
  }

  server {
    listen 443 ssl;
    server_name api.example.com;
    # ssl_certificate ... ssl_certificate_key ...

    location /socket.io/ {
      proxy_pass http://socket_nodes;

      proxy_http_version 1.1;                          # REQUIRED for WebSocket
      proxy_set_header Upgrade $http_upgrade;          # REQUIRED
      proxy_set_header Connection $connection_upgrade; # REQUIRED

      proxy_set_header Host $host;
      proxy_set_header X-Real-IP $remote_addr;
      proxy_set_header X-Forwarded-For $proxy_add_x_forwarded_for;
      proxy_set_header X-Forwarded-Proto $scheme;

      proxy_read_timeout 120s;   # must exceed pingInterval (25 s) comfortably; default is 60 s
      proxy_send_timeout 120s;
      proxy_buffering off;       # don't buffer long-polling responses
    }
  }
}

Missing proxy_http_version 1.1 or the Upgrade/Connection headers is the #1 reason "WebSocket doesn't work behind nginx" (clients silently stay on polling, or get websocket error). In DevTools you'll see polling requests every ~25 s and no transport=websocket 101 response.

If you serve Socket.IO under a sub-path (/api/socket.io/), set path on both client and server, or rewrite carefully.

HAProxy

frontend http_front
  bind *:443 ssl crt /etc/ssl/site.pem
  default_backend socket_nodes
  timeout client 120s

backend socket_nodes
  balance roundrobin
  cookie SERVERID insert indirect nocache   # cookie-based stickiness
  timeout server 120s
  timeout tunnel 1h                          # for long-lived WebSocket connections
  server app01 app01:3000 check cookie app01
  server app02 app02:3000 check cookie app02

(Or balance source for IP-based stickiness.)

Apache httpd

Needs mod_proxy, mod_proxy_http, mod_proxy_wstunnel (or ProxyPass … upgrade=websocket in 2.4.47+), and mod_proxy_balancer with stickysession for multiple nodes.

AWS

  • ALB (Application Load Balancer): supports WebSocket natively. Enable target group stickiness (load-balancer-generated cookie AWSALB, or application cookie). For cross-origin, set client withCredentials: true and server CORS credentials: true so the ALB cookie is sent on polling requests. Idle timeout default 60 s → keep pingInterval (25 s) below it (it is, by default) or raise it.
  • NLB (Network Load Balancer, layer 4): passes TCP through; stickiness by source IP; idle timeout for TCP is 350 s.
  • API Gateway WebSocket APIs are not Socket.IO servers (custom WS protocol with routes) — you can't point a Socket.IO client at them.
  • Elastic Beanstalk: enable WebSocket in its nginx proxy config.
  • ECS/EKS: same as below for Kubernetes.

Cloudflare / CDNs

  • WebSockets supported (proxy enabled). Cloudflare closes idle connections after ~100 s of inactivity → heartbeats (25 s) keep them alive.
  • Cloudflare load balancing has session affinity (cookie) options.
  • Long-polling requests through CDNs: ensure they're not cached (t= param and headers handle this).

Heroku / Render / Railway / Fly.io etc.

Most support WebSockets. Heroku's router supports session affinity as a feature flag (heroku features:enable http-session-affinity); its 55-second idle timeout is reset by heartbeat traffic.

8.8 Kubernetes

Typical setup: Deployment with N replicas → Service → Ingress (ingress-nginx) → clients.

Ingress-nginx sticky sessions + timeouts:

apiVersion: networking.k8s.io/v1
kind: Ingress
metadata:
  name: realtime
  annotations:
    nginx.ingress.kubernetes.io/affinity: "cookie"
    nginx.ingress.kubernetes.io/session-cookie-name: "route"
    nginx.ingress.kubernetes.io/session-cookie-max-age: "172800"
    nginx.ingress.kubernetes.io/proxy-read-timeout: "3600"
    nginx.ingress.kubernetes.io/proxy-send-timeout: "3600"
spec:
  ingressClassName: nginx
  rules:
    - host: rt.example.com
      http:
        paths:
          - path: /socket.io/
            pathType: Prefix
            backend:
              service: { name: realtime, port: { number: 3000 } }

(ingress-nginx handles the Upgrade headers for WebSocket automatically.)

Other notes:

  • Service.spec.sessionAffinity: ClientIP gives IP stickiness at the Service level, but behind an Ingress all traffic comes from the ingress pods — use Ingress-level affinity.
  • Graceful shutdown on rolling deploys: when a pod is terminated, every client on it disconnects and reconnects elsewhere. Spread this out:
  • terminationGracePeriodSeconds long enough,
  • a preStop hook (e.g. sleep 10) so the endpoint is removed from the LB first,
  • on SIGTERM: stop accepting new connections, optionally notify clients, then io.close() (Chapter 11),
  • roll pods slowly (maxSurge, maxUnavailable) to avoid a reconnection storm on the remaining pods.
  • Autoscaling: CPU-based HPA is often misleading for socket servers (idle connections use memory, not CPU). Scale on connection count / event-loop lag via custom metrics (Prometheus adapter). Note that scale-out doesn't move existing connections — new pods only get new connections. Scale-in disconnects clients.
  • Readiness probes: mark not-ready during shutdown so no new connections arrive.
  • Liveness probes: don't make them depend on Redis; a Redis outage shouldn't restart all pods.

8.9 Capacity planning & OS tuning checklist

  • ulimit -n (open files) ≥ expected connections × ~1.2 (e.g. 100 000+); systemd LimitNOFILE.
  • Kernel: net.core.somaxconn, net.ipv4.tcp_max_syn_backlog for connection bursts; net.ipv4.ip_local_port_range on proxies (each upstream connection uses an ephemeral port — a single nginx → single Node upstream is limited to ~28k–64k connections per destination IP:port; add more upstream IPs/ports or use more Node processes).
  • Memory: measure per-connection memory in a load test (heap snapshot); disable perMessageDeflate unless needed; keep socket.data small.
  • Node: --max-old-space-size appropriate to container memory.
  • Redis: monitor pub/sub throughput, client-output-buffer-limit pubsub (slow subscribers get disconnected when their output buffer exceeds limits → that node silently stops receiving broadcasts until reconnect!).

8.10 Scaling patterns and anti-patterns

Patterns

  • Stateless socket servers: keep durable state in DB/Redis; any node can serve any user after reconnection.
  • User rooms (user:<id>) instead of id maps → works across nodes.
  • Presence in Redis (sets/sorted sets with TTL heartbeats) rather than fetchSockets() on every request.
  • Partition by tenant/region for very large systems (separate clusters per region; route via DNS/geo).
  • Gateway pattern: Socket.IO servers only handle connections; business logic in services; communication via broker/emitter.
  • Backpressure: throttle/coalesce high-frequency updates (send state every 100 ms, not every change).

Anti-patterns

  • Storing userId → socket objects in a local Map and trying to emit from another node.
  • Calling io.fetchSockets() on hot paths.
  • Broadcasting huge payloads to huge rooms (send a "changed" notification + let clients fetch via HTTP/CDN instead).
  • One giant room everybody is in, with chatty events.
  • Forgetting sticky sessions → random 400 Session ID unknown and reconnect loops.
  • No error handler on Redis clients.

8.11 Chapter summary

  • Two problems at scale: sticky sessions (for polling) and cross-node broadcast (adapter).
  • Sticky sessions via IP hash, LB cookies, @socket.io/sticky; or skip them with WebSocket-only transports.
  • Adapters: Redis (classic, sharded), Redis Streams (resilient, CSR), Mongo, Postgres, cluster, cloud. Emitters let non-socket processes broadcast.
  • serverSideEmit for server-to-server events.
  • Proxy config: HTTP/1.1 + Upgrade/Connection headers + timeouts > ping interval.
  • Kubernetes: ingress cookie affinity, long timeouts, graceful shutdown, scale on connections.

Next: Chapter 9 — TypeScript, frameworks and clients

TypeScript, Frontend Frameworks, Mobile and Other Languages

Goal: use Socket.IO correctly in real stacks: fully typed events with TypeScript, React (hooks, context, StrictMode), Vue, Angular, Next.js, NestJS, React Native, native mobile, and servers/clients in other languages.

9.1 TypeScript — fully typed events

Since v4, both Server and client Socket accept generic event maps. Define them once in a shared package and import from both sides — this is the single most valuable practice for large teams (typos and payload mismatches become compile errors).

Shared event definitions

// packages/realtime-contracts/src/events.ts
export interface Message {
  id: string;
  conversationId: string;
  authorId: string;
  text: string;
  createdAt: string; // ISO (Dates become strings on the wire!)
}

export type Ack<T> = (res: { ok: true; data: T } | { ok: false; error: { code: string; message?: string } }) => void;

// events the SERVER emits to clients (client listens)
export interface ServerToClientEvents {
  "message:new": (msg: Message) => void;
  "user:typing": (p: { conversationId: string; userId: string }) => void;
  "presence:update": (p: { userId: string; online: boolean }) => void;
  "noArg": () => void;
  "withAck": (question: string, callback: (answer: number) => void) => void;
}

// events the CLIENT emits to the server (server listens)
export interface ClientToServerEvents {
  "message:send": (p: { conversationId: string; text: string; clientMsgId: string }, ack: Ack<{ id: string }>) => void;
  "conversation:join": (p: { conversationId: string }, ack: Ack<{ history: Message[] }>) => void;
  "typing": (p: { conversationId: string }) => void;
}

// events between Socket.IO servers (serverSideEmit)
export interface InterServerEvents {
  "cache:invalidate": (key: string) => void;
}

// type of socket.data
export interface SocketData {
  user: { id: string; name: string; roles: string[] };
}

Typed server

import { Server, Socket } from "socket.io";
import type { ClientToServerEvents, ServerToClientEvents, InterServerEvents, SocketData } from "@acme/realtime-contracts";

const io = new Server<ClientToServerEvents, ServerToClientEvents, InterServerEvents, SocketData>(httpServer, {
  cors: { origin: process.env.WEB_ORIGIN },
});

type AppSocket = Socket<ClientToServerEvents, ServerToClientEvents, InterServerEvents, SocketData>;

io.on("connection", (socket: AppSocket) => {
  socket.data.user.name;                                   // typed

  socket.on("message:send", async (payload, ack) => {      // payload & ack typed
    const msg = await messages.create(socket.data.user.id, payload);
    io.to(`conversation:${payload.conversationId}`).emit("message:new", msg); // typed
    ack({ ok: true, data: { id: msg.id } });
  });

  // @ts-expect-error — unknown event
  socket.emit("mesage:new", {});
});

io.serverSideEmit("cache:invalidate", "users:1");          // typed
io.on("cache:invalidate", (key) => cache.delete(key));     // typed

Typed client

import { io, Socket } from "socket.io-client";
import type { ClientToServerEvents, ServerToClientEvents } from "@acme/realtime-contracts";

// NOTE: generic order is REVERSED on the client: <ListenEvents, EmitEvents>
const socket: Socket<ServerToClientEvents, ClientToServerEvents> = io(URL);

socket.on("message:new", (msg) => render(msg));          // msg: Message
socket.emit("typing", { conversationId: "c1" });

const res = await socket.timeout(5000).emitWithAck("message:send", { conversationId: "c1", text: "hi", clientMsgId: crypto.randomUUID() });

Interview / bug note: on the server the order is Server<ListenEvents (client→server), EmitEvents (server→client), …>; on the client it's Socket<ListenEvents (server→client), EmitEvents (client→server)>. Both are "what I listen to, what I emit" from that side's perspective. Mixing them up gives confusing type errors.

Typing acks with timeout

When you use socket.timeout(ms).emit(..., cb), the type system automatically adds the err first parameter to the callback (DecorateAcknowledgements). With broadcast, io.timeout(ms).emit(...) types the callback as (err, responses[]).

Runtime validation is still needed

Types vanish at runtime. A malicious client can send anything. Pair the TS interfaces with runtime schemas (zod/valibot/typebox) — or derive TS types from schemas:

const SendSchema = z.object({ conversationId: z.string().uuid(), text: z.string().max(2000), clientMsgId: z.string().uuid() });
type SendPayload = z.infer<typeof SendSchema>;

9.2 React

The rules (each prevents a real, common bug)

  1. Create the socket once — at module level or in a context provider — not inside a component body (that creates a new connection per render).
  2. Register listeners in useEffect and remove them in the cleanup (socket.off(event, handler) with the same function reference) — otherwise listeners accumulate on every re-mount → duplicated messages and memory leaks.
  3. React 18+ StrictMode mounts/unmounts/re-mounts effects in development → effects run twice. Correct cleanup makes this harmless. Don't "fix" it by removing StrictMode.
  4. Avoid stale closures: handlers registered once capture old state — use functional setState(prev => …) or refs.
  5. Decide where connect/disconnect happens (e.g. connect after login, disconnect on logout) — autoConnect: false helps.

socket module

// src/socket.ts
import { io, Socket } from "socket.io-client";
import type { ServerToClientEvents, ClientToServerEvents } from "@acme/realtime-contracts";

export const socket: Socket<ServerToClientEvents, ClientToServerEvents> = io(import.meta.env.VITE_WS_URL, {
  autoConnect: false,                                   // connect explicitly after login
  auth: (cb) => cb({ token: localStorage.getItem("token") }), // fresh token on each (re)connect
  transports: ["websocket", "polling"],
});

Connection state hook

import { useEffect, useState } from "react";
import { socket } from "./socket";

export function useSocketStatus() {
  const [connected, setConnected] = useState(socket.connected);

  useEffect(() => {
    const onConnect = () => setConnected(true);
    const onDisconnect = () => setConnected(false);
    socket.on("connect", onConnect);
    socket.on("disconnect", onDisconnect);
    return () => {
      socket.off("connect", onConnect);       // same references → removes exactly these
      socket.off("disconnect", onDisconnect);
    };
  }, []);

  return connected;
}

Generic event hook

import { useEffect, useRef } from "react";
import { socket } from "./socket";

export function useSocketEvent<E extends keyof ServerToClientEvents>(event: E, handler: ServerToClientEvents[E]) {
  const handlerRef = useRef(handler);
  handlerRef.current = handler;                 // always call the latest handler (no stale closure)

  useEffect(() => {
    const listener = ((...args: any[]) => (handlerRef.current as any)(...args)) as any;
    socket.on(event, listener);
    return () => { socket.off(event, listener); };
  }, [event]);
}

// usage
function Chat({ conversationId }: { conversationId: string }) {
  const [messages, setMessages] = useState<Message[]>([]);

  useSocketEvent("message:new", (msg) => {
    if (msg.conversationId === conversationId) setMessages((prev) => [...prev, msg]); // functional update
  });

  useEffect(() => {
    let cancelled = false;
    socket.timeout(5000).emitWithAck("conversation:join", { conversationId })
      .then((res) => { if (!cancelled && res.ok) setMessages(res.data.history); })
      .catch(() => {/* timeout */});
    return () => { cancelled = true; /* optionally emit "conversation:leave" */ };
  }, [conversationId]);

  return <MessageList messages={messages} />;
}

Connect on login, disconnect on logout

useEffect(() => {
  if (user) socket.connect();
  return () => { socket.disconnect(); };
}, [user]);

Where to put socket data in state management

  • Small apps: component state + context.
  • Redux Toolkit: a middleware that owns the socket and dispatches actions on events (don't put the socket object in the store — it's not serializable).
  • React Query/TanStack Query: on events, queryClient.setQueryData or invalidateQueries → server state stays the source of truth.
  • Zustand: a store action subscribes once and updates state.

Next.js

  • Client components only ("use client") for socket.io-client — sockets don't exist during server rendering. Guard against SSR (useEffect runs only in the browser).
  • Hosting the server:
  • Use a custom server (server.js that creates the Next handler and a Socket.IO Server on the same http server), or
  • better for production: run Socket.IO as a separate service (its own deployment) and point the client at it (CORS).
  • Serverless/edge functions (Vercel functions, Lambda) can't host Socket.IO — they are short-lived and don't keep connections. Use a long-running Node host, or a managed service.
// server.js (custom Next.js server)
const { createServer } = require("node:http");
const next = require("next");
const { Server } = require("socket.io");

const app = next({ dev: process.env.NODE_ENV !== "production" });
const handler = app.getRequestHandler();

app.prepare().then(() => {
  const httpServer = createServer(handler);
  const io = new Server(httpServer);
  io.on("connection", (socket) => { /* ... */ });
  httpServer.listen(3000);
});

9.3 Vue 3

// socket.js
import { reactive } from "vue";
import { io } from "socket.io-client";

export const state = reactive({ connected: false, messages: [] });

export const socket = io(import.meta.env.VITE_WS_URL, { autoConnect: false });

socket.on("connect", () => { state.connected = true; });
socket.on("disconnect", () => { state.connected = false; });
socket.on("message:new", (m) => { state.messages.push(m); });   // registered ONCE at module level

In components using component-scoped listeners, register in onMounted and remove in onBeforeUnmount/onUnmounted with socket.off(event, handler). With Pinia, subscribe once in a store action (bindEvents()), guarded against double binding.

9.4 Angular

Wrap the socket in an injectable service, expose events as RxJS Observables, and let subscriptions handle cleanup:

@Injectable({ providedIn: "root" })
export class RealtimeService implements OnDestroy {
  private socket = io(environment.wsUrl, { autoConnect: false, auth: (cb) => cb({ token: this.auth.token }) });

  constructor(private auth: AuthService, private zone: NgZone) {}

  connect() { this.socket.connect(); }

  on<T>(event: string): Observable<T> {
    return new Observable<T>((subscriber) => {
      const handler = (data: T) => this.zone.run(() => subscriber.next(data)); // run inside Angular zone → change detection
      this.socket.on(event, handler);
      return () => this.socket.off(event, handler);                            // teardown on unsubscribe
    });
  }

  emitWithAck<T>(event: string, payload: unknown): Promise<T> {
    return this.socket.timeout(5000).emitWithAck(event, payload);
  }

  ngOnDestroy() { this.socket.disconnect(); }
}

(ngx-socket-io is a popular wrapper library.) With zoneless Angular / signals, update signals in the handler instead of zone.run.

9.5 NestJS (server)

// chat.gateway.ts
import {
  WebSocketGateway, WebSocketServer, SubscribeMessage, MessageBody, ConnectedSocket,
  OnGatewayInit, OnGatewayConnection, OnGatewayDisconnect,
} from "@nestjs/websockets";
import { Server, Socket } from "socket.io";

@WebSocketGateway({ namespace: "/chat", cors: { origin: process.env.WEB_ORIGIN } })
export class ChatGateway implements OnGatewayInit, OnGatewayConnection, OnGatewayDisconnect {
  @WebSocketServer() server: Server;  // the Namespace ("/chat") instance when a namespace is set

  afterInit(server: Server) { /* e.g. server.use(authMiddleware) */ }

  handleConnection(client: Socket) { /* ... */ }
  handleDisconnect(client: Socket) { /* ... */ }

  @SubscribeMessage("message:send")
  async onMessage(@MessageBody() dto: SendMessageDto, @ConnectedSocket() client: Socket) {
    const msg = await this.messages.create(client.data.user.id, dto);
    this.server.to(`conversation:${dto.conversationId}`).emit("message:new", msg);
    return { ok: true, data: { id: msg.id } };   // returned value becomes the ACK response
  }
}

NestJS specifics to know:

  • Packages: @nestjs/websockets + @nestjs/platform-socket.io.
  • Return value of a @SubscribeMessage handler = acknowledgement (if the client passed a callback). Returning { event, data } (a WsResponse) emits an event back instead.
  • Guards (@UseGuards(WsAuthGuard)), Pipes (ValidationPipe with class-validator DTOs), Interceptors and exception filters (WsException, BaseWsExceptionFilter) work on gateways. Note: guards run per message, not on connection — do connection auth in handleConnection (disconnect if invalid) or in a server.use() middleware in afterInit/a custom adapter.
  • Redis adapter in Nest: extend IoAdapter, override createIOServer to call server.adapter(createAdapter(pub, sub)), then app.useWebSocketAdapter(redisIoAdapter).
export class RedisIoAdapter extends IoAdapter {
  private adapterConstructor: ReturnType<typeof createAdapter>;
  async connectToRedis() {
    const pubClient = createClient({ url: process.env.REDIS_URL });
    const subClient = pubClient.duplicate();
    await Promise.all([pubClient.connect(), subClient.connect()]);
    this.adapterConstructor = createAdapter(pubClient, subClient);
  }
  createIOServer(port: number, options?: ServerOptions) {
    const server = super.createIOServer(port, options);
    server.adapter(this.adapterConstructor);
    return server;
  }
}
// main.ts
const redisIoAdapter = new RedisIoAdapter(app);
await redisIoAdapter.connectToRedis();
app.useWebSocketAdapter(redisIoAdapter);

9.6 React Native / Expo

import { io } from "socket.io-client";
const socket = io("https://api.example.com", { transports: ["websocket"] });
  • transports: ["websocket"] is commonly recommended in React Native: the polling transport relies on XHR implementations that sometimes behave differently, and WebSocket is reliable on mobile networks.
  • Use the device's real address (not localhost) when testing on a device/emulator (Android emulator → 10.0.2.2, or your LAN IP). Android blocks cleartext http:// by default (use HTTPS or allow cleartext for dev).
  • Handle app state: disconnect/pause when the app goes to background (AppState), and reconnect when it returns; use push notifications (FCM/APNs) for background delivery.

9.7 Native mobile clients

Platform Official client Notes
iOS / macOS Socket.IO-Client-Swift SocketManager(socketURL:config:), manager.defaultSocket; config options like .log(true), .compress, .forceWebsockets(true); check version compatibility (v16+ for Socket.IO v3/v4 servers)
Android / Java socket.io-client-java IO.socket(uri, options); v2.x of the Java client for Socket.IO v3/v4 servers; callbacks run on a background thread → post UI updates to the main thread
C++ socket.io-client-cpp v3.x for Socket.IO v3/v4
Dart / Flutter socket_io_client (community) Match its major version to your server version per its compatibility table
Python python-socketio Both client and server
.NET SocketIOClient (community)
Rust rust_socketio (client), socketioxide (server)
Go e.g. googollee/go-socket.io (server; historically v2-only for a long time — check protocol support), others Verify protocol v5 support before choosing

Always check each client's compatibility table with your server version — mismatched protocol versions are a top cause of "it connects in the browser but not on Android/iOS".

9.8 Server implementations in other languages

Language Library Notes
Python python-socketio (+ ASGI/WSGI integrations: aiohttp, FastAPI via ASGI app, Flask-SocketIO) Mature, supports Redis/Kafka/RabbitMQ managers for scaling
Java netty-socketio (historically v2 protocol; check forks for v4), Spring integrations Check protocol version
Go various (googollee/go-socket.io, zishang520/socket.io) Check protocol support
Rust socketioxide Tower/axum/hyper integration
Deno official port (deno.land/x/socket_io)
PHP community (workerman/phpsocket.io)
Elixir/.NET prefer native solutions (Phoenix Channels, SignalR)

Interop works as long as both sides implement the same protocol versions (Engine.IO v4 / Socket.IO v5 for modern clients).

9.9 Node.js-to-Node.js (server-side client)

Use socket.io-client in Node to connect services or to write integration tests/load tests:

const { io } = require("socket.io-client");
const socket = io("https://rt.example.com", {
  transports: ["websocket"],
  auth: { token: SERVICE_TOKEN },
  extraHeaders: { "x-service": "billing" }, // works in Node (not in browsers' WebSocket)
});

Note: a Node client keeps the process alive while connected; call socket.disconnect() to let it exit.

9.10 Chapter summary

  • Share typed event maps between client and server; remember the reversed generic order on the client; still validate at runtime.
  • React: one socket instance, listeners in useEffect with off cleanup, functional updates/refs to avoid stale closures, StrictMode-safe.
  • Vue/Angular: register once in a module/service; Angular → Observables + teardown.
  • Next.js: client components only; host Socket.IO on a long-running server, not serverless.
  • NestJS: gateways, return value = ack, guards per message, Redis via custom IoAdapter.
  • Mobile: prefer WebSocket transport; check client/server compatibility tables.

Next: Chapter 10 — Testing, debugging and monitoring

Testing, Debugging and Monitoring

Goal: be able to prove your real-time code works (unit, integration, end-to-end and load tests), find out why it doesn't (debug logs, DevTools, Admin UI), and know what's happening in production (metrics, logs, traces, alerts).

10.1 ELI5

  • Testing = practising the pizza phone call with a pretend customer before opening the shop.
  • Debugging = recording the phone calls to find out where the order got mixed up.
  • Monitoring = a big screen in the shop showing how many phones are ringing, how many calls dropped, and how long customers wait.

10.2 Unit testing handlers (no network)

Keep business logic in plain functions/services, and make handlers thin. Then unit-test handlers with fake sockets:

// handlers/chat.js
exports.makeSendMessage = ({ messages, io }) => async (socket, payload, ack) => {
  if (!payload?.text) return ack({ ok: false, error: { code: "VALIDATION" } });
  const msg = await messages.create(socket.data.user.id, payload);
  io.to(`conversation:${payload.conversationId}`).emit("message:new", msg);
  ack({ ok: true, data: { id: msg.id } });
};

// handlers/chat.test.js (jest/vitest style)
test("sends and broadcasts", async () => {
  const emit = vi.fn();
  const io = { to: vi.fn(() => ({ emit })) };
  const messages = { create: vi.fn(async () => ({ id: "m1" })) };
  const ack = vi.fn();
  const socket = { data: { user: { id: "u1" } } };

  await makeSendMessage({ messages, io })(socket, { conversationId: "c1", text: "hi" }, ack);

  expect(io.to).toHaveBeenCalledWith("conversation:c1");
  expect(emit).toHaveBeenCalledWith("message:new", { id: "m1" });
  expect(ack).toHaveBeenCalledWith({ ok: true, data: { id: "m1" } });
});

There are also community mocking libraries (e.g. socket.io-mock), but fake objects as above are usually enough.

10.3 Integration testing with a real server and real clients

Spin up a real server on a random port (listen(0)), connect real clients, assert on events. The test below was run and passes on socket.io 4.8.4 with Node's built-in test runner (node --test); the same structure works in Jest, Vitest, Mocha.

const { test, before, after } = require("node:test");
const assert = require("node:assert/strict");
const { createServer } = require("node:http");
const { Server } = require("socket.io");
const { io: ioc } = require("socket.io-client");

function waitFor(socket, event) {
  return new Promise((resolve) => socket.once(event, resolve));
}

let io, serverSocket, clientSocket, port;

before(async () => {
  const httpServer = createServer();
  io = new Server(httpServer);
  io.use((socket, next) => {
    if (socket.handshake.auth.token === "good") { socket.data.user = { id: "u1" }; return next(); }
    const err = new Error("unauthorized"); err.data = { code: "AUTH" }; next(err);
  });
  await new Promise((resolve) => httpServer.listen(0, resolve));   // random free port
  port = httpServer.address().port;
  io.on("connection", (socket) => {
    serverSocket = socket;
    socket.on("sum", (a, b, ack) => ack(a + b));
    socket.on("join", (room, ack) => { socket.join(room); ack(); });
  });
  clientSocket = ioc(`http://localhost:${port}`, { auth: { token: "good" }, transports: ["websocket"] });
  await waitFor(clientSocket, "connect");
});

after(() => { io.close(); clientSocket.disconnect(); });   // ALWAYS clean up, or the runner hangs

test("server -> client event", async () => {
  const p = waitFor(clientSocket, "hello");   // start listening BEFORE triggering
  serverSocket.emit("hello", "world");
  assert.equal(await p, "world");
});

test("client -> server with ack", async () => {
  const result = await clientSocket.timeout(1000).emitWithAck("sum", 2, 3);
  assert.equal(result, 5);
});

test("server -> client with ack", async () => {
  clientSocket.once("question", (cb) => cb(42));
  const answer = await serverSocket.timeout(1000).emitWithAck("question");
  assert.equal(answer, 42);
});

test("rejects bad token", async () => {
  const bad = ioc(`http://localhost:${port}`, { auth: { token: "bad" }, transports: ["websocket"] });
  const err = await waitFor(bad, "connect_error");
  assert.equal(err.message, "unauthorized");
  assert.deepEqual(err.data, { code: "AUTH" });
  assert.equal(bad.active, false);           // middleware rejection → no auto-reconnect
  bad.disconnect();
});

test("room broadcast excludes sender", async () => {
  const other = ioc(`http://localhost:${port}`, { auth: { token: "good" }, transports: ["websocket"] });
  await waitFor(other, "connect");
  await clientSocket.emitWithAck("join", "r1");
  await other.emitWithAck("join", "r1");
  let selfGot = false;
  clientSocket.once("news", () => (selfGot = true));
  const p = waitFor(other, "news");
  io.of("/").sockets.get(clientSocket.id).to("r1").emit("news", "x"); // the SENDER's server-side socket
  assert.equal(await p, "x");
  await new Promise((r) => setTimeout(r, 50));
  assert.equal(selfGot, false);
  other.disconnect();
});

A real lesson from writing this test: the first version used the global serverSocket for the room test — but serverSocket had been overwritten when the second client connected, so it pointed to the other client's socket, and socket.to("r1") excluded the very client that was supposed to receive the event. The test hung forever. Moral: in tests (and in production code!), never keep "the current socket" in a shared variable; look sockets up by id (io.of("/").sockets.get(id)) or keep per-connection state inside the connection closure.

Integration testing tips

  • transports: ["websocket"] in tests makes them faster and deterministic (no upgrade). Also test polling at least once if your users rely on it.
  • Register the listener before triggering the action (race conditions otherwise).
  • Use timeouts on acks in tests, so failures fail fast instead of hanging.
  • Always disconnect() clients and io.close() the server in after/afterAll; otherwise Jest says "did not exit one second after the test run" / runners hang.
  • Use forceNew: true when a test needs several independent clients to the same URL and namespace — or simply call ioc() again (a second io() to the same namespace creates a new Manager anyway — see Chapter 4).
  • Testing multi-node behaviour: start 2 servers in the test with the Redis adapter (Redis via Docker/Testcontainers), connect clients to different ports, assert that a broadcast on server A reaches a client on server B.
  • Testing reconnection: io.close() or serverSocket.disconnect(true) (note: a server-side disconnect() gives io server disconnect → no auto-reconnect; to simulate network loss use serverSocket.conn.close() → client sees transport close and reconnects).
  • Testing timeouts: use fake timers carefully — Socket.IO uses real timers internally for heartbeats; prefer small real timeouts in tests.

10.4 End-to-end tests (browser)

Use Playwright/Cypress against the real app:

  • Open two browser contexts (two users) and assert that a message sent in one appears in the other.
  • Use page.waitForEvent("websocket") (Playwright) to inspect frames, or assert on UI.
  • Simulate offline: context.setOffline(true) → assert "reconnecting…" UI → back online → assert recovery/sync.
// Playwright sketch
const alice = await browser.newContext(); const bob = await browser.newContext();
const pa = await alice.newPage(); const pb = await bob.newPage();
await Promise.all([pa.goto(url + "/chat/c1"), pb.goto(url + "/chat/c1")]);
await pa.fill("#input", "hello bob"); await pa.press("#input", "Enter");
await expect(pb.locator(".message").last()).toHaveText(/hello bob/);

10.5 Load testing

What to measure: max concurrent connections, connection rate (handshakes/s), message throughput (in and out, especially broadcast fan-out), latency percentiles (p50/p95/p99 of ack round-trip or emit→receive), memory per connection, CPU, event loop lag, Redis throughput, reconnection storm behaviour (kill a node and watch).

Tools:

  • Artillery — has a built-in Socket.IO engine (engine: socketio), scenario files with emit, acks, think times. Very common in interviews and companies.
config:
  target: "https://rt.staging.example.com"
  phases: [{ duration: 300, arrivalRate: 50 }]   # 50 new virtual users per second for 5 min
  engines: { socketio: {} }
  socketio: { transports: ["websocket"] }
scenarios:
  - engine: socketio
    flow:
      - emit: { channel: "conversation:join", data: { conversationId: "load-1" } }
      - loop:
          - emit: { channel: "message:send", data: { conversationId: "load-1", text: "hi" } }
          - think: 5
        count: 20
  • Custom Node scripts with socket.io-client (spawn N clients across several machines; a single Node process can simulate several thousand clients).
  • k6 speaks raw WebSocket; it doesn't implement the Socket.IO protocol natively (you'd have to hand-encode 40, 42[...], answer pings with 3) — prefer Artillery or custom scripts.

Load-testing pitfalls: the load generator becomes the bottleneck (CPU, ephemeral ports, file descriptors), so distribute it; test through the real load balancer; include realistic idle connections (most real connections are idle); test the polling path too if you allow it (much more expensive server-side).

10.6 Debugging

Debug logs (the debug module)

Socket.IO, Engine.IO and the client use the debug package — logs are off by default.

Server (Node):

DEBUG=socket.io* node server.js                 # Socket.IO layer (server, namespace, socket, parser, adapter)
DEBUG=engine,socket.io* node server.js          # + Engine.IO server
DEBUG=* node server.js                          # everything (very noisy)

Node client:

DEBUG=socket.io-client:*,engine.io-client:* node client.js

Browser client:

localStorage.debug = "socket.io-client:*";  // then reload the page
localStorage.debug = "*";                   // everything

Typical log lines: socket.io:socket emitting packet with ack id 1, engine:socket transport is now writable, engine upgrade completed.

Note: the minified browser bundle (socket.io.min.js) strips debug logs; use the non-minified build or an npm-bundled dev build to see them.

Browser DevTools

  1. Network tab, filter socket.io: - Handshake: GET …?EIO=4&transport=polling → response 0{"sid":…}. If it's HTML/404/CORS error → path/URL/proxy/CORS problem. - WebSocket: …transport=websocket → status 101. Click it → Messages tab shows every frame (2, 3, 40, 42[…]). - Repeated polling requests and no 101 → WebSocket upgrade failing (proxy headers). - 400 with {"code":1,"message":"Session ID unknown"} → sticky sessions missing.
  2. Console: CORS errors, WebSocket connection to … failed, mixed content (ws:// from an https:// page is blocked — use wss://).

Logging every event (temporary, dev/staging)

io.on("connection", (socket) => {
  socket.onAny((event, ...args) => logger.debug({ sid: socket.id, event, args }, "in"));
  socket.onAnyOutgoing((event, ...args) => logger.debug({ sid: socket.id, event }, "out"));
});

// client
socket.onAny((event, ...args) => console.debug("⬇", event, args));
socket.onAnyOutgoing((event, ...args) => console.debug("⬆", event, args));

Don't log full payloads in production (PII, volume).

Engine-level errors on the server

io.engine.on("connection_error", (err) => {
  logger.warn({ code: err.code, message: err.message, context: err.context, url: err.req?.url }, "engine connection error");
});

The error code tells you the cause (0 Transport unknown, 1 Session ID unknown → sticky sessions, 3 Bad request, 4 Forbidden → allowRequest, 5 protocol mismatch).

A systematic debugging procedure (use in incidents)

  1. Can the client reach the server? curl https://host/socket.io/?EIO=4&transport=polling → expect 0{…}.
  2. Is it CORS? browser console; check cors.origin exactly matches (scheme + host + port, no trailing slash).
  3. Is the path right? client path == server path; URL path is the namespace.
  4. Versions compatible? v2 client vs v4 server → allowEIO3 or upgrade.
  5. Does middleware reject? connect_error message/data; server logs.
  6. Does WebSocket upgrade? DevTools 101; proxy Upgrade headers.
  7. Multi-node? Session ID unknown → sticky sessions; events not reaching other node's clients → adapter misconfigured / different Redis key.
  8. Disconnect reason? log disconnect reasons on both sides (ping timeout → event-loop blocking or network; transport close every N seconds → proxy idle timeout).
  9. Turn on DEBUG on both sides and reproduce.

10.7 Admin UI (@socket.io/admin-ui)

A dashboard showing servers, connected sockets (with handshake details, rooms, data), rooms, events, and lets you join/leave/disconnect sockets.

const { instrument } = require("@socket.io/admin-ui");

const io = new Server(httpServer, {
  cors: { origin: ["https://admin.socket.io"], credentials: true },
});

instrument(io, {
  auth: {
    type: "basic",
    username: "admin",
    password: process.env.ADMIN_UI_BCRYPT_HASH,   // bcrypt hash ($2b$…; $2y$ not supported by bcrypt pkg)
  },
  mode: process.env.NODE_ENV === "production" ? "production" : "development", // production = less detail, less memory
  readonly: true,                                     // forbid join/leave/disconnect from the UI
  namespaceName: "/admin",                            // default; creates a namespace for the UI
  // serverId: `${os.hostname()}#${process.pid}`,     // distinct ids if several servers per machine
  // store: new RedisStore(redisClient),              // for basic auth across multiple servers
});

Then open https://admin.socket.io (a static site; it connects from your browser to your server) or self-host the UI files from the package's ui/dist folder. auth is mandatory (auth: false explicitly disables authentication — only in local dev). Works with v4 servers and in clusters (with an adapter). Security: it creates an /admin namespace on your server — protect it, consider disabling it in production, or expose it only on an internal network.

10.8 Monitoring in production

Key metrics (put these on a dashboard)

Metric How to get it Why
Connected clients (Engine.IO) io.engine.clientsCount Capacity; sudden drops = incident
Sockets per namespace io.of("/x").sockets.size (local) Feature usage
Connections/disconnections per second counters in connection/disconnect Reconnection storms
Disconnect reasons counter labelled by reason ping timeout spikes → event loop/network; transport close periodic → proxy timeouts
connect_error rate & messages client telemetry; server middleware rejections Auth problems, outages
Transport distribution socket.conn.transport.name, socket.conn.on("upgrade") % stuck on polling (proxy issues, cost)
Events in/out per second (by name) socket.onAny/onAnyOutgoing counters Traffic shape, abuse
Ack latency (p50/p95/p99) measure time from receive to ack, and client-side emit→ack User-perceived latency
Event loop lag perf_hooks.monitorEventLoopDelay() Root cause of ping timeouts and latency
Heap/RSS memory process.memoryUsage() Leaks (listeners, rooms, pending acks)
Redis adapter health Redis latency, pub/sub channels, client output buffers Cross-node delivery
Handler errors counter in your safe() wrapper Bugs

Prometheus example (prom-client)

const client = require("prom-client");

const connected = new client.Gauge({ name: "socketio_connected_clients", help: "Connected Engine.IO clients" });
const disconnects = new client.Counter({ name: "socketio_disconnects_total", help: "Disconnects", labelNames: ["reason"] });
const eventsIn = new client.Counter({ name: "socketio_events_in_total", help: "Incoming events", labelNames: ["event"] });
const handlerDuration = new client.Histogram({
  name: "socketio_handler_duration_seconds", help: "Handler duration", labelNames: ["event"],
  buckets: [0.005, 0.01, 0.05, 0.1, 0.25, 0.5, 1, 2.5],
});

setInterval(() => connected.set(io.engine.clientsCount), 5000).unref();

io.on("connection", (socket) => {
  socket.on("disconnect", (reason) => disconnects.inc({ reason }));
  socket.onAny((event) => eventsIn.inc({ event }));   // event names must be a FIXED set (label cardinality!)
});

app.get("/metrics", async (_req, res) => {
  res.set("Content-Type", client.register.contentType);
  res.end(await client.register.metrics());
});

Label cardinality: never use socket ids, user ids or dynamic event names as metric labels — it explodes your time-series database. This is another reason event names must be static.

Tracing

OpenTelemetry has a Socket.IO instrumentation (@opentelemetry/instrumentation-socket.io) that creates spans for emits and received events. Propagate a correlation id in payloads (or in the ack envelope) to connect HTTP requests, queue messages and socket events in logs.

Structured logging

Log with context on every line: socketId, userId, namespace, event, transport, ip (careful with PII), nodeId/hostname. Log connection lifecycle at info level, events at debug level.

Client-side telemetry

Send to your analytics/RUM: time to connect, connect_error messages, reconnect counts, time spent disconnected, transport used, ack latency. Many real outages (corporate proxy blocking WebSocket, a CDN misconfiguration) are only visible from the client side.

Alerts (examples)

  • Connected clients drop > 30% in 5 min.
  • ping timeout disconnect rate > baseline × 3.
  • Event loop lag p99 > 200 ms for 5 min.
  • Handler error rate > 1%.
  • Redis adapter errors / reconnects.
  • connect_error rate from clients > X%.

10.9 Chapter summary

  • Unit-test thin handlers with fakes; integration-test with a real server on port 0 and real clients; always clean up; register listeners before triggering.
  • E2E with two browser contexts; load test with Artillery/custom clients through the real LB.
  • Debug with DEBUG=…, localStorage.debug, DevTools Network/Messages, onAny, engine connection_error codes, and a systematic checklist.
  • Admin UI for live inspection (protect it).
  • Monitor connections, disconnect reasons, transports, event rates, ack latency, event loop lag, memory, Redis; keep metric labels low-cardinality.

Next: Chapter 11 — Performance and production

Performance Tuning and Running in Production

Goal: make Socket.IO fast and cheap, avoid memory leaks, deploy and shut down safely, evolve your event API without breaking old clients, and operate it like a senior engineer.

11.1 ELI5

A fast pizza shop: phones with good batteries (efficient WebSocket engine), short sentences (small payloads), one loudspeaker announcement instead of calling each customer (broadcast to rooms, encode once), not answering every tiny question immediately but grouping them (batching/throttling), no forgotten orders piling up in the corner (memory leaks), and a calm closing routine at night that tells customers "we're closing, call the other branch" (graceful shutdown).

11.2 Server-level performance tuning

1. Native add-ons for ws

npm install --save-optional bufferutil utf-8-validate
  • bufferutil speeds up masking/unmasking of WebSocket frames.
  • utf-8-validate speeds up UTF-8 validation of text frames (less important on recent Node versions that provide a fast built-in validator).
  • They're optional: ws falls back to JS implementations if they're missing.

2. Use uWebSockets.js (v4.4+)

uWebSockets.js is a C++ HTTP/WebSocket server with much lower memory per connection and higher throughput than Node's http + ws.

const { App } = require("uWebSockets.js");   // installed from GitHub, not npm registry
const { Server } = require("socket.io");

const app = App();
const io = new Server();

io.attachApp(app);

io.on("connection", (socket) => { /* ... */ });

app.listen(3000, (token) => {
  if (!token) console.warn("port already in use");
});

Caveats: different HTTP API (Express middleware doesn't plug in directly), native dependency to build/ship, fewer people know it. Use when connection density/cost matters.

3. Alternative wsEngine

The wsEngine option lets you replace ws with a compatible implementation (e.g. a native one). Verify maintenance status before adopting any alternative engine.

4. Discard the initial HTTP request object

Each Engine.IO connection keeps a reference to its first HTTP request (socket.request), which holds headers, the parsed URL, etc. — memory per connection. If you don't need it after the handshake:

io.engine.on("connection", (rawSocket) => {
  rawSocket.request = null;
});

(Verified on 4.8.4: socket.handshake.headers is still available in your connection handler; only socket.request becomes null. Don't do this if you use socket.request.session/socket.request.user from io.engine.use() middleware.)

5. Compression trade-offs

  • perMessageDeflate (WebSocket) is off by default. Turning it on reduces bandwidth for large compressible text but costs CPU and significant memory per connection. If you enable it, tune:
new Server(httpServer, {
  perMessageDeflate: {
    threshold: 2048,                 // only compress messages larger than 2 KB
    zlibDeflateOptions: { level: 3 }, // cheaper compression
    serverNoContextTakeover: true,   // don't keep compression context between messages → less memory
    clientNoContextTakeover: true,
  },
});
  • httpCompression (polling) is on by default with a 1 KB threshold — fine.
  • Use socket.compress(false) for already-compressed binary.

6. Parser choice

The default JSON parser is fast in V8. socket.io-msgpack-parser can reduce size for numeric/binary-heavy payloads. Measure with your real messages; switch only with evidence (and switch both client and server).

7. Heartbeat settings

Shorter pingInterval = more packets (tiny) and faster detection; longer = fewer wakeups. Defaults are reasonable; with 1M connections, 25 s interval = 40k pings/s per cluster — still fine. Don't go below a few seconds.

8. Node process settings

  • Run one process per CPU core (cluster/PM2/containers) with sticky sessions + adapter.
  • Set container memory limits and --max-old-space-size consistently.
  • Keep the event loop free: CPU-heavy work (image processing, big JSON, crypto, PDF) → worker threads or other services. A blocked loop delays every socket and causes ping timeout storms.

11.3 Application-level performance

Encode once: broadcast instead of looping

// ❌ serializes N times, N writes through the full emit path
for (const s of await io.in("room").fetchSockets()) s.emit("update", bigPayload);

// ✅ encodes once, adapter fans out (and works across nodes)
io.to("room").emit("update", bigPayload);

Send less data

  • Send diffs/patches, not entire objects ({ id, field: newValue }).
  • Short property names only if traffic is huge (readability matters more usually).
  • Don't broadcast large payloads to large rooms: broadcast a small notification ("doc 42 changed, version 17") and let clients fetch details via HTTP (cacheable, CDN).
  • Strip server-only fields; use DTO projections.

Throttle, debounce, batch, coalesce

// client: throttle cursor updates to 20/s and use volatile
let last = 0;
document.addEventListener("mousemove", (e) => {
  const now = performance.now();
  if (now - last < 50) return;
  last = now;
  socket.volatile.emit("cursor", { x: e.clientX, y: e.clientY });
});

// server: coalesce many updates into one tick per room every 100 ms
const pending = new Map(); // room -> latest state
function queueUpdate(room, state) { pending.set(room, state); }
setInterval(() => {
  for (const [room, state] of pending) io.to(room).volatile.emit("state", state);
  pending.clear();
}, 100);

Rooms over filtering

Let the adapter select recipients (rooms) instead of iterating sockets and checking conditions. Precompute rooms for common audiences (org:1:admins).

Avoid expensive per-event work

  • Cache permission checks (e.g. "is user in conversation" cached in socket.data or Redis with TTL; invalidate on membership change).
  • Avoid DB queries on very high-frequency events (typing, cursor).
  • Batch DB writes for high-volume streams.

Backpressure / slow clients

A client on a bad network can't keep up; the server buffers outgoing packets for it (socket.conn.writeBuffer). For high-throughput streams:

  • prefer volatile (drop when not writable),
  • send snapshots instead of every delta,
  • disconnect consumers whose buffers grow beyond a limit:
setInterval(() => {
  for (const [, s] of io.of("/").sockets) {
    if (s.conn.writeBuffer.length > 1000) s.disconnect(true);
  }
}, 10_000).unref();

11.4 Memory leaks — the usual suspects

Leak Example Fix
Listeners on global emitters per connection bus.on("price", (p) => socket.emit("price", p)) in connection handler, never removed Remove on disconnect, or better: use rooms (io.to("prices").emit)
Timers per socket setInterval(() => socket.emit(...), 1000) clearInterval on disconnect
Maps of sockets/users never cleaned users.set(socket.id, …) without delete Delete on disconnect (and prefer rooms/Redis)
Pending acks without timeout socket.emit("x", cb) to unresponsive clients socket.timeout(ms).emit(…)
Client-side listener accumulation socket.on inside React effect without off / inside connect handler Cleanup / register once
Huge socket.data storing user profile + permissions blob Keep ids only
Connection state recovery buffer high broadcast volume × 2-minute window Lower maxDisconnectionDuration, use Streams adapter with maxLen
Dynamic namespaces never removed tenants created on the fly cleanupEmptyChildNamespaces: true
perMessageDeflate zlib contexts per connection Disable or tune
Retained socket.request per-connection request objects rawSocket.request = null (if unused) 
// ✅ correct per-socket resource management
io.on("connection", (socket) => {
  const onPrice = (p) => socket.volatile.emit("price", p);
  priceBus.on("price", onPrice);
  const timer = setInterval(() => socket.emit("tick"), 5000);

  socket.on("disconnect", () => {
    priceBus.off("price", onPrice);
    clearInterval(timer);
  });
});

Diagnose with heap snapshots (Chrome DevTools via node --inspect, or heapdump), comparing two snapshots after many connect/disconnect cycles; look for retained Socket objects. Node's MaxListenersExceededWarning on a global emitter is a strong leak hint.

11.5 Graceful shutdown and deployments

When a server restarts, all its clients disconnect and reconnect (to other nodes, with back-off). Make it smooth:

let shuttingDown = false;

app.get("/health/ready", (_req, res) => res.status(shuttingDown ? 503 : 200).end());

async function shutdown(signal) {
  if (shuttingDown) return;
  shuttingDown = true;
  logger.info({ signal }, "shutting down");

  // 1) give the load balancer time to stop sending NEW connections (readiness probe fails)
  await new Promise((r) => setTimeout(r, 10_000));

  // 2) optionally tell clients so they can show a notice / reconnect elsewhere with jitter
  io.emit("server:restarting");

  // 3) close Socket.IO: disconnects all sockets ("server shutting down") and closes the HTTP server
  await io.close();

  // 4) close other resources
  await Promise.allSettled([pubClient.quit(), subClient.quit(), db.end()]);
  process.exit(0);
}

process.on("SIGTERM", () => shutdown("SIGTERM"));
process.on("SIGINT", () => shutdown("SIGINT"));

Notes:

  • io.close() closes all client connections and the underlying HTTP server (returns a Promise in v4).
  • Clients see transport close (or the reason relayed) and reconnect automatically with jitter. With connection state recovery + a shared store (Redis Streams adapter), server shutting down is a recoverable reason, so sessions can resume on another node.
  • Rolling deploys: replace pods/instances gradually; each step moves ~1/N of the connections; watch connection counts before continuing.
  • Blue/green with long-lived connections: old (blue) servers keep their connections until you drain them — plan a deliberate drain step.
  • Spread reconnection storms: if you disconnect many clients at once, consider disconnecting in batches over a few seconds.

11.6 Evolving your event API (versioning & backward compatibility)

Mobile apps and long-open browser tabs run old client versions for weeks. Rules:

  1. Additive changes only (new optional fields, new events) are safe.
  2. Never rename/remove an event or required field without a deprecation period.
  3. Introduce breaking changes as new events (order:updated:v2) or with a v field; emit both old and new during migration.
  4. Send the client version at connection (auth: { token, clientVersion }); reject unsupported versions with a clear error:
io.use((socket, next) => {
  if (semver.lt(socket.handshake.auth.clientVersion ?? "0.0.0", MIN_CLIENT)) {
    const err = new Error("UPGRADE_REQUIRED");
    err.data = { minVersion: MIN_CLIENT };
    return next(err);
  }
  next();
});
  1. Keep the event contracts in a shared, versioned package (TS types + runtime schemas) and/or an AsyncAPI specification (the OpenAPI equivalent for event-driven APIs) — generate docs from it.
  2. Upgrading Socket.IO itself: v3 → v4 is non-breaking for clients; v2 → v4 requires allowEIO3: true during the migration window, then migrating clients.
  3. When you deploy a new web client, old tabs keep the old JS: either keep backward compatibility, or emit app:new-version to prompt a reload.

11.7 Configuration per environment

const io = new Server(httpServer, {
  path: process.env.SIO_PATH ?? "/socket.io/",
  cors: { origin: process.env.WEB_ORIGINS.split(","), credentials: true },
  serveClient: false,
  pingInterval: Number(process.env.SIO_PING_INTERVAL ?? 25000),
  pingTimeout: Number(process.env.SIO_PING_TIMEOUT ?? 20000),
  maxHttpBufferSize: Number(process.env.SIO_MAX_BUFFER ?? 1e6),
  transports: (process.env.SIO_TRANSPORTS ?? "polling,websocket").split(","),
  connectionStateRecovery: process.env.SIO_CSR === "1" ? { maxDisconnectionDuration: 60_000 } : undefined,
  adapter: await buildAdapter(),  // in-memory locally, Redis in staging/prod
});

Keep dev/staging/prod as similar as possible (same adapter type, same proxy) — many production-only bugs come from "it works on localhost" without a proxy or a second node.

11.8 Cost optimisation

  • Idle connections cost memory: scale servers on memory/connection count.
  • Prefer WebSocket-only for clients that can (polling costs many HTTP requests).
  • Reduce message volume (throttle, coalesce, volatile, diffs).
  • Consider uWebSockets.js for high connection density.
  • Disconnect idle users after long inactivity (e.g. background tabs for > 30 min) and reconnect on visibility change — can cut concurrent connections dramatically.
  • Managed alternatives (Azure Web PubSub for Socket.IO, Ably, Pusher) trade money for ops effort — evaluate total cost.

11.9 Production readiness checklist

Correctness - [ ] Handlers wrapped (sync + async errors), always ack commands. - [ ] Inputs validated, actions authorized, idempotency for retried commands. - [ ] State derived from DB/user on (re)connection; client re-syncs on connect. - [ ] Client listeners registered once, cleaned up in UI frameworks.

Scalability - [ ] Adapter configured (Redis/Streams/…) with error handlers and unique key. - [ ] Sticky sessions or WebSocket-only transport. - [ ] Proxy: HTTP/1.1 + Upgrade headers + timeouts > ping interval. - [ ] No local userId→socket maps; user rooms instead. - [ ] Load tested at 2–3× expected peak, including reconnection storms.

Reliability - [ ] Graceful shutdown; readiness probe; rolling deploy strategy. - [ ] Reconnection back-off sane; jitter on. - [ ] Missed-message strategy (CSR and/or offset sync).

Security — see Chapter 6 checklist.

Observability - [ ] Metrics: connections, disconnect reasons, transports, event rates, ack latency, event loop lag, memory, errors. - [ ] Structured logs with socket/user ids; DEBUG toggle available. - [ ] Alerts and runbooks (Chapter 12).

11.10 Chapter summary

  • Speed: bufferutil, uWebSockets.js via attachApp, discard request, careful compression, measured parser choice, keep event loop free.
  • App-level: broadcast via rooms (encode once), smaller payloads, throttle/coalesce, volatile, backpressure handling.
  • Leaks: global listeners, timers, maps, pending acks, client listener accumulation.
  • Graceful shutdown with readiness, io.close(), staged drains; rolling deploys.
  • Version your event API; clients lag behind.

Next: Chapter 12 — Troubleshooting

Troubleshooting: Common Bugs, Symptoms, Causes and Fixes

Goal: a field manual. Each entry: Symptom → Likely causes → How to confirm → Fix. Read it once fully; come back to it during incidents. Entries are grouped by area.

12.0 First-aid kit (do these first, every time)

  1. Log the reasons. Server: disconnect reason + io.engine.on("connection_error") (code/message). Client: connect_error (err.message, err.data, socket.active) and disconnect reason.
  2. Open DevTools → Network → filter socket.io. Look at the handshake response, the WebSocket (101?) and its Messages tab.
  3. curl the handshake: curl -i "https://host/socket.io/?EIO=4&transport=polling" → must return 0{"sid":…}.
  4. Enable debug logs: DEBUG=engine,socket.io* (server), localStorage.debug = "socket.io-client:*" (browser).
  5. Check versions of client and server (npm ls socket.io socket.io-client).
  6. Reproduce without the proxy (directly against Node) to split app bugs from infrastructure bugs.

A. Connection problems

A1. Client can't connect at all — xhr poll error / websocket error in connect_error

  • Causes: wrong URL/port; server not running; server attached to a different HTTP server (app.listen bug); firewall; HTTPS page connecting to http:///ws:// (mixed content blocked); certificate errors.
  • Confirm: curl the handshake; check the browser console for mixed-content/TLS errors.
  • Fix: httpServer.listen(...) (the one passed to new Server); use https://; correct URL; valid certificate.

A2. 404 on /socket.io/?EIO=4…

  • Causes: app.listen() instead of httpServer.listen(); client path ≠ server path; proxy not forwarding /socket.io/; serving behind a sub-path (/api) without adjusting path; Next.js/Vite dev server receiving the request instead of the Socket.IO server.
  • Fix: listen on the correct server; align path on both sides (e.g. path: "/api/socket.io/"); proxy rule for the path; in Vite dev, configure server.proxy with ws: true for /socket.io.

A3. CORS error in the browser

Access to XMLHttpRequest at '…/socket.io/?EIO=4&transport=polling…' from origin '…' has been blocked by CORS policy
  • Causes: no cors option (v3+ default is no CORS); origin mismatch (http vs https, port, trailing slash https://app.com/ ≠ https://app.com); credentials: true with origin: "*"; client withCredentials: true without server credentials: true.
  • Fix: cors: { origin: ["https://app.example.com"], credentials: true } exactly matching; or serve frontend and API on the same origin. Note: a CORS error can also be a masked 4xx/5xx from a proxy that doesn't add CORS headers on errors — check the actual status.

A4. connect_error: "Invalid namespace"

  • Cause: the client connects to a namespace the server doesn't define — often by accident: io("https://api.example.com/api") thinking /api is a path.
  • Fix: define io.of("/api"), or use io("https://api.example.com", { path: "/api/socket.io/" }).

A5. connect_error with your middleware's message (e.g. "unauthorized")

  • Cause: io.use called next(err).
  • Behaviour: socket.active === false → no automatic retry.
  • Fix: refresh credentials then socket.connect(); show login. Check token presence/format (auth vs query), clock skew for JWT exp/nbf, algorithm/secret mismatch between services.

A6. Client stays "connecting" forever, no error, then reconnects every ~45 s

  • Cause: a connection middleware never calls next() in some code path (e.g. an await that never resolves, a missing return next() after a condition, an exception in async middleware that's swallowed).
  • Confirm: server debug logs show the handshake but no connection; the raw connection is closed after connectTimeout (45 s).
  • Fix: ensure every path calls next()/next(err); wrap async middleware in try/catch.

A7. connect_error: "timeout"

  • Cause: the connection attempt exceeded the client timeout (20 s): server overloaded (event loop blocked), network very slow, proxy buffering.
  • Fix: investigate server load/event loop lag; proxy buffering off; increase timeout only if the network is genuinely slow.

A8. {"code":5,"message":"Unsupported protocol version"} / client says "v2.x server with a v3.x client"

  • Cause: version mismatch. v2 client → v3/v4 server needs allowEIO3: true. v3/v4 client → v2 server is impossible (upgrade the server).
  • Fix: align versions; use allowEIO3 temporarily during migrations. Third-party clients (Swift/Java/Flutter/Python/Go): check their compatibility table.

A9. {"code":0,"message":"Transport unknown"}

  • Cause: client requests a transport disabled on the server (e.g. server transports: ["websocket"] but client starts with polling).
  • Fix: align transports on both sides.

A10. Works locally, fails in production

  • Usual suspects: proxy WebSocket headers; sticky sessions; CORS with the real domain; TLS; path behind a prefix; CDN; corporate proxy at the client; different env vars.
  • Fix: go through the first-aid kit against production; compare proxy configs.

A11. Works in the browser, fails from a mobile app

  • Causes: client library version incompatible with server protocol; cleartext HTTP blocked on Android/iOS (ATS); localhost used on a device; self-signed certs; polling issues on React Native.
  • Fix: compatible client version; HTTPS; real host/IP; transports: ["websocket"] on RN.

B. Transport / upgrade / proxy problems

B1. Never upgrades to WebSocket (stays on polling)

  • Symptoms: DevTools shows a polling request every ~25 s and no transport=websocket 101; higher latency; socket.io.engine.transport.name === "polling".
  • Causes: proxy missing proxy_http_version 1.1 + Upgrade/Connection headers; load balancer without WebSocket support; corporate proxy/antivirus; upgrade: false or transports: ["polling"] somewhere; server allowUpgrades: false.
  • Fix: proxy configuration (Chapter 8); verify with wscat/websocat directly.

B2. Random 400 Bad Request with {"code":1,"message":"Session ID unknown"}

  • Cause: missing sticky sessions with multiple nodes (polling requests reaching a node that didn't create the session), or the node restarted.
  • Fix: enable sticky sessions (cookie/IP hash, @socket.io/sticky for cluster/PM2), or transports: ["websocket"]. With ALB cookies cross-origin → withCredentials: true.

B3. Clients disconnect every 60 s (or another fixed interval) with transport close

  • Cause: a proxy/LB idle timeout or proxy_read_timeout lower than the traffic interval, or a misconfigured heartbeat (pingInterval raised above the LB timeout).
  • Fix: proxy timeouts > pingInterval (e.g. 120 s+); keep defaults for heartbeats.

B4. WebSocket works but some users can't connect at all

  • Cause: you forced transports: ["websocket"] and those users are behind WebSocket-blocking networks.
  • Fix: allow polling fallback (["websocket", "polling"] + fallback on connect_error, or tryAllTransports: true in 4.8+), with sticky sessions.

B5. Mixed content: ws:// blocked on https:// pages

  • Fix: use https:// URL in io() (the client derives wss://), terminate TLS at the proxy.

B6. WebSocket is closed before the connection is established

  • Causes: the socket disconnected during connection (component unmounted and socket.disconnect() called — e.g. React StrictMode double effect), proxy rejected the upgrade, server crashed. Mostly harmless in dev StrictMode.
  • Fix: don't create/destroy the socket in effects that StrictMode double-invokes, or accept the dev-only warning; check proxy upgrade config.

C. Events not received / received wrongly

C1. Events are received 2×, 3×, N× (duplicates growing over time)

  • Causes (by frequency): 1. Listener registered inside socket.on("connect") → one extra listener per reconnection. 2. React/Vue component registering socket.on without socket.off cleanup (or with a different function reference) → one extra per mount/re-render. 3. Multiple io() calls (each creates a new connection) → each connection gets the event. 4. Server registers handlers in a loop or per-request (e.g. io.on("connection") inside an Express route). 5. Client-side retries (retries option) without server idempotency.
  • Confirm: socket.listeners("event").length; count connections in DevTools.
  • Fix: register once / cleanup with the same reference; single socket instance; dedupe by message id.

C2. Client never receives an event the server "sent"

  • Event name typo / case mismatch ("chatMessage" vs "chat:message"). → shared typed event map.
  • Emitting to the wrong namespace (io.emit = main namespace; the client is on /chat).
  • Emitting to a room the socket isn't in (join failed/authorization; or socket reconnected → new socket not re-joined).
  • Using socket.to(room) when the sender itself should receive it (socket.to excludes the sender).
  • Emitting before the client registered its listener (e.g. emitted in connection handler, but the client attaches socket.on("welcome") asynchronously later) → register listeners before connecting (autoConnect: false then connect()), or have the client request the data (emitWithAck("init")).
  • Multi-node without adapter: the target client is on another server.
  • Different Redis key prefix between nodes / emitter.
  • The client was disconnected at that moment (at-most-once) → sync strategy.
  • volatile packet dropped.
  • Payload > maxHttpBufferSize → the connection was closed instead.

C3. Server never receives a client event

  • Handlers registered after an await in an async connection handler. The client emits right after connect; the packet arrives while the server is still awaiting (DB call), finds no listener and is silently dropped (verified on 4.8.4):
// ❌ events sent during the await are lost
io.on("connection", async (socket) => {
  const rooms = await db.loadRooms(socket.data.user.id);
  socket.on("hello", handler);
});
// ✅ register synchronously first, then do async work (or do it in io.use middleware)
io.on("connection", (socket) => {
  socket.on("hello", handler);
  db.loadRooms(socket.data.user.id).then((rooms) => socket.join(rooms));
});
  • Handler registered on the wrong namespace's connection handler.
  • Client emitting before connection and volatile (dropped) — or emits buffered but page reloaded before reconnecting.
  • A socket.use() middleware not calling next(), or calling next(err) (check the server socket's error event).
  • Rate limiter dropping silently.
  • Event name is reserved → emit threw on the client (check console).

C4. The ack callback never fires

  • Receiver handler doesn't call the callback in some path (error path!), or the handler threw before calling it.
  • Receiver expected the callback at a different argument position (e.g. client sent emit("x", cb) but server handler is (data, cb) → data is the callback, cb undefined → cb is not a function).
  • Middleware dropped the event.
  • Connection lost in between.
  • Fix: always use timeout()/ackTimeout on the sender; validate typeof ack === "function" on the receiver; wrap handlers so errors still ack.

C5. Ack callback's first argument is an Error/null unexpectedly

  • Cause: you used .timeout() — callback signature becomes (err, ...responses).
  • Fix: (err, response) => {} with timeout; (response) => {} without.

C6. Broadcast ack returns immediately with "operation has timed out"

  • Cause: io.emit("x", cb) / io.to(room).emit("x", cb) without .timeout().
  • Fix: io.timeout(5000).emit("x", (err, responses) => …).

C7. Received data looks wrong (Date is a string, Map is {}, BigInt error)

  • Cause: JSON serialization rules (Chapter 3).
  • Fix: convert explicitly; send ISO strings/epoch numbers; arrays for Map/Set; strings for BigInt.

C8. Events arrive in the "wrong order" in processing

  • Cause: async handlers completing out of order; or events from different sockets/ nodes (no global ordering).
  • Fix: per-key queue; sequence numbers; server-assigned versions.

C9. "Cannot emit reserved event" error

  • Cause: socket.emit("connect" | "disconnect" | "connect_error" | "disconnecting" | "newListener" | "removeListener").
  • Fix: rename your event.

D. Disconnections and reconnection

D1. Mass ping timeout disconnections

  • Causes: event loop blocked on the server (sync CPU work, huge JSON, GC pauses from memory pressure); overloaded Node; network congestion; client devices sleeping (normal in small amounts).
  • Confirm: event loop lag metric spikes at the same time; CPU 100%.
  • Fix: move CPU work off the loop; scale out; reduce message volume; fix memory pressure.

D2. Client doesn't reconnect after disconnect

  • Cause: reason io server disconnect (server called disconnect()), io client disconnect, or middleware rejection — auto-reconnect is intentionally disabled.
  • Fix: handle those cases explicitly (socket.connect() after fixing auth).

D3. socket.on("reconnect") never fires

  • Cause: v3+ moved it to the Manager.
  • Fix: socket.io.on("reconnect", …), or simply use socket.on("connect") (fires on every successful connection, including reconnections).

D4. After reconnection, the user no longer receives room messages

  • Cause: a reconnection creates a new server-side socket with no rooms; you joined rooms only on explicit client actions.
  • Fix: re-join rooms in connection based on the user (DB), or have the client re-subscribe on every connect; or enable connection state recovery (and still handle recovered === false).

D5. Reconnection storm after a deploy/outage (servers overloaded, crash loop)

  • Cause: all clients reconnect simultaneously; expensive handshake (auth DB queries, loading rooms).
  • Fix: keep jitter; increase reconnectionDelayMax for large fleets; make connection setup cheap (cache, JWT instead of DB lookups); rate-limit handshakes at the edge; scale ahead of deploys; roll deploys slowly; CSR with skipMiddlewares.

D6. socket.id changed / server lost my identity

  • Cause: ids change on reconnection (expected).
  • Fix: use authenticated user ids and user rooms.

D7. A flood of old events hits the server when a client comes back online

  • Cause: client sendBuffer accumulated emits while offline.
  • Fix: volatile for droppable events; if (socket.connected) guards; clear socket.sendBuffer on reconnect when stale.
