D8. Presence shows users "online" long after they left (or flickers)

  • Causes: dead connections only detected after pingInterval + pingTimeout; presence tracked per socket instead of per user (multiple tabs); node crash left stale Redis entries; immediate "offline" on brief reconnections → flicker.
  • Fix: count sockets per user (online if ≥ 1); TTL-based heartbeats in Redis; grace period (e.g. 5–10 s) before broadcasting "offline" (Chapter 13).

E. Scaling / multi-node problems

E1. Broadcasts reach only some users

  • Cause: no adapter (in-memory per node), adapter not applied to the namespace in use, Redis connection broken, different key prefixes, emitter/adapter mismatch.
  • Confirm: connect two clients to two different nodes and test; check Redis PUBSUB CHANNELS socket.io*.
  • Fix: configure the adapter on every node; same key; Redis error handlers.

E2. fetchSockets() is slow or times out

  • Cause: one node slow/dead → waits requestsTimeout (5 s); huge numbers of sockets.
  • Fix: don't use on hot paths; maintain presence/counters in Redis; tune timeout.

E3. Redis adapter suddenly stops delivering cross-node messages

  • Causes: subscriber disconnected (and not resubscribed), Redis client-output-buffer-limit pubsub exceeded → Redis kills slow subscribers; network partition.
  • Fix: monitor/alert on Redis client reconnects; raise limits carefully; consider the Redis Streams adapter (resumes from offset).

E4. Process crashes with an unhandled 'error' event from Redis

  • Fix: pubClient.on("error", …) and subClient.on("error", …).

E5. Uneven load across nodes

  • Cause: IP-hash stickiness behind NAT (corporate networks); long-lived connections stay where they started after scale-out.
  • Fix: cookie-based stickiness; least-connection balancing for new connections; periodic gentle rebalancing (disconnect a fraction of clients on overloaded nodes).

F. Performance and memory

F1. Memory grows steadily until OOM

  • Causes: see Chapter 11 leak table (global listeners, timers, maps, pending acks, perMessageDeflate, CSR buffers, dynamic namespaces).
  • Confirm: heap snapshots; MaxListenersExceededWarning; memory vs connection count correlation (if memory grows while connections are flat → leak).
  • Fix: clean up on disconnect; timeouts on acks; disable/tune compression.

F2. High CPU with few messages

  • Causes: heavy compression, huge payloads JSON-encoded often, many polling clients (HTTP overhead), TLS in Node, logging every event at info level, fetchSockets loops.
  • Fix: profile (node --cpu-prof, clinic.js flame graphs) and fix hot spots.

F3. Latency spikes

  • Causes: event loop blocking, GC, Redis latency, big broadcasts, Nagle/proxy buffering.
  • Fix: measure event loop lag; split work; proxy_buffering off; smaller messages.

F4. Server closes the connection when sending a file/large message

  • Cause: payload > maxHttpBufferSize (1 MB default).
  • Fix: increase deliberately, chunk with acks, or use HTTP uploads.

G. Framework-specific

G1. React: multiple connections in DevTools

  • Cause: io() inside a component body or in an effect without cleanup; StrictMode double-invoking effects; hot module reload creating new sockets.
  • Fix: module-level singleton or context; cleanup with disconnect() if you create in effects.

G2. React: handler sees stale state

  • Cause: closure captured old state when the listener was registered.
  • Fix: functional setState(prev => …), refs, or re-register on deps change with proper cleanup.

G3. Angular: UI not updating on socket events

  • Cause: handler running outside Angular zone.
  • Fix: NgZone.run, signals, or ChangeDetectorRef.markForCheck().

G4. Next.js: "window is not defined" / socket created on the server

  • Fix: client components only, create sockets in effects or lazily; don't import the client socket in server components.

G5. Jest: "did not exit one second after the test run has completed"

  • Fix: disconnect clients and io.close() in afterAll; forceExit is a last resort.

G6. NestJS: guard doesn't run on connection

  • Cause: guards run per message, not on the connection handshake.
  • Fix: authenticate in handleConnection or with server.use() middleware in afterInit.

H. Security incidents

H1. Someone receives other users' messages

  • Causes: room join not authorized (client asked to join any room); identity taken from payload; io.emit instead of io.to(room); room name collision ("1" used for both user 1 and conversation 1 → prefix rooms by type: user:1, conv:1).
  • Fix: authorize joins; prefixes; audit emits.

H2. Abuse / spam floods

  • Fix: per-user Redis rate limits; connection caps per user/IP; edge rate limiting; disconnect offenders; reduce maxHttpBufferSize.

H3. Server crashes on a malicious packet

  • Fix: update socket.io, engine.io, socket.io-parser, ws (historic CVEs); handlers wrapped; process manager auto-restart; alerting.

12.9 Incident runbook template (copy to your wiki)

Title: Realtime connections dropping / not connecting
1. Impact: % of clients affected? (connected clients graph, connect_error rate)
2. Recent changes? (deploys of app/proxy/LB/Redis, certificate renewals, config)
3. Check disconnect reasons distribution (ping timeout / transport close / server disconnect)
4. Check event loop lag, CPU, memory per node
5. Check Redis health (latency, connected clients, pubsub buffers)
6. Check LB/proxy error logs (502/400 Session ID unknown/timeouts)
7. curl the handshake from outside; test WebSocket with wscat/websocat
8. Mitigate: roll back / scale out / raise timeouts / disable heavy feature flag
9. Post-incident: add the missing metric/alert, update this runbook

12.10 Chapter summary

Most real-world Socket.IO problems come from a short list: wrong server listened on, CORS, path vs namespace confusion, proxy WebSocket headers/timeouts, missing sticky sessions, missing adapter, listeners registered repeatedly, rooms lost on reconnection, acks without timeouts, blocked event loop, and version mismatches. Know these by heart and you can fix 95% of issues in minutes.

Next: Chapter 13 — Architecture, patterns and projects

Architecture, Patterns, Organisational Best Practices and Full Projects

Goal: design real-time features the way experienced teams do: proven patterns (presence, typing, read receipts, notifications, live data, collaboration, games, tracking, uploads, progress), a reference architecture for big organisations, team standards, and a "what to do when…" decision guide.

13.1 Reference architecture (large organisation)

           ┌─────────── Clients (web, iOS, Android) ───────────┐
           │  socket.io-client  ·  REST/GraphQL for CRUD/history │
           └───────────────┬─────────────────────┬──────────────┘
                           │ wss (sticky)        │ https
                    ┌──────▼──────┐        ┌─────▼─────┐
                    │  LB / Ingress│        │ API GW    │
                    └──────┬──────┘        └─────┬─────┘
         ┌─────────────────▼───────────┐   ┌─────▼────────────┐
         │ Realtime Gateway (Socket.IO)│   │ Domain services   │
         │ N pods, stateless           │   │ (orders, chat,    │
         │ auth, rooms, fan-out        │   │  billing, …)      │
         └───┬───────────────▲─────────┘   └───┬──────────────┘
             │ adapter        │ consume         │ publish domain events
         ┌───▼────┐      ┌────┴──────────────────▼───┐
         │ Redis  │      │ Broker (Kafka/RabbitMQ/SQS)│
         │(adapter│      └────────────────────────────┘
         │presence│
         │ratelim)│        ┌──────────┐
         └────────┘        │ Database │  (source of truth: messages, notifications)
                           └──────────┘

Principles:

  1. The gateway owns connections, not business logic. It authenticates, authorizes subscriptions (room joins), validates, and fans out. Domain services publish events to a broker; the gateway consumes and emits to rooms (or services use an emitter).
  2. Database is the source of truth. Sockets deliver notifications of change; clients can always re-sync via REST/acks.
  3. Commands via socket or HTTP, both fine — but commands must be idempotent and acknowledged. Many teams send writes via HTTP (easier caching, auth, retries, observability) and use sockets only for server→client push. Both are valid; be consistent.
  4. Stateless gateway pods + adapter + sticky sessions → horizontal scaling.
  5. Contracts (event names, payload schemas) are versioned and shared.

13.2 Pattern: Presence (online/offline) — robust version

Requirements: a user is online if any of their devices is connected; no flicker on brief reconnections; correct across many nodes; self-healing after crashes.

// Redis keys:
//   presence:conn:<userId>  -> sorted set of connectionIds, score = lastSeen (ms)
const GRACE_MS = 10_000;     // wait before announcing "offline"
const STALE_MS = 60_000;     // connections not refreshed for this long are dead (crashed node)

io.on("connection", async (socket) => {
  const userId = socket.data.user.id;
  const connKey = `presence:conn:${userId}`;
  const connId = `${process.env.HOSTNAME}:${socket.id}`;

  const wasOnline = (await redis.zCard(connKey)) > 0;
  await redis.zAdd(connKey, { score: Date.now(), value: connId });
  if (!wasOnline) io.to(`presence:${userId}`).emit("presence:update", { userId, online: true });

  // heartbeat to refresh lastSeen (crash safety)
  const hb = setInterval(() => redis.zAdd(connKey, { score: Date.now(), value: connId }), 20_000);

  socket.on("disconnect", async () => {
    clearInterval(hb);
    await redis.zRem(connKey, connId);
    setTimeout(async () => {
      await redis.zRemRangeByScore(connKey, 0, Date.now() - STALE_MS); // drop stale entries
      if ((await redis.zCard(connKey)) === 0) {
        await redis.set(`presence:lastSeen:${userId}`, Date.now());
        io.to(`presence:${userId}`).emit("presence:update", { userId, online: false });
      }
    }, GRACE_MS);
  });

  // subscribe to presence of contacts (authorized!)
  socket.on("presence:subscribe", async ({ userIds }, ack) => {
    const allowed = await contacts.filterVisible(userId, userIds.slice(0, 500));
    await socket.join(allowed.map((id) => `presence:${id}`));
    const states = await Promise.all(allowed.map(async (id) => ({
      userId: id, online: (await redis.zCard(`presence:conn:${id}`)) > 0,
    })));
    ack?.({ ok: true, data: states });
  });
});

Key ideas to explain in interviews: count connections per user, grace period, TTL/heartbeat for crash safety, subscribe to presence rooms instead of broadcasting every presence change to everyone (O(N²) problem), authorize who can see whose presence, last seen timestamp.

13.3 Pattern: Typing indicator

// client: emit at most every 2 s while typing; stop event when idle
let typingTimer, lastSent = 0;
input.addEventListener("input", () => {
  const now = Date.now();
  if (now - lastSent > 2000) {
    socket.volatile.emit("typing:start", { conversationId });
    lastSent = now;
  }
  clearTimeout(typingTimer);
  typingTimer = setTimeout(() => socket.volatile.emit("typing:stop", { conversationId }), 3000);
});

// server: relay to others in the conversation (membership checked)
socket.on("typing:start", ({ conversationId }) => {
  if (!socket.rooms.has(`conversation:${conversationId}`)) return;
  socket.to(`conversation:${conversationId}`).volatile.emit("typing", { conversationId, userId: socket.data.user.id, typing: true });
});

// receiving client: auto-expire the indicator after 5 s without refresh (in case "stop" is lost)

Properties: volatile (losing one is fine), throttled, self-expiring on the receiver, excludes sender, no DB writes.

13.4 Pattern: Chat message flow with optimistic UI, acks and dedupe

Client                                   Server                               Others
  | render "sending…" (clientMsgId)        |                                     |
  |-- message:send {clientMsgId,text} ---->| validate, authorize                 |
  |                                        | INSERT … ON CONFLICT(clientMsgId) DO NOTHING (idempotent)
  |                                        |-- io.to(conv).emit(message:new) --->| render
  |<-- ack {ok, id, createdAt} ------------|                                     |
  | replace temp message with server data  |                                     |
  | (also receives message:new → dedupe by id/clientMsgId)                       |
  | on timeout → retry with SAME clientMsgId (safe) or show "tap to retry"       |
  • Ordering in the conversation: server-assigned monotonic ids/timestamps; clients sort by server order, not by arrival.
  • Missed messages: on connect, sync from last known id (Chapter 5).
  • Read receipts: client emits conversation:read { conversationId, upToMessageId } (throttled); server stores per-user pointer and emits receipt:update to the room — store a pointer per user, not a flag per message.
  • Delivery receipts ("delivered" ✓✓): the recipient client acks receipt or reports its latest received id on sync.
  • Unread counts: computed from the read pointer; push unread:update to user:<id>.

13.5 Pattern: Notifications system

  1. Service creates a notification → persist in DB (notifications table with user_id, read_at).
  2. Publish event → gateway → io.to("user:" + userId).emit("notification:new", n).
  3. Clients online get it instantly; offline users see it on next load (REST GET /notifications?unread=true) and/or via push notifications (FCM/APNs/Web Push) — Socket.IO doesn't reach closed apps.
  4. Mark as read via event or REST; broadcast notification:read to the user's other devices (user:<id> room) so badges sync across tabs.
  5. Don't push to users who aren't connected through sockets and via push for the same notification without dedupe on the client.

13.6 Pattern: Live data subscriptions (dashboards, tickers, sports scores)

socket.on("ticker:subscribe", async ({ symbols }, ack) => {
  const allowed = symbols.filter(isValidSymbol).slice(0, 50);   // cap per socket
  await socket.join(allowed.map((s) => `ticker:${s}`));
  ack?.({ ok: true, data: await snapshot(allowed) });          // initial snapshot
});
socket.on("ticker:unsubscribe", ({ symbols }) => symbols.forEach((s) => socket.leave(`ticker:${s}`)));

// market data consumer (one per node or via a single publisher + adapter)
feed.on("tick", (t) => latest.set(t.symbol, t));
setInterval(() => {                                              // coalesce to 4 updates/s
  for (const [symbol, t] of latest) io.to(`ticker:${symbol}`).volatile.emit("tick", t);
  latest.clear();
}, 250);

Patterns: snapshot + deltas, subscription rooms, coalescing, volatile, caps. If every node consumes the full feed itself, emit with io.local to avoid re-broadcasting through the adapter (each node delivers to its own sockets).

13.7 Pattern: Collaborative editing

Naively relaying edits causes conflicts. Use a convergence algorithm:

  • OT (Operational Transformation) — Google Docs style; server transforms concurrent ops (e.g. ShareDB).
  • CRDTs — e.g. Yjs or Automerge: every client applies updates in any order and converges. Socket.IO can be the transport (e.g. the y-socket.io provider, or relay binary Yjs updates yourself).

Socket.IO responsibilities: rooms per document (doc:<id>), authorization, relaying binary updates (socket.to(room).emit("doc:update", update)), awareness (cursors — volatile, throttled), persistence of document state (periodically snapshot to DB), initial sync on join (state vector exchange in Yjs). Mention binary payloads and ordering per doc.

13.8 Pattern: Multiplayer game (server-authoritative)

// server: authoritative simulation at a fixed tick rate
const TICK_MS = 50; // 20 Hz
setInterval(() => {
  for (const match of matches.values()) {
    match.applyInputs();               // inputs queued from clients
    match.step(TICK_MS);
    io.to(`match:${match.id}`).volatile.compress(false).emit("state", match.snapshot()); // compact snapshot
  }
}, TICK_MS);

socket.on("input", ({ seq, keys }) => {
  const match = matches.get(socket.data.matchId);
  match?.queueInput(socket.data.playerId, seq, keys);  // validate: rate, legal moves (anti-cheat)
});
  • Clients send inputs, not positions (anti-cheat); server validates.
  • Clients do prediction + reconciliation using input sequence numbers, and interpolation of other players between snapshots.
  • volatile for state snapshots; binary/msgpack for compactness.
  • One match = one room; matchmaking assigns players to a node (or route all players of a match to the same node — sharding by match id — to keep simulation local).
  • For twitchy shooters, TCP-based transports (WebSocket) suffer head-of-line blocking; consider WebRTC data channels / WebTransport datagrams.

13.9 Pattern: Live location tracking (delivery/ride apps)

  • Driver app emits location every N seconds (adaptive: faster when moving), volatile, throttled; server validates (speed sanity), stores latest in Redis (GEOADD) and appends to a history store in batches.
  • Riders/customers join trip:<id> (authorized) and receive throttled updates.
  • Dispatchers subscribe to geo areas (geo:<geohash-prefix> rooms) — update the driver's area room membership when it moves across cells.
  • Background location on mobile requires native background modes + HTTP fallback (sockets die in background).

13.10 Pattern: Chunked file upload with flow control

// client
async function upload(file) {
  const CHUNK = 256 * 1024; // < maxHttpBufferSize
  const { uploadId } = await socket.timeout(5000).emitWithAck("upload:start", { name: file.name, size: file.size, type: file.type });
  for (let offset = 0; offset < file.size; offset += CHUNK) {
    const chunk = await file.slice(offset, offset + CHUNK).arrayBuffer();
    await socket.timeout(10000).emitWithAck("upload:chunk", { uploadId, offset, chunk }); // ack = backpressure
    onProgress(Math.min(1, (offset + CHUNK) / file.size));
  }
  return socket.timeout(10000).emitWithAck("upload:end", { uploadId });
}

// server
socket.on("upload:chunk", async ({ uploadId, offset, chunk }, ack) => {
  const u = uploads.get(uploadId);
  if (!u || u.owner !== socket.id || offset !== u.received) return ack({ ok: false, error: "BAD_STATE" });
  await u.stream.write(chunk);    // Buffer
  u.received += chunk.length;
  ack({ ok: true });
});

Each chunk waits for its ack → natural backpressure; offsets allow resume after reconnection. For large files, HTTP (multipart/resumable/pre-signed S3 URLs) is usually better; use this pattern when you need upload over an existing socket.

13.11 Pattern: Long-running job progress

  1. Client starts the job via HTTP → gets jobId.
  2. Client emits job:subscribe { jobId } (authorized) → joins job:<jobId>; ack returns current status (so a late subscriber isn't stuck).
  3. Workers publish progress via emitter: emitter.to("job:" + id).emit("job:progress", { pct }), throttled (e.g. at most every 500 ms).
  4. On completion: job:done with result URL; job status persisted so a reconnecting client can query it.

13.12 Pattern: Auctions / inventory / anything with races

  • Server is the authority; clients send bids (commands with ack).
  • Use atomic DB operations (UPDATE … SET price = $1 WHERE id = $2 AND price < $1 or Redis Lua scripts) — never "read, compare in JS, write" across nodes.
  • Broadcast the accepted state with a version number; clients ignore older versions.
  • Server time for deadlines (send serverNow so clients can compute clock offset).
  • Anti-sniping extensions handled server-side.

13.13 Pattern: Multi-tenancy

Options:

  1. Room prefixes per tenant (t:<tenantId>:…), tenant id from the authenticated user — simplest, works with one namespace.
  2. Dynamic namespaces per tenant (/tenant-<id>) with tenant-specific middleware; cleanupEmptyChildNamespaces: true.
  3. Separate clusters per large tenant/region (isolation, data residency).

Always derive the tenant from auth, never from the client's choice, and include the tenant in every room name to prevent cross-tenant leaks.

13.14 Pattern: RPC over sockets

Acks give you request/response. A typed RPC helper:

async function rpc<T>(event: string, payload: unknown, ms = 8000): Promise<T> {
  const res = await socket.timeout(ms).emitWithAck(event, payload);
  if (!res.ok) throw Object.assign(new Error(res.error.message ?? res.error.code), { code: res.error.code });
  return res.data as T;
}

Use for interactions that are inherently bound to the live session (join room + get history). For generic CRUD prefer HTTP (caching, tooling).

13.15 Organisational standards (put these in your engineering handbook)

Code structure

  • Feature-based handler modules: registerXHandlers(io, socket, deps).
  • Handlers are thin: validate → authorize → call service → emit/ack.
  • Services are transport-agnostic (callable from HTTP, jobs, tests).
  • A single createRealtimeServer(httpServer, config) factory used by app and tests.

Event contract standards

  • Naming: domain:action lowercase; commands imperative (message:send), facts past tense (message:created) — pick and document one style.
  • Single object payload; ids as strings; times as ISO strings or epoch ms (documented).
  • Ack envelope: { ok: true, data } | { ok: false, error: { code, message?, details? } }.
  • Error codes catalogue: VALIDATION, UNAUTHORIZED, FORBIDDEN, NOT_FOUND, RATE_LIMITED, CONFLICT, INTERNAL, UPGRADE_REQUIRED.
  • Every command has a timeout on the client and is idempotent on the server (clientMsgId/requestId).
  • Contracts in a shared package (TS types + zod schemas), documented with AsyncAPI; breaking changes require a new event name/version and a deprecation window.

Room naming

  • type:id (user:42, conversation:9, org:3:role:admin), never bare ids.
  • Room membership changes only on the server, after authorization.

Reliability standards

  • Clients: one socket instance; listeners registered once; re-sync on every connect; handle connect_error with socket.active; UI shows connection state.
  • Servers: wrapped handlers; graceful shutdown; adapter + stickiness; CSR where useful.

Observability standards

  • Required metrics & alerts (Chapter 10); logs include socketId, userId, event.
  • Dashboards per environment; runbooks linked from alerts.

Security standards

  • Chapter 6 checklist as a PR template section for any realtime change.

Code review checklist for Socket.IO changes

  • [ ] Event name follows convention and exists in the contract package.
  • [ ] Payload validated; ack always called (all paths); timeout on sender side.
  • [ ] Authorization check for every data access/room join.
  • [ ] Right broadcast target (socket.to vs io.to vs socket.emit), right namespace.
  • [ ] Works with multiple nodes (no local maps), works after reconnection.
  • [ ] No listener/timer leak; cleanup on disconnect/unmount.
  • [ ] High-frequency events throttled/volatile.
  • [ ] Backward compatible with old clients.
  • [ ] Tests: integration test for the event (happy path + auth failure + validation failure).
  • [ ] Metrics/logging added.

Ownership & SLOs

  • A team owns the realtime gateway; others integrate via events.
  • SLO examples: 99.9% of connection attempts succeed within 3 s; p95 event delivery latency < 300 ms; < 0.1% handler error rate.

13.16 "What do I do when…?" — decision guide

Situation Do this
You need to notify a specific user from a REST endpoint io.to("user:" + id).emit(...) (user room joined at connection)
Same, from a worker process Redis emitter / broker → gateway
You need to know if a user is online Presence in Redis (Section 13.2), not fetchSockets per request
Users complain messages are missing after network blips Persist + sync on connect + dedupe; optionally CSR
Duplicate messages in the UI Listener cleanup / single socket / dedupe by id
You added a 2nd server and things broke Sticky sessions + adapter
Server CPU spikes when a popular room is active Coalesce/throttle, smaller payloads, volatile, shard rooms, notification + HTTP fetch
Token expires during long sessions Expiry timer + refresh flow; auth as a function
A user must be kicked out everywhere io.in("user:" + id).disconnectSockets(true)
You must send a 50 MB file HTTP upload (pre-signed URL); socket only for progress
Mobile app needs messages while backgrounded Push notifications (FCM/APNs)
Corporate users can't connect via WebSocket Keep polling fallback + sticky sessions
You want zero sticky-session complexity transports: ["websocket"] (accept no fallback)
Need guaranteed processing of client commands Ack + retries + idempotent handler
Need server push guaranteed DB persistence + offsets + client sync
Thousands of tenants Room prefixes or dynamic namespaces + cleanup
Need to broadcast to rooms A AND B (intersection) Dedicated combined room or filter via fetchSockets (rarely)
Need to deploy without disconnect storms Graceful shutdown, slow rolling deploy, jitter
Debugging "it doesn't connect" Chapter 12 first-aid kit

13.17 Full mini-project: production-style chat server (all pieces together)

// src/realtime/index.js
const { Server } = require("socket.io");
const { createAdapter } = require("@socket.io/redis-adapter");
const { z } = require("zod");

function safe(socket, handler) {
  return async (...args) => {
    const ack = typeof args.at(-1) === "function" ? args.pop() : null;
    try {
      const data = await handler(...args);
      ack?.({ ok: true, data });
    } catch (err) {
      const code = err.code ?? (err instanceof z.ZodError ? "VALIDATION" : "INTERNAL");
      if (code === "INTERNAL") socket.data.log.error({ err }, "handler failed");
      ack?.({ ok: false, error: { code, message: code === "INTERNAL" ? "Internal error" : err.message } });
    }
  };
}

const forbidden = () => Object.assign(new Error("Forbidden"), { code: "FORBIDDEN" });

exports.createRealtimeServer = async function ({ httpServer, redis, auth, chat, logger, config }) {
  const pub = redis.duplicate(); const sub = redis.duplicate();
  pub.on("error", (err) => logger.error({ err }, "redis pub"));
  sub.on("error", (err) => logger.error({ err }, "redis sub"));
  await Promise.all([pub.connect(), sub.connect()]);

  const io = new Server(httpServer, {
    cors: { origin: config.webOrigins, credentials: true },
    allowRequest: (req, cb) => cb(null, !req.headers.origin || config.webOrigins.includes(req.headers.origin)),
    serveClient: false,
    maxHttpBufferSize: 1e5,
    adapter: createAdapter(pub, sub, { key: "chat-rt" }),
    connectionStateRecovery: undefined, // classic Redis adapter doesn't support CSR → we use sync
  });

  io.use(async (socket, next) => {
    try {
      socket.data.user = await auth.verifyAccessToken(socket.handshake.auth?.token);
      socket.data.log = logger.child({ sid: socket.id, uid: socket.data.user.id });
      next();
    } catch (e) {
      next(Object.assign(new Error(e.code === "EXPIRED" ? "TOKEN_EXPIRED" : "UNAUTHORIZED"), { data: { code: e.code } }));
    }
  });

  io.use(async (socket, next) => {
    // load the rooms BEFORE "connection", so handlers can be registered synchronously
    try {
      socket.data.conversationIds = await chat.conversationIdsFor(socket.data.user.id);
      next();
    } catch (err) {
      next(new Error("INTERNAL"));
    }
  });

  io.on("connection", (socket) => {   // NOT async: register every handler synchronously
    const { id: userId } = socket.data.user;
    socket.data.log.info({ transport: socket.conn.transport.name }, "connected");
    socket.join(`user:${userId}`);
    // re-join conversations from the source of truth (works after any reconnection)
    socket.join(socket.data.conversationIds.map((id) => `conversation:${id}`));

    const Send = z.object({ conversationId: z.string(), text: z.string().trim().min(1).max(4000), clientMsgId: z.string().uuid() }).strict();
    const Sync = z.object({ conversationId: z.string(), afterId: z.string().nullable() }).strict();

    socket.on("message:send", safe(socket, async (raw) => {
      const p = Send.parse(raw);
      if (!socket.rooms.has(`conversation:${p.conversationId}`)) throw forbidden();
      const { message, created } = await chat.createMessageIdempotent(userId, p);
      if (created) io.to(`conversation:${p.conversationId}`).emit("message:new", message);
      return { id: message.id, createdAt: message.createdAt };
    }));

    socket.on("conversation:sync", safe(socket, async (raw) => {
      const p = Sync.parse(raw);
      if (!socket.rooms.has(`conversation:${p.conversationId}`)) throw forbidden();
      return chat.messagesAfter(p.conversationId, p.afterId, 500);
    }));

    socket.on("typing:start", ({ conversationId } = {}) => {
      if (typeof conversationId !== "string" || !socket.rooms.has(`conversation:${conversationId}`)) return;
      socket.to(`conversation:${conversationId}`).volatile.emit("typing", { conversationId, userId });
    });

    socket.on("disconnect", (reason) => socket.data.log.info({ reason }, "disconnected"));
  });

  // used by other modules (REST controllers) to push events
  chat.on("conversation:member-added", ({ conversationId, userId }) =>
    io.in(`user:${userId}`).socketsJoin(`conversation:${conversationId}`)); // cluster-wide
  chat.on("conversation:member-removed", ({ conversationId, userId }) =>
    io.in(`user:${userId}`).socketsLeave(`conversation:${conversationId}`));

  return io;
};

// client (browser)
import { io } from "socket.io-client";

export const socket = io(RT_URL, {
  autoConnect: false,
  transports: ["websocket", "polling"],
  auth: (cb) => cb({ token: tokenStore.get() }),
  withCredentials: true,
});

const lastIds = new Map(); // conversationId -> last message id
const seen = new Set();

socket.on("message:new", onMessage);              // registered ONCE
socket.on("typing", showTyping);

socket.on("connect", async () => {
  ui.setConnected(true);
  for (const conversationId of openConversations()) {
    try {
      const res = await socket.timeout(10000).emitWithAck("conversation:sync", { conversationId, afterId: lastIds.get(conversationId) ?? null });
      if (res.ok) res.data.forEach(onMessage);
    } catch { /* timeout: will retry on next connect or via user action */ }
  }
});

socket.on("disconnect", () => ui.setConnected(false));

socket.on("connect_error", async (err) => {
  if (!socket.active) {
    if (err.message === "TOKEN_EXPIRED") { await tokenStore.refresh(); socket.connect(); }
    else ui.showLogin();
  }
});

function onMessage(m) {
  if (seen.has(m.id)) return;
  seen.add(m.id);
  lastIds.set(m.conversationId, m.id);
  ui.renderMessage(m);
}

export async function sendMessage(conversationId, text) {
  const clientMsgId = crypto.randomUUID();
  ui.renderPending(clientMsgId, text);
  for (let attempt = 0; attempt < 3; attempt++) {
    try {
      const res = await socket.timeout(8000).emitWithAck("message:send", { conversationId, text, clientMsgId });
      if (res.ok) return ui.confirmPending(clientMsgId, res.data);
      return ui.failPending(clientMsgId, res.error.code);   // business error: don't retry
    } catch { /* timeout → retry with the SAME clientMsgId (idempotent server) */ }
  }
  ui.failPending(clientMsgId, "TIMEOUT");
}

This small codebase demonstrates almost every best practice in this book: auth in middleware, async loading done in middleware so the connection handler registers every listener synchronously (events emitted by the client right after connect are never lost), origin check, CORS allow-list, payload size limit, Redis adapter with error handlers, user rooms, re-joining from DB on every connection, cluster-wide room management with socketsJoin, validated & authorized handlers with an ack envelope, idempotent writes, volatile typing, client single registration, sync on connect, dedupe, token-expiry handling, retries with the same id.

13.18 Chapter summary

  • Architecture: stateless gateway + adapter + broker + DB as source of truth.
  • Patterns: presence (per-user count, grace, TTL), typing (volatile, throttled), chat flow (optimistic UI, idempotent, ack, sync, dedupe), notifications (persist + push), live data (snapshot + coalesced deltas), collaboration (CRDT/OT), games (authoritative ticks), tracking, chunked uploads, job progress, auctions (atomic ops), multi-tenancy.
  • Standards: naming, ack envelope, error codes, contracts, review checklist, SLOs.

Next: Chapter 14 — Interview question bank

Interview Question Bank (Entry Level → Senior/Architect)

How to use this chapter: questions are grouped by topic and tagged by level: [E] entry/junior, [M] mid-level, [S] senior/lead/architect. Answers are written the way you should say them: start with a one-line direct answer, then the depth. Chapter references point to the full explanation.

The question list was assembled from the topics interviewers and hiring guides consistently probe for Socket.IO / real-time roles — WebSocket vs Socket.IO, rooms vs namespaces, reconnection and dropped connections, authentication and data leaks, horizontal scaling with the Redis adapter and sticky sessions, and practical scenarios such as chat apps with rooms and presence or multiplayer games — plus the standard "design a real-time chat system" system-design round (stateless servers, pub/sub fan-out, offline delivery via last-seen ids, presence with TTL heartbeats, ordering, backoff), and the version-specific details that appear in technical screens.

Part A — Fundamentals

A1. [E] What is Socket.IO? A library for low-latency, bidirectional, event-based communication between clients and a server. It has a Node.js server (socket.io) and clients (socket.io-client for browser/Node, plus Java, Swift, C++, Python, etc.). It's built on Engine.IO, which handles the low-level connection (HTTP long-polling, WebSocket, WebTransport), heartbeats and transport upgrades; Socket.IO adds named events, acknowledgements, rooms, namespaces, broadcasting, automatic reconnection, buffering, middleware and multi-server adapters.

A2. [E] Is Socket.IO the same as WebSocket? Can a WebSocket client connect to a Socket.IO server? No. WebSocket is a protocol (RFC 6455) for a full-duplex channel. Socket.IO is a library with its own protocol that may use WebSocket as a transport. Every Socket.IO message carries metadata (Engine.IO packet type, Socket.IO packet type, namespace, ack id), e.g. 42["chat","hi"]. A plain WebSocket client doesn't speak that protocol (it would have to handle the 0{…} open packet, send 40 to connect to a namespace, answer pings 2 with 3, encode events), and a Socket.IO client can't connect to a plain WebSocket server.

A3. [E] Why use Socket.IO instead of raw WebSockets? Polling fallback when WebSocket is blocked; automatic reconnection with exponential back-off; heartbeat-based disconnection detection; buffering while offline; named events; acknowledgements (request/response, with timeouts and promises); rooms and broadcasting; namespaces/multiplexing; middleware for auth; binary support with automatic serialization; horizontal scaling via adapters; connection state recovery; a typed API; an admin UI. With raw WebSockets you'd build all that yourself.

A4. [E] When would you NOT use Socket.IO? When clients must be standard WebSocket clients (third-party devices, graphql-ws, MQTT-over-WS); when only one-way server→client updates are needed (SSE is simpler); for extremely latency-sensitive/high-frequency use where every byte matters (custom binary over raw WS, WebRTC/WebTransport datagrams); for durable messaging needs (use a broker behind it); in serverless runtimes that can't hold connections; or when a managed service is preferable.

A5. [E] Explain polling, long polling, SSE and WebSocket. Short polling: client requests every N seconds — simple, wasteful, latency up to N. Long polling: server holds the request until data is available, client immediately re-requests — near-real-time over plain HTTP, but header overhead and a separate request for sending. SSE: a long-lived HTTP response streaming text events server→client with automatic reconnection — one-way, text-only. WebSocket: an HTTP request upgraded (101 Switching Protocols) to a persistent full-duplex framed channel — lowest overhead/latency, text and binary, no built-in reconnection. Socket.IO uses long polling and WebSocket (and optionally WebTransport). (Ch. 1)

A6. [E] What are the main components on the server and client? Server: Server (io), Namespace, Socket (one per client per namespace), internal Client (one per connection), Adapter (rooms/broadcast), parser, Engine.IO server and socket, transport. Client: Socket (per namespace) and Manager (owns the Engine.IO connection and reconnection, accessible via socket.io). (Ch. 7)

A7. [E] How do you set up Socket.IO with Express? Create an HTTP server from the Express app and pass it to Socket.IO; listen on the HTTP server:

const app = express();
const httpServer = http.createServer(app);
const io = new Server(httpServer, { cors: { origin: "https://app.example.com" } });
io.on("connection", (socket) => { /* … */ });
httpServer.listen(3000);   // NOT app.listen(3000)

app.listen() would create a second server Socket.IO isn't attached to → 404s on /socket.io/.

A8. [E] What does io.on("connection", socket => …) give you? It fires once for each client connecting to the main namespace (after middleware accepted it). socket is that client's server-side Socket: socket.id, socket.handshake (headers, query, auth, address…), socket.data, socket.rooms, socket.emit/on/join/leave/disconnect, etc. You register that client's event handlers inside it.

A9. [E] What is socket.id? Can I use it as a user id? A random identifier of a Socket.IO socket in a namespace. It changes on every reconnection (unless connection state recovery restored the session), it's different per namespace and per tab. So no — use your authenticated user id, and a user:<id> room to reach all of a user's sockets. Also, since v3 it differs from the Engine.IO session id (socket.conn.id) for security.

A10. [E] What's the difference between socket.emit, io.emit, socket.broadcast.emit? socket.emit → only that client. io.emit → every client in the main namespace (incl. the "sender"). socket.broadcast.emit → every client in the socket's namespace except that socket. Add rooms: io.to(room).emit (everyone in room), socket.to(room).emit (everyone in room except sender). (Ch. 2 cheat sheet)

A11. [E] Can a client broadcast to other clients directly? No. broadcast, rooms and to() exist only on the server. The client emits to the server, which relays (after validating/authorizing).

A12. [E] Which transports does Socket.IO use and in which order? By default the client starts with HTTP long-polling, then tries to upgrade to WebSocket (client default list ["polling", "websocket", "webtransport"]; WebTransport only if the server enables it). You can change the order or restrict (transports: ["websocket"]).

A13. [M] Why does Socket.IO start with polling and not WebSocket? Reliability: polling works almost everywhere and gives an immediately usable connection; some proxies/antivirus accept a WebSocket handshake but then fail, and waiting for that to time out would delay the user. Once connected, the client tests WebSocket in the background (2probe/3probe) and upgrades without losing or reordering packets. The trade-off: more initial requests and the need for sticky sessions in multi-node setups.

A14. [M] What changed between Socket.IO v2 and v3/v4? v3 (Nov 2020) was the breaking release: CORS no longer enabled by default; heartbeat direction reversed (server pings, client pongs); reconnection events moved to the Manager (socket.io.on("reconnect")); socket.rooms is a Set; auth option and socket.handshake.auth; middleware errors → connect_error with err.data; no implicit default-namespace connection; maxHttpBufferSize 1 MB default (was 100 MB); cookie and perMessageDeflate off by default; Engine.IO v4 payload encoding (\x1e separator); socket id ≠ engine id; rewritten in TypeScript. v4 is compatible with v3 clients; it added typed events, io.except, fetchSockets/socketsJoin/socketsLeave/disconnectSockets, serverSideEmit, ack timeouts, broadcast acks, emitWithAck, connection state recovery, client retries, io.engine.use, WebTransport, uWebSockets.js support. v2 clients need allowEIO3: true on a v3/v4 server.

A15. [M] Are Socket.IO client and server versions independent? Yes, they're separate packages, but they must speak compatible protocols: a v3/v4 client with a v3/v4 server; a v2 client with a v4 server only with allowEIO3; never a v3/v4 client with a v2 server. Third-party clients have their own compatibility tables.

A16. [E] What is serveClient? A server option (default true) that serves the client bundle at /socket.io/socket.io.js. Set it to false when you bundle socket.io-client yourself.

A17. [E] What's the difference between the URL path and the path option? In io("https://host/admin"), /admin is the namespace. The HTTP path where the server listens is the path option (default /socket.io/), which must match on both sides. Confusing the two causes "Invalid namespace" or 404 errors.

A18. [E] What is the default maxHttpBufferSize and what happens if exceeded? 1 MB (1e6 bytes). A single message larger than that makes the server close the connection. Increase it deliberately, chunk the data, or use HTTP for large files.

Part B — Events, data and acknowledgements

B1. [E] How do you send and receive events? socket.emit("name", ...args) and socket.on("name", (...args) => {}), symmetric on both sides. Any number of arguments; prefer one object.

B2. [E] What data types can be sent? Anything JSON-serializable plus binary (Buffer, ArrayBuffer, typed arrays, Blob/ File). Dates become ISO strings, Map/Set become {}, undefined properties are dropped, NaN/Infinity → null, BigInt throws, functions can't be sent except as the last argument which becomes an ack callback. (Ch. 3)

B3. [E] What is an acknowledgement? A callback passed as the last argument of emit; the receiver gets it as the last argument of its handler and calls it to send a response back to the sender — request/ response over the socket.

socket.emit("create", data, (res) => console.log(res));
socket.on("create", (data, cb) => cb({ ok: true }));

B4. [M] What happens if the receiver never calls the ack? How do you handle it? The sender waits forever and keeps the callback in memory. Use socket.timeout(ms).emit(...) — the callback then receives an error as first argument on timeout — or await socket.timeout(ms).emitWithAck(...) which rejects. On the receiver, always call the ack in every code path (including errors), and verify typeof ack === "function".

B5. [M] What's emitWithAck? (v4.6+) A Promise-based version of emit-with-ack: const res = await socket.emitWithAck("x", data). Combined with timeout(ms) it rejects on timeout. On the server with broadcast (io.timeout(ms).emitWithAck) it resolves to an array of responses.

B6. [M] Can you use acknowledgements with broadcasts? Yes, since v4.5, with a timeout: io.timeout(5000).emit("x", (err, responses) => {}) — responses is an array, one per client; err is set if some clients didn't answer in time. Without .timeout() the callback fires almost immediately with a timeout error. Works across nodes with supporting adapters.

B7. [M] What does the .timeout() modifier change in the callback signature? It adds an err first parameter: (err, response). Forgetting this is a classic bug — people read the response from the first argument.

B8. [M] What are volatile events? Give use cases. socket.volatile.emit(...) — the packet is dropped (instead of buffered) if the underlying transport isn't currently writable — on the server (e.g. no pending polling request) and on the client (which includes being disconnected). For superseded high-frequency data: cursor/mouse positions, typing indicators, game state ticks, live prices, sensor readings.

B9. [M] What happens to events emitted by the client while disconnected? They're buffered in socket.sendBuffer and sent upon reconnection (in order). Risk: flooding the server with stale events after long offline periods. Mitigate with volatile, if (socket.connected) checks, or clearing sendBuffer on reconnect.

B10. [M] What are reserved event names? connect, connect_error, disconnect, disconnecting, newListener, removeListener. Emitting them throws. Server-side namespace events (connection, connect, new_namespace) are also reserved for serverSideEmit.

B11. [M] What are catch-all listeners? socket.onAny((event, ...args) => {}) for incoming events and (4.5+) socket.onAnyOutgoing(...) for outgoing; plus prependAny, offAny, listenersAny. Useful for logging, metrics, debugging. They don't receive reserved events.

B12. [M] Does Socket.IO guarantee message ordering? Yes — per connection, in both directions, regardless of transport and across the upgrade. Not across different sockets or across nodes. Async handlers can still finish out of order; serialize with a per-key queue if processing order matters.

B13. [M] What is the delivery guarantee by default? At-most-once: messages can be lost if the connection breaks or the client is disconnected when the server emits. Plus ordering. The client buffers its own emits while disconnected (in memory only).

B14. [S] How would you implement at-least-once delivery in both directions? Client→server: acks + retries (v4.6 retries and ackTimeout options, or manual) + idempotent server handlers (client-generated id + unique constraint) + optional persistent outbox on the client. Server→client: persist messages with monotonic ids; client tracks the last processed id; on every (re)connection the client syncs "everything after id X"; client dedupes by id. Optionally connection state recovery for short disconnections. "Exactly-once" is achieved as at-least-once + idempotent processing.

B15. [M] How do client retries work? With ackTimeout and retries options set, each emit waits for an ack; if none in ackTimeout, it's re-sent, up to retries times. Packets are queued and sent one at a time to preserve order. The server may receive duplicates → idempotency required.

B16. [M] How do you send binary/files? Pass a Buffer/ArrayBuffer/Blob/File as an argument; Socket.IO detects binary and sends it as attachments (binary WebSocket frames, base64 in polling). Respect maxHttpBufferSize; for large files chunk with acks (backpressure, resume) or use HTTP uploads.

B17. [M] What does socket.compress(false) do? Disables compression for that packet (relevant if perMessageDeflate is enabled or for HTTP compression in polling). Useful for already-compressed data to save CPU.

B18. [S] Why is perMessageDeflate disabled by default? Compression contexts consume significant memory per connection and CPU per message, and most real-time messages are small. Enable it only for large compressible payloads, with a threshold and noContextTakeover options.

B19. [M] How do you handle errors in event handlers? Socket.IO doesn't catch them: a sync throw propagates (can crash) and async rejections become unhandled rejections (crash by default in modern Node). Wrap handlers in a helper that validates, try/catches, logs, and always acks with an error envelope { ok: false, error: { code } }.

B20. [M] What's socket.send()? Shorthand for emit("message", ...); listened with on("message").

Part C — Rooms, namespaces, broadcasting

C1. [E] What is a room? A server-side named group of sockets used for broadcasting to a subset of clients. Sockets join/leave rooms only on the server; rooms are created on first join and deleted when empty; sockets leave all rooms on disconnect; each socket is in a room named after its id.

C2. [E] How do you send a private message to one user? Join each socket to user:<userId> at connection (after auth) and emit io.to("user:" + userId).emit(...) — reaches all their tabs/devices and works across nodes and reconnections. To a single socket: io.to(socketId).emit(...).

C3. [M] Difference between io.to(room).emit and socket.to(room).emit? io.to sends to all sockets in the room (including the current one if it's in it); socket.to excludes the sender.

C4. [M] What happens with io.to("a").to("b").emit()? Does a socket in both receive it twice? It's a union (sockets in a OR b); each socket receives it once. There's no built-in intersection.

C5. [M] What is a namespace? How is it different from a room? A namespace is a communication channel the client chooses to connect to, with its own event handlers, middleware and rooms, multiplexed over the same underlying connection. A room is a server-managed group inside a namespace used for broadcasting. Use rooms for grouping users (chats, documents, users); namespaces for separating concerns or permissions (/admin with its own auth middleware), or multi-tenancy via dynamic namespaces.

C6. [M] What is multiplexing? Multiple namespace sockets sharing a single underlying Engine.IO connection on the client (one Manager). io(url) and io(url + "/admin") share one WebSocket. forceNew: true or multiplex: false creates separate connections. Calling io() twice for the same URL and same namespace also creates a new connection.

C7. [M] What are dynamic namespaces? Namespaces matched by a RegExp or function, created on demand: io.of(/^\/tenant-\w+$/). The parent namespace's middleware and handlers apply to all children; socket.nsp is the child; parent.emit broadcasts to all children. cleanupEmptyChildNamespaces: true deletes empty children.

C8. [M] How do you get all sockets in a room, across several servers? await io.in("room").fetchSockets() — returns local Sockets and RemoteSockets (id, handshake, rooms, data; can emit/join/leave/disconnect). Requires an adapter that supports it (Redis etc.). Expensive — don't use on hot paths.

C9. [M] How do you make a user (all their sockets, any server) join/leave a room or disconnect? io.in("user:" + id).socketsJoin("room"), socketsLeave("room"), disconnectSockets(true).

C10. [M] What's the difference between disconnecting and disconnect? disconnecting fires before the socket leaves its rooms (so socket.rooms is still populated — useful to notify rooms); disconnect fires after (rooms empty).

C11. [M] What are adapter room events? io.of("/").adapter.on("create-room" | "delete-room" | "join-room" | "leave-room", …). They fire for private id-rooms too and are local to each node.

C12. [S] How would you list "real" rooms (not socket-id rooms)? [...adapter.rooms.keys()].filter(r => !adapter.sids.has(r)) (local node only).

C13. [M] What does io.local.emit do? Broadcasts only to sockets connected to the current node, skipping the adapter's cross-node forwarding.

C14. [M] What's io.except()? Excludes sockets in given rooms from a broadcast: io.except("banned").emit(...), io.to("room1").except("room2").emit(...).

C15. [M] Is socket.join sync or async? With the default in-memory adapter it's synchronous; with some adapters it can return a Promise (Promise<void> | void). await socket.join(room) is safe in both cases.

C16. [S] Room design for a Slack-like app? user:<id> (all devices), channel:<id> (members), workspace:<id> (announcements), maybe presence:<id> (subscribers to someone's presence), thread:<id> if threads are subscribed separately. Joins authorized on the server, re-joined from the DB on every connection; membership changes propagated cluster-wide with socketsJoin/socketsLeave.

Part D — Connection lifecycle and reliability

D1. [E] Which events does the client receive on connection problems? connect_error (couldn't connect or rejected by middleware), disconnect (with reason), and Manager events reconnect_attempt, reconnect, reconnect_error, reconnect_failed on socket.io.

D2. [M] List the client disconnect reasons and which ones auto-reconnect. io server disconnect (server called disconnect — no auto-reconnect), io client disconnect (client called disconnect — no), ping timeout, transport close, transport error, parse error (all auto-reconnect).

D3. [M] List server-side disconnect reasons. server namespace disconnect, client namespace disconnect, server shutting down, ping timeout, transport close, transport error, parse error, forced close, forced server close.

D4. [M] How does the heartbeat work in v4? The server sends a PING every pingInterval (25 s); the client must answer PONG within pingTimeout (20 s), otherwise the server closes with ping timeout. The client expects a PING within pingInterval + pingTimeout (45 s) or closes with ping timeout and reconnects. The values are sent in the handshake. In v2 the direction was reversed.

D5. [S] Why did v3 reverse the heartbeat direction? Browsers throttle timers in background tabs; a client-driven ping could be delayed and the server would wrongly consider the client dead. Server-driven pings only require the client to respond to a received message, which isn't affected the same way.

D6. [M] Why are heartbeats needed if TCP exists? TCP may not detect a dead peer for a long time (sleeping laptop, lost mobile network, NAT dropping state — no FIN/RST sent). Heartbeats detect zombies, update presence, and keep proxies/LBs from closing idle connections.

D7. [M] Explain the reconnection algorithm. Exponential back-off with jitter: delay starts at reconnectionDelay (1 s), doubles each attempt, randomized ±randomizationFactor (50%), capped at reconnectionDelayMax (5 s), unlimited attempts by default (reconnectionAttempts: Infinity). Each attempt has a timeout (20 s). Jitter avoids thundering-herd reconnection storms.

D8. [M] After reconnecting, my user no longer receives room messages. Why? A reconnection creates a new server-side socket with a new id and no rooms or data. Re-join rooms in the connection handler based on the authenticated user (DB), or have the client re-subscribe on every connect, or use connection state recovery (and still handle the non-recovered case).

D9. [M] Why do I get duplicate messages after reconnection? Listeners registered inside socket.on("connect") (or React effects without cleanup) accumulate. Register listeners once outside connect, or remove them with off using the same reference.

D10. [M] socket.on("reconnect") doesn't fire — why? Since v3 reconnection events are on the Manager: socket.io.on("reconnect", …).

D11. [M] What's socket.active? True if the socket is connected or will try to reconnect automatically. In connect_error, socket.active === false means the server rejected the connection (middleware) and you must fix and call socket.connect() yourself.

D12. [S] What is connection state recovery and how does it work? (v4.6+) Enabled with connectionStateRecovery: { maxDisconnectionDuration, skipMiddlewares }. The server gives the client a private session id (pid); each emitted event gets an offset appended (client tracks the last one). On a recoverable disconnect (transport error/close, ping timeout, forced close, server shutting down), the adapter stores {sid, pid, rooms, data}. On reconnection the client sends {pid, offset}; if the session exists and hasn't expired, the server restores id, rooms, data, sets socket.recovered = true, and replays missed packets. Limits: not for packets with acks or volatile packets; memory for stored packets; best-effort; requires a supporting adapter (in-memory, Redis Streams, MongoDB — not the classic Redis pub/sub adapter, not Postgres).

D13. [S] Clients should not miss chat messages even after a 10-minute outage. Design it. Persist messages in the DB before broadcasting (server-assigned increasing ids per conversation). Clients store the last seen id per conversation; on every connect they call sync(afterId) via ack or HTTP (paginated); live events continue meanwhile; dedupe by id; render sorted by server id. Push notifications for apps in background. CSR can reduce sync load for short blips but isn't relied on.

D14. [M] What happens on page reload? The browser closes the connection; the server sees transport close; the new page creates a new connection with a new socket id. The server can't distinguish reload from network loss → presence grace periods.

D15. [M] What timeouts exist? (pingInterval, pingTimeout, upgradeTimeout, connectTimeout, client timeout…) See Ch. 5 table: 25 s / 20 s / 10 s / 45 s on the server; client timeout 20 s; reconnection delays 1–5 s; ackTimeout none by default; Redis adapter requestsTimeout 5 s.

D16. [M] What's connectTimeout on the server? Time (45 s) a client has, after the Engine.IO handshake, to successfully join a namespace; if it hasn't, the server closes the low-level connection. Relevant when middleware never calls next().

D17. [S] How do you handle access-token expiry on long-lived connections? Validate at connection; store exp in socket.data; schedule a disconnect at expiry (or emit auth:expired); client refreshes the token and reconnects (use auth as a function so each attempt reads the latest token) or performs in-band refresh; revoke with io.in("user:" + id).disconnectSockets(true); re-check permissions on sensitive events.

D18. [M] How do you detect on the client that the connection is down and show a banner? Listen to disconnect (show "reconnecting…") and connect (hide), use socket.connected for initial state; optionally Manager reconnect_attempt for attempt counts and reconnect_failed for a final error.

D19. [S] How do mobile apps receive messages in the background? They don't via Socket.IO — the OS suspends the app and the connection dies. Use push notifications (APNs/FCM) for background delivery, and sync on foreground.

Part E — Middleware, authentication and security

E1. [E] How do you authenticate Socket.IO connections? Client sends credentials in the auth option (io(url, { auth: { token } })); server middleware io.use((socket, next) => …) verifies socket.handshake.auth.token, sets socket.data.user, and calls next() or next(new Error("unauthorized")). Alternatively cookies/sessions via io.engine.use(sessionMiddleware).

E2. [M] Why auth instead of query string or headers? Query strings end up in logs/history (token leakage). Browser WebSocket API can't set custom headers (extraHeaders only applies to polling in browsers). auth is sent in the CONNECT packet body and is per-namespace.

E3. [M] What does the client receive when middleware rejects? A connect_error event with err.message and err.data (from err.data set on the server). The client doesn't auto-reconnect (socket.active === false).

E4. [M] What are the middleware types? io.engine.use() (Express-style, raw HTTP requests, v4.6+), io.use() / nsp.use() (once per connection per namespace), socket.use() (per incoming event; next(err) emits error on the server socket), plus the allowRequest option (earliest gate).

E5. [M] How do you share an Express session with Socket.IO? io.engine.use(sessionMiddleware) then socket.request.session in handlers. For Passport: apply session + passport.session() for the handshake request only, then socket.request.user. (Pre-4.6: wrap middleware with io.use(wrap(mw)).)

E6. [S] What is Cross-Site WebSocket Hijacking and how do you prevent it? A malicious site opens a WebSocket to your server from the victim's browser; cookies are attached automatically, and WebSocket handshakes aren't protected by CORS/SOP. If you authenticate only via cookies, the attacker gets an authenticated channel. Prevent by checking Origin (allowRequest), using tokens (not ambient cookies), SameSite cookies, short-lived tickets.

E7. [M] How do you configure CORS and what's a common mistake? new Server(httpServer, { cors: { origin: ["https://app.com"], credentials: true } }); client withCredentials: true if cookies are needed. Mistakes: origin: "*" with credentials, trailing slashes/ports mismatches, believing CORS protects the WebSocket transport (it doesn't).

E8. [M] How do you authorize actions, not just connections? Every event checks permissions server-side using socket.data.user (never ids from the payload), especially room joins and data access; per-event middleware (socket.use) for role checks; re-validate for sensitive operations.

E9. [M] How do you rate-limit Socket.IO events? socket.use middleware with rate-limiter-flexible keyed by user id (Redis-backed in clusters); reply with RATE_LIMITED via ack; disconnect repeat offenders; cap connections per user/IP; edge rate limits for handshakes.

E10. [M] What security risks exist with broadcasting user content? XSS (render with textContent/framework escaping), data leaks to wrong rooms (authorize joins, prefix rooms), oversized payloads (validation, maxHttpBufferSize), injection into DB queries (validate/parameterize).

E11. [S] How would you protect against DoS on a Socket.IO server? Edge protections (WAF, connection/request rate limits), maxHttpBufferSize lowered, per-user rate limits, connection caps, validation of payload size/shape, timeouts on acks, limiting dynamic room/namespace creation, slow-consumer disconnects, keeping engine.io/socket.io-parser/ws patched (historic crash CVEs), autoscaling and process restarts.

E12. [M] How do you get the real client IP behind a proxy? socket.handshake.address gives the proxy IP; read x-forwarded-for (first entry) or cf-connecting-ip only when your proxy is trusted and sets it.

E13. [M] Is socket.handshake.auth trustworthy? It's client-supplied — trustworthy only after you verify it (e.g. JWT signature check).

E14. [S] Name some real vulnerabilities in the Socket.IO ecosystem. Permissive default CORS in socket.io < 2.4.0; an unhandled error event that could crash socket.io < 4.6.2; uncaught exceptions from crafted requests in engine.io (fixed in 6.2.1 / 6.4.2) and polling connection exhaustion (fixed in 6.6.7); socket.io-parser decoding/validation bugs and attachment-based memory exhaustion (fixed in 4.0.5, 4.2.3, 4.2.6, 4.2.7); ws DoS via many headers (8.17.1), memory disclosure (8.20.1) and fragment-based memory exhaustion (8.21.0). Pattern: malicious clients crashing or exhausting servers with malformed/oversized packets. Lesson: keep the whole stack patched (check the lockfile with npm ls ws engine.io socket.io-parser), keep maxHttpBufferSize small, rate-limit, and restart crashed processes. (Ch. 6.10 has the full table with advisory ids.)

Part F — Internals and protocol

F1. [M] What is Engine.IO? The low-level layer: handshake, transports (polling/WebSocket/WebTransport), upgrade mechanism, heartbeats, packet encoding (Engine.IO protocol v4). Socket.IO (protocol v5) runs on top of it.

F2. [M] Describe the handshake. Client: GET /socket.io/?EIO=4&transport=polling. Server: 0{"sid":"…","upgrades":["websocket"], "pingInterval":25000,"pingTimeout":20000,"maxPayload":1000000}. Then the client sends a Socket.IO CONNECT 40 (with auth payload if any), server responds 40{"sid":"<socket id>"} or 44{"message":…} on error.

F3. [S] Explain the upgrade mechanism. After connecting over polling, the client opens a WebSocket with the same sid, sends 2probe, server answers 3probe; the client pauses polling (waits for pending requests; the server closes a pending GET with a 6 noop), sends 5 (upgrade), and all traffic moves to the WebSocket; buffered packets flush. No loss/reordering. upgradeTimeout (10 s) bounds it.

F4. [M] Engine.IO packet types? 0 open, 1 close, 2 ping, 3 pong, 4 message, 5 upgrade, 6 noop.

F5. [M] Socket.IO packet types? 0 CONNECT, 1 DISCONNECT, 2 EVENT, 3 ACK, 4 CONNECT_ERROR, 5 BINARY_EVENT, 6 BINARY_ACK.

F6. [M] Decode 42/admin,7["q"] and 431[{"ok":true}]. First: Engine.IO message (4) + Socket.IO EVENT (2) in namespace /admin with ack id 7, event q. Second: message (4) + ACK (3) for ack id 1 with response {ok:true}.

F7. [S] How is binary data transmitted? The parser replaces binary values with placeholders {"_placeholder":true,"num":i}, sends a BINARY_EVENT header like 451-["bin",{"_placeholder":true,"num":0}] (1 attachment), then each attachment as a separate Engine.IO message: a raw binary WebSocket frame, or b<base64> over polling. The receiver reassembles.

F8. [M] How are multiple packets sent over polling? Concatenated in one HTTP body separated by the record separator \x1e (Engine.IO v4).

F9. [M] What's the parser and why change it? It encodes/decodes Socket.IO packets. Default: JSON + binary attachments. Alternatives: socket.io-msgpack-parser (smaller for numeric/binary data), custom parsers. Both sides must match.

F10. [S] What is the adapter and what does the default one do? Per-namespace component storing room membership (rooms, sids maps) and performing broadcasts (computing recipients, encoding once, writing to each). The default in-memory adapter works within one process; distributed adapters forward broadcasts and requests (fetchSockets, socketsJoin, serverSideEmit, broadcast acks) between nodes, and some persist sessions for CSR.

F11. [S] Why does io.emit to 10k sockets not serialize 10k times? The adapter encodes the packet once and writes the same encoded buffer/string to each target client.

F12. [M] What are socket.conn and socket.request? socket.conn is the underlying Engine.IO socket (transport, remoteAddress, id, readyState, upgrade event). socket.request is the first HTTP request (headers, session/user if middleware attached them).

F13. [M] How can you see which transport a client uses? Server: socket.conn.transport.name and socket.conn.once("upgrade", t => …). Client: socket.io.engine.transport.name and socket.io.engine.on("upgrade", …).

F14. [S] What is rememberUpgrade? Client option: if a previous connection successfully used WebSocket (static flag in the page), start directly with WebSocket next time.

F15. [M] What's WebTransport support? Since 4.7, an HTTP/3-based transport (requires HTTPS and an HTTP/3 server, enabled in server transports). Benefits: no head-of-line blocking, faster setup; limited browser support so WebSocket stays the default.

Part G — Scaling and architecture

G1. [M] How do you scale Socket.IO horizontally? Run multiple processes/servers behind a load balancer with (1) sticky sessions (or WebSocket-only transport) so all HTTP requests of a session reach the same node, and (2) a distributed adapter (Redis, Redis Streams, MongoDB, Postgres, cluster, cloud pub/sub) so broadcasts and room operations span all nodes. Keep nodes stateless (state in DB/Redis), use user rooms, and handle reconnection storms.

G2. [M] Why are sticky sessions needed? With HTTP long-polling, a session is many separate HTTP requests; the session lives in the memory of the node that did the handshake. If a request hits another node, it answers 400 {"code":1,"message":"Session ID unknown"}. Even the WebSocket upgrade request must reach the node holding the session.

G3. [M] When are sticky sessions not needed? When clients connect with transports: ["websocket"] only — a single long-lived TCP connection stays on one node. Trade-off: no polling fallback.

G4. [M] How do you implement sticky sessions? IP hash (nginx ip_hash/hash $remote_addr consistent, HAProxy balance source), cookie-based affinity (AWS ALB stickiness, HAProxy cookie, ingress-nginx affinity: cookie, nginx Plus sticky), Socket.IO's cookie option for cookie hashing, @socket.io/sticky for Node cluster, @socket.io/pm2 for PM2. Cross-origin cookie stickiness needs withCredentials: true on the client.

G5. [M] Why isn't the Node.js cluster module enough by itself? It distributes TCP connections round-robin; a polling session's requests use different TCP connections and would hit different workers. @socket.io/sticky makes the primary route by sid, and @socket.io/cluster-adapter lets workers broadcast to each other.

G6. [M] How does the Redis adapter work? Each node publishes broadcasts to Redis channels (socket.io#/# or socket.io#/#<room># for single-room broadcasts) and pattern-subscribes to socket.io#/#*; on receiving, a node broadcasts to its local sockets (ignoring its own messages). Requests (fetchSockets, socketsJoin, serverSideEmit, broadcast acks) use request/response channels with a requestsTimeout (5 s). It needs two Redis connections (pub and sub).

G7. [S] Limitations of the Redis pub/sub adapter? Pub/sub is fire-and-forget: a node disconnected from Redis misses messages; every node receives every broadcast (pattern subscription) → fan-out cost; no connection state recovery; Redis client-output-buffer-limit pubsub can disconnect slow subscribers. Alternatives: sharded adapter (Redis 7 sharded pub/sub, dynamic per-room subscriptions), Redis Streams adapter (resumes from offset, supports CSR).

G8. [M] How do you emit from a process that isn't a Socket.IO server (worker/cron/microservice)? Use an emitter (@socket.io/redis-emitter, Postgres/Mongo emitters) that publishes into the adapter's channels: emitter.to("user:1").emit("x", data). Or publish to a broker consumed by the realtime gateway. Emitters can't receive acks.

G9. [M] What is serverSideEmit? Events between Socket.IO servers in a cluster: io.serverSideEmit("reload", data) → other nodes receive via io.on("reload"); with ack and timeout you get responses from each node (4.5+). Use for cache invalidation/config coordination.

G10. [M] How do you configure nginx for Socket.IO? proxy_http_version 1.1; proxy_set_header Upgrade $http_upgrade; proxy_set_header Connection "upgrade"; plus Host/X-Forwarded-For headers, proxy_read_timeout above the ping interval (e.g. 120 s+), sticky upstream (hash $remote_addr consistent or ip_hash), and proxy_buffering off.

G11. [S] Clients disconnect every 60 seconds in production — what's happening? Almost certainly an idle timeout in a proxy/LB (nginx proxy_read_timeout 60 s default, ALB 60 s idle timeout) shorter than the traffic gap — e.g. heartbeat interval increased above it, or some proxy not counting traffic. Fix timeouts > pingInterval; check disconnect reason (transport close) and the periodicity.

G12. [S] How many connections can one Node server handle? How do you size a cluster? Typically tens of thousands per process; depends on memory per connection, message rates, fan-out (messages × recipients), compression, handler work. Load-test real traffic, measure memory per connection, CPU per message, event loop lag; size with headroom (2–3× peak) and for reconnection storms when a node dies (remaining nodes absorb its clients). Raise ulimit -n, watch ephemeral ports on proxies.

G13. [S] How do you deploy without disconnecting everyone at once? Rolling deploys a few nodes at a time; readiness probe fails first so LB stops new connections; graceful shutdown (io.close()) after a drain delay; clients reconnect with jitter to other nodes; CSR with a shared store (Redis Streams) to recover sessions; watch connection metrics between steps.

G14. [S] How do you autoscale socket servers? On connection count / memory / event loop lag (custom metrics), not just CPU. Remember scale-out only affects new connections; scale-in disconnects clients (graceful shutdown). Pre-scale for known peaks.

G15. [S] Design presence for millions of users. Track connections per user in Redis (set/sorted set with heartbeat timestamps, TTL for crash safety); user online if ≥1 live connection; grace period before "offline"; publish presence changes only to subscribers (presence:<userId> rooms for contacts/visible users) rather than to everyone; store last seen; batch/coalesce updates; shard Redis.

G16. [S] How do you avoid the "everyone receives everything" fan-out problem with big rooms? Avoid huge chatty rooms; send compact notifications and let clients fetch details over HTTP/CDN; coalesce updates per tick; shard large rooms; use sharded Redis adapter (dynamic subscriptions); io.local when each node consumes the source itself; separate read-heavy broadcast infrastructure (e.g. a dedicated fan-out tier).

G17. [S] How would you integrate Socket.IO in a microservices architecture? A dedicated realtime gateway service owns connections, auth and rooms; domain services publish domain events to a broker (Kafka/RabbitMQ/SNS-SQS); the gateway consumes and emits to rooms (or services use the Redis emitter). Commands go via HTTP or socket→gateway→ service (RPC/broker). DB remains the source of truth; clients re-sync on reconnect.

G18. [M] Can Socket.IO run on serverless (Lambda, Vercel functions)? Not as a server — those runtimes are short-lived and don't keep connections. Use long-running containers/VMs, or managed services (Azure Web PubSub for Socket.IO, API Gateway WebSockets with a different protocol, Ably/Pusher).

G19. [S] Multi-region deployment? Route users to the nearest region (geo-DNS); each region has its own Socket.IO cluster and adapter; cross-region events go through a global broker/replication (with higher latency); keep users of the same conversation in one "home" region if strong ordering is needed, or accept per-region ordering + server ids for convergence.

Part H — Performance and production

H1. [M] How do you improve Socket.IO performance? Install bufferutil/utf-8-validate; consider uWebSockets.js via io.attachApp; keep perMessageDeflate off unless needed; discard socket.request if unused; broadcast via rooms (encode once); smaller payloads/diffs; throttle/coalesce/volatile for high-frequency data; msgpack parser if measured beneficial; WebSocket-only where possible; keep the event loop free (worker threads); horizontal scaling.

H2. [M] Common causes of memory leaks in Socket.IO apps? Listeners added to global emitters per connection and never removed; per-socket timers not cleared; maps of sockets never cleaned; pending acks without timeouts; client listener accumulation; large socket.data; CSR packet storage; dynamic namespaces; compression contexts.

H3. [M] What causes mass ping timeout disconnections? Blocked event loop (CPU-heavy sync work, large JSON, GC pressure) preventing timely PING/PONG processing; overloaded servers; network issues. Check event-loop lag metrics.

H4. [M] How do you shut down gracefully? Fail readiness, wait for LB to drain new connections, optionally notify clients, call await io.close() (disconnects sockets with server shutting down and closes the HTTP server), close Redis/DB, exit. Handle SIGTERM.

H5. [S] How do you version a Socket.IO event API? Additive changes only; new event names for breaking changes (x:v2) with a deprecation window; send client version in auth and reject too-old clients with UPGRADE_REQUIRED; shared contract package + AsyncAPI docs.

H6. [M] What metrics do you monitor? Connected clients (io.engine.clientsCount), connect/disconnect rates, disconnect reasons, connect_error rates, transport distribution, events per second by name, ack latency, handler errors, event loop lag, memory, Redis health. Low-cardinality labels only.

H7. [M] How do you debug Socket.IO? DEBUG=socket.io*,engine on the server, localStorage.debug = "socket.io-client:*" in the browser; DevTools Network (handshake response, 101 WebSocket, Messages tab); onAny/onAnyOutgoing logging; io.engine.on("connection_error") codes; Admin UI; curl the handshake.

H8. [M] What does io.engine.on("connection_error") tell you? Low-level failures with codes: 0 Transport unknown, 1 Session ID unknown (sticky sessions!), 2 Bad handshake method, 3 Bad request, 4 Forbidden (allowRequest), 5 Unsupported protocol version.

H9. [M] What is the Socket.IO Admin UI? @socket.io/admin-ui: instrument(io, { auth: {...}, mode, readonly }) creates an /admin namespace; the UI (admin.socket.io or self-hosted) shows servers, sockets, rooms, events, and can join/leave/disconnect sockets. Protect it.

H10. [M] How do you test Socket.IO code? Unit-test thin handlers with fakes; integration tests with a real server on port 0 and real socket.io-client instances (wait for events with promises, use ack timeouts, always close); E2E with Playwright (two contexts); load tests with Artillery's Socket.IO engine or custom clients through the real LB.

Part I — Frameworks, TypeScript, clients

I1. [M] How do you type Socket.IO events in TypeScript? Define ServerToClientEvents, ClientToServerEvents, InterServerEvents, SocketData interfaces; new Server<ClientToServerEvents, ServerToClientEvents, InterServerEvents, SocketData>() on the server; Socket<ServerToClientEvents, ClientToServerEvents> on the client (reversed order: listen, emit). Share them in a package; still validate at runtime.

I2. [M] How do you use Socket.IO in React correctly? Single socket instance (module/context), autoConnect: false and connect after login; register listeners in useEffect with cleanup socket.off(event, sameHandler); avoid stale closures (functional updates/refs); StrictMode-safe; don't create sockets in render.

I3. [M] Why do I see two connections in React development? io() called in a component/effect that StrictMode runs twice (mount→unmount→mount), or on every render, or HMR. Create the socket outside components; cleanup properly.

I4. [M] How do you integrate with Angular? An injectable service wrapping the socket; expose events as Observables that off on unsubscribe; run handlers in NgZone (or update signals) so change detection runs.

I5. [M] How does NestJS support Socket.IO? @WebSocketGateway() classes with @SubscribeMessage() handlers (return value = ack), @WebSocketServer() for the server/namespace, lifecycle hooks (afterInit, handleConnection, handleDisconnect), guards/pipes/filters per message, IoAdapter subclass for the Redis adapter.

I6. [M] How do you use Socket.IO with Next.js? Client code only in client components/effects; the server either a custom Node server attaching Socket.IO to the same HTTP server, or (better) a separate long-running service. Not on serverless functions.

I7. [M] What's special about React Native? Use transports: ["websocket"]; real device IPs; HTTPS (cleartext blocked by default); handle AppState (background disconnects) and push notifications.

I8. [M] Which clients exist for other platforms? Official: JavaScript, Java (Android), Swift (iOS), C++ — plus Python (python-socketio), Dart/Flutter, .NET, Rust, Go community clients. Check protocol compatibility tables.

Part J — Scenario / debugging questions (very common for experienced roles)

J1. [M] Users report the app works at home but not at the office. Corporate proxy/firewall blocks/breaks WebSocket. If you forced WebSocket-only, add polling fallback (with sticky sessions); use TLS (wss) since proxies tamper less with encrypted traffic; check connect_error telemetry by network.

J2. [M] After adding a second server, clients randomly reconnect and some messages are missing. Missing sticky sessions (Session ID unknown) and/or missing adapter (broadcasts don't cross nodes). Add both; verify with clients on different nodes.

J3. [M] Messages appear twice in the chat UI. Duplicate listeners (registered in connect handler or effects without cleanup), multiple socket instances, or retries without dedupe. Fix registration, single instance, dedupe by message id/clientMsgId.

J4. [M] The client never receives the "welcome" event emitted right at connection. The client registered the listener after connecting (race). Register listeners before connecting (autoConnect: false → register → connect()), or request initial state via an ack.

J5. [S] The server misses the first event the client sends after connecting. Handlers registered after an await in an async connection handler — events arriving meanwhile have no listener and are dropped. Register handlers synchronously first; do async loading in middleware or afterwards.

J6. [M] CPU spikes to 100% and mass ping timeouts happen when a big room is active. Fan-out of a high-frequency event to many recipients and/or big payloads; event loop saturation. Throttle/coalesce, volatile, smaller payloads, shard rooms, scale out, profile.
