J7. [M] Clients disconnect exactly every 60 s with "transport close". Proxy idle timeout — see G11.

J8. [M] connect_error: xhr poll error in production only. URL/port/path wrong, server down, CORS-masked proxy errors, TLS issues, proxy not routing /socket.io/. Curl the handshake, check proxy logs.

J9. [M] The WebSocket never upgrades — polling every 25 seconds in DevTools. Proxy missing Upgrade headers / HTTP 1.1; LB without WebSocket support. Fix proxy config.

J10. [S] Memory grows linearly with time even with constant connections. A leak: listeners/timers not cleaned on disconnect, pending acks without timeout, maps, CSR storage. Heap snapshots comparing before/after many connect/disconnect cycles.

J11. [S] After a deploy, the cluster went down repeatedly. Reconnection storm / thundering herd: all clients reconnect to remaining nodes at once, expensive handshakes (DB lookups) overwhelm them. Jitter + larger max delay, cheap handshakes (JWT), edge rate limits, slower rolling deploys, pre-scaling.

J12. [M] An ack callback receives Error: operation has timed out immediately on a broadcast. Broadcast with ack without .timeout(). Use io.timeout(ms).emit(...).

J13. [S] A user removed from a group still receives group messages. Their sockets are still in the room. On membership change call io.in("user:" + id).socketsLeave("group:" + gid) (cluster-wide), and check membership in handlers rather than relying solely on room membership.

J14. [S] Some users see other tenants' notifications. Room name collisions ("42" used for several entity types/tenants), client-chosen rooms without authorization. Prefix rooms by type and tenant, authorize joins.

J15. [M] After upgrading the server to v4, old mobile apps can't connect. They use a v2 client (Engine.IO v3). Enable allowEIO3: true temporarily, then update the apps (compatible client versions).

J16. [M] socket.emit throws "is a reserved event name". Using connect, disconnect, etc. as custom names. Rename.

J17. [S] Presence flickers online/offline when users refresh. No grace period; per-socket presence. Count connections per user and delay offline announcements by a few seconds.

J18. [S] Redis restarted and cross-node broadcasts silently stopped on one node. Subscriber didn't resubscribe / client in a broken state; missing error handling. Ensure reconnect logic and error listeners, monitor adapter health, consider Streams adapter.

J19. [M] Large file upload disconnects the client. Exceeds maxHttpBufferSize. Chunk with acks or use HTTP upload; adjust limit carefully.

J20. [M] Node test runner/Jest hangs after Socket.IO tests. Open handles: clients not disconnected / server not closed. Clean up in after.

Part K — Coding questions (write the code)

K1. [E] Write a server that echoes every message back to the sender and broadcasts a join notice.

io.on("connection", (socket) => {
  socket.broadcast.emit("user:joined", socket.id);
  socket.on("echo", (msg, ack) => {
    socket.emit("echo", msg);
    if (typeof ack === "function") ack({ ok: true });
  });
});

K2. [M] Implement JWT auth middleware with an error payload.

io.use((socket, next) => {
  try {
    const p = jwt.verify(socket.handshake.auth?.token, PUBLIC_KEY, { algorithms: ["RS256"] });
    socket.data.user = { id: p.sub, roles: p.roles };
    next();
  } catch (e) {
    const err = new Error(e.name === "TokenExpiredError" ? "TOKEN_EXPIRED" : "UNAUTHORIZED");
    err.data = { reason: e.name };
    next(err);
  }
});

K3. [M] Implement chat rooms with join authorization and history in the ack. (See Ch. 4 worked example: channel:join checks ACL, await socket.join, notifies others with socket.to, acks with history.)

K4. [M] Implement a typing indicator with throttling and auto-expiry. (Ch. 13.3.)

K5. [M] Write a promise-based request() helper with timeout and error envelope on the client.

async function request(event, payload, ms = 5000) {
  const res = await socket.timeout(ms).emitWithAck(event, payload); // rejects on timeout
  if (!res.ok) throw Object.assign(new Error(res.error.code), res.error);
  return res.data;
}

K6. [M] Count online users across a cluster. Approximate & cheap: Redis counter/sets maintained in connection/disconnect (with TTL heartbeats). Exact but expensive: (await io.fetchSockets()).length; distinct users: new Set((await io.fetchSockets()).map(s => s.data.user.id)).size.

K7. [M] Kick a user from all devices.

io.in(`user:${userId}`).emit("session:revoked");
io.in(`user:${userId}`).disconnectSockets(true);

K8. [M] Write a per-socket rate limiter without libraries (token bucket).

io.on("connection", (socket) => {
  let tokens = 10, last = Date.now();
  socket.use(([event, ...args], next) => {
    const now = Date.now();
    tokens = Math.min(10, tokens + ((now - last) / 1000) * 5); // refill 5/s, burst 10
    last = now;
    if (tokens < 1) {
      const ack = typeof args.at(-1) === "function" ? args.at(-1) : null;
      return ack?.({ ok: false, error: { code: "RATE_LIMITED" } });
    }
    tokens -= 1;
    next();
  });
});

K9. [S] Write a handler wrapper that validates with zod, catches errors and always acks. (Ch. 3.10 / 13.17 safe().)

K10. [S] Implement message sync on reconnect with dedupe on the client. (Ch. 5.9 option B.)

K11. [M] Test that a broadcast excludes the sender. (Ch. 10.3 test "room broadcast excludes sender".)

K12. [S] Set up the Redis adapter with error handling. (Ch. 8.4.)

K13. [M] React hook that subscribes to an event safely. (Ch. 9.2 useSocketEvent.)

K14. [S] Broadcast to all clients and collect their responses within 3 seconds.

try {
  const responses = await io.timeout(3000).emitWithAck("health:check");
  console.log(`${responses.length} clients answered`);
} catch (err) {
  // some clients did not respond in time
}

K15. [S] Chunked upload with acks and resume. (Ch. 13.10.)

Part L — System design rounds

L1. [S] Design a WhatsApp/Slack-like chat system. Structure your answer: 1. Requirements: 1:1 and group chat, delivery/read receipts, presence, typing, media, history, push notifications, multi-device; scale (e.g. 10M concurrent), latency (< 300 ms p95), durability (no message loss), ordering per conversation. 2. Connection tier: Socket.IO gateway nodes (stateless) behind L7 LB with sticky sessions (or WS-only for mobile), TLS; auth via token in auth; user:<id> rooms; heartbeat defaults; capacity ~50–100k connections/node → hundreds of nodes; graceful deploys. 3. Message flow: client message:send with clientMsgId → validate/authorize → persist (Cassandra/Scylla/Postgres partitioned by conversation, server-assigned time-ordered ids) → publish to fan-out (adapter or broker) → io.to(conversation room or member user rooms).emit → ack to sender. Idempotency via clientMsgId. 4. Offline/missed delivery: last-seen id sync on reconnect; push notifications for offline/background users; dedupe. 5. Fan-out: small groups → per-member user rooms; large channels → room subscriptions or pull-on-notify; Redis sharded pub/sub / Kafka for cross-node. 6. Presence/typing: Redis TTL heartbeats, grace periods, subscription-based presence broadcast; typing volatile & throttled. 7. Receipts: per-user read pointer per conversation; batched updates. 8. Media: HTTP upload to object storage (pre-signed URLs), message carries the URL. 9. Ordering & consistency: per-conversation ordering via server ids; clients sort. 10. Security: authz per conversation, rate limits, E2E encryption option. 11. Observability & ops: metrics, alerts, rolling deploys, multi-region.

L2. [S] Design a live notifications service for 50M users. Persist notifications; gateway with user rooms; event-driven pipeline from services via broker; presence-aware routing (only push via socket if online, else mobile push); read-state sync across devices; unread counters in Redis; backpressure and batching; priority lanes.

L3. [S] Design a live sports score/stock ticker for millions of viewers. Subscription rooms per match/symbol, snapshot on subscribe + coalesced deltas (e.g. 4/s), volatile delivery, each node consuming the feed and using io.local (avoid adapter fan-out), CDN for heavy data, WebSocket-only for efficiency, horizontal scaling by viewer count.

L4. [S] Design collaborative document editing (Google Docs). Rooms per document; CRDT (Yjs) or OT (ShareDB) for convergence; binary update relay; awareness/cursors volatile & throttled; persistence snapshots + update log; initial sync via state vectors; authorization per doc; ordering per doc (route a doc's editors to the same node or rely on CRDT commutativity); offline editing merges on reconnect.

L5. [S] Design a real-time multiplayer game backend. Matchmaking assigns matches to nodes (all players of a match on one node); authoritative simulation tick loop; clients send inputs with sequence numbers; server broadcasts compact snapshots (volatile, binary/msgpack) at 10–30 Hz; client prediction and interpolation; anti-cheat validation; reconnect within grace period resumes the same player slot (CSR or game-level session tokens).

L6. [S] Design a ride-hailing live location system. Drivers emit throttled volatile location; gateway writes latest to Redis GEO and batches history; trip rooms for riders; geo-cell rooms for dispatch; adaptive frequency; push and HTTP fallback for background; scale by region.

L7. [S] Design a live auction platform. Bids as acknowledged commands; atomic conditional DB/Redis updates; broadcast accepted state with versions; server-authoritative timers with clock-offset; anti-sniping; idempotency; audit log; high-traffic rooms coalesced.

Part M — Experience / behavioural questions (prepare your stories)

  • "Tell me about a real-time feature you built." Cover requirements, why Socket.IO, architecture (rooms, auth, scaling), a problem you hit (e.g. duplicate listeners, sticky sessions, proxy timeouts, reconnection storms), how you diagnosed it (metrics, DEBUG, DevTools), the fix, and the measurable result.
  • "How did you ensure reliability?" Acks, idempotency, sync on reconnect, CSR, monitoring disconnect reasons, graceful deploys.
  • "How did you scale it?" Adapter choice and why, stickiness approach, load tests and numbers, bottleneck found (fan-out, event loop) and fix.
  • "A production incident involving WebSockets?" Use the runbook structure from Ch. 12.9.
  • "How do you keep the event API maintainable across teams?" Contracts package, naming conventions, ack envelope, AsyncAPI docs, review checklist, versioning.

Part N — Rapid-fire (one-liners)

  1. Default path? → /socket.io/.
  2. Default pingInterval / pingTimeout? → 25 000 / 20 000 ms.
  3. Default maxHttpBufferSize? → 1 MB (1e6).
  4. Default connectTimeout? → 45 000 ms.
  5. Default upgradeTimeout? → 10 000 ms.
  6. Default client reconnection delay / max / jitter? → 1 000 ms / 5 000 ms / 0.5.
  7. Default reconnectionAttempts? → Infinity.
  8. Default client connection timeout? → 20 000 ms.
  9. Is CORS enabled by default (v3+)? → No.
  10. Is perMessageDeflate enabled by default? → No.
  11. Is the io cookie enabled by default? → No.
  12. Default transports (server)? → ["polling", "websocket"].
  13. What is io.sockets? → Alias of io.of("/").
  14. Room every socket joins automatically? → Its own id.
  15. Are rooms client-side? → No, server-only.
  16. Event to read rooms before leaving? → disconnecting.
  17. Where are reconnection events (v3+)? → socket.io (Manager).
  18. Property telling if the client will auto-reconnect? → socket.active.
  19. Promise-based ack API? → emitWithAck.
  20. Ack timeout API? → socket.timeout(ms).
  21. Drop-if-not-ready modifier? → volatile.
  22. Broadcast only to sockets connected to THIS node? → io.local.
  23. Server-to-server events? → serverSideEmit.
  24. Fetch sockets cluster-wide? → fetchSockets().
  25. Accept v2 clients? → allowEIO3: true.
  26. Express middleware on engine? → io.engine.use() (4.6+).
  27. Engine error "Session ID unknown" code? → 1.
  28. Engine error for disabled transport? → 0 "Transport unknown".
  29. Packet 42 means? → Engine.IO message + Socket.IO event.
  30. Polling payload separator (EIO4)? → \x1e.
  31. Binary over polling prefix? → b + base64.
  32. Upgrade probe packets? → 2probe / 3probe, then 5.
  33. CSR introduced in? → 4.6.
  34. WebTransport support introduced in? → 4.7.
  35. uWebSockets.js support via? → io.attachApp(app) (4.4+).
  36. Adapters supporting CSR? → in-memory, Redis Streams, MongoDB.
  37. Package for sticky sessions in Node cluster? → @socket.io/sticky.
  38. Package to emit from non-Socket.IO processes? → @socket.io/redis-emitter (and others).
  39. Admin dashboard package? → @socket.io/admin-ui.
  40. Can socket.emit("disconnect") be used? → No, reserved.

Cheat Sheet, API Reference and Glossary

Quick reference for daily work and last-minute interview revision. Verified against socket.io@4.8.4 / socket.io-client@4.8.4.

15.1 Server API

Server (io)

API Description
new Server(httpServer?, options?) / new Server(port, options?) Create a server
io.listen(port \| httpServer) / io.attach(...) Attach/listen
io.attachApp(uwsApp) Attach to uWebSockets.js (4.4+)
io.on("connection", (socket) => …) New connection on main namespace (alias connect)
io.use((socket, next) => …) Connection middleware (main namespace)
io.of(name \| RegExp \| fn) Get/create a namespace (dynamic with RegExp/fn)
io.sockets Main namespace (io.of("/"))
io.emit(ev, ...args) Broadcast to all in main namespace
io.to(room \| room[]) / io.in(...) Target rooms
io.except(room \| room[]) Exclude rooms
io.local Only this node
io.volatile Droppable
io.compress(bool) Compression flag
io.timeout(ms) Ack timeout for broadcast with acks
io.emitWithAck(...) (with timeout) Promise of responses array
io.fetchSockets() Promise<(Socket\|RemoteSocket)[]> cluster-wide
io.socketsJoin(rooms) / io.socketsLeave(rooms) Bulk join/leave
io.disconnectSockets(close?) Bulk disconnect
io.serverSideEmit(ev, ...args) / serverSideEmitWithAck Events to other servers
io.on(ev, handler) for server-side events Receive serverSideEmit events
io.allSockets() Promise<Set<SocketId>> (older API; prefer fetchSockets)
io.close() Close all connections and the HTTP server (Promise)
io.engine Engine.IO server: clientsCount, generateId, use(), events connection, connection_error, headers, initial_headers
io.adapter(Adapter) Set adapter (or adapter option)
io.path(), io.serveClient(), io.connectTimeout() Getters/setters

Namespace

nsp.name, nsp.sockets (Map of local sockets), nsp.adapter, nsp.use(), nsp.on("connection"), nsp.emit, nsp.to/in/except, nsp.local, nsp.volatile, nsp.compress, nsp.timeout, nsp.fetchSockets, nsp.socketsJoin/socketsLeave, nsp.disconnectSockets, nsp.serverSideEmit.

Server-side Socket

API Description
socket.id Socket id (per namespace)
socket.handshake { headers, time, address, xdomain, secure, issued, url, query, auth }
socket.data Arbitrary per-socket data (serialized for fetchSockets)
socket.rooms Set<Room> (includes own id)
socket.nsp Namespace
socket.conn Engine.IO socket (transport.name, remoteAddress, on("upgrade"), writeBuffer)
socket.request First HTTP request
socket.connected / socket.disconnected State
socket.recovered CSR succeeded (4.6+)
socket.emit(ev, ...args[, ack]) Emit to this client
socket.emitWithAck(ev, ...args) Promise ack (use with timeout)
socket.timeout(ms) Ack timeout (4.5+)
socket.volatile, socket.compress(bool) Flags
socket.broadcast Broadcast to all except this socket
socket.to(room) / socket.in(room) / socket.except(room) Broadcast to rooms, excluding sender
socket.join(room \| room[]) / socket.leave(room) Room membership

Events disconnecting, disconnect, error

15.4 Emit cheat sheet

socket.emit("e", data);                     // this client
io.emit("e", data);                         // everyone (main namespace)
socket.broadcast.emit("e", data);           // everyone except sender
io.to("r").emit("e", data);                 // room r
socket.to("r").emit("e", data);             // room r except sender
io.to(["a", "b"]).emit("e");                // a ∪ b, once each
io.except("banned").emit("e");              // everyone except room
io.to(socketId).emit("e");                  // one socket
io.of("/admin").to("r").emit("e");          // room in namespace
io.local.emit("e");                         // this node only
socket.volatile.emit("e");                  // droppable
socket.compress(false).emit("e");           // no compression
socket.emit("e", data, (res) => {});        // ack
socket.timeout(5000).emit("e", (err, res) => {});          // ack + timeout
await socket.timeout(5000).emitWithAck("e", data);         // promise
io.timeout(5000).emit("e", (err, responses) => {});        // broadcast ack
io.serverSideEmit("e", data);               // other servers

15.5 Disconnect reasons

Server Client
server namespace disconnect io server disconnect (no auto-reconnect)
client namespace disconnect io client disconnect (no auto-reconnect)
server shutting down ping timeout
ping timeout transport close
transport close transport error
transport error parse error
parse error
forced close
forced server close

15.6 Protocol cheat sheet

Engine.IO: 0 open · 1 close · 2 ping · 3 pong · 4 message · 5 upgrade · 6 noop. Socket.IO: 0 CONNECT · 1 DISCONNECT · 2 EVENT · 3 ACK · 4 CONNECT_ERROR · 5 BINARY_EVENT · 6 BINARY_ACK. Format: <type>[<attachments>-][<nsp>,][<ackId>][<json>] → 42/admin,7["q"]. Engine errors: 0 Transport unknown · 1 Session ID unknown · 2 Bad handshake method · 3 Bad request · 4 Forbidden · 5 Unsupported protocol version.

@socket.io/redis-adapter Redis pub/sub (and sharded) adapter
@socket.io/redis-streams-adapter Redis Streams adapter
@socket.io/mongo-adapter / @socket.io/postgres-adapter DB adapters
@socket.io/cluster-adapter / @socket.io/sticky / @socket.io/pm2 Single-machine multi-core
@socket.io/gcp-pubsub-adapter / @socket.io/aws-sqs-adapter / @socket.io/azure-service-bus-adapter Cloud adapters
@socket.io/redis-emitter / @socket.io/postgres-emitter / @socket.io/mongo-emitter Emit from other processes
@socket.io/admin-ui Admin dashboard
rate-limiter-flexible, zod Rate limiting, validation (third-party)
Artillery Load testing

15.8 Glossary

  • Ack (acknowledgement): callback-based reply to an emitted event.
  • Adapter: stores rooms and performs broadcasts; distributed adapters link servers.
  • At-most-once / at-least-once / exactly-once: delivery guarantees; Socket.IO default is at-most-once.
  • Back-off (exponential) & jitter: growing randomized delays between reconnection attempts.
  • Broadcast: one emit to many sockets.
  • Connection state recovery (CSR): restoring id, rooms, data and missed packets after a short disconnection.
  • CSWSH: Cross-Site WebSocket Hijacking.
  • Emitter: library to emit through an adapter from non-Socket.IO processes.
  • Engine.IO: low-level transport layer under Socket.IO.
  • Fan-out: number of deliveries a broadcast generates.
  • Handshake: first request establishing the session (sid, ping settings).
  • Heartbeat: ping/pong to detect dead connections.
  • Idempotency: processing a duplicate request has no extra effect.
  • Long polling: server holds HTTP requests open until data is available.
  • Manager: client object owning the connection and reconnection.
  • Multiplexing: several namespaces sharing one connection.
  • Namespace: client-chosen channel with its own handlers, middleware, rooms.
  • Parser: encodes/decodes Socket.IO packets.
  • Presence: online/offline tracking.
  • Room: server-side group of sockets for broadcasting.
  • Sticky session (session affinity): routing a client's requests to the same server.
  • Thundering herd: many clients reconnecting simultaneously.
  • Transport: polling, WebSocket, WebTransport.
  • Upgrade: switching from polling to WebSocket.
  • Volatile: an emit that may be dropped instead of buffered.

15.9 The 30-second final revision

  1. Socket.IO ≠ WebSocket; Engine.IO underneath; polling → WebSocket upgrade.
  2. Events + acks (+ timeout, emitWithAck), volatile, binary, reserved names.
  3. Rooms (server-side, auto id-room, auto-leave) vs namespaces (client-chosen, own middleware).
  4. socket.to excludes sender; io.to doesn't; multiple to = union.
  5. Heartbeat: server pings 25 s / 20 s timeout; reconnection 1–5 s with jitter.
  6. Disconnect reasons; no auto-reconnect after server/client disconnect or middleware rejection.
  7. At-most-once by default; at-least-once = acks + retries + idempotency + sync.
  8. CSR (4.6): restore session; limitations; adapter support.
  9. Auth via auth + io.use; CORS ≠ WebSocket protection; check Origin; authorize every action.
  10. Scale: sticky sessions + adapter (Redis/Streams…); emitters; proxy Upgrade headers & timeouts.
  11. Production: graceful shutdown, metrics (disconnect reasons, event loop lag), leaks, versioning.
  12. Common bugs: app.listen, CORS, path vs namespace, duplicate listeners, rooms lost on reconnect, async connection handler, missing sticky sessions/adapter, ack without timeout.

You've finished Part I. Part II goes beyond the core: Chapter 16 — Native mobile clients. Or go back to the table of contents.

Native Mobile Clients: Android (Java/Kotlin), iOS (Swift) and Flutter (Dart)

Goal: connect native mobile apps to a Socket.IO server correctly — installation, version compatibility, authentication, events, acknowledgements with timeouts, reconnection, threading, app lifecycle, background behaviour, and the bugs that only happen on phones.

How this chapter was checked: the Java client code was compiled with Maven and run against a real Socket.IO 4.8.4 server (connect with auth, emit with an ack + timeout, server→client ack). The Swift and Dart APIs were checked line by line against the libraries' current source code and READMEs (socket.io-client-swift master, socket_io_client 3.1.6 on pub.dev); they could not be executed in this environment (no Xcode / Dart SDK).

16.1 ELI5

Your phone app wants the same walkie-talkie the website has. There are official walkie-talkies built for each kind of phone: one for Android (Java/Kotlin), one for iPhone (Swift), and a community one for Flutter (Dart). They all speak the same Socket.IO language — but only if their version matches the server's version, just like two walkie-talkies must be on the same channel.

Phones are special because: - they sleep — when the app goes to the background, the operating system pauses it and the connection dies; - their network changes all the time (Wi-Fi → 4G → elevator → no signal); - UI code must run on the main thread, but Socket.IO callbacks arrive on a background thread.

16.2 Version compatibility (check this FIRST)

Client Version Works with Socket.IO server
Java / Android (io.socket:socket.io-client) 1.x 2.x
2.x (latest 2.1.2) 3.x and 4.x
Swift (socket.io-client-swift) 15.x 2.x
16.x 2.x (with .version(.two)), 3.x and 4.x (default)
Dart / Flutter (socket_io_client) 0.9.x – 1.x 2.x
2.x 3.x – 4.6.x
3.x (latest 3.1.6) 4.7.x – 4.x

The Dart table is copied from the package's README. "Works in the browser but not on Android/iOS" is very often a client/server protocol mismatch — check this table before anything else.

16.3 Android — Java

Installation (Gradle)

// app/build.gradle.kts
dependencies {
    implementation("io.socket:socket.io-client:2.1.2") {
        // Android already ships org.json — exclude the duplicate to avoid conflicts
        exclude(group = "org.json", module = "json")
    }
}

<!-- AndroidManifest.xml -->
<uses-permission android:name="android.permission.INTERNET" />

Android 9+ blocks cleartext HTTP by default. Use https:// in production; for local development either use HTTPS or allow cleartext only for your dev host with a network_security_config. On the Android emulator, your computer's localhost is 10.0.2.2; on a real device use your computer's LAN IP.

A complete, verified example

This exact code (minus the Android-specific UI parts) was compiled and run against a Node.js Socket.IO 4.8.4 server:

import io.socket.client.Ack;
import io.socket.client.AckWithTimeout;
import io.socket.client.IO;
import io.socket.client.Socket;
import org.json.JSONObject;

import java.net.URI;
import java.util.Collections;

IO.Options options = IO.Options.builder()
    .setAuth(Collections.singletonMap("token", accessToken)) // → socket.handshake.auth on the server
    .setTransports(new String[] { "websocket" })             // recommended on mobile (see 16.6)
    .setReconnectionDelay(1000)
    .setReconnectionDelayMax(5000)
    .build();

Socket socket = IO.socket(URI.create("https://api.example.com"), options);

socket.on(Socket.EVENT_CONNECT, args -> {
    System.out.println("connected, id=" + socket.id());
});

socket.on(Socket.EVENT_CONNECT_ERROR, args -> {
    // args[0] is an Exception; for middleware rejections its message is the server's err.message
    System.out.println("connect_error: " + args[0]);
});

socket.on(Socket.EVENT_DISCONNECT, args -> {
    System.out.println("disconnect: " + args[0]);   // e.g. "io server disconnect", "transport close"
});

// receive an event
socket.on("chat:message", args -> {
    JSONObject msg = (JSONObject) args[0];          // JSON objects arrive as org.json.JSONObject
    String text = msg.optString("text");
});

// server -> client with acknowledgement: the Ack is the LAST argument
socket.on("question", args -> {
    Ack ack = (Ack) args[args.length - 1];
    ack.call("from java");
});

socket.connect();

// emit with acknowledgement AND timeout (AckWithTimeout, client 2.1+)
JSONObject payload = new JSONObject().put("a", 2).put("b", 40);
socket.emit("sum", new Object[] { payload }, new AckWithTimeout(5000) {
    @Override public void onSuccess(Object... res) { System.out.println("sum ack: " + res[0]); } // 42
    @Override public void onTimeout() { System.out.println("no answer in 5 s"); }
});

// simple emit and emit with a plain Ack (no timeout)
socket.emit("typing", new JSONObject().put("conversationId", "c1"));
socket.emit("ping-me", "hello", (Ack) res -> System.out.println(res[0]));

Real output of that run:

connected, id=Uu7oWvS3c96eMpVEAAAA
sum ack: 42
(server log) server got answer from java client: from java

Data type mapping (Java ↔ JSON)

JavaScript (server) Java (client)
object org.json.JSONObject
array org.json.JSONArray
string / number / boolean String / Integer/Long/Double / Boolean
null JSONObject.NULL
Buffer / ArrayBuffer byte[]

To send an object, build a JSONObject (or JSONArray). Binary: pass a byte[].

Threading — the #1 Android bug

All Socket.IO callbacks run on the client's internal event thread, not the Android main (UI) thread. Touching views from them throws CalledFromWrongThreadException ("Only the original thread that created a view hierarchy can touch its views").

socket.on("chat:message", args -> {
    JSONObject msg = (JSONObject) args[0];
    runOnUiThread(() -> adapter.add(msg));     // Activity
    // or: new Handler(Looper.getMainLooper()).post(() -> ...);
    // or: liveData.postValue(msg);  (postValue is thread-safe; setValue is NOT)
});

Other Java client APIs worth knowing

socket.id();                    // null until connected
socket.connected();             // boolean
socket.isActive();              // will it reconnect automatically?
socket.off("chat:message");     // remove all listeners of an event
socket.off("chat:message", listener); // remove one (keep a reference to the Emitter.Listener!)
socket.off();                   // remove ALL listeners
socket.disconnect();            // no auto-reconnect after this
socket.io().on(Manager.EVENT_RECONNECT_ATTEMPT, args -> {});   // Manager-level events
socket.io().reconnection(false);

// other namespace on the same connection (multiplexing)
Socket admin = socket.io().socket("/admin");

// extra headers / query (Manager-level)
IO.Options.builder()
    .setExtraHeaders(Collections.singletonMap("X-App-Version", Collections.singletonList("5.2.0")))
    .setQuery("platform=android")
    .build();

Note the difference between setAuth(...) (sent in the Socket.IO CONNECT packet — recommended for tokens) and setQuery(...) / setExtraHeaders(...) (HTTP-level; with WebSocket on Android, headers can be sent, unlike in browsers).

16.4 Android — Kotlin (same library, idiomatic usage)

Kotlin uses the same io.socket:socket.io-client library. A production-style wrapper with coroutines/Flow:

import io.socket.client.AckWithTimeout
import io.socket.client.IO
import io.socket.client.Socket
import kotlinx.coroutines.channels.awaitClose
import kotlinx.coroutines.flow.Flow
import kotlinx.coroutines.flow.MutableStateFlow
import kotlinx.coroutines.flow.StateFlow
import kotlinx.coroutines.flow.callbackFlow
import kotlinx.coroutines.suspendCancellableCoroutine
import org.json.JSONObject
import java.net.URI
import kotlin.coroutines.resume
import kotlin.coroutines.resumeWithException

enum class ConnState { CONNECTING, CONNECTED, DISCONNECTED }

class RealtimeClient(url: String, private val tokenProvider: () -> String) {

    // The client keeps a reference to THIS map and reads it on every connection attempt,
    // so updating it before connect() sends the fresh token (verified with client 2.1.2).
    private val auth = HashMap<String, String>()

    private val socket: Socket = IO.socket(
        URI.create(url),
        IO.Options.builder()
            .setAuth(auth)
            .setTransports(arrayOf("websocket"))
            .setReconnectionDelayMax(10_000)
            .build()
    )

    private val _state = MutableStateFlow(ConnState.DISCONNECTED)
    val state: StateFlow<ConnState> = _state

    init {
        socket.on(Socket.EVENT_CONNECT) { _state.value = ConnState.CONNECTED }
        socket.on(Socket.EVENT_DISCONNECT) { _state.value = ConnState.DISCONNECTED }
        socket.on(Socket.EVENT_CONNECT_ERROR) { args ->
            _state.value = ConnState.DISCONNECTED
            if (!socket.isActive) {
                // rejected by server middleware → refresh the token and try again (or log out)
            }
        }
    }

    fun connect() {
        // refresh auth before EVERY connect so new connections use the latest token
        auth["token"] = tokenProvider()
        _state.value = ConnState.CONNECTING
        socket.connect()
    }

    fun disconnect() = socket.disconnect()

    /** Events as a cold Flow: listener added on collect, removed on cancel (no leaks). */
    fun events(name: String): Flow<JSONObject> = callbackFlow {
        val listener = io.socket.emitter.Emitter.Listener { args -> trySend(args[0] as JSONObject) }
        socket.on(name, listener)
        awaitClose { socket.off(name, listener) }
    }

    /** Request/response with timeout, as a suspend function. */
    suspend fun request(event: String, payload: JSONObject, timeoutMs: Long = 5_000): Any? =
        suspendCancellableCoroutine { cont ->
            socket.emit(event, arrayOf(payload), object : AckWithTimeout(timeoutMs) {
                override fun onSuccess(vararg args: Any?) { cont.resume(args.firstOrNull()) }
                override fun onTimeout() { cont.resumeWithException(RuntimeException("ack timeout: $event")) }
            })
        }
}

Refreshing the token (verified): Socket's own auth field is private, so you can't assign a new map. But the socket stores a reference to the map you passed to setAuth(...) and serializes it on every CONNECT. Keep that map in a field and update it before calling connect() — a test run printed token-1 for the first connection and token-2 for the second after auth.put("token", "token-2"). Note that automatic reconnections reuse whatever the map contains at that moment, so update it as soon as you refresh the token.

Usage in a ViewModel:

class ChatViewModel(private val rt: RealtimeClient) : ViewModel() {
    val messages = rt.events("chat:message")
        .map { Message.fromJson(it) }
        .shareIn(viewModelScope, SharingStarted.WhileSubscribed(5_000))

    fun send(text: String) = viewModelScope.launch {
        try {
            rt.request("message:send", JSONObject().put("text", text).put("clientMsgId", UUID.randomUUID().toString()))
        } catch (e: Exception) { /* show retry */ }
    }
}

Collecting a Flow on the main dispatcher solves the threading problem automatically (trySend is thread-safe, collection happens where you collect).

Lifecycle on Android

  • Where to own the socket: one instance for the whole app (Application-scoped singleton / DI @Singleton), not per Activity — otherwise rotations create new connections and duplicate listeners.
  • Foreground/background: observe ProcessLifecycleOwner:
ProcessLifecycleOwner.get().lifecycle.addObserver(object : DefaultLifecycleObserver {
    override fun onStart(owner: LifecycleOwner) = rt.connect()     // app visible
    override fun onStop(owner: LifecycleOwner) = rt.disconnect()   // app in background
})

Keeping a socket open in the background drains battery and Android (Doze, App Standby) will cut network access anyway. - Background delivery: use Firebase Cloud Messaging (FCM). A Socket.IO connection is not a substitute for push notifications. If you truly need a long-lived connection while in background (e.g. a ride in progress), use a foreground service with a visible notification — and expect the OS to still be strict. - Network changes: register a ConnectivityManager.NetworkCallback; on onAvailable call socket.connect() if not connected (faster than waiting for the back-off). - ProGuard/R8: the library works with R8; if you use reflection-based JSON mapping on top, keep your model classes.

16.5 iOS — Swift (socket.io-client-swift)

Installation

Swift Package Manager (Xcode → File → Add Packages), or Package.swift:

.package(url: "https://github.com/socketio/socket.io-client-swift", .upToNextMinor(from: "16.1.1"))
// target dependency: "SocketIO"

CocoaPods: pod 'Socket.IO-Client-Swift', '~> 16.1.1'. The library depends on Starscream (WebSocket implementation).

Core concepts

  • SocketManager = the connection (like the JS Manager): one per server URL. You must keep a strong reference to it (e.g. a property of a long-lived service). If the manager is deallocated, the socket silently disconnects — the most common iOS bug.
  • SocketIOClient = one namespace socket: manager.defaultSocket (namespace /) or manager.socket(forNamespace: "/admin").

A complete example

import SocketIO

final class RealtimeService {
    static let shared = RealtimeService()

    // keep STRONG references for the lifetime of the app
    private let manager: SocketManager
    private let socket: SocketIOClient

    private init() {
        manager = SocketManager(
            socketURL: URL(string: "https://api.example.com")!,
            config: [
                .log(false),
                .compress,
                .forceWebsockets(true),     // skip long-polling (see 16.6)
                .reconnects(true),
                .reconnectAttempts(-1),     // -1 = never give up
                .reconnectWait(1),          // seconds (min delay)
                .reconnectWaitMax(10),      // seconds (max delay)
                .randomizationFactor(0.5),
                .extraHeaders(["X-App-Version": "5.2.0"]),
                .connectParams(["platform": "ios"]),   // query string parameters
            ]
        )
        socket = manager.defaultSocket
        registerHandlers()
    }

    private func registerHandlers() {
        socket.on(clientEvent: .connect) { data, ack in
            print("connected", self.socket.sid ?? "")
        }
        socket.on(clientEvent: .disconnect) { data, ack in
            print("disconnected", data)
        }
        socket.on(clientEvent: .error) { data, ack in
            // connection errors, including server middleware rejections (connect_error)
            print("error", data)
        }
        socket.on(clientEvent: .reconnectAttempt) { data, ack in
            print("reconnect attempt", data)
        }

        socket.on("chat:message") { data, ack in
            guard let msg = data.first as? [String: Any], let text = msg["text"] as? String else { return }
            print("message:", text)
        }

        // server -> client with acknowledgement
        socket.on("question") { data, ack in
            ack.with("from swift")
        }
    }

    func connect(token: String) {
        // the payload becomes socket.handshake.auth on the server
        socket.connect(withPayload: ["token": token])
    }

    func disconnect() { socket.disconnect() }

    func send(text: String, conversationId: String) {
        socket.emitWithAck("message:send", ["conversationId": conversationId, "text": text])
            .timingOut(after: 5) { data in
                if data.first as? String ?? "passed" == SocketAckStatus.noAck {
                    print("server did not answer within 5 s")   // timeout
                    return
                }
                print("ack:", data)
            }
    }
}

Facts to remember (verified in the library source):

JS concept Swift API
io(url, opts) SocketManager(socketURL:config:) + manager.defaultSocket
auth option socket.connect(withPayload: [...])
query .connectParams([...])
transports: ["websocket"] .forceWebsockets(true) (.forcePolling(true) for polling only)
reconnectionDelay/Max .reconnectWait(sec) / .reconnectWaitMax(sec) — seconds, not ms
reconnectionAttempts .reconnectAttempts(n) (-1 = infinite)
forceNew .forceNew(true)
path .path("/socket.io/")
v2 server .version(.two) (default is .three = Socket.IO 3/4)
Self-signed cert (dev) .selfSigned(true); certificate pinning: .security(...)
socket.on("connect") socket.on(clientEvent: .connect)
Client events .connect, .disconnect, .error, .ping, .pong, .reconnect, .reconnectAttempt, .statusChange, .websocketUpgrade
emit with ack socket.emitWithAck("e", items).timingOut(after: seconds) { data in } — timeout detected by comparing with SocketAckStatus.noAck
reply to an ack ack.with(items...)
other namespace manager.socket(forNamespace: "/admin")
socket.id socket.sid

No offline buffering in the Swift client

Unlike the JavaScript and Java clients, the Swift client does not buffer emits while disconnected: emit on a socket that isn't connected triggers the .error client event with "Tried emitting when not connected" and the event is dropped (from SocketIOClient.swift). Check socket.status == .connected before emitting, and keep important messages in your own outbox (Chapter 21) until they're acknowledged.

Also: a server middleware rejection (CONNECT_ERROR) arrives as the "error" event on the Swift socket, with the error payload as data.

Threading on iOS

By default handlers are called on the main queue (handleQueue defaults to DispatchQueue.main), so you can update UIKit/SwiftUI directly. For heavy processing, set .handleQueue(someBackgroundQueue) and hop back to main for UI.

SwiftUI integration

@MainActor
final class ChatStore: ObservableObject {
    @Published var messages: [String] = []
    @Published var connected = false
    private let manager = SocketManager(socketURL: URL(string: "https://api.example.com")!,
                                        config: [.forceWebsockets(true)])
    private var socket: SocketIOClient { manager.defaultSocket }

    init() {
        socket.on(clientEvent: .connect) { [weak self] _, _ in self?.connected = true }
        socket.on(clientEvent: .disconnect) { [weak self] _, _ in self?.connected = false }
        socket.on("chat:message") { [weak self] data, _ in
            if let m = data.first as? [String: Any], let t = m["text"] as? String { self?.messages.append(t) }
        }
    }
    func start(token: String) { socket.connect(withPayload: ["token": token]) }
}

Use [weak self] in handlers to avoid retain cycles (the socket holds the closures; the store holds the socket).

iOS lifecycle

  • iOS suspends apps a few seconds after they go to background; the connection dies. Observe scenePhase (SwiftUI) or UIApplication.didEnterBackgroundNotification and disconnect cleanly; reconnect on .active.
  • Use APNs push notifications for background delivery.
  • App Transport Security (ATS) blocks plain http:// by default — use HTTPS in production; for local dev add an ATS exception only for your dev host.
  • Simulator can reach your Mac's localhost directly; a real device needs your Mac's LAN IP.

16.6 Why websocket-only is common on mobile

  • Mobile apps control their networking stack (no corporate proxies inside the app as with enterprise browsers), and WebSocket is reliable on 4G/5G/Wi-Fi.
  • It removes the need for sticky sessions on your load balancer.
  • Fewer HTTP requests → less battery and data.
  • On iOS/Android, polling implementations differ subtly from browsers' XHR; WebSocket-only avoids a class of issues.

Keep polling enabled only if your users are known to be behind WebSocket-hostile networks (some enterprise MDM VPNs).

16.7 Flutter / Dart (socket_io_client)

Installation

# pubspec.yaml
dependencies:
  socket_io_client: ^3.1.6   # for Socket.IO server 4.7+; see the compatibility table

A complete example

import 'package:socket_io_client/socket_io_client.dart' as IO;

class RealtimeService {
  late final IO.Socket socket;

  RealtimeService(String url, String Function() tokenProvider) {
    socket = IO.io(
      url,
      IO.OptionBuilder()
          .setTransports(['websocket'])      // REQUIRED for Flutter / Dart VM (see note)
          .disableAutoConnect()              // connect explicitly
          .setAuth({'token': tokenProvider()})
          .setReconnectionDelay(1000)
          .setReconnectionDelayMax(5000)
          .setExtraHeaders({'x-app-version': '5.2.0'})
          .build(),
    );

    socket.onConnect((_) => print('connected ${socket.id}'));
    socket.onConnectError((err) => print('connect_error $err'));
    socket.onDisconnect((reason) => print('disconnected $reason'));

    socket.on('chat:message', (data) {
      final text = (data as Map)['text'];
      print('message: $text');
    });

    // server -> client with ack. When an event carries MORE than one value (here: the
    // payload + the ack function), the handler receives ONE List; the ack is its last item.
    socket.on('question', (data) {
      final list = data as List;
      final question = list.first;
      final ack = list.last as Function;
      ack('from dart');     // callable once; later calls are ignored
    });
  }

  void connect() => socket.connect();     // don't call connect() if autoConnect is enabled
  void disconnect() => socket.disconnect();

  // emit with ack (callback)
  void send(String text) {
    socket.emitWithAck('message:send', {'text': text}, ack: (res) {
      print('ack: $res');
    });
  }

  // emit with ack (Future) + timeout
  Future<dynamic> request(String event, dynamic data) {
    return socket.timeout(5000).emitWithAckAsync(event, data);
  }

  void dispose() {
    socket.dispose();   // removes listeners and closes — call in your widget/service dispose
  }
}

Facts (verified in the package source, v3.1.6):

  • OptionBuilder methods include setTransports, setAuth (and setAuthFn for a function computed per connection), setQuery, setPath, setExtraHeaders, enableAutoConnect/disableAutoConnect, enableReconnection/disableReconnection, setReconnectionAttempts, setReconnectionDelay, setReconnectionDelayMax, setRandomizationFactor, setTimeout, enableForceNew, enableMultiplex/disableMultiplex, enableWithCredentials, setRememberUpgrade, setUpgrade, setParser, enableAddTrailingSlash/disableAddTrailingSlash.
  • emitWithAck(event, data, {ack}) and emitWithAckAsync(event, data) (a Future); socket.timeout(ms) exists; with a timeout, the Future completes with an error when the server doesn't answer.
  • Client retries (at-least-once) are supported as in the JS client.
  • socket.active mirrors the JS property.
  • setTransports(['websocket']) is required on Flutter mobile/desktop (the README states it); the polling transport is meant for Flutter Web.
  • Receiving multiple arguments: the Dart client calls your handler with a single value when the event has one argument, but with a List of all arguments when it has more than one (including the ack function). So a server socket.emit("move", x, y) arrives as [x, y] in Dart. Prefer a single object payload on the server — it keeps every platform simple.
  • Data shape: if you emit a List, its items are spread as separate arguments (the source does sendData.addAll(data) for iterables). To send a list as one argument, wrap it: socket.emit('items', [myList]) or send a Map.

Flutter lifecycle

  • Own the socket in a long-lived service (Provider/Riverpod/GetIt), not in a widget's build.
  • If a StatefulWidget registers handlers, remove them in dispose() (socket.off('event', handler)), or use socket.dispose() when the whole service goes away.
  • Use WidgetsBindingObserver.didChangeAppLifecycleState to disconnect in paused and reconnect in resumed.
  • Background delivery → FCM/APNs (e.g. firebase_messaging).

16.8 Mobile-specific production checklist

  • [ ] Client library version compatible with server version (16.2 table).
  • [ ] HTTPS/WSS (ATS on iOS, cleartext policy on Android).
  • [ ] One socket instance per app (singleton/DI); strong reference to SocketManager on iOS.
  • [ ] Handlers update UI on the main thread (Android) / handlers don't create retain cycles (iOS).
  • [ ] Listeners removed when screens are destroyed (or use Flows/Observables with teardown).
  • [ ] Token passed via auth (setAuth, connect(withPayload:)), refreshed before reconnecting.
  • [ ] Disconnect on background, reconnect on foreground; push notifications for background delivery.
  • [ ] Re-sync missed data on every connect (last message id) — mobile networks drop often.
  • [ ] websocket-only transport unless polling is genuinely needed.
  • [ ] Send app version in auth/headers; server rejects too-old clients with a clear error.
  • [ ] Test on real devices: airplane mode on/off, Wi-Fi↔cellular switch, lock screen, low-power mode.

16.9 Interview questions for this chapter

Q1. [M] Which Java client version works with a Socket.IO v4 server? The 2.x line (latest 2.1.2). 1.x speaks the v2 protocol.

Q2. [M] My Android app crashes with "Only the original thread that created a view hierarchy can touch its views" when a message arrives. Why? Socket.IO Java callbacks run on the client's event thread, not the main thread. Post to the UI thread (runOnUiThread, Handler(Looper.getMainLooper()), LiveData.postValue, or collect a Flow on the main dispatcher).

Q3. [M] My iOS socket disconnects immediately after connecting, with no error. Why? Most often the SocketManager was a local variable and got deallocated. Keep a strong reference (property of a long-lived object).

Q4. [M] How do you send an auth token from Swift / Java / Dart? Swift: socket.connect(withPayload: ["token": t]). Java: IO.Options.builder().setAuth(map). Dart: OptionBuilder().setAuth({...}) or setAuthFn. All land in socket.handshake.auth on the server.

Q5. [M] How do you do an ack with timeout on each platform? Java: AckWithTimeout(ms) with onSuccess/onTimeout. Swift: emitWithAck(...).timingOut(after: seconds) { data in } and check SocketAckStatus.noAck. Dart: socket.timeout(ms).emitWithAckAsync(...). JS: socket.timeout(ms).emitWithAck(...).

Q6. [S] How do mobile apps get messages while in the background? They don't, through Socket.IO — the OS suspends the app. Use FCM/APNs push, then sync over the socket/HTTP when the app returns to the foreground.

Q7. [M] Why do many teams use WebSocket-only transport on mobile? Reliability, no sticky sessions needed, fewer requests (battery), and polling implementations on mobile platforms are less battle-tested than browsers'.

Q8. [M] Why is Swift .reconnectWait(1) different from JS reconnectionDelay: 1000? Same meaning, different unit — Swift uses seconds, JS/Java use milliseconds.

Q9. [M] A Flutter client connects in Flutter Web but not on Android. What's the first thing to check? setTransports(['websocket']) (required on Flutter mobile per the package docs), then the package/server version compatibility table and cleartext/HTTPS settings.

Next: Chapter 17 — Servers and clients in other languages

Socket.IO Servers and Clients in Other Languages (Python, Go, Rust, Java)

Goal: run Socket.IO servers outside Node.js — Python (FastAPI/ASGI, aiohttp, Flask-SocketIO), Go, Rust and Java — and know exactly what is the same, what differs, and what breaks when you mix languages.

How this chapter was checked: every server below was built and run, and a real Node.js socket.io-client 4.8.4 connected to it and exercised authentication rejection, events, acknowledgements (both directions where supported) and room broadcasts excluding the sender. Versions used: python-socketio 5.17.0 / python-engineio 4.14.0 / Flask-SocketIO 5.7.0, Go github.com/zishang520/socket.io/servers/socket/v3 v3.0.6, Rust socketioxide 0.18.7 with axum 0.8, Java netty-socketio 2.0.13.

17.1 ELI5

Socket.IO is a language (a protocol). The Node.js library is the most famous speaker, but people have written speakers in Python, Go, Rust, Java and more. As long as both sides speak the same version of the language (Engine.IO v4 + Socket.IO v5 for modern clients), a browser using socket.io-client can't tell which language the server is written in.

What does not travel between languages: the multi-server plumbing. A Python server's Redis "message queue" and the Node Redis adapter speak different internal formats — you can't put Python and Node servers in the same cluster and expect broadcasts to cross.

17.2 Choosing a server implementation

Language Library Protocol (modern clients) Scaling across servers Notes
Node.js socket.io ✅ reference implementation Adapters (Redis, Streams, Mongo, Postgres, …) Everything in this book
Python python-socketio (+ python-engineio) ✅ "Client managers": Redis, Kafka, RabbitMQ/AioPika, Kombu, ZeroMQ asyncio (ASGI/aiohttp) and WSGI (Flask, threading/eventlet/gevent)
Python/Flask Flask-SocketIO ✅ (built on python-socketio) Same managers via message_queue= Flask-style decorators, request context
Go github.com/zishang520/socket.io (v3 module path …/servers/socket/v3) ✅ (Socket.IO v4+ protocol) Adapters (see its docs) Close port of the Node API: On, Emit, To, Use, Of
Go googollee/go-socket.io ⚠️ historically v2-era protocol — Check protocol support before choosing
Rust socketioxide ✅ v5 (v4 behind a feature flag) socketioxide-redis, -mongodb, -postgres Tower layer; axum/hyper integration; extractors
Java netty-socketio ✅ claims client 1.x–4.x (verified events/acks/rooms with a 4.8.4 client) Redisson / Hazelcast store Netty-based; see caveats in 17.6
Deno official Deno port ✅ — See Chapter 20

Choose based on your team's language, and check: protocol version, acknowledgement support, binary support, namespaces, scaling story, maintenance activity.

17.3 Python — python-socketio

Install

pip install python-socketio uvicorn fastapi        # asyncio + ASGI
pip install python-socketio aiohttp                # asyncio + aiohttp
pip install flask flask-socketio simple-websocket  # Flask (threading mode with WebSocket support)
pip install redis                                  # for the Redis manager

Concepts mapped from Node.js

Node.js python-socketio
new Server(opts) socketio.AsyncServer(async_mode="asgi", ...) (asyncio) or socketio.Server(...) (WSGI/threads)
io.on("connection", socket => …) @sio.event async def connect(sid, environ, auth)
socket object a sid string — every API takes the sid
socket.data await sio.save_session(sid, {...}) / await sio.get_session(sid) / async with sio.session(sid) as s:
next(new Error("x")) in middleware raise socketio.exceptions.ConnectionRefusedError("x") (or return False) in connect
socket.on("e", (data, ack) => ack(x)) @sio.on("e") async def h(sid, data): return x — the return value is the ack
socket.emit("e", data) await sio.emit("e", data, to=sid)
io.to(room).emit await sio.emit("e", data, room=room) (or to=room)
socket.to(room).emit (exclude sender) await sio.emit("e", data, room=room, skip_sid=sid)
socket.join(room) / leave await sio.enter_room(sid, room) / await sio.leave_room(sid, room)
socket.timeout(ms).emitWithAck(...) await sio.call("e", data, to=sid, timeout=5) (seconds)
emit with ack callback await sio.emit("e", data, to=sid, callback=fn)
socket.disconnect() await sio.disconnect(sid)
namespaces namespace="/admin" argument everywhere, or class-based socketio.AsyncNamespace
pingInterval: 25000 (ms) ping_interval=25 (seconds)
maxHttpBufferSize max_http_buffer_size=1000000
cors: { origin } cors_allowed_origins=[...] ("*" for all; [] to disable checks)
adapter (Redis) client_manager=socketio.AsyncRedisManager(url)
emitter (Redis) socketio.AsyncRedisManager(url, write_only=True)

Defaults (from the python-engineio 4.14 constructor): ping_interval=25, ping_timeout=20, max_http_buffer_size=1000000, allow_upgrades=True, http_compression=True, compression_threshold=1024, cors_credentials=True, async_handlers=True — the same values as Node's defaults, but in seconds.

Ordering difference: with async_handlers=True (the default), python-socketio runs each incoming event in its own task, so two events from the same client can be processed concurrently and finish out of order — even though they arrived in order. If order matters, set async_handlers=False (events of a client are then processed one after another) or serialize per key yourself.

FastAPI + Socket.IO on one port (ASGI) — verified

# server.py
import socketio
from fastapi import FastAPI

sio = socketio.AsyncServer(async_mode="asgi", cors_allowed_origins=["http://localhost:5173"])
api = FastAPI()

@api.get("/health")
async def health():
    return {"ok": True}

# Socket.IO handles /socket.io/, everything else goes to FastAPI
app = socketio.ASGIApp(sio, other_asgi_app=api)

@sio.event
async def connect(sid, environ, auth):
    if not auth or auth.get("token") != "good":
        raise socketio.exceptions.ConnectionRefusedError("unauthorized")   # → client connect_error
    await sio.save_session(sid, {"user_id": "u1"})                         # ≈ socket.data
    await sio.enter_room(sid, "user:u1")                                   # per-user room
    await sio.emit("welcome", {"sid": sid}, to=sid)

@sio.on("sum")
async def sum_(sid, data):
    return data["a"] + data["b"]          # return value = acknowledgement

@sio.on("join")
async def join(sid, room):
    await sio.enter_room(sid, room)
    await sio.emit("joined", {"room": room}, room=room, skip_sid=sid)     # room, excluding sender
    return {"ok": True}

@sio.on("ask-client")
async def ask_client(sid):
    answer = await sio.call("question", "2+2?", to=sid, timeout=5)     # server -> client with ack
    return {"client_said": answer}

@sio.event
async def disconnect(sid, reason):
    print("disconnect", sid, reason)

Run: uvicorn server:app --port 8765.

What the Node.js client observed (real output):

bad token -> unauthorized active: false        ← middleware-style rejection, no auto-retry
welcome string
sum ack: 5
join ack: { ok: true }
call: { client_said: 4 }                       ← server-initiated ack round trip
transport: websocket                           ← upgraded from polling
(server) disconnect GE5M_tNyD_-xOgj_AAAD client disconnect

Note the disconnect(sid, reason) signature: recent python-socketio versions pass the reason (e.g. "client disconnect", "server disconnect", "ping timeout", "transport close"); older versions call disconnect(sid) only.

Class-based namespaces

class ChatNamespace(socketio.AsyncNamespace):
    async def on_connect(self, sid, environ, auth):
        await self.enter_room(sid, "lobby")

    async def on_message(self, sid, data):          # handles the "message" event
        await self.emit("message", data, room="lobby", skip_sid=sid)
        return {"ok": True}

    async def on_disconnect(self, sid, reason):
        pass

sio.register_namespace(ChatNamespace("/chat"))

Method name = on_ + event name. This is the Python equivalent of feature-based handler modules.

aiohttp

from aiohttp import web
import socketio

sio = socketio.AsyncServer(async_mode="aiohttp")
app = web.Application()
sio.attach(app)          # serves /socket.io/ on the aiohttp app

if __name__ == "__main__":
    web.run_app(app, port=8000)

Flask-SocketIO — verified

from flask import Flask, request
from flask_socketio import SocketIO, emit, join_room, ConnectionRefusedError

app = Flask(__name__)
socketio = SocketIO(app, cors_allowed_origins=["http://localhost:5173"])

@socketio.on("connect")
def on_connect(auth):
    if not auth or auth.get("token") != "good":
        raise ConnectionRefusedError("unauthorized")
    join_room("lobby")                              # uses request.sid implicitly
    emit("welcome", {"sid": request.sid})           # to this client

@socketio.on("chat")
def on_chat(data):
    emit("chat", data, to="lobby", include_self=False)   # room broadcast excluding sender
    return {"ok": True}                                   # ack

if __name__ == "__main__":
    socketio.run(app, port=8780, allow_unsafe_werkzeug=True)  # dev server only

Observed with two Node clients: welcome received, the second client got { text: 'hi' }, and the sender received the ack { ok: true }.

Flask-SocketIO specifics: - request.sid is the current client's sid; emit/send/join_room without to= target the current client (context-aware, like Flask's request). - include_self=False ≈ Node's socket.to(room) / socket.broadcast. - Production async modes: threading (with simple-websocket for WebSocket), eventlet or gevent (with monkey patching). The Werkzeug dev server (allow_unsafe_werkzeug=True) is for development only — use gunicorn with an appropriate worker (e.g. gunicorn -k eventlet -w 1 app:app or --threads for threading mode). One worker process per gunicorn instance: multiple workers in one gunicorn need sticky sessions, which gunicorn's load balancing can't do — run several single-worker instances behind nginx with sticky sessions + a message_queue instead. - Scale-out: SocketIO(app, message_queue="redis://...").

Scaling Python servers with Redis — verified

import socketio

mgr = socketio.AsyncRedisManager("redis://localhost:6379/0")
sio = socketio.AsyncServer(async_mode="asgi", client_manager=mgr)
app = socketio.ASGIApp(sio)

@sio.event
async def connect(sid, environ, auth):
    await sio.enter_room(sid, "news")

@sio.on("shout")
async def shout(sid, msg):
    await sio.emit("news", msg, room="news")    # reaches clients on ALL servers

Test: two uvicorn processes on ports 8771 and 8772, client A on 8771 emits shout, client B on 8772 printed client on server B got: hello from server A.

Other managers: AsyncAioPikaManager (RabbitMQ), KafkaManager, KombuManager, ZmqManager (and sync versions RedisManager etc.). As in Node, sticky sessions are required when polling is enabled.

Emitting from a worker process (write-only manager) — verified

import asyncio, socketio

async def main():
    external = socketio.AsyncRedisManager("redis://localhost:6379/0", write_only=True)
    await external.emit("news", "hello from a worker process", room="news")

asyncio.run(main())

A Node client connected to a Python server received client got: hello from a worker process. This is the Python equivalent of @socket.io/redis-emitter. (Celery tasks commonly use this to push progress updates.)

The Python client

import asyncio, socketio

async def main():
    sio = socketio.AsyncClient(reconnection=True)

    @sio.event
    async def connect():
        print("connected", sio.sid)

    @sio.on("news")
    async def on_news(msg):
        print("got", msg)

    await sio.connect("https://api.example.com", auth={"token": "..."}, transports=["websocket"])
    result = await sio.call("sum", {"a": 1, "b": 2}, timeout=5)    # emit with ack
    await sio.emit("typing", {"conversationId": "c1"})
    await sio.wait()                                               # keep running

asyncio.run(main())

It works against Node servers too (the Python client and server both speak the standard protocol). There's also socketio.SimpleClient / AsyncSimpleClient for scripts: client.connect(url), client.emit(...), event = client.receive().

Python-specific pitfalls

  • Units: seconds everywhere (ping_interval, timeout), Node uses ms.
  • Blocking calls in async handlers (sync DB drivers, time.sleep, requests) block the event loop → ping timeouts. Use async libraries or asyncio.to_thread.
  • Monkey patching (eventlet/gevent) must happen before other imports.
  • cors_allowed_origins default (None) only allows the same origin as the request's host (and the X-Forwarded-Proto/Host variant behind a proxy); set it explicitly.
  • Origin checks apply to WebSocket too in Python — unlike Node. Tested with a raw WebSocket client sending a forged Origin:
Server Allowed origin Origin: https://evil.example
python-socketio (cors_allowed_origins=["http://localhost:5173"]) accepted rejected, HTTP 403
Node socket.io (cors: { origin: "http://localhost:5173" }) accepted accepted

So python-engineio gives you Cross-Site WebSocket Hijacking protection out of the box, while in Node you must add an allowRequest origin check yourself (Chapter 6.5). - Concurrent handler execution (async_handlers=True) → ordering surprises (see above). - Testing: socketio.AsyncClient against a server started in the test, or flask_socketio.SocketIOTestClient (socketio.test_client(app)) for Flask.

17.4 Go — github.com/zishang520/socket.io — verified

go get github.com/zishang520/socket.io/servers/socket/v3

package main

import (
    "log"
    "net/http"

    "github.com/zishang520/socket.io/servers/socket/v3"
)

func main() {
    server := socket.NewServer(nil, nil)

    // connection middleware: reject unauthenticated clients with a CONNECT_ERROR
    server.Use(func(client *socket.Socket, next func(*socket.ExtendedError)) {
        if token, _ := client.Handshake().Auth["token"].(string); token != "good" {
            next(socket.NewExtendedError("unauthorized", map[string]any{"code": "AUTH"}))
            return
        }
        next(nil)
    })

    server.On("connection", func(clients ...any) {
        client := clients[0].(*socket.Socket)
        client.Join("lobby")
        client.Emit("welcome", map[string]any{"sid": string(client.Id())})

        // the ack callback is the LAST argument
        client.On("sum", func(args ...any) {
            p := args[0].(map[string]any)
            ack := args[len(args)-1].(socket.Ack)
            ack([]any{p["a"].(float64) + p["b"].(float64)}, nil) // JSON numbers decode as float64
        })

        client.On("chat", func(args ...any) {
            client.To("lobby").Emit("chat", args[0]) // room, excluding sender
            if ack, ok := args[len(args)-1].(socket.Ack); ok {
                ack([]any{map[string]any{"ok": true}}, nil)
            }
        })

        client.On("disconnect", func(reason ...any) {
            log.Println("disconnect", client.Id(), reason[0])
        })
    })

    http.Handle("/socket.io/", server.ServeHandler(nil))
    log.Fatal(http.ListenAndServe(":8795", nil))
}

Real output with Node clients: bad -> unauthorized active: false, sum: 42, chat ack: { ok: true }, the other client received { text: 'hi go' }, the sender did not; server logged disconnect … client namespace disconnect.

Go-specific notes: - Handlers are variadic func(args ...any); you type-assert. JSON numbers arrive as float64; objects as map[string]any. - The ack is socket.Ack = func([]any, error); call it with a slice of response values. - API mirrors Node: server.Of("/admin", nil), server.To(room).Emit, client.Timeout(d), client.EmitWithAck, socket.DefaultServerOptions() with SetPingInterval, SetMaxHttpBufferSize, SetCors(&types.Cors{...}). - Integrates with net/http, fasthttp and Fiber (via adaptors) through server.ServeHandler(opts). - DEBUG=socket.io* enables logs, like Node.

17.5 Rust — socketioxide — verified

# Cargo.toml
[dependencies]
socketioxide = { version = "0.18", features = ["state"] }
axum = "0.8"
tokio = { version = "1", features = ["full"] }
serde = { version = "1", features = ["derive"] }
serde_json = "1"
anyhow = "1"

use axum::routing::get;
use serde::Deserialize;
use serde_json::{json, Value};
use socketioxide::{
    extract::{AckSender, Data, SocketRef, TryData},
    handler::ConnectHandler,
    SocketIo,
};

#[derive(Debug, Deserialize)]
struct Auth { token: String }

#[derive(Debug, Deserialize)]
struct Sum { a: i64, b: i64 }

// connect middleware: Err(...) rejects the connection with a CONNECT_ERROR (message = Display)
async fn auth_middleware(TryData(auth): TryData<Auth>) -> Result<(), anyhow::Error> {
    match auth {
        Ok(a) if a.token == "good" => Ok(()),
        _ => Err(anyhow::anyhow!("unauthorized")),
    }
}

async fn on_connect(socket: SocketRef) {
    socket.join("lobby");
    socket.emit("welcome", &json!({ "sid": socket.id.to_string() })).ok();

    socket.on("sum", async |Data(p): Data<Sum>, ack: AckSender| {
        ack.send(&(p.a + p.b)).ok();
    });

    socket.on("chat", async |s: SocketRef, Data(msg): Data<Value>, ack: AckSender| {
        s.to("lobby").emit("chat", &msg).await.ok();   // to room, excluding sender
        ack.send(&json!({ "ok": true })).ok();
    });

    socket.on_disconnect(async |s: SocketRef| {
        println!("disconnected {}", s.id);
    });
}

#[tokio::main]
async fn main() -> Result<(), Box<dyn std::error::Error>> {
    let (layer, io) = SocketIo::new_layer();
    io.ns("/", on_connect.with(auth_middleware));      // namespace + connect middleware

    let app = axum::Router::new()
        .route("/health", get(|| async { "ok" }))
        .layer(layer);                                  // Socket.IO is a Tower layer

    let listener = tokio::net::TcpListener::bind("0.0.0.0:8790").await?;
    axum::serve(listener, app).await?;
    Ok(())
}

Real output with Node clients: bad -> unauthorized active: false, sum: 42, chat ack: { ok: true }, the other client got { text: 'hi rust' }, the sender didn't.

Rust-specific notes: - Extractors (axum-style): SocketRef, Data<T> (deserialized payload), TryData<T> (deserialization result, lets you handle bad payloads), AckSender, State<T> (shared state, state feature), Extension, HttpExtension. - Payload types are your own serde structs → type-checked deserialization of client data, a nice security property (malformed payloads fail to deserialize). - In 0.18, connect middlewares must be async functions returning Result<(), E> where E: Display (a synchronous fn fails to compile with "Invalid ConnectMiddleware"). - Room broadcasts are async (.emit(...).await) because they may go through an adapter. - Store per-socket data with socket.extensions.insert(...). - Scaling adapters: socketioxide-redis, socketioxide-mongodb, socketioxide-postgres; emitting from other processes: socketioxide-emitter. - Supports protocol v5 by default and the old v4 protocol (v2 JS clients) behind the v4 feature flag.

17.6 Java — netty-socketio (server) — verified with caveats

<dependency>
  <groupId>com.corundumstudio.socketio</groupId>
  <artifactId>netty-socketio</artifactId>
  <version>2.0.13</version>
</dependency>

import com.corundumstudio.socketio.AuthorizationResult;
import com.corundumstudio.socketio.Configuration;
import com.corundumstudio.socketio.SocketIOServer;
import java.util.Map;

Configuration config = new Configuration();
config.setHostname("0.0.0.0");
config.setPort(8810);
// runs on the HTTP handshake (before any namespace CONNECT): here, a query parameter check
config.setAuthorizationListener(data ->
    "web".equals(data.getSingleUrlParam("platform"))
        ? AuthorizationResult.SUCCESSFUL_AUTHORIZATION
        : AuthorizationResult.FAILED_AUTHORIZATION);

SocketIOServer server = new SocketIOServer(config);

server.addConnectListener(client -> client.joinRoom("lobby"));

server.addEventListener("sum", Map.class, (client, data, ackRequest) -> {
    int a = ((Number) data.get("a")).intValue(), b = ((Number) data.get("b")).intValue();
    if (ackRequest.isAckRequested()) ackRequest.sendAckData(a + b);
});

server.addEventListener("chat", Map.class, (client, data, ackRequest) -> {
    server.getRoomOperations("lobby").sendEvent("chat", client, data);  // `client` is excluded
    ackRequest.sendAckData(Map.of("ok", true));
});

server.addDisconnectListener(client -> { /* ... */ });
server.start();

Observed with Node socket.io-client 4.8.4: sum: 42, chat ack: { ok: true }, room broadcast reached the other client and excluded the sender. Caveats seen in the test:

  1. The authorization listener runs at the HTTP handshake, so a rejection reaches the JS client as a transport failure (connect_error: "xhr poll error") — not as a middleware-style connect_error with your message, and socket.active stays true (the client keeps retrying). Plan your client error handling accordingly.
  2. In this test, client.getHandshakeData().getAuthToken() returned null for the main namespace even though the client sent an auth payload. Pass tokens in the query string (over HTTPS) or verify auth support for your exact version and namespace before relying on it.

Scaling: netty-socketio supports Redisson (Redis) and Hazelcast "stores" for multi-node setups — internal to netty-socketio, not compatible with Node adapters.

For Spring Boot teams, alternatives include running a Node/Rust/Go Socket.IO gateway next to the Java services, or using Spring's native WebSocket/STOMP support when you control both clients and server.

17.7 Mixing languages — rules that matter

  1. Clients ↔ servers interoperate across languages as long as protocol versions match (all verified above with the Node client).
  2. Servers of different languages cannot share a cluster. Node's Redis adapter, python-socketio's Redis manager, socketioxide-redis and netty-socketio's Redisson store all use their own message formats/channels. Pick one server implementation per cluster.
  3. Emitters are implementation-specific too: use @socket.io/redis-emitter with Node adapter clusters, AsyncRedisManager(write_only=True) with Python clusters, etc. For a polyglot backend, put a message broker (Kafka/RabbitMQ/SQS) in front of the realtime gateway and let any service publish domain events there.
  4. Units differ: Node/Java/JS clients use milliseconds; Python and Swift use seconds.
  5. Data types differ: JSON numbers become float64 in Go, Number/Integer in Java, int/float in Python; dates are strings everywhere (unless you use a custom parser — Chapter 19).
  6. Ack styles differ: return value (Python, NestJS), AckSender (Rust), socket.Ack last arg (Go), AckRequest (netty-socketio), callback last arg (Node).

17.8 Interview questions for this chapter

Q1. [M] Can a Python Socket.IO server serve a React app using socket.io-client? Yes — python-socketio implements the same protocol; the client can't tell the difference. Verified: auth rejection, acks, rooms, server→client calls, upgrade to WebSocket.

Q2. [M] How do you acknowledge an event in python-socketio? Return a value from the handler; the client's ack callback receives it. For server→client acks use await sio.call(event, data, to=sid, timeout=s).

Q3. [S] Can I run Node.js and Python Socket.IO servers in the same cluster with Redis? No. Their Redis adapter/manager formats are different, so cross-server broadcasts won't be understood. Use one implementation per cluster, and a broker for polyglot event publishing.

Q4. [M] What's the python-socketio equivalent of socket.to(room).emit()? await sio.emit(event, data, room=room, skip_sid=sid); in Flask-SocketIO emit(event, data, to=room, include_self=False).

Q5. [S] Why might events from one client be processed out of order in python-socketio? async_handlers=True (default) runs each event in its own task. Set it to False or serialize per key when order matters.

Q6. [M] How do you push from a Celery worker to Socket.IO clients of a Python cluster? socketio.RedisManager(url, write_only=True).emit(...) (or the async variant) — the Python counterpart of @socket.io/redis-emitter.

Q7. [M] What's specific about Rust's socketioxide? Tower layer usable with axum/hyper; axum-like extractors (Data<T>, TryData<T>, AckSender, State<T>); typed payload deserialization via serde; async connect middlewares; Redis/Mongo/Postgres adapters.

Q8. [M] In the Go server, how do you get the ack callback? It's the last variadic argument, of type socket.Ack (func([]any, error)).

Next: Chapter 18 — WebTransport in practice

WebTransport in Practice (HTTP/3)

Goal: understand what WebTransport is, when it's worth it, and set it up end to end with Socket.IO — certificates, the HTTP/3 server, browser and Node clients, upgrades, deployment and debugging.

How this chapter was checked: the server and client code below was run with socket.io 4.8.4, @fails-components/webtransport 1.6.10 (+ its quiche HTTP/3 transport) and Node 22. Results: a WebTransport-only client connected and exchanged acks; a client using the default transport order connected over polling and upgraded directly to WebTransport. Browser behaviour is described from the WebTransport specification and browser documentation (not executed here — no browser in this environment).

18.1 ELI5

WebSocket is a walkie-talkie that runs over one rope (a TCP connection). If one message gets stuck on the rope (a lost network packet), every message behind it waits — even unrelated ones. That's called head-of-line blocking.

WebTransport runs over HTTP/3 (QUIC), which is like having many small ropes inside one connection: a stuck message only blocks its own rope. It can also send "throw-away" messages (datagrams) that are never retried. It connects faster too.

Socket.IO can use WebTransport as a third transport, next to polling and WebSocket.

18.2 What WebTransport brings (and what Socket.IO uses)

Property WebSocket WebTransport
Underlying protocol TCP (+ TLS) QUIC over UDP (TLS 1.3 built in)
Head-of-line blocking Yes (one TCP stream) Not across streams
Connection setup TCP + TLS handshakes Faster (QUIC combines them; 0-RTT resumption possible)
Network changes (Wi-Fi ↔ 4G) Connection breaks QUIC supports connection migration
Unreliable datagrams No Yes
Requires TLS Optional (ws://) Always (HTTPS only)
Proxy/firewall friendliness Good (TCP 443) UDP 443 is blocked on some networks

Socket.IO uses a WebTransport bidirectional stream as its transport (reliable, ordered — the same guarantees as WebSocket), so your application code doesn't change. The benefits you get are mostly faster connection setup and better behaviour on lossy or changing networks. Socket.IO's API does not expose unreliable datagrams.

Is it worth it? For most apps, WebSocket is fine. Consider WebTransport for mobile-heavy audiences on lossy networks, latency-sensitive interactive apps, or when you already run HTTP/3 infrastructure. Always keep WebSocket/polling as fallbacks — UDP may be blocked and browser support isn't universal (check current support tables, e.g. caniuse, before relying on it; Safari historically lagged behind Chromium and Firefox).

18.3 Requirements

  1. HTTPS with a valid certificate. Browsers refuse WebTransport without TLS. - In production: a normal CA-signed certificate for your domain. - In development: a self-signed certificate pinned by hash with the serverCertificateHashes option. The WebTransport spec restricts such certificates: ECDSA keys (e.g. P-256) and a validity of at most 14 days.
  2. An HTTP/3 server. Node.js has no built-in HTTP/3 server (as of Node 22), so you add one: @fails-components/webtransport (with its quiche-based transport package).
  3. UDP open on the port (typically 443) through firewalls, security groups and load balancers.
  4. "webtransport" added to the server's transports option (it is disabled by default on the server).

18.4 Step 1 — certificates

Development certificate (ECDSA P-256, 14 days), plus its SHA-256 fingerprint for pinning:

openssl req -new -x509 -nodes \
  -newkey ec -pkeyopt ec_paramgen_curve:prime256v1 \
  -keyout key.pem -out cert.pem -days 14 \
  -subj '/CN=localhost' -addext "subjectAltName=DNS:localhost,IP:127.0.0.1"

# SHA-256 of the DER-encoded certificate, as hex (used by serverCertificateHashes)
node -e 'const {X509Certificate,createHash}=require("crypto");const fs=require("fs");
const c=new X509Certificate(fs.readFileSync("cert.pem"));
console.log(createHash("sha256").update(c.raw).digest("hex"))' > cert.hash

Remember to regenerate before the 14 days expire (and update the pinned hash in clients).

18.5 Step 2 — the server (verified)

// wt-server.mjs
import { readFileSync } from "node:fs";
import { createServer } from "node:https";
import { Server } from "socket.io";
import { Http3Server } from "@fails-components/webtransport";

const key = readFileSync("./key.pem");
const cert = readFileSync("./cert.pem");

// 1) a classic HTTPS server for polling + websocket (TCP)
const httpsServer = createServer({ key, cert });
const io = new Server(httpsServer, {
  transports: ["polling", "websocket", "webtransport"],   // WebTransport must be enabled explicitly
});

io.on("connection", (socket) => {
  console.log("server: connected via", socket.conn.transport.name);
  socket.conn.on("upgrade", (t) => console.log("server: upgraded to", t.name));
  socket.on("ping-me", (msg, ack) => ack("pong: " + msg));
});

httpsServer.listen(3443);

// 2) an HTTP/3 server on the same port number — but UDP
const h3Server = new Http3Server({
  port: 3443,
  host: "0.0.0.0",
  secret: "change-me",   // used by QUIC for stateless tokens — use a real random secret
  cert,
  privKey: key,
});
h3Server.startServer();

// 3) hand every WebTransport session opened on /socket.io/ to Engine.IO
const stream = await h3Server.sessionStream("/socket.io/");
const reader = stream.getReader();
while (true) {
  const { done, value } = await reader.read();
  if (done) break;
  io.engine.onWebTransportSession(value);
}

Key points: - TCP 3443 (HTTPS: polling/WebSocket) and UDP 3443 (HTTP/3: WebTransport) can share the same port number because they are different protocols. - io.engine.onWebTransportSession(session) is the Engine.IO hook (added in engine.io 6.5, shipped with socket.io 4.7) that adopts a WebTransport session. - The session path must match the Socket.IO path (/socket.io/ by default).

18.6 Step 3 — clients

Browser

import { io } from "socket.io-client";

const socket = io("https://localhost:3443", {
  // default order is ["polling", "websocket", "webtransport"]; you can keep it
  transportOptions: {
    webtransport: {
      // only for self-signed dev certificates; omit in production with a CA-signed cert
      serverCertificateHashes: [{ algorithm: "sha-256", value: hexToBytes(CERT_HASH_HEX) }],
    },
  },
});

function hexToBytes(hex) {
  return new Uint8Array(hex.match(/../g).map((h) => parseInt(h, 16)));
}

socket.on("connect", () => {
  console.log("transport:", socket.io.engine.transport.name);
  socket.io.engine.on("upgrade", (t) => console.log("upgraded to", t.name)); // "webtransport"
});

Node.js (uses a WebTransport implementation from the same package) — verified

Node has no global WebTransport, so the test client installs the one from @fails-components/webtransport:

// wt-client.mjs
import { readFileSync } from "node:fs";
import { WebTransport, quicheLoaded } from "@fails-components/webtransport";
import { io } from "socket.io-client";

await quicheLoaded;
globalThis.WebTransport = WebTransport;               // browsers have this natively

const hash = Buffer.from(readFileSync("./cert.hash", "utf8").trim(), "hex");

const socket = io("https://localhost:3443", {
  transports: ["webtransport"],                       // WebTransport only
