  transportOptions: {
    webtransport: { serverCertificateHashes: [{ algorithm: "sha-256", value: hash }] },
  },
});

socket.on("connect", async () => {
  console.log("client: transport =", socket.io.engine.transport.name);
  console.log("client: ack =", await socket.timeout(3000).emitWithAck("ping-me", "hi"));
});

Real output:

client: transport = webtransport
client: ack = pong: hi
server: connected via webtransport

Upgrade behaviour — verified

With the default transport order (["polling", "websocket", "webtransport"]) and the server advertising WebTransport, the connection started on polling and upgraded straight to WebTransport, skipping WebSocket:

client: connected via polling
client: upgraded to webtransport
server: connected via polling
server: upgraded to webtransport

This matches the client source: when "webtransport" is among the server's advertised upgrades, the client probes it in preference to WebSocket. If the WebTransport probe fails (UDP blocked, unsupported browser), the client stays on polling; with tryAllTransports: true (4.8+) a failing initial transport makes the client try the next one in the list.

Recommended client configurations:

Goal Client option
Best compatibility (default) default transports; WebTransport used when possible
Skip polling, prefer WT, fall back to WS transports: ["webtransport", "websocket"], tryAllTransports: true
WT only (testing) transports: ["webtransport"]

18.7 Deployment considerations

  • Load balancers: you need one that forwards UDP/QUIC (e.g. AWS NLB with UDP listeners, or an HTTP/3-aware proxy). Many classic L7 load balancers terminate HTTP/1.1 and HTTP/2 only. Check whether your edge (CDN, ALB, ingress) supports WebTransport pass-through; HTTP/3 support for normal requests does not imply WebTransport support.
  • Sticky sessions: a WebTransport-only connection is a single QUIC connection, so like WebSocket-only it doesn't need stickiness — but a client that starts on polling and upgrades needs the upgrade to reach the same node, i.e. sticky routing that works across TCP and UDP (often by client IP). QUIC connection migration can change the client address mid-connection — consider this when routing by IP.
  • Firewalls: open UDP 443 (and your health checks). Corporate networks frequently block UDP → keep fallbacks.
  • Certificates: production must use CA-signed certs; serverCertificateHashes is for dev/self-signed scenarios with the ≤14-day/ECDSA constraints.
  • Monitoring: add the transport name to your metrics (socket.conn.transport.name) to see what share of users actually get WebTransport.

18.8 Troubleshooting WebTransport

Symptom Likely cause Fix
Always stays on polling/WebSocket "webtransport" missing from server transports; session stream not wired to io.engine.onWebTransportSession; UDP blocked Enable it; wire the session stream; open UDP
serverCertificateHashes is not valid fingerprint (Node polyfill) Hash not the SHA-256 of the DER certificate, or wrong encoding Hash X509Certificate.raw; pass bytes (Uint8Array/Buffer), not a hex string
Browser refuses the connection with a self-signed cert Certificate is RSA or valid for more than 14 days ECDSA P-256, -days 14 (or less), regenerate regularly
Works on localhost, fails in production LB/CDN doesn't pass QUIC/WebTransport; UDP closed UDP-capable LB, open ports, keep fallback
connect_error: webtransport error Server unreachable on UDP, TLS mismatch, path mismatch Check sessionStream path = path; cert; port

18.9 Interview questions for this chapter

Q1. [M] What is WebTransport and how does it differ from WebSocket? A browser API over HTTP/3 (QUIC/UDP) offering multiple independent streams (no head-of-line blocking across streams), unreliable datagrams, faster connection setup and connection migration; always encrypted. WebSocket is a single ordered TCP stream.

Q2. [M] How do you enable WebTransport in Socket.IO? Add "webtransport" to the server's transports, run an HTTP/3 server (@fails-components/webtransport in Node), and pass each session from h3Server.sessionStream("/socket.io/") to io.engine.onWebTransportSession(). Clients use it automatically when advertised (or transports: ["webtransport"]).

Q3. [S] Does Socket.IO's WebTransport transport give you unreliable delivery? No — it uses a reliable bidirectional stream, so semantics match WebSocket. The gains are setup latency and resilience on lossy/changing networks.

Q4. [M] What certificate constraints apply to serverCertificateHashes? ECDSA certificate with a validity of at most 14 days; you pin the SHA-256 of the DER certificate. In production use a normal CA-signed certificate.

Q5. [S] Why should you keep WebSocket/polling fallbacks? UDP is blocked on some networks, and browser/platform support for WebTransport is not universal.

Q6. [M] With default client settings, which transport does a client end up on when the server offers WebTransport? It starts on polling and upgrades directly to WebTransport (verified), falling back to staying on polling if the probe fails.

Next: Chapter 19 — Custom adapters and custom parsers

Writing a Custom Adapter and a Custom Parser

Goal: understand the two main extension points of Socket.IO well enough to build your own: an adapter (how servers in a cluster talk to each other) and a parser (how packets are encoded on the wire).

How this chapter was checked: both the adapter and the parser below were run. The adapter was used by two Socket.IO 4.8.4 servers in separate processes connected through Redis 7: a broadcast crossed servers, fetchSockets() returned sockets from both servers, serverSideEmit() reached the other server, and serverCount() returned 2. The parser round-tripped a JavaScript Date as a real Date in both directions.

19.1 ELI5

  • An adapter is the post office inside each server. It knows which customers are in which rooms, and when there are several post offices it sends letters between them. You write a custom one when you want the post offices to talk through a different "road" (NATS, Kafka, a database, a cloud service…).
  • A parser is the envelope format. The default one writes JSON. You write a custom one when you want a different envelope (MessagePack, Protobuf, JSON that keeps Dates…).

19.2 When should you write a custom adapter?

Almost never — first check the official ones (Redis, Redis Streams, MongoDB, Postgres, cluster, GCP Pub/Sub, AWS SQS, Azure Service Bus) and community ones. Valid reasons:

  • your organisation's standard bus is something else (NATS, Kafka, RabbitMQ, ZeroMQ);
  • you need special routing (per-tenant isolation, cross-region rules);
  • you're building a platform/product on top of Socket.IO.

Writing one is also the best way to really understand clustering — a frequent senior interview topic.

19.3 The adapter API

Every namespace has one adapter instance. The base class Adapter (in socket.io-adapter) is the in-memory implementation; distributed adapters override parts of it.

Method Called when
addAll(id, rooms) / del(id, room) / delAll(id) a local socket joins/leaves rooms
broadcast(packet, opts) io.emit, io.to(...).emit, socket.broadcast.emit…
broadcastWithAck(packet, opts, clientCountCallback, ack) broadcast with timeout() + ack
addSockets(opts, rooms) / delSockets(opts, rooms) io.socketsJoin / io.socketsLeave
disconnectSockets(opts, close) io.disconnectSockets
fetchSockets(opts) io.fetchSockets
serverSideEmit(packet) io.serverSideEmit
serverCount() number of servers in the cluster
persistSession(session) / restoreSession(pid, offset) connection state recovery
init() / close() lifecycle

Writing all of that by hand is error-prone. Since socket.io-adapter 2.5.3, two base classes do the hard part:

  • ClusterAdapter — implements every cluster operation (broadcasts, requests with responses, acks, fetchSockets…) on top of two abstract methods:
  • doPublish(message) → send a message to all other servers; returns an offset (only meaningful for connection state recovery);
  • doPublishResponse(requesterUid, response) → send a response to one server. Your transport calls this.onMessage(message, offset) and this.onResponse(response) when something arrives.
  • ClusterAdapterWithHeartbeat — the same, plus heartbeats so each server knows how many servers are alive (needed to know how many responses to wait for in fetchSockets() / serverSideEmit with ack). Options: heartbeatInterval (default 5 000 ms), heartbeatTimeout (default 10 000 ms).

Each server has a random uid; messages from yourself are ignored automatically.

19.4 A complete adapter in ~50 lines (verified)

A Redis pub/sub adapter written from scratch on ClusterAdapterWithHeartbeat, using the redis (node-redis v4+) client and MessagePack (notepack.io) so binary payloads survive:

// mini-redis-adapter.js
const { ClusterAdapterWithHeartbeat } = require("socket.io-adapter");
const { encode, decode } = require("notepack.io"); // msgpack: keeps Buffers (binary) intact

function createAdapter(pubClient, subClient, opts = {}) {
  const prefix = opts.channelPrefix ?? "mini-sio";
  const namespaceToAdapters = new Map(); // nsp name -> adapter (one adapter per namespace)

  // one shared subscription for every namespace of this server
  subClient.pSubscribe(`${prefix}#*`, (raw, channel) => {
    const [, nspName, target] = channel.toString().split("#"); // "mini-sio#/#all" or "mini-sio#/#<uid>"
    const adapter = namespaceToAdapters.get(nspName);
    if (!adapter) return;
    const message = decode(raw);
    if (target === "all") adapter.onMessage(message);   // a message for every server
    else adapter.onResponse(message);                   // a response addressed to this server
  }, true /* receive Buffers */);

  return function (nsp) {
    const adapter = new MiniRedisAdapter(nsp, pubClient, prefix, opts);
    namespaceToAdapters.set(nsp.name, adapter);
    const close = adapter.close.bind(adapter);
    adapter.close = () => { namespaceToAdapters.delete(nsp.name); close(); };
    return adapter;
  };
}

class MiniRedisAdapter extends ClusterAdapterWithHeartbeat {
  constructor(nsp, pubClient, prefix, opts) {
    super(nsp, opts);           // opts: { heartbeatInterval, heartbeatTimeout }
    this.pub = pubClient;
    this.prefix = prefix;
  }

  // send a message to ALL other servers (broadcasts, socketsJoin, fetchSockets requests, heartbeats…)
  async doPublish(message) {
    await this.pub.publish(`${this.prefix}#${this.nsp.name}#all`, encode(message));
    return ""; // an offset, only meaningful for adapters that support connection state recovery
  }

  // send a response to ONE server (the one that made a request, e.g. fetchSockets)
  async doPublishResponse(requesterUid, response) {
    await this.pub.publish(`${this.prefix}#${this.nsp.name}#${requesterUid}`, encode(response));
  }
}

module.exports = { createAdapter };

Using it:

const { Server } = require("socket.io");
const { createClient } = require("redis");
const { createAdapter } = require("./mini-redis-adapter");

const pub = createClient({ url: process.env.REDIS_URL });
const sub = pub.duplicate();
pub.on("error", console.error);
sub.on("error", console.error);
await Promise.all([pub.connect(), sub.connect()]);

const io = new Server(port, { adapter: createAdapter(pub, sub, { heartbeatInterval: 1000 }) });

io.on("connection", (socket) => {
  socket.data.name = socket.handshake.auth.name;
  socket.join("room1");
  socket.on("go", async (ack) => {
    io.to("room1").emit("hello", `from ${port}`);                          // cross-server broadcast
    const names = (await io.fetchSockets()).map((s) => s.data.name).sort(); // cross-server fetch
    io.serverSideEmit("ping-servers", port);                                // server-to-server
    ack({ names, servers: await io.of("/").adapter.serverCount() });
  });
});
io.on("ping-servers", (from) => console.log(`server ${port} got serverSideEmit from ${from}`));

Real output with two servers (ports 4001 and 4002), Alice on 4001 and Bob on 4002:

bob (on server 4002) received: from 4001
ack: { names: [ 'alice', 'bob' ], servers: 2 }
(server 4002 log) server 4002 got serverSideEmit from 4001

A bug I hit while writing it (and what it teaches)

The first version did channel.split("#") and crashed with TypeError: channel.split is not a function. With node-redis' buffer mode (the true argument to pSubscribe, needed to receive binary message bodies), the channel name is a Buffer too. Fix: channel.toString(). Lesson: in pub/sub adapters, be explicit about binary vs string at every boundary — payloads must stay binary (msgpack, attachments), channel names are text.

Design decisions you must make in a real adapter

  1. Channel layout. One channel for everything (simple, every server gets everything) vs per-room channels (servers only receive rooms they host — what the sharded Redis adapter does in dynamic mode).
  2. Encoding. MessagePack (binary-safe, compact) vs JSON (must handle Buffers yourself).
  3. Delivery guarantees. Pub/sub loses messages during disconnects. A log-based transport (Redis Streams, Kafka, NATS JetStream) can resume from an offset and enables connection state recovery: return the stored message id from doPublish, and implement persistSession / restoreSession (see the Redis Streams adapter source as a reference).
  4. Request timeouts. fetchSockets() and acks wait for every live server; heartbeats decide who is alive. Tune heartbeatInterval/heartbeatTimeout.
  5. Message size limits of your bus (Postgres NOTIFY is limited to 8000 bytes — the Postgres adapter stores bigger payloads in a table).
  6. Cleanup. Unsubscribe and clear timers in close().

Testing a custom adapter

Run 2–3 servers (separate processes, or several Server instances on different ports in one test) against a real bus (Redis in Docker/Testcontainers) and assert:

  • broadcast to all / to a room / with except reaches clients on every server exactly once;
  • socket.to(room) excludes the sender everywhere;
  • fetchSockets() returns sockets from all servers, with data;
  • socketsJoin / socketsLeave / disconnectSockets affect remote sockets;
  • serverSideEmit with and without ack;
  • broadcast with ack collects responses from all servers;
  • a server going down is removed after heartbeatTimeout (no hanging requests);
  • binary payloads survive.

19.5 Parsers

The contract

A parser module exports two classes, Encoder and Decoder. Both client and server must be configured with the same parser (new Server({ parser }), io(url, { parser })).

class Encoder {
  /** packet: { type, nsp, data?, id? } → array of strings and/or binary chunks */
  encode(packet) { /* … */ return [chunk1, chunk2]; }
}

class Decoder extends Emitter {           // from "@socket.io/component-emitter"
  add(chunk) {                            // called for every chunk received from the transport
    /* … when a whole packet has been decoded: */ this.emit("decoded", packet);
  }
  destroy() {}                            // called when the connection closes
}

Packet types: 0 CONNECT, 1 DISCONNECT, 2 EVENT, 3 ACK, 4 CONNECT_ERROR (plus 5/6 binary variants used by the default parser). Validate decoded packets: a throw inside add() makes the connection close, which is the right reaction to garbage from a malicious client — and historic security advisories in socket.io-parser were exactly about insufficient validation (Chapter 6.10).

Example: a JSON parser that preserves Date objects (verified)

The default parser turns Dates into ISO strings. This parser tags them so the receiver gets real Date objects back (JSON-only: no binary support, which is fine for many apps):

// date-parser.js
const { Emitter } = require("@socket.io/component-emitter");

const DATE_TAG = "__date__";

const replacer = function (key, value) {
  // JSON.stringify calls Date#toJSON() BEFORE the replacer, so read the original value from `this`
  const original = this[key];
  return original instanceof Date ? { [DATE_TAG]: original.toISOString() } : value;
};
const reviver = (key, value) =>
  value && typeof value === "object" && typeof value[DATE_TAG] === "string" ? new Date(value[DATE_TAG]) : value;

class Encoder {
  encode(packet) {
    return [JSON.stringify(packet, replacer)];      // must return an ARRAY of encoded chunks
  }
}

class Decoder extends Emitter {
  add(chunk) {
    if (typeof chunk !== "string") throw new Error("binary not supported by this parser");
    const packet = JSON.parse(chunk, reviver);
    if (!isPacketValid(packet)) throw new Error("invalid packet");   // a throw closes the connection
    this.emit("decoded", packet);
  }
  destroy() {}
}

function isPacketValid({ type, data, nsp, id }) {
  const isNamespaceValid = typeof nsp === "string";
  const isAckIdValid = id === undefined || Number.isInteger(id);
  if (!isNamespaceValid || !isAckIdValid) return false;
  switch (type) {
    case 0: return data === undefined || typeof data === "object";     // CONNECT
    case 1: return data === undefined;                                 // DISCONNECT
    case 2: return Array.isArray(data) && typeof data[0] === "string"; // EVENT
    case 3: return Array.isArray(data);                                // ACK
    case 4: return typeof data === "object";                           // CONNECT_ERROR
    default: return false;
  }
}

module.exports = { Encoder, Decoder };

Test (real output):

const parser = require("./date-parser");
const io = new Server(4400, { parser });
io.on("connection", (s) =>
  s.on("when", (d, ack) => ack({ isDate: d instanceof Date, plusOneDay: new Date(+d + 86400000) })));

const c = ioClient("http://localhost:4400", { parser });
const r = await c.timeout(2000).emitWithAck("when", new Date("2026-01-01T00:00:00Z"));
// → server saw a Date: true | client got a Date back: true 2026-01-02T00:00:00.000Z

The subtle part (a classic JavaScript gotcha): JSON.stringify calls Date#toJSON() before passing the value to the replacer, so value is already a string — you must look at this[key] to see the original Date.

MessagePack parser (official)

// server
const msgpackParser = require("socket.io-msgpack-parser");
const io = new Server(httpServer, { parser: msgpackParser });

// client
import msgpackParser from "socket.io-msgpack-parser";
const socket = io(URL, { parser: msgpackParser });
// or the prebuilt browser bundle: socket.io.msgpack.min.js

Binary-encodes everything (no separate attachments). Smaller for numeric/binary-heavy data; similar for text-heavy data. Not human-readable in DevTools. Every client (including mobile clients) must support the same parser — check before adopting.

Other ideas for custom parsers

  • Protobuf / FlatBuffers with a schema per event (very compact, typed, but every client must share the schemas).
  • Compression of large textual payloads (but prefer perMessageDeflate first).
  • Per-message encryption (prefer TLS + application-level E2EE, Chapter 21).
  • Validation in the parser (reject unknown event names early).

19.6 Interview questions for this chapter

Q1. [S] What do you need to implement to build a cluster adapter today? Extend ClusterAdapterWithHeartbeat (or ClusterAdapter) from socket.io-adapter and implement doPublish(message) (to all servers) and doPublishResponse(uid, response) (to one server); feed incoming traffic to onMessage() / onResponse(). The base class implements broadcasts, acks, fetchSockets, socketsJoin/Leave, disconnectSockets, serverSideEmit, and server counting via heartbeats.

Q2. [S] Why do heartbeats matter in a cluster adapter? Requests like fetchSockets() wait for one response per server; heartbeats tell each node how many servers are alive so it neither waits for dead nodes nor misses live ones.

Q3. [S] How would an adapter support connection state recovery? Use a log-based bus (streams), return the stored message id as the offset from doPublish, and implement persistSession / restoreSession so missed packets can be replayed from the offset.

Q4. [M] What must a custom parser export? Encoder with encode(packet) returning an array of chunks, and Decoder (an event emitter) with add(chunk) emitting "decoded" and destroy(). Same parser on both sides.

Q5. [M] Why validate packets in the decoder? Malformed input from malicious clients must not crash or confuse the server; throwing closes that connection. Past parser vulnerabilities came from insufficient validation.

Q6. [M] Why does a naive "keep Dates" replacer not work with JSON.stringify? toJSON() runs before the replacer, so the replacer sees a string; inspect this[key].

Next: Chapter 20 — Other runtimes: Electron, service workers, Bun, Deno

Other Runtimes: Electron, Web/Shared/Service Workers, Edge, Bun and Deno

Goal: use Socket.IO outside the classic "browser page + Node.js server" setup.

How this chapter was checked: - Fetch transport (for service workers): verified — a client configured with transports: [Fetch, WebSocket] connected over fetch-based polling and exchanged acks. - Bun 1.4.2 running the npm socket.io server: verified with a Node client (connect, acks, WebSocket upgrade). - Deno 2.9.6 running npm:socket.io@4.8.4: verified (connect, acks, upgraded to WebSocket). - The official Deno port on deno.land/x could not be downloaded from this sandbox (host blocked), and Electron could not be run here (no display) — those sections are written from the libraries' and Electron's documented behaviour and are marked as such.

20.1 ELI5

The Socket.IO client is like a phone that normally lives in a web page. But you can also put that phone in other places: in a desktop app (Electron), in a helper that runs behind the web page (workers), or in other JavaScript engines (Bun, Deno). Each place has its own rules — some places don't allow certain phone features, some go to sleep, some can't keep a line open at all.

20.2 Web Workers and SharedWorkers

socket.io-client works inside dedicated workers and shared workers — they have WebSocket and XMLHttpRequest.

One connection for all tabs with a SharedWorker

Ten open tabs normally mean ten connections (ten times the server load, ten copies of every notification). A SharedWorker can own a single connection and relay to tabs:

// shared-socket-worker.js  (bundle socket.io-client into it, or importScripts the UMD build)
import { io } from "socket.io-client";

const socket = io("https://api.example.com", {
  transports: ["websocket"],
  auth: (cb) => cb({ token: currentToken }),
});
let currentToken = null;
const ports = new Set();

socket.onAny((event, ...args) => {
  for (const port of ports) port.postMessage({ type: "event", event, args });
});
socket.on("connect", () => broadcastStatus(true));
socket.on("disconnect", () => broadcastStatus(false));

function broadcastStatus(connected) {
  for (const port of ports) port.postMessage({ type: "status", connected });
}

onconnect = (e) => {
  const port = e.ports[0];
  ports.add(port);
  port.onmessage = async ({ data }) => {
    if (data.type === "token") { currentToken = data.token; if (!socket.connected) socket.connect(); }
    if (data.type === "emit") {
      try {
        const res = await socket.timeout(8000).emitWithAck(data.event, data.payload);
        port.postMessage({ type: "ack", requestId: data.requestId, res });
      } catch (err) {
        port.postMessage({ type: "ack", requestId: data.requestId, error: "TIMEOUT" });
      }
    }
    if (data.type === "close") ports.delete(port);   // tabs should send this on "pagehide"
  };
  port.postMessage({ type: "status", connected: socket.connected });
};

// in each tab
const worker = new SharedWorker(new URL("./shared-socket-worker.js", import.meta.url), { type: "module" });
worker.port.start();
worker.port.postMessage({ type: "token", token: accessToken });
worker.port.onmessage = ({ data }) => { if (data.type === "event") dispatch(data.event, data.args); };
addEventListener("pagehide", () => worker.port.postMessage({ type: "close" }));

Caveats: - Browser support: SharedWorker isn't available everywhere (notably some mobile browsers). Feature-detect and fall back to one socket per tab. - Rooms on the server now represent the browser, not the tab; per-tab subscriptions must be multiplexed by the worker (reference-count subscriptions). - An alternative without SharedWorker: leader election between tabs via BroadcastChannel / Web Locks API — one tab holds the socket and rebroadcasts.

20.3 Service workers and the fetch transport

Service workers have no XMLHttpRequest, so the classic polling transport can't run there. Since socket.io-client 4.8, you can choose transport implementations, including a polling transport based on fetch():

import { io, Fetch, WebSocket } from "socket.io-client";

const socket = io("https://api.example.com", {
  transports: [Fetch, WebSocket],   // constructors instead of names
});

Verified in Node 22 (which has fetch): with transports: [Fetch, WebSocket] and upgrade: false, the client connected and the server reported transport polling; acks worked (transport: polling | ack: hello polling).

socket.io-client 4.8 exports these implementations: Fetch, XHR, NodeXHR, WebSocket, NodeWebSocket, WebTransport.

But should a service worker hold a socket? Usually no: - Browsers terminate idle service workers after a short time (tens of seconds); an open WebSocket doesn't reliably keep one alive. A long-lived connection is not what service workers are for. - For background notifications use the Push API (Web Push) — that's the platform's mechanism to wake a service worker. - Valid uses: short-lived exchanges during sync/push event handling, or runtimes that lack XHR but aren't service workers.

20.4 Edge and serverless runtimes

  • Cloudflare Workers, Vercel Edge Functions, AWS Lambda: you cannot host a Socket.IO server there — no long-running Node HTTP server, and request lifetimes are limited. (Cloudflare Durable Objects can hold raw WebSockets, but that's not the Socket.IO protocol.)
  • Clients in edge runtimes: the fetch transport (20.3) makes short-lived client usage possible, but invocation time limits make it unsuitable for persistent connections.
  • Pattern: keep Socket.IO servers on long-running infrastructure (containers/VMs), and have serverless functions publish through an emitter (Redis) or a broker (Chapter 8.5) — e.g. a Lambda that processes a payment publishes emitter.to("user:42").emit("payment:done").

20.5 Electron (desktop apps)

(Not executed in this environment — based on Electron's documented process model and security guidance.)

Electron apps have two kinds of processes, and you can run the Socket.IO client in either:

Where Pros Cons
Renderer (a Chromium page) Same code as your web app; DevTools Network tab shows frames Reloading the window drops the connection; subject to the page's security settings
Main process (Node.js) Survives window reloads/closing; one connection for many windows; can set custom headers (Node client) You must relay events to windows via IPC

Renderer with secure defaults

With contextIsolation: true, sandbox: true and nodeIntegration: false (the recommended settings), import socket.io-client in your bundled renderer code like in any web app. Don't expose Node APIs to the page.

Add your API host to the Content Security Policy:

<meta http-equiv="Content-Security-Policy"
      content="default-src 'self'; connect-src 'self' https://api.example.com wss://api.example.com">

Main process + IPC (recommended for apps that should stay connected)

// main.js
const { app, BrowserWindow, ipcMain, powerMonitor } = require("electron");
const { io } = require("socket.io-client");

let socket;
app.whenReady().then(() => {
  socket = io("https://api.example.com", {
    transports: ["websocket"],
    auth: (cb) => cb({ token: loadTokenFromSecureStorage() }),   // e.g. safeStorage / OS keychain
    extraHeaders: { "x-client": "desktop/5.2.0" },              // allowed in the Node client
  });

  // relay server events to every window
  socket.onAny((event, ...args) => {
    for (const win of BrowserWindow.getAllWindows()) win.webContents.send("rt:event", event, args);
  });

  // windows send commands through IPC; the main process applies a timeout and returns the ack
  ipcMain.handle("rt:emit", (_e, event, payload) => socket.timeout(8000).emitWithAck(event, payload));

  // laptops sleep: reconnect promptly on resume instead of waiting for back-off
  powerMonitor.on("resume", () => { if (!socket.connected) socket.connect(); });
});

// preload.js — expose a minimal, safe API (contextBridge), never the raw socket
const { contextBridge, ipcRenderer } = require("electron");
contextBridge.exposeInMainWorld("realtime", {
  emit: (event, payload) => ipcRenderer.invoke("rt:emit", event, payload),
  on: (handler) => {
    const listener = (_e, event, args) => handler(event, args);
    ipcRenderer.on("rt:event", listener);
    return () => ipcRenderer.removeListener("rt:event", listener);   // unsubscribe
  },
});

Optionally allow-list event names in the preload/main process so a compromised renderer can't emit arbitrary events.

Origins and CORS in Electron

  • Pages loaded from file:// don't have a normal web origin. Requests from them carry an unusual Origin (an opaque/file://-style origin) that your cors allow-list won't match by default. Log req.headers.origin on the server to see what your build sends, and avoid simply allowing "null" (sandboxed iframes on any website also send null).
  • Better options: create the socket in the main process (no browser CORS rules; send an app identifier header), or serve the UI from a custom privileged scheme (protocol.registerSchemesAsPrivileged with standard/secure privileges) and allow that exact origin.
  • Authenticate with tokens, not origin alone — desktop apps can be inspected and modified by their users.

20.6 Bun

The npm socket.io server runs on Bun through its Node.js compatibility layer. Verified with Bun 1.4.2:

// bun-server.js
const { Server } = require("socket.io");
const io = new Server(4101);
io.on("connection", (s) => s.on("hi", (ack) => ack("hello from bun via " + s.conn.transport.name)));

bun bun-server.js

A Node socket.io-client connected and got hello from bun via websocket (and still websocket after the upgrade window).

Notes: - Compatibility is good for the core features shown here; test adapters (Redis), native add-ons (bufferutil) and uWebSockets.js separately on your Bun version. - Bun also has its own fast native WebSocket server (Bun.serve({ websocket })) — that's plain WebSocket, not the Socket.IO protocol.

20.7 Deno

npm socket.io on Deno — verified (Deno 2.9.6)

// npm-server.ts
import { Server } from "npm:socket.io@4.8.4";

const io = new Server(4500, { cors: { origin: "*" } });
io.on("connection", (s) =>
  s.on("hi", (ack: (m: string) => void) => ack("hello from Deno (npm:socket.io) via " + s.conn.transport.name)));

// deno.json — needed when the folder also has a package.json
{ "nodeModulesDir": "auto" }

deno install --entrypoint npm-server.ts
deno run -A npm-server.ts     # or narrower: --allow-net --allow-env --allow-read

Result with a Node client: hello from Deno (npm:socket.io) via websocket.

The first attempt failed with "Could not find a matching package for 'npm:socket.io@4.8.4' in the node_modules directory" because the folder contained a package.json; Deno then expects dependencies to be installed. Adding "nodeModulesDir": "auto" (or running deno install) fixed it.

The official Deno port

There is also an official Socket.IO server port written for Deno (published on deno.land/x/socket_io), which plugs into Deno's HTTP server via io.handler():

// not executed here — deno.land was unreachable from the test sandbox
import { serve } from "https://deno.land/std@0.224.0/http/server.ts";
import { Server } from "https://deno.land/x/socket_io@0.2.1/mod.ts";

const io = new Server();
io.on("connection", (socket) => { /* same API style as Node */ });
await serve(io.handler(), { port: 3000 });

It implements a subset of the Node server's features; check its README for what's supported (adapters, etc.) before choosing it over npm:socket.io.

20.8 Node.js-only client features (for completeness)

When the client runs in Node.js (CLI tools, tests, Electron main, services):

  • extraHeaders apply to all transports (browsers can't set WebSocket headers).
  • TLS options: ca, cert, key, pfx, passphrase, rejectUnauthorized (never false in production), ciphers.
  • agent for HTTP proxies (e.g. https-proxy-agent).
  • autoUnref: true → the heartbeat timer doesn't keep the process alive.
  • With withCredentials: true, the Node client keeps cookies between requests (this is what made HAProxy cookie-based stickiness work in the Chapter 23 lab).

20.9 Interview questions for this chapter

Q1. [M] How can several browser tabs share one Socket.IO connection? A SharedWorker owning the socket and relaying via MessagePorts, or leader election between tabs (BroadcastChannel / Web Locks). Mind browser support and per-tab subscriptions.

Q2. [M] Can you use Socket.IO in a service worker? The client can run there using the fetch-based polling transport (transports: [Fetch, WebSocket], 4.8+), but service workers are terminated when idle, so they're unsuitable for persistent connections; use Web Push for background notifications.

Q3. [M] Can you host a Socket.IO server on Lambda / Cloudflare Workers / Vercel functions? No — they don't keep long-lived connections. Host servers on long-running infrastructure and publish from serverless code through an emitter or broker.

Q4. [S] Where should an Electron app hold its socket? Usually the main process (survives reloads, one connection, Node client features) with a narrow IPC API exposed via contextBridge; or the renderer for simple apps. Use secure renderer settings, CSP connect-src, and token auth (file:// pages have unusual origins).

Q5. [M] Does npm:socket.io work on Bun and Deno? Yes for core functionality (verified: connect, acks, WebSocket upgrade on Bun 1.4.2 and Deno 2.9.6). Test adapters and native add-ons on your exact runtime version.

Next: Chapter 21 — Offline-first clients and end-to-end encryption

Offline-First Clients and End-to-End Encryption

Goal: two advanced client-side topics that come up in serious products and senior interviews: (1) never losing a user's action, even across offline periods and page reloads (outbox pattern), and (2) making messages unreadable to the server (end-to-end encryption, E2EE) while still using Socket.IO as the transport.

How this chapter was checked: both implementations were run in Node 22 against a Socket.IO 4.8.4 server. The outbox test used fake-indexeddb to provide the browser's IndexedDB API: two messages written while the server was down survived a simulated page reload and were delivered exactly once when the server came back. The E2EE test used the standard Web Crypto API (identical in browsers): the server only saw a 12-byte IV and a 32-byte ciphertext; the recipient decrypted the original text.

21.1 ELI5

Outbox: when you write a letter but the post office is closed, you put it in a box by your door (saved on disk). Every time the post office opens, you hand over the letters from the box one by one, and you only throw a letter away when the post office gives you a receipt. If you move house (reload the page), the box comes with you.

E2EE: you and your friend each have a special padlock and key. You lock your letter with a padlock only your friend can open. The postman (the server) carries the locked box but can't read what's inside.

21.2 Why Socket.IO's built-in buffering isn't enough

Recap (Chapter 5): the JS client keeps emits made while disconnected in socket.sendBuffer and sends them on reconnection. But:

Situation Built-in buffer Outbox
Short network blip ✅ delivered ✅ delivered
Page reload / tab closed / app killed while offline ❌ lost (memory only) ✅ persisted in IndexedDB
Server received it but the ack was lost ⚠️ not retried → you don't know ✅ retried with the same id → server dedupes
Server rejected it (validation) you see the ack, nothing stored ✅ removed with an error state for the UI
Swift client (no offline buffering at all, Chapter 16) ❌ ✅

Use the outbox for user-generated commands that matter: chat messages, form submissions, likes, edits in collaborative apps, field data entry.

21.3 The outbox pattern — design

  1. Persist first, send second. The UI writes the command to durable local storage (IndexedDB) with a client-generated unique id before sending.
  2. Optimistic UI. Show the item immediately as "pending".
  3. Send in order, one at a time, with an ack + timeout.
  4. Remove on success (or on a permanent rejection, surfaced to the user).
  5. Retry on reconnect (connect event), and on app start.
  6. Server is idempotent: a unique constraint on the client id makes retries harmless.
  7. Stop at the first transient failure to preserve order.

21.4 Implementation (verified)

// outbox.mjs — offline-first outbox: messages survive offline periods AND page reloads.
import { get, set } from "idb-keyval";       // tiny IndexedDB wrapper (works in browsers)

const KEY = "outbox:v1";

export class Outbox {
  constructor(socket, { event = "message:send", ackTimeout = 8000 } = {}) {
    this.socket = socket;
    this.event = event;
    this.ackTimeout = ackTimeout;
    this.flushing = false;
    socket.on("connect", () => this.flush());           // retry everything pending on every (re)connect
  }

  async #load() { return (await get(KEY)) ?? []; }
  async #save(items) { await set(KEY, items); }

  // 1) persist FIRST, 2) then try to send
  async enqueue(payload) {
    const item = { id: crypto.randomUUID(), payload, createdAt: Date.now() };
    await this.#save([...(await this.#load()), item]);
    this.flush();
    return item.id;                                     // use it as the key of the "pending" UI item
  }

  async pending() { return this.#load(); }

  async flush() {
    if (this.flushing || !this.socket.connected) return;
    this.flushing = true;
    try {
      for (const item of await this.#load()) {           // in order, one at a time
        if (!this.socket.connected) break;
        try {
          const res = await this.socket.timeout(this.ackTimeout)
            .emitWithAck(this.event, { ...item.payload, clientMsgId: item.id }); // SAME id on every retry
          if (res?.ok || res?.error?.code === "VALIDATION") {
            // delivered (or permanently rejected → show it to the user) → remove from the outbox
            await this.#save((await this.#load()).filter((i) => i.id !== item.id));
          } else {
            break;                                       // transient error: keep order, retry later
          }
        } catch {
          break;                                         // ack timeout: retry on next connect / flush
        }
      }
    } finally {
      this.flushing = false;
    }
  }
}

Server side — idempotent handler:

socket.on("message:send", async (m, ack) => {
  // UNIQUE index on client_msg_id → the second insert of a retried message does nothing
  const { inserted, message } = await db.insertMessageIfAbsent(m.clientMsgId, socket.data.user.id, m);
  if (inserted) io.to(`conversation:${m.conversationId}`).emit("message:new", message);
  ack({ ok: true, data: { id: message.id }, duplicate: !inserted });
});

Test scenario and real output

// phase 1: server is DOWN; the user sends 2 messages, then the page "reloads" (client destroyed)
let c1 = io(URL, { reconnectionDelay: 200, reconnectionDelayMax: 300 });
let ob1 = new Outbox(c1);
await ob1.enqueue({ text: "written offline #1" });
await ob1.enqueue({ text: "written offline #2" });
console.log("pending before reload:", (await ob1.pending()).length);
c1.close();                                   // the in-memory sendBuffer would be lost here

// phase 2: new page load, server comes back
startServer();
const c2 = io(URL);
const ob2 = new Outbox(c2);                    // reads the persisted items and flushes on connect
await sleep(800);
console.log("pending after reconnect:", (await ob2.pending()).length);
console.log("server stored:", [...stored.values()]);

pending before reload: 2
pending after reconnect: 0
server stored: [ 'written offline #1', 'written offline #2' ]

Production refinements

  • Multiple tabs: two tabs flushing the same IndexedDB outbox can double-send (harmless thanks to idempotency, but wasteful). Use the Web Locks API (navigator.locks.request("outbox", …)) so only one tab flushes at a time.
  • Per-conversation ordering instead of one global queue, so one stuck item doesn't block unrelated conversations.
  • Expiry & poison messages: after N attempts or a max age, mark as failed and let the user retry or discard.
  • Large items (attachments): store blobs separately; upload via HTTP; the outbox item references the upload.
  • Service worker Background Sync can trigger a flush after connectivity returns even if the page is closed (where supported) — it would use HTTP, not the socket.
  • Encryption at rest: IndexedDB isn't encrypted; don't store secrets there.
  • Mobile equivalents: SQLite/Room (Android), Core Data/SQLite (iOS), Hive/Isar/sqflite (Flutter) with the same algorithm.

Server → client: the inbox side

The outbox covers client→server. For server→client completeness, combine with the sync pattern (Chapter 5.9): persist on the server, client syncs "after last id" on every connect, dedupes by id. Together they give at-least-once delivery both ways with effectively-once processing.

21.5 End-to-end encryption (E2EE)

What E2EE means here

TLS (wss://) already encrypts traffic between client and server, but the server sees plaintext. With E2EE, clients encrypt before emitting; the server stores and relays ciphertext only. Socket.IO is just the transport: binary payloads (Uint8Array) travel as attachments.

Things the server still sees: who talks to whom, when, message sizes, room membership — metadata. E2EE protects content, not metadata.

The building blocks (Web Crypto API — available in browsers and Node 20+)

  • ECDH (P-256) key agreement: each user has a key pair; combining my private key with your public key yields the same shared secret on both sides.
  • HKDF to derive a proper AES key from that secret (with a salt/context, e.g. the conversation id).
  • AES-GCM (256) for authenticated encryption, with a random 12-byte IV per message and associated data (AAD) binding the ciphertext to its context.
// e2ee.mjs — identical code runs in browsers and Node 20+
const subtle = globalThis.crypto.subtle;
const enc = new TextEncoder(), dec = new TextDecoder();

export async function createIdentity() {
  // private key not extractable: it can't be exported by scripts
  const keyPair = await subtle.generateKey({ name: "ECDH", namedCurve: "P-256" }, false, ["deriveBits"]);
  const publicJwk = await subtle.exportKey("jwk", keyPair.publicKey);
  return { keyPair, publicJwk };
}

export async function deriveConversationKey(myKeyPair, theirPublicJwk, conversationId) {
  const theirKey = await subtle.importKey("jwk", theirPublicJwk, { name: "ECDH", namedCurve: "P-256" }, false, []);
  const sharedBits = await subtle.deriveBits({ name: "ECDH", public: theirKey }, myKeyPair.privateKey, 256);
  const hkdfKey = await subtle.importKey("raw", sharedBits, "HKDF", false, ["deriveKey"]);
  return subtle.deriveKey(
    { name: "HKDF", hash: "SHA-256", salt: enc.encode(conversationId), info: enc.encode("chat-v1") },
    hkdfKey, { name: "AES-GCM", length: 256 }, false, ["encrypt", "decrypt"],
  );
}

export async function encrypt(key, plaintext, aad) {
  const iv = crypto.getRandomValues(new Uint8Array(12));           // NEVER reuse an IV with the same key
  const ct = await subtle.encrypt({ name: "AES-GCM", iv, additionalData: enc.encode(aad) }, key, enc.encode(plaintext));
  return { iv, ct: new Uint8Array(ct) };                            // binary → sent as Socket.IO attachments
}

export async function decrypt(key, { iv, ct }, aad) {
  const pt = await subtle.decrypt({ name: "AES-GCM", iv, additionalData: enc.encode(aad) }, key, ct);
  return dec.decode(pt);                                            // throws if tampered with
}

Server: an untrusted relay

const publicKeys = new Map();   // in production: a DB table of (userId, deviceId, publicKey)

io.on("connection", (s) => {
  const user = s.data.user.id;              // from auth middleware
  s.join(`user:${user}`);
  s.on("keys:publish", (jwk, ack) => { publicKeys.set(user, jwk); ack(true); });
  s.on("keys:get", (u, ack) => ack(publicKeys.get(u) ?? null));
  s.on("e2e:send", (msg) => {
    // the server only sees the routing info and opaque bytes
    io.to(`user:${msg.to}`).emit("e2e:message", { from: user, iv: msg.iv, ct: msg.ct });
  });
});

Clients

const me = await createIdentity();
await socket.emitWithAck("keys:publish", me.publicJwk);

const theirKey = await socket.emitWithAck("keys:get", "bob");
const key = await deriveConversationKey(me.keyPair, theirKey, "dm:alice:bob");

// send
const { iv, ct } = await encrypt(key, "meet at 7pm 🍕", "dm:alice:bob");
socket.emit("e2e:send", { to: "bob", iv, ct });

// receive (Bob)
socket.on("e2e:message", async (m) => {
  const iv = new Uint8Array(m.iv), ct = new Uint8Array(m.ct);   // Buffers in Node, ArrayBuffers in browsers
  console.log(await decrypt(bobKey, { iv, ct }, "dm:alice:bob"));
});

Real output of the test run:

server sees: { to: 'bob', ivBytes: 12, ciphertextBytes: 32 }
bob decrypted: meet at 7pm 🍕

(32 bytes = 16 bytes of UTF-8 text including the emoji + 16-byte GCM authentication tag.)

What this demo deliberately leaves out (read before shipping E2EE)

This is a teaching example, not a complete secure messenger. Real systems add:

  1. Key authenticity. The server hands out public keys — a malicious server could hand out its own (man-in-the-middle). Real apps let users verify safety numbers / QR codes, or use key transparency logs.
  2. Forward secrecy and post-compromise security. One static key per pair means a stolen private key decrypts all past messages. The Signal protocol (X3DH + Double Ratchet) rotates keys per message. Use a vetted library (e.g. libsignal) rather than inventing a protocol.
  3. Multiple devices per user (a key per device; messages encrypted per device).
  4. Groups: sender keys or MLS (Messaging Layer Security, RFC 9420) instead of pairwise encryption to every member.
  5. Key storage: non-extractable CryptoKey objects can be stored in IndexedDB (structured clone) so they survive reloads without exposing raw key material.
  6. Replay protection & ordering: include message ids/counters in the AAD.
  7. Server-side features you lose: search, moderation, link previews and push notification content on the server. Plan alternatives (client-side search, generic push text, client-side reporting).

Interplay with Socket.IO features

  • Binary payloads use attachments (Chapter 7) — fine; with the msgpack parser they're inline.
  • maxHttpBufferSize still applies to ciphertext size (encryption adds IV + 16-byte tag).
  • Connection state recovery and offline outboxes work unchanged — they store opaque bytes.
  • Logging: never log decrypted content on the client in production; on the server there's nothing to log but metadata.

21.6 Interview questions for this chapter

Q1. [S] Socket.IO already buffers emits while offline — why build an outbox? The buffer is in memory: lost on reload/app kill; and it doesn't retry when an ack is lost. An outbox persists commands with client-generated ids, retries with the same id on every reconnect, and relies on server idempotency → at-least-once delivery, effectively-once processing.

Q2. [M] How does the server make retries safe? A unique constraint on the client-generated id (INSERT … ON CONFLICT DO NOTHING), and it acks duplicates as success.

Q3. [M] Why send outbox items one at a time? To preserve order and avoid flooding; stop at the first transient failure and resume later.

Q4. [S] What is the difference between TLS and E2EE in a Socket.IO app? TLS protects the hop client↔server (server sees plaintext). E2EE encrypts on the sender device and decrypts on the recipient device; the server only relays ciphertext but still sees metadata.

Q5. [S] Which primitives would you use for a simple E2EE chat over Socket.IO, and what's missing for production? ECDH (P-256/X25519) + HKDF + AES-GCM with random IVs and AAD. Missing: key verification (MITM by server), forward secrecy (Double Ratchet/Signal), multi-device and group key management (sender keys/MLS), secure key storage, replay protection.

Q6. [M] How do encrypted bytes travel through Socket.IO? As binary arguments (Uint8Array) → sent as binary attachments over WebSocket frames (or base64 in polling); arrive as Buffer in Node and ArrayBuffer in browsers.

Next: Chapter 22 — Complete options reference

Complete Options Reference (Server and Client)

Goal: every configuration option of the Socket.IO server and client — not just the common ones — with its default, what it does, and when you'd change it.

Source of truth: the option lists were extracted from the TypeScript declarations shipped in socket.io 4.8.4, engine.io 6.6.11, socket.io-client 4.8.4 and engine.io-client 6.6.x, and the defaults from the code that applies them. Where the declaration comment and runtime behaviour differ, the runtime behaviour is given.

The Socket.IO server's options are the union of three layers: Socket.IO options + Engine.IO server options + Engine.IO attach options. The client's options are Manager options + Socket options + Engine.IO client options. All can be passed in the single options object.

22.1 Server — Socket.IO layer

Option Default What it does When to change
path "/socket.io/" HTTP path the server intercepts Serving under a prefix (/api/socket.io/), or several Socket.IO servers on one host. Must match the client's path
serveClient true Serve /socket.io/socket.io.js (and .map, .esm variants) Set false when you bundle socket.io-client (smaller attack surface, no version disclosure)
adapter in-memory Adapter Room storage and broadcasting implementation Multi-server: Redis, Redis Streams, Mongo, Postgres, cluster… (Chapter 8)
parser default socket.io-parser Packet encoder/decoder msgpack or custom parser (Chapter 19) — same on clients
connectTimeout 45000 ms Time for a client to join a namespace after the Engine.IO handshake before the raw connection is closed Lower to drop half-open connections faster; it's also the delay before a client stuck in a middleware that never calls next() is cut
connectionStateRecovery disabled { maxDisconnectionDuration: 120000, skipMiddlewares: true } when enabled — restore id, rooms, data, missed packets Enable for short-disconnection resilience with a compatible adapter (Chapter 5.8)
cleanupEmptyChildNamespaces false Remove dynamic child namespaces once they have no sockets Enable with dynamic namespaces per tenant/room

22.2 Server — Engine.IO layer

Option Default What it does When to change
pingInterval 25000 ms Interval between server PINGs Faster dead-connection detection (lower) or fewer wakeups (higher); keep below every proxy idle timeout
pingTimeout 20000 ms Time to wait for a PONG before closing (ping timeout) Raise on very slow networks; lower for faster detection
upgradeTimeout 10000 ms Max time to complete a transport upgrade Rarely
maxHttpBufferSize 1e6 (1 MB) Max size of one message (and polling body); larger → connection closed Lower (e.g. 1e5) for small JSON apps (DoS protection); raise deliberately for bigger payloads
allowRequest — (req, cb) => cb(err, allowed) called for handshake/upgrade requests Origin checks (CSWSH), IP rules, early rejection
transports ["polling", "websocket"] Allowed transports ("webtransport" opt-in) WebSocket-only setups; enable WebTransport
allowUpgrades true Allow polling → WebSocket/WebTransport upgrades Rarely disabled (debugging)
perMessageDeflate false WebSocket compression (true or ws options; threshold defaults to 1024) Large compressible messages; costs memory/CPU per connection
httpCompression true (threshold 1024 bytes) gzip/deflate for polling responses Disable if a proxy already compresses
wsEngine require("ws").Server WebSocket server implementation (must match the ws interface) Alternative engines (e.g. the C++ eiows addon mentioned in engine.io's docs) — verify maintenance
initialPacket — Extra packet concatenated to the handshake response Rare
cookie false Set an io cookie (name, path, httpOnly, sameSite, …; defaults when enabled: name: "io", path: "/", httpOnly: true, sameSite: "lax") Cookie-based sticky sessions at a load balancer
cors — (disabled) Options for the cors package, applied to HTTP (polling) requests Cross-origin browsers. Doesn't protect WebSocket (Chapter 6.5)
allowEIO3 false Accept Socket.IO v2 clients (Engine.IO protocol 3) During v2 → v4 migrations

22.3 Server — attach layer

Option Default What it does When to change
path (see above) Path to capture —
destroyUpgrade true For WebSocket upgrade requests on other paths, end the socket if nothing has written a response within destroyUpgradeTimeout (engine.io checks socket.bytesWritten <= 0) Set false if another upgrade handler on the same HTTP server may take longer than the timeout to answer (e.g. it does async authentication before completing the handshake). A second WebSocket server that answers immediately is not affected
destroyUpgradeTimeout 1000 ms Delay before unhandled upgrade requests are ended Rarely
addTrailingSlash true Normalize the path with a trailing slash (/socket.io/) Set false (and on the client) if a proxy/router strips trailing slashes and you get 404s

When destroyUpgrade bites: "my other WebSocket endpoint on the same server fails to connect, but only when its handshake is slow" → its upgrade handler awaited something (DB/auth) for more than 1 s before answering, and engine.io ended the idle socket. Set destroyUpgrade: false or raise destroyUpgradeTimeout. (Behaviour read from engine.io 6.6.11 attach().)

22.4 Client — Manager options (the connection)

Option Default What it does
forceNew false Always create a new Manager (separate connection)
multiplex true Reuse the cached Manager for other namespaces on the same URL
path "/socket.io/" HTTP path (must match server)
reconnection true Automatic reconnection
reconnectionAttempts Infinity Max attempts before reconnect_failed
reconnectionDelay 1000 ms Initial back-off
reconnectionDelayMax 5000 ms Max back-off (cap applied after jitter)
randomizationFactor 0.5 Jitter (±50%)
timeout 20000 ms Timeout of each connection attempt (connect_error: timeout)
autoConnect true Connect immediately in io(); set false to call socket.connect() later (e.g. after login, after registering listeners)
parser default Must match the server's parser

22.5 Client — Socket options (per namespace)

Option Default What it does
auth — Object or (cb) => cb({...}) sent in the CONNECT packet → socket.handshake.auth. Use the function form to refresh tokens on every attempt
ackTimeout — Default timeout for acks; enables the error-first ack signature
retries — Number of retries for unacknowledged emits (requires acks; packets queued one at a time)

22.6 Client — Engine.IO options (transport level)

Option Default What it does / when to use
transports ["polling", "websocket", "webtransport"] Order of transports. Accepts names or (4.8+) implementations: [Fetch, WebSocket]
tryAllTransports false (4.8+) If the first transport fails, try the next ones instead of aborting
upgrade true Try to upgrade from polling
rememberUpgrade false If WebSocket worked before (in this page), start directly with it
query — Extra query parameters on every Engine.IO request (socket.handshake.query)
extraHeaders — Extra HTTP headers. In browsers only applies to polling; Node applies them to all transports
withCredentials false Send cookies with cross-origin polling requests (needed for cookie auth and LB-cookie stickiness — verified in Chapter 23). In Node, also enables the client's cookie jar
timestampRequests timestamps on (the client adds t= unless set to false) Cache-busting parameter on polling requests
timestampParam "t" Name of that parameter
requestTimeout — Timeout for polling HTTP requests (ms)
transportOptions {} Per-transport overrides, e.g. { polling: { extraHeaders }, webtransport: { serverCertificateHashes } }
closeOnBeforeunload false Close silently on beforeunload (uniform behaviour across browsers)
addTrailingSlash true Match the server's setting
protocols [] WebSocket sub-protocols
perMessageDeflate { threshold: 1024 } WebSocket compression settings (Node client)
useNativeTimers false Use native timers even if mocked (fake timers in tests)
autoUnref false Heartbeat timer doesn't keep the Node process alive
host, hostname, secure, port from URL Override parts of the URL
agent false HTTP agent (Node), e.g. for proxies
pfx, key, passphrase, cert, ca, ciphers — TLS options (Node)
rejectUnauthorized true Verify TLS certificates (Node). Never false in production

22.7 Configuration recipes

Small internal app, single server

new Server(httpServer, { serveClient: false, maxHttpBufferSize: 1e5, cors: { origin: APP_ORIGIN } });

Public SPA behind a load balancer, cookies for auth

// server
new Server(httpServer, {
  serveClient: false,
  cors: { origin: ["https://app.example.com"], credentials: true },
  allowRequest: (req, cb) => cb(null, req.headers.origin === "https://app.example.com"),
  adapter: createAdapter(pub, sub),
  maxHttpBufferSize: 1e5,
});
// client
io("https://rt.example.com", { withCredentials: true });

Mobile apps only, no sticky sessions

// server
new Server(httpServer, { transports: ["websocket"], serveClient: false, adapter: createAdapter(pub, sub) });
// clients: transports ["websocket"] / forceWebsockets(true) / setTransports(['websocket'])

High-churn mobile networks, recovery

new Server(httpServer, {
  pingInterval: 20000, pingTimeout: 15000,
  connectionStateRecovery: { maxDisconnectionDuration: 60_000 },
  adapter: createAdapter(redisClient),   // from "@socket.io/redis-streams-adapter" (supports recovery)
});

Sharing the HTTP server with another WebSocket service whose handshake is slow

new Server(httpServer, { path: "/socket.io/", destroyUpgrade: false });

Large fleets (thundering herd protection) — client

io(URL, { reconnectionDelay: 2000, reconnectionDelayMax: 30000, randomizationFactor: 0.5 });

Next: Chapter 23 — Hands-on exercises and labs (with solutions)

Hands-On Labs and Exercises (with Solutions)

Goal: turn reading into skill. Four labs let you see the protocol, the load-balancer behaviour and session recovery with your own eyes; then 18 exercises from beginner to senior, each with a solution.

Every lab output below is real (Socket.IO 4.8.4, Node 22, nginx 1.24, HAProxy 2.8, Redis 7). Exercises 5, 6 and 7 were run as automated tests and pass; the others reuse code already verified in earlier chapters (references given).

Setup for all labs:

mkdir sio-labs && cd sio-labs && npm init -y
npm install socket.io socket.io-client @socket.io/redis-adapter redis

Lab A — Speak the protocol by hand with curl

Goal: understand exactly what a Socket.IO client does, by doing it yourself.

// lab-server.js
const { Server } = require("socket.io");
const io = new Server(3000);
io.on("connection", (socket) => {
  socket.on("hello", (name, ack) => ack(`hi ${name}!`));
  socket.emit("welcome", "you are connected");
});

Run node lab-server.js, then in another terminal:

B="http://localhost:3000/socket.io/?EIO=4&transport=polling"

# 1) Engine.IO handshake
OPEN=$(curl -s "$B"); echo "$OPEN"
SID=$(echo "${OPEN:1}" | python3 -c "import sys,json;print(json.load(sys.stdin)['sid'])")

# 2) Socket.IO CONNECT to the main namespace
curl -s -X POST -d '40' "$B&sid=$SID"

# 3) Receive (long-poll): the CONNECT reply + the "welcome" event, separated by \x1e
curl -s "$B&sid=$SID" | cat -v

# 4) Emit "hello" with ack id 1
curl -s -X POST -d '421["hello","curl"]' "$B&sid=$SID"

# 5) Receive the ack
curl -s "$B&sid=$SID"

# 6) A made-up session id (what you get without sticky sessions)
curl -s "http://localhost:3000/socket.io/?EIO=4&transport=polling&sid=doesnotexist"

# 7) An old (v2) client protocol version
curl -s "http://localhost:3000/socket.io/?EIO=3&transport=polling"

Real output:

1) 0{"sid":"wZjDFReArxzgbgpgAAAA","upgrades":["websocket"],"pingInterval":25000,"pingTimeout":20000,"maxPayload":1000000}
2) ok
3) 40{"sid":"d2PtE1O5hpksWhMjAAAA"}^^42["welcome","you are connected"]
4) ok
5) 431["hi curl!"]
6) {"code":1,"message":"Session ID unknown"}
7) {"code":5,"message":"Unsupported protocol version"}

(^^ is how cat -v prints the \x1e record separator.)

Questions to answer yourself (answers in Chapter 7): Why are the two sid values different? What does the 4 prefix mean? What would happen if you waited 45+ seconds before step 3 without answering pings? (The server sends 2 pings in the long-poll responses; if you don't POST 3 back within pingTimeout, the session is closed.)

Lab B — Load balancers, sticky sessions and the Redis adapter

Goal: reproduce the two scaling problems (Chapter 8) and their fixes.

Requirements: Redis on localhost:6379 (here: port 6390), nginx, HAProxy.

// node.js — one Socket.IO node; run 3 copies on ports 5001-5003
const { Server } = require("socket.io");
const { createClient } = require("redis");
const { createAdapter } = require("@socket.io/redis-adapter");

(async () => {
  const pub = createClient({ url: "redis://localhost:6390" }); const sub = pub.duplicate();
  pub.on("error", console.error); sub.on("error", console.error);
  await Promise.all([pub.connect(), sub.connect()]);
  const port = Number(process.argv[2]);
  const io = new Server(port, { adapter: createAdapter(pub, sub) });
  io.engine.on("connection_error", (e) => console.log(`[${port}] engine error code=${e.code} ${e.message}`));
  io.on("connection", (s) => {
    s.join("all");
    s.on("whoami", (ack) => ack({ node: port, transport: s.conn.transport.name }));
    s.on("shout", (m) => io.to("all").emit("shout", `${m} (via node ${port})`));
  });
})();

# nginx.conf (listen 8080) — the Chapter 8 configuration
events { worker_connections 1024; }
http {
  map $http_upgrade $connection_upgrade { default upgrade; '' close; }
  upstream socket_nodes {
    hash $remote_addr consistent;
    server 127.0.0.1:5001; server 127.0.0.1:5002; server 127.0.0.1:5003;
  }
  server {
    listen 8080;
    location /socket.io/ {
      proxy_pass http://socket_nodes;
      proxy_http_version 1.1;
      proxy_set_header Upgrade $http_upgrade;
      proxy_set_header Connection $connection_upgrade;
      proxy_set_header Host $host;
      proxy_set_header X-Forwarded-For $proxy_add_x_forwarded_for;
      proxy_read_timeout 120s;
      proxy_send_timeout 120s;
      proxy_buffering off;
    }
  }
}

# haproxy-cookie.cfg (port 8081): round-robin + cookie stickiness
defaults
  mode http
  timeout connect 5s
  timeout client 120s
  timeout server 120s
  timeout tunnel 1h
frontend http_front
  bind *:8081
  default_backend socket_nodes
backend socket_nodes
  balance roundrobin
  cookie SERVERID insert indirect nocache
  server app01 127.0.0.1:5001 check cookie app01
  server app02 127.0.0.1:5002 check cookie app02
  server app03 127.0.0.1:5003 check cookie app03

Make a second HAProxy config on port 8082 without the two cookie parts (pure round-robin). Start: three nodes, nginx -c $PWD/nginx.conf, haproxy -f haproxy-cookie.cfg, haproxy -f haproxy-nosticky.cfg. Then connect clients:

// lbtest.js (abridged)
const test = (label, url, opts) => /* connect with reconnection:false, call "whoami" */;
await test("nginx (polling→websocket)", "http://localhost:8080");
await test("haproxy cookie sticky, polling only", "http://localhost:8081", { transports: ["polling"], withCredentials: true });
await test("haproxy cookie sticky, NO withCredentials", "http://localhost:8081", { transports: ["polling"] });
await test("haproxy NO sticky, polling only", "http://localhost:8082", { transports: ["polling"] });
await test("haproxy NO sticky, websocket only", "http://localhost:8082", { transports: ["websocket"] });
// cross-node broadcast: client A on node 5001 shouts, client B on node 5002 listens

Real results:

nginx (polling→websocket): OK node=5001 transport=websocket (client: websocket)
haproxy cookie sticky, polling only: OK (3 runs → nodes 5001, 5002, 5003)
haproxy cookie sticky, NO withCredentials: connect_error xhr poll error / xhr post error (3 of 3 runs)
haproxy NO sticky, polling only: connect_error xhr poll error
haproxy NO sticky, websocket only: OK node=5002 transport=websocket
cross-node broadcast received by B: hello (via node 5001)

node logs:  [5002] engine error code=1 Session ID unknown   (several, from the failing cases)

What you learned: (1) polling needs stickiness, WebSocket-only doesn't; (2) cookie stickiness only works if the client sends the cookie back (withCredentials: true — in browsers this matters for cross-origin setups); (3) the failure signature is always Session ID unknown (engine error code 1); (4) the Redis adapter makes broadcasts cross nodes.

Extension: remove proxy_http_version 1.1 and the Upgrade/Connection headers from the nginx config and watch the client stay on polling.

Lab C — Connection state recovery

const { Server } = require("socket.io");
const { io } = require("socket.io-client");

const srv = new Server(4600, { connectionStateRecovery: { maxDisconnectionDuration: 60_000 } });
srv.on("connection", (socket) => {
  console.log(`server: connection id=${socket.id} recovered=${socket.recovered} rooms=${[...socket.rooms]}`);
  if (!socket.recovered) {
    socket.join("news");
    socket.data.name = "alice";
  }
  socket.on("cut-me", () => socket.conn.close());     // simulate a network drop (recoverable)
});

const client = io("http://localhost:4600", { reconnectionDelay: 300, reconnectionDelayMax: 300 });
client.on("connect", () => console.log(`client: connect id=${client.id} recovered=${client.recovered}`));
client.on("disconnect", (r) => {
  console.log("client: disconnect", r);
  srv.to("news").emit("news", "sent while you were offline");   // broadcast during the outage
});
client.on("news", (m) => console.log("client: news ->", m));

let step = 0;
client.on("connect", async () => {
  if (++step === 1) {
    srv.to("news").emit("news", "first news");
    setTimeout(() => client.emit("cut-me"), 200);
  } else {
    const [s] = await srv.fetchSockets();
    console.log("server: socket.data after recovery =", s.data);
    setTimeout(() => { client.close(); srv.close(); }, 200);
  }
});

Real output:

server: connection id=kZ7vlpsnXqdTmdgRAAAA recovered=false rooms=kZ7vlpsnXqdTmdgRAAAA
client: connect id=kZ7vlpsnXqdTmdgRAAAA recovered=false
client: news -> first news
client: disconnect transport close
server: connection id=kZ7vlpsnXqdTmdgRAAAA recovered=true rooms=kZ7vlpsnXqdTmdgRAAAA,news
client: news -> sent while you were offline
client: connect id=kZ7vlpsnXqdTmdgRAAAA recovered=true
server: socket.data after recovery = { name: 'alice' }

What you learned: after recovery the same socket id, rooms and socket.data come back, and the event broadcast during the outage is replayed. Note the order: the missed news arrived before the client's connect handler ran — so register your event listeners before connecting, and don't assume connect comes first.

Extension: change socket.conn.close() to socket.disconnect() (a deliberate server-side disconnect) — recovery won't happen (io server disconnect is not a recoverable reason and the client doesn't even reconnect automatically).

Lab D — WebTransport

Follow Chapter 18 step by step (certificate, HTTP/3 server, Node client with the @fails-components/webtransport implementation). Expected: client: transport = webtransport, and with default client transports, polling → upgrade to webtransport.

Exercises

Levels: [E] entry, [M] mid, [S] senior. Try each before reading the solution.

Exercise 1 [E] — Echo with acknowledgement

Write a server event echo that returns whatever it receives, and a client that prints the response with a 3-second timeout.

Solution 
// server
io.on("connection", (socket) => {
  socket.on("echo", (data, ack) => { if (typeof ack === "function") ack(data); });
});
// client
const res = await socket.timeout(3000).emitWithAck("echo", { hello: "world" });
console.log(res); // { hello: "world" }

Exercise 2 [E] — Join/leave rooms with notifications

Implement room:join / room:leave; notify other members (not the sender) with room:user-joined / room:user-left; ack with { ok: true }.

Solution 
socket.on("room:join", async (room, ack) => {
  await socket.join(room);
  socket.to(room).emit("room:user-joined", { id: socket.id, room });
  ack?.({ ok: true });
});
socket.on("room:leave", async (room, ack) => {
  await socket.leave(room);
  socket.to(room).emit("room:user-left", { id: socket.id, room });
  ack?.({ ok: true });
});
socket.on("disconnecting", () => {
  for (const room of socket.rooms) if (room !== socket.id) socket.to(room).emit("room:user-left", { id: socket.id, room });
});

In production: authorize the join first (Chapter 4.7).

Exercise 3 [E] — Private messages that reach every device

User 42 has a phone and two tabs open. Send them a DM from a REST endpoint.

Solution

Join a user room at connection (after auth) and emit to it:

io.on("connection", (socket) => socket.join(`user:${socket.data.user.id}`));
app.post("/api/dm", (req, res) => {
  io.to(`user:${req.body.to}`).emit("dm:new", { from: req.user.id, text: req.body.text });
  res.sendStatus(202);
});

Never map userId → socket.id (breaks with multiple devices, reconnections, multiple servers).

Exercise 4 [M] — Typing indicator

Throttle to one event per 2 s per user, droppable, excluded sender, auto-expiring on the receiver.

Solution

See Chapter 13.3 (client throttle + volatile, server socket.to(room).volatile.emit, receiver timeout of ~5 s).

Exercise 5 [M] — Auth with token expiry (tested)

Reject connections without a valid token. Disconnect the client exactly when its token expires; the client must not auto-reconnect with the expired token.

Solution 
io.use((socket, next) => {
  const payload = verifyToken(socket.handshake.auth.token);   // throws/returns null if invalid
  if (!payload) return next(new Error("UNAUTHORIZED"));
  socket.data.exp = payload.exp * 1000;
  next();
});

io.on("connection", (socket) => {
  const timer = setTimeout(() => {
    socket.emit("auth:expired");
    socket.disconnect(true);
  }, Math.max(0, socket.data.exp - Date.now()));
  socket.on("disconnect", () => clearTimeout(timer));
});

// client
socket.on("disconnect", async (reason) => {
  if (reason === "io server disconnect") {        // no automatic reconnection for this reason
    await refreshToken();                          // auth is a function → picks up the new token
    socket.connect();
  }
});

Test result: bad token → connect_error: UNAUTHORIZED; valid short token → client disconnect with reason io server disconnect and socket.active === false.

Exercise 6 [M] — Rate limiter without libraries (tested)

Allow bursts of 5 events, refilling 5 tokens/second; over-limit events must receive { ok: false, error: "RATE_LIMITED" } via their ack and must not reach handlers.

Solution 
io.on("connection", (socket) => {
  let tokens = 5, last = Date.now();
  socket.use(([event, ...args], next) => {
    const now = Date.now();
    tokens = Math.min(5, tokens + ((now - last) / 1000) * 5);
    last = now;
    if (tokens < 1) {
      const ack = args.at(-1);
      if (typeof ack === "function") ack({ ok: false, error: "RATE_LIMITED" });
      return;                                  // don't call next(): the handler never runs
    }
    tokens -= 1;
    next();
  });
});

Test result: 8 simultaneous emits → 5 succeeded, 3 got RATE_LIMITED. In a cluster, key the limiter by user id in Redis (Chapter 6.7).

Exercise 7 [M] — A live poll with broadcast acks (tested)

A host starts a poll; every connected client answers; the host receives all answers within 1 s — and still gets the answers that arrived if someone doesn't respond.

Solution 
socket.on("start-poll", (question, ack) => {
  // callback form: on timeout `err` is set, but `answers` still has every reply received
  io.timeout(1000).emit("poll", question, (err, answers) => ack({ complete: !err, answers }));
});

// voters
socket.on("poll", (question, ack) => ack("yes"));

Test result: with one connected client that never answered, the result was { complete: false, answers: [3 answers] }. The Promise form (await io.timeout(1000).emitWithAck(...)) rejected in the same situation and lost the 3 answers — that's why the callback form is the right choice here.

Exercise 8 [M] — Fix the bug: duplicated messages

socket.on("connect", () => {
  socket.on("chat", (m) => render(m));
});
Solution

Each reconnection adds another listener. Register socket.on("chat", render) once, outside the connect handler (Chapter 5.6).

Exercise 9 [M] — Fix the bug: events lost right after connecting

io.on("connection", async (socket) => {
  const rooms = await db.roomsFor(socket.data.user.id);
  socket.join(rooms);
  socket.on("message:send", handler);
});
Solution

Events sent by the client during the await find no listener and are dropped (verified in Chapter 12, C3). Register handlers synchronously first, or load the rooms in an io.use middleware and keep the connection handler synchronous (Chapter 13.17).

Exercise 10 [M] — Fix the bug: "Cannot GET /socket.io/"

const app = express();
const server = http.createServer(app);
const io = new Server(server);
app.listen(3000);
Solution

app.listen() creates a second HTTP server that Socket.IO isn't attached to. Use server.listen(3000).

Exercise 11 [M] — Fix the bug: the ack callback receives null as the response

socket.timeout(5000).emit("getUser", 42, (user) => console.log(user.name));
Solution

With .timeout(), the first callback argument is the error: (err, user) => { if (err) …; console.log(user.name); }.

Exercise 12 [M] — Write an integration test

Test that a broadcast to a room excludes the sender.

Solution

Chapter 10.3, test "room broadcast excludes sender" (and the lesson about not keeping "the current socket" in a shared variable).

Exercise 13 [S] — Online users across a cluster

Show "N users online" (distinct users, multiple tabs count once), correct with 10 nodes.

Solution

Per-user connection sets in Redis with heartbeats and a grace period (Chapter 13.2); count users with SCARD on a set of online user ids updated when a user's connection count goes 0→1 / 1→0. Avoid fetchSockets() for this at scale.

Exercise 14 [S] — Never lose a chat message

Messages must survive offline periods, reloads and lost acks, without duplicates.

Solution

Client outbox in IndexedDB with client ids + idempotent server insert (Chapter 21, verified across a simulated reload) + server-side persistence with sync-after-last-id on every connect + client dedupe (Chapter 5.9).

Exercise 15 [S] — Upload a 20 MB file over the socket

Solution

Chunk it (e.g. 256 KB) and await an ack per chunk for backpressure and resumability (Chapter 13.10) — or better, upload over HTTP to object storage and use the socket only for progress.

Exercise 16 [S] — Kick a banned user everywhere, now

Solution

io.in("user:" + id).emit("session:revoked"); io.in("user:" + id).disconnectSockets(true); — cluster-wide with an adapter; also revoke tokens so reconnection fails in middleware.

Exercise 17 [S] — Protect against Cross-Site WebSocket Hijacking

Solution

allowRequest origin allow-list (verified: forged origin → HTTP 403 for polling / 400 for the WebSocket upgrade, engine code 4), token auth in auth rather than ambient cookies, SameSite cookies (Chapter 6.5).

Exercise 18 [S] — Design review

Your team proposes: "one namespace per chat conversation, created dynamically; the client connects to /conv-<id> to join a conversation." Review it.

Solution

Namespaces are client-chosen, so authorization must be done in namespace middleware for every connection; thousands of dynamic namespaces cost memory (need cleanupEmptyChildNamespaces), each one is a separate Socket object and CONNECT round-trip, and server-side membership changes (removing someone from a conversation) are harder. Rooms (conversation:<id>) joined by the server after authorization are simpler and cheaper; keep namespaces for separating concerns (/admin) or tenants.

Next: Chapter 24 — Visual guide (diagrams)

Visual Guide (Diagrams)

Goal: the most important mechanisms of the book as pictures. The diagrams are written in Mermaid, which GitHub, GitLab, many IDEs and documentation tools render as real diagrams. Every diagram here was rendered successfully with the Mermaid CLI. The flows match behaviour verified in earlier chapters (packet captures in Chapter 7, recovery lab in Chapter 23).

24.1 The layers

flowchart TB
    App["Your code<br/>socket.emit('chat', msg)"]
    SIO["Socket.IO layer<br/>events · acks · rooms · namespaces · broadcast · reconnection · middleware"]
    EIO["Engine.IO layer<br/>handshake · heartbeat · upgrade · packet framing"]
    T1["HTTP long-polling"]
    T2["WebSocket"]
    T3["WebTransport"]
    TCP["TCP"]
    QUIC["QUIC / UDP"]
    App --> SIO --> EIO
    EIO --> T1 & T2 & T3
    T1 --> TCP
    T2 --> TCP
    T3 --> QUIC

24.2 Connection: handshake, CONNECT and upgrade

sequenceDiagram
    autonumber
    participant C as Client
    participant S as Server
    C->>S: GET /socket.io/?EIO=4&transport=polling
    S-->>C: 0{"sid":"…","upgrades":["websocket"],"pingInterval":25000,"pingTimeout":20000,"maxPayload":1000000}
    C->>S: POST 40 (CONNECT "/" + auth)
    Note over S: io.use() middlewares run
    alt accepted
        S-->>C: 40{"sid":"<socket id>"} (via pending GET)
        Note over S: io.on("connection") fires
    else rejected
        S-->>C: 44{"message":"…","data":{…}}
        Note over C: connect_error, socket.active = false
    end
    C->>S: WebSocket upgrade request (same sid)
    C->>S: 2probe
    S-->>C: 3probe
    Note over C: pause polling, flush pending requests
    C->>S: 5 (upgrade)
    Note over C,S: all traffic now over WebSocket
    S-->>C: 2 (ping) every pingInterval
    C->>S: 3 (pong)

24.3 Event with acknowledgement

sequenceDiagram
    participant C as Client
    participant S as Server
    C->>S: 421["hello","world"]  (EVENT, ack id 1)
    Note over S: handler(data, ack)
    S-->>C: 431[{"ok":true}]  (ACK id 1)
    Note over C: callback / emitWithAck resolves
    Note over C: with .timeout(ms): no ACK in time → callback(err) / Promise rejects

24.4 Client connection state machine

stateDiagram-v2
    [*] --> Connecting: io(url) / socket.connect()
    Connecting --> Connected: CONNECT accepted
    Connecting --> Rejected: middleware next(err)
    Connecting --> Reconnecting: transport failure / timeout
    Connected --> Reconnecting: ping timeout / transport close / transport error
    Connected --> Disconnected: socket.disconnect()  [io client disconnect]
    Connected --> Disconnected: server socket.disconnect()  [io server disconnect]
    Reconnecting --> Connecting: after back-off (1s, 2s, 4s… ≤5s, ±50%)
    Reconnecting --> Failed: reconnectionAttempts reached
    Rejected --> Connecting: fix auth, socket.connect()
    Disconnected --> Connecting: socket.connect()
    Failed --> Connecting: socket.connect()

24.5 Heartbeat and dead-connection detection

sequenceDiagram
    participant S as Server
    participant C as Client
    loop every pingInterval (25 s)
        S->>C: PING (2)
        C-->>S: PONG (3) within pingTimeout (20 s)
    end
    Note over C: phone enters a tunnel — nothing arrives any more
